RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

# 8 OPERATING PROCEDURES

## 8.0 General Operating Procedures

### 8.0.1 Objectives

8.0.1.1 Our primary operations objective is to conduct our air transport activity safely. It is also Ryanair policy that Flight Operations shall be conducted efficiently and punctually. This policy devolves on Flight Crew as their basic duty.

8.0.1.2 Commanders and Co-Pilots are required to adhere to the policies and directions contained in the Operations Manual.

### 8.0.1.3 Endangering Safety

CAT.GEN.MPA.175

Ryanair personnel and service providers shall take all reasonable measures to ensure that no person recklessly or negligently acts or omits to act so as to:

- endanger an aeroplane or person therein.
- cause or permit an aeroplane to endanger any person or property.

Ryanair approved procedures ensure that flight crew undergo a psychological assessment before commencing line flying in order to:

1. identify psychological attributes and suitability of the flight crew in respect of the work environment; and
2. reduce the likelihood of negative interference with the safe operation of the aircraft.

### 8.0.1.4 Unauthorised Carriage

Ryanair Commanders shall take all reasonable measures to ensure that no person secretes himself/herself or cargo on board a Ryanair aircraft. This is effected through security checks in the cabin, galleys and toilets, and by the pilot external walkaround checking holds and wheelwells. The handling agent checks that all holds are secured and closed prior to departure.

### 8.0.2 Standard Operating Procedures

8.0.2.1 Standard Operating Procedures (SOPs) fall into two categories, the first of which identifies a core of mandatory actions or procedures that must always be performed because the failure to do so has a direct adverse impact on the safety of operation.

These SOPs include formal items such as Standard Call-outs, altimeter setting and checking procedures, emergency drills, and non-exceedance of limitations.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

# PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

The formal SOPs are also categorised by the imperatives of “shall” and “must” and include those procedures which require a singular means of compliance e.g. engine failure at take-off.

The operating philosophies are presented in the FCOM Vol 1, Normal procedures, QRH and FCTM (Flight Crew Training Manual).

8.0.2.2

The second category of standard operating procedure has the purpose of standardising procedures to make it possible to crew any two Flight Crew members together on a non-regular basis without lowering safety standards.

The consequential requirement is that personal methods or practices shall not be introduced. New or changed procedures may only be introduced or promulgated on the authority of the NPFO. The standardised operating procedures must always be used.

The operating philosophies are enunciated in the Vol 1 Normal procedures, QRH and FCTM.

8.0.2.3

A significant number of operating practices allow the discretion to the Commander to decide how the flight should be flown. These are characterised with the need to exercise sound judgement and prudent assessment and may be referred to as sound operating practises (included under the more general heading of airmanship during an earlier period).

There has been a trend within the industry to refer to all operating activity under the heading of SOPs or a desire to specify all procedures within such a concept. Such broadening of the scope of SOPs diminishes the merit and value of the concept and hence the need to highlight this differentiation.

## 8.0.3

## Monitoring and Cross Checking

8.0.3.1

The primary task either Flight Crew member has to perform is to fly the aircraft. Monitoring and look-out are important secondary duties. Full freedom to monitor can only be achieved by making the most efficient use of flight deck resources and aircraft equipment.

8.0.3.2

The purpose of monitoring is to detect unintentional deviation from the planned flight path parameters and permit effective correction. FCOM Vol 1, Normal procedures directs the crew to the monitoring policy which creates the ideal of full awareness of both Flight Crew members as to aircraft position, situation and configuration.

Both Flight Crew members must be alert to the possibility of the other making errors in calculation and tabular selections, e.g. take-off or landing weights, engine power settings, speeds, descent range etc. Figures shall not be blindly accepted. There is a need to always check mentally for gross errors.

# PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

8.0.3.3 For monitoring in a Non-Normal or Emergency situation, refer to the Checklist introduction section in the QRH for normal and non normal checklist usage, and Chapter 8 in the FCTM. Section 8.0.6 also refers.

## 8.0.4 Briefing

8.0.4.1 The object of briefing is to ensure that both Flight Crew members are aware of and agree with a proposed plan of action.

8.0.4.2 Formal briefings are required before each take-off and prior to each descent and landing. The Take-off briefing is presented in FCOM Vol 1, Normal procedures. The timings of this and other briefings are presented in FCOM Vol 1, Normal procedures. Section 8.3 also refers.

8.0.4.3 The PF shall be aware of the need to let the PM know how he intends to conduct the operation. The term PM shall be applied in context as described in the FCOM Vol 1.

8.0.4.4 These briefing activities produce an environment in which the non-handling Flight Crew member can carry out his prime function of monitoring the flight more effectively.

8.0.4.5 Airfield Briefs contain important information for operations and when published, shall be used in the briefing processes including RIBETS and DALTA.

## 8.0.5 Checklists

### 8.0.5.1 General

One of the most important factors in good flight deck management is the proper utilisation of the checklist. Reliable normal operations and repeatable success in dealing with abnormal and emergency situations are clearly attributable to the checklist system. The system does, however, depend absolutely on the command being given for the correct checklist, and ensuring its completion.

Modern aircraft are increasingly complex. Crews “must know” items consist of recall items in QRH, limitations in FCOM Vol 1, and SOPs as presented in Vol 1. Crews shall complete appropriate checklists and time permitting refer to technical descriptions in FCOM Vol 2 and Boeing bulletins as appropriate. It is not best practise to perform actions other than QRH memory items from memory. Flight Crew members shall be familiar with the Checklist introduction section in the QRH for normal and non normal checklist usage, and Chapter 8 in the FCTM, Non Normal Operations. Guidance is provided on the balance required between “recall” technical knowledge and relying on written technical descriptions in FCOM Vol 2 (and Boeing bulletins) in this chapter – Basic Aerodynamics and System Knowledge.

The operating policies and procedures are enunciated in the FCOM Vol 1 Normal procedures, QRH and FCTM

## 8.0.5.2 Location of Manuals and Checklists

All normal, non normal checklists and Manuals required to complete assigned duties are contained in EFB/VPOS.

Normal and Non-Normal Checklists are contained in the ships library, in the Flight Crew Operations Manual (FCOM) Vol 1, the Quick Reference Handbook (QRH), and laminated checklists on the glareshield in the cockpit.

## 8.0.5.3 Normal Checklists

The operation and philosophy of checklist usage is described in FCOM Vol 1, Normal procedure <ryr>. Supplementary Procedure, and QRH, Checklist Introduction.

Where a checklist is interrupted, the check must be discontinued and when called for again, the challenge shall begin again at the last item previously called.

If any untoward situation should develop or discrepancy arise while a checklist is being carried out causing distraction then the complete checklist shall be reread.

## 8.0.5.4 Non-Normal and Emergency Procedures

Non-Normal and Emergency Procedures and the corresponding checklists require to be dealt with on a different basis.

This is described in the OMB, QRH, Non-Normal Checklist Operation and FCTM.

This does not mean that in all emergency/abnormal situations the Commander will take over the controls.

## 8.0.5.5 Overweight Landing

8.0.5.5.1 An Overweight landing is recommended in the following conditions:

- A serious malfunction that affects the airworthiness of the aircraft.
- A condition where an expeditious landing would prevent a non-normal situation developing into an unsafe situation (reduce the exposure to a degrading level of safety).
- A serious illness on board requiring prompt medical attention.
- If directed by the QRH to “land at the nearest suitable airfield”.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26</ryr>

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

Overweight landings may be safely accomplished by using normal landing procedures and techniques. There are no adverse handling characteristics associated with overweight landings. Landing distance is normally less than take-off distances for flaps 30 or 40 landings at all gross weights.

If stopping distance is a concern, reduce landing weight as much as possible. At the Captain’s discretion reduce weight by holding at low altitude with a high drag configuration (gear down) to achieve maximum fuel burn off.

Overweight autolands are not recommended. Autopilots on Boeing airplanes are not certified for autolands above maximum certified landing weight. At higher than normal speeds and weights, the performance of these systems may not be satisfactory and has not been thoroughly tested. An automatic approach may be attempted however the pilot should disengage the autopilot prior to flare height and accomplish a manual landing.

In an emergency, should the pilot determine that an overweight landing is the safest course of action, the approach and landing will be closely monitored by the pilot and the following factors considered:

- Touchdown may be beyond the normal touchdown zone, allow for additional landing distance.
- Touchdown at higher than normal sink rates may result in exceeding structural limits.
- Plan for a go-around or manual landing if autoland performance is unsatisfactory; automatic go arounds can be initiated until just prior to touchdown, and can be continued even if the airplane touches down after initiation of the go-around.

## 8.0.5.6 Suspected Hard Landing

Ryanair policy is that a pilot report is the only factor that consistently identifies a hard landing. If the aircraft commander believes that a hard landing may have occurred, a Technical Log entry must be completed and Maintrol must be informed. A hard landing inspection must be carried out by a suitably qualified engineer before the aircraft can be dispatched. The flight data g reading if available will only be used to determine the level of technical inspection required.

## 8.0.6 Monitoring in a Non-Normal or Emergency Situation

8.0.6.1 The importance of the monitoring of one Flight Crew member by the other during all non-normal and emergency procedures cannot be over-emphasised.

Particular attention is required to monitor the actions when shutting down an engine, and the flight instruments during the completion of the vital actions.

# PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

8.0.6.2 In the event of an engine failure during take-off the engine failure checklist will not be read until the clean enroute configuration is established, or the aircraft has stopped following an RTO, or, in the case of a return landing stabilised level flight is attained.

## 8.0.7 Standard Call-outs

8.0.7.1 Standard Call-outs are SOPs.

The standard call-outs are presented in FCOM Vol 1, Normal Procedures, and flight profiles are presented with configuration actions in FCOM Vol 1, Normal Procedures.

Non-normal flight profiles and configuration actions are presented in the QRH, Non-normal Manoeuvres.

8.0.7.2 Standard Calls, using unequivocal ENGLISH language as challenge and response, form the principal discipline to verify correct flight progress, highlight deviation and provide an alert system to detect crew incapacitation.

It is very important that the exact terminology is used precisely so that misunderstandings do not occur between crew members.

8.0.7.3 If a standard callout is not responded to, a possibility of Flight Crew member incapacitation exists and needs to be addressed with some further identification activity.

The QRH, Checklist Introduction describes Ryanair policy in this area. Operational Policies are described in FCTM, Chapter 1.

## 8.0.8 Communications

### 8.0.8.1 General

Effective communications is an important factor in securing safety in aviation and includes clear unambiguous transmission of messages, careful listening, and positive feedback where appropriate.

Communication refers not only to radio (RT) communication (OMC, Jeppesen Publications (Pubs)). It is ATC communication, inter-cockpit verbal exchange, Cabin Crew liaison and briefings, P.A. announcements and dealings with other Ryanair staff and agents.

## 8.0.8.1.1 Use of Headsets, Crew Member Interphone System and Flight Crew Interphone System

CAT.IDE.A.170, AMC1.IDE.A.170, AMC1.CAT.IDE.A.325

## Flight Crew Interphone System

Ryanair shall not operate an aeroplane on which a Flight Crew of more than one is required unless it is equipped with a Flight Crew interphone system, including headsets and microphones, not of a hand-held type, for use by all members of the Flight Crew.

Ryanair shall not operate an aeroplane with a maximum certificated take-off mass exceeding 15,000 kg or having a maximum approved passenger seating configuration of more than 19 unless it is equipped with a crew member interphone System.

The crew member interphone system required by this paragraph must:

1. Operate independently of the public address system except for handsets, headsets, microphones, selector switches and signalling devices;
2. Provide a means of two-way communication between the Flight Crew compartment and:
   a. Each passenger compartment;
   b. Each galley located other than on a passenger deck level; and
   c. Each remote crew compartment that is not on the passenger deck and is not easily accessible from a passenger compartment.
3. Be readily accessible for use from each of the required Flight Crew stations in the Flight Crew compartment;
4. Be readily accessible for use at required cabin crew member stations close to each separate or pair of floor level emergency exits;
5. Have an alerting system incorporating aural or visual signals for use by Flight Crew members to alert the cabin crew and for use by cabin crew members to alert the Flight Crew;
6. Have a means for the recipient of a call to determine whether it is a normal call or an emergency call; and
7. Provide on the ground a means of two-way communication between ground personnel and at least two Flight Crew members.

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

Each Flight Crew member required to be on flight deck duty shall wear a headset with boom microphone (where available) or equivalent and use it as the primary device to listen to the voice communications with ATS:

- On the ground when receiving the ATC departure clearance via voice communications.
- On the ground when engine(s) are running.
- In flight except when in the Cruise.
- Whenever deemed necessary by the Commander.

The boom microphone or equivalent shall be in a position which permits its use for two-way radio communications.

Headsets shall be used whenever possible during pushback and start up for communication with ground crew. When it is not possible to use headsets, standard ICAO hand signals shall be used with ground crew. On self-manoeuvring stands, unless local bylaws require use of headsets, the marshaller will provide standard hand signals (as per Standardised rules of the Air) from the designated marshalling position for engine start and taxi guidance off the stand.

On self-manoeuvring stands, unless local bylaws require use of headsets, the marshaller will provide standard ICAO hand signals from the designated marshalling position for engine start and taxi guidance on/off the stand.

Ryanair headsets should be left hanging from the pilot grab handles located above the L1 and R1 windows during turnarounds when in use to avoid damage from operation of the L2 or R2 window.

Ryanair supplied pilot headsets, which are not being used, must be stored in the forward facing, storage compartment behind the Right-Hand Seat.

Two headsets are required for dispatch and can be either the Ryanair provided TELEX or a pilot’s personal headset which meets regulatory requirements. MEL refers. Missing or broken Ryanair provided headsets should be recorded in the aircraft’s Technical Log.

## 8.0.8.2 Inter-cockpit Communication

Inter-cockpit communication includes the following formal items:

- Checklists
- Standard Call-outs
- Briefings

- Monitoring Cross Checks

## 8.0.8.3 Reading of Checklists

Reading of checklists requires the use of clear diction and must be audible. Challenge and response checklists will reflect the tone of challenge, and responses shall be equally positive.

Response shall reflect positive unambiguous feedback. This is made more effective by reading and speaking where specified, the standard terminology.

## 8.0.8.4 Briefings

Briefings ensure that both Flight Crew members are aware of the proposed plan of action.

## 8.0.8.5 Drawing Attention to Errors and Deviations

Monitoring and cross checking is only effective to the extent that the PM feels that he can draw attention to errors and deviations. It is important therefore, that the inter cockpit relationship encourages this to happen. This is a function not only of the PF but also of the PM.

## 8.0.8.6 Sterile Cockpit

AMC1 ORO.GEN.110 (f)

Flight Crew shall avoid conversation not directly related to the safe operation of the aircraft from commencement of push back to TOC, and from TOD to engine shutdown on stand. Headsets shall be worn and boom microphones used in preference to hand microphones for communication with ATC during these phases of flight. No unnecessary paperwork shall be accomplished during sterile cockpit operations.

### Sterile Comms

Flight Crew shall avoid operating on or listening to any frequency not required for current operations from commencement of pushback to TOC and from TOD to engine shutdown on stand. The intention is that both pilots are giving full attention to live ATC frequency. All unnecessary calls to handling agents, maintenance, unnecessary weather, are prohibited.

## 8.0.8.7 Cabin Crew Liaison

Cabin Crew Members are called Customer Service Agents (CSAs) and the Cabin Services Supervisor is called the Customer Service Supervisor (CSS). Cabin Crew come under the command of the Commander when they go aboard an aircraft for the purpose of making a flight. To be effective in their safety function the relationship between cabin and cockpit is reinforced by communications.

The operating crew should introduce themselves to one another at the reporting point. The Commander shall then follow the guidance in OMA 8.1.0.2 on where to conduct the briefing.

This will include as a minimum:

1. The aircraft registration and location (if applicable).
2. Establish a security code, review security procedures and security status for the day.
3. Discuss operational issues such as flight times, slots, special considerations, weather/icing conditions/turbulence.
4. Defects which will impact on the operation, e.g. A/C serviceability, air stairs inoperative, health and safety issues, any conflicts, etc.

If the crew complement is incomplete at this time, pertinent issues shall be addressed with all crew prior to dispatch.

The Commander, shall make a point of re-confirming that he would like to be informed of anything untoward which may occur in the cabin such as unusual noises and reports from passengers and shall confirm the cockpit security code once on board. Cabin Crew shall never take for granted that the Flight Crew are aware of serious malfunctions such as engine fires, fuel leaks or loose external structure.

Cabin Crew shall alert the flight deck using the cabin interphone or other prescribed mechanism. When time permits the flight deck shall acknowledge this communication and receive the Cabin Crew report.

Formal reports between cabin and cockpit crew are listed in checklists. Routine or non-operational liaison between cockpit and Cabin Crew shall not take place during climb or descent.

It is particularly important to advise Cabin Crew of the de-icing requirements anticipated throughout the day; brief the associated threats and remind Cabin Crew of their responsibility to report wing contamination to the flight deck. Cabin Crew shall be alerted to the possibility of momentary fumes in the cabin and also to close cabin doors as required. Cabin Crew have received training on the effects

and appearance of contamination on wing surfaces and may be able to make a valuable contribution to safety by advising the Flight Crew of any contaminant on a wing before or after de-icing/anti-icing treatments.

The Cabin Crew will relay any passenger concern about wing contamination to the flight crew and a pre-take-off contamination check will be considered.

## 8.0.8.8 PA Announcements

The passenger PA has a very important safety role as well as being a useful commercial tool. The 'European Action Plan for the Prevention of Runway Incursions' recommends the taxi phase of operations be considered a 'Critical Phase of Flight'. This has implications for the use of the PA system, as detailed below.

The following bullet points outline the safety and operational procedures in relation to the use of the passenger PA system:

- The PA system must be checked for serviceability prior to departure on the first flight of the day. It is prudent this be done prior to passenger boarding. The checklist item 'PA' will be used to confirm that the PA is complete and the equipment is serviceable and confirmed by the No.1 to be audible in the cabin.
- The Captain must always make the initial welcoming PA. This establishes his/her voice, which is important in the event of a subsequent Non Normal or Emergency situation where a passenger PA is required. This PA must be done prior to pushback.
- Only essential PA's will be made during taxi out or taxi in. Stop the aircraft and set the parking brake when doing so. When advising the Cabin Crew to take their seats for departure keep the address short and concise (eg 'Cabin Crew Seats for departure'). Do not let the PA announcements infringe on runway safety.
- During the cruise the Captain, or at his/her discretion the First Officer, should make a PA advising of the expected time of arrival, weather and anything else of interest.
- Only essential or safety related PAs shall be made in the descent or holding. Standard calls "Cabin Crew 10 mins to landing" and "Cabin Crew seats for landing" shall be made at the appropriate time.
- Do not make a PA crossing or entering a runway except in a emergency.
- There is NO requirement to make a PA on taxi in except for essential safety PAs.

When the surface wind at the destination exceeds 20 kt the Captain will make the following PA when releasing Cabin Crew;

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

"Cabin Crew, doors to manual and crosscheck – exercise caution due to high wind."

A laminated PA list is available on the flight deck listing PA text for various circumstances for use by crews.

Use PA laminated text. PAs are presented on the laminated checklist text on the flight deck as follows: on time; delay; diversion – medical/technical; diversion – wx/other; diversion on ground; holding; descent; technical; threat ground; threat airborne 7500.

Note PA following a diversion. Ryanair Policy is that passengers are generally not to disembark at the diversion airport when it is intended that the flight will continue to the original destination for security. Therefore your PA should focus on the short delay to the continued flight to your intended destination.

## 8.0.8.9 Communication with Maintenance Personnel

It shall be considered best practice that in addition to reporting aircraft defects in the technical log, it is extremely helpful to the subsequent maintenance investigations if the Commander amplifies the problem experienced and the indications experienced by verbal debriefing of maintenance personnel. This may be achieved by utilising the base maintenance frequency, by telephone, or any other means.

This process helps to generate improved understanding of operating problems and leads to improved Engineering response.

This process may be utilised in flight, only during periods of reduced workload and consistent with the sterile cockpit policy. When a non normal condition is identified after take-off and the aircraft is not intended to climb to FL100 the above procedure shall be utilised when the requisite checklists are completed and the aircraft is established in a holding pattern or is under radar control and ATC have been advised that communications with engineering is required.

## 8.0.8.10 Crew and Passenger Use of Portable Electronic Devices (PEDs)

## 8.0.8.10.1 Crew Use of Portable Electronic Devices (PEDs)

The Ryanair EFB is classified as a C-PED (Controlled Portable Electronic Device). The only device authorised for the provision of any data associated with the operation of any flight is the company issued Electronic Flight Bag. Information obtained from the EFB may be used for the planning or operation of any Ryanair flight. Crew must follow company procedures to ensure up to date information is available on the EFB.

## 8.0.8.10.1.1 Restrictions on Flight Crew Use of Personal Portable Electronic Devices (PEDs)

The use of PEDs by flight crew other than C-PEDs, on ground or in-flight, is only permitted for operationally justified purposes and subject to the prior approval of the Commander.

Under no circumstance is a flight crew member permitted to use PEDs for entertainment purposes or to connect a PED, by any means, to an operational aircraft headset.

## 8.0.8.10.1.2 Restrictions on Cabin Crew Use of Personal Portable Electronic Devices (PED)

The use of personal PEDs is not permitted during critical stages of the flight or during the performance of any safety and security related duty e.g., pre-departure inspections, boarding and disembarking, security check/search, arming/disarming slides etc.

Cabin Crew members are only permitted to charge a PED on board the aircraft if the power source is kept out of baggage and monitored at all times.

The use of headphones by Cabin Crew is strictly prohibited onboard the aircraft during a flight duty period.

## 8.0.8.10.2 Passenger Use of Portable Electronic Devices (PEDs)

Mobile phones and tablet computers are permitted for use for the whole duration of the flight in Airplane or Flight mode. Devices that do not have Airplane or Flight mode must be switched off and remain switched off for the whole duration of the flight. Mobile phones and other transmitting PED’s must be selected to Airplane or Flight mode from the time the doors are closed for departure until the doors are open on arrival.

## 8.0.8.10.3 All devices that transmit or receive calls or text messages and do not have an Airplane or Flight mode must be completely turned off for take-off and landing and for the duration of the flight except:

Devices adhering to the Bluetooth standard (e.g. wireless headsets that use a Bluetooth connection to a PC) except telephones, may be used during flights but must be switched off during take-off and landing.

Devices that weigh one kilo or less and have an Airplane or Flight mode setting may be used in flight provided the Airplane or Flight mode is enabled after the main doors are closed. Laptops and larger PEDs shall be stowed during taxi, take-off and landing (any other time as requested by the Captain) to prevent possible injuries from projectiles and must not obstruct a passengers exit from a seat row in the event of an emergency.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

The use of the wireless LAN (WLAN) is not permitted on Ryanair aircraft. Any device with this facility (i.e. laptops, electronic games, mobile phones, iPads, etc.) must have the facility switched off if they are to be used in flight. Devices in which it is not possible to deactivate WLAN/WiFi may not be used at any time during flight.

Larger PEDs such as disc players and similar entertainment equipment may be used during flight but must be switched off during take-off and landing.

8.0.8.10.4 Cabin Crew must ensure that all passenger headphones are removed for the Safety Demo when completing ‘cabin secure’ checks. Cabin Crew must ensure that laptops and larger PEDs are properly stowed. Take the opportunity to remind passengers that are using allowed devices to ensure that they are in Airplane or Flight mode.

8.0.8.10.5 During push and hold, whether or not the engines are running, the Commander is authorised to permit the use of mobile phones providing an announcement is made to ensure all phones are switched off prior to take-off.

8.0.8.10.6 Ryanair Boeing 737 aircraft meet the requirements set out in EASA SIB No 20 13-21 2.a and are not susceptible to spurious electromagnetic interference from non-transmitting PEDs in the passenger cabin. However, crews shall be vigilant to the possibility of transmitting PEDs being active in the cabin. Possible indications of spurious electromagnetic interference include noise interference or distortion to the PA system. Possible flight deck indications include erratic cabin pressure control, VHF radio interference or radio altimeter indications. Excessive aileron and elevator activity during the approach with the autopilot engaged. Crews shall always follow Ryanair SOPs and approved checklists in any event. If spurious electromagnetic interference is suspected the aircraft Commander shall instruct the Cabin Crew to ensure that all portable electronic devices are switched off for the remainder of the flight. On landing a Technical Log entry shall be made and an ASR submitted.

8.0.9 Aircraft Operating Limitations

"Each aeroplane shall be operated in compliance with the terms of its Certificate of Airworthiness and within the approved limitations contained in its Aeroplane Flight Manual." The Airplane Flight Manual limitations are expanded into the FCOM Vol 1, which shall be used.

Compliance with this regulation is ensured by adherence to the Ryanair normal and emergency checklists, Standard Operating Procedures and observation of the Aircraft Flight Manual Limitations.

Commanders are responsible for ensuring compliance with this regulation.

## 8.0.10 Co-pilot Flying (ORO.FC.200)

It is the Company's policy that the Co-pilot will maintain flying proficiency. It is recognised in the industry that this activity is part of the important process of developing a safe airline environment. While elements of the process and mechanisms by which safety is achieved is considered elsewhere in the Operations Manual, some constraints in this area must be recognised.

Co-pilots generally have less experience than Commanders who will therefore exercise judgement in deciding which sectors to offer the Co-pilot particularly when the conditions are challenging at the destination airfield. This choice shall be a premeditated one by the Captain who will advise the Co-pilot to allow timely adjustment and re-arrangements of functions. The Commander obviously retains the prerogative to change his decision if circumstances change or deteriorate. The advice also seeks to pre-empt non-judgemental complacent routines such as “leg and leg about” from developing.

A Co-pilot who has successfully completed his/her Command Check may exercise the privilege of his/her licence by operating from the LHS, subject to the occupant of the RHS being an LTC, TRI or TRE and then only at his discretion, pending his formal letter of appointment as a Commander from the NPFO.

The Commander, when flying with inexperienced co-pilots or a new co-pilot, shall perform the take-off or landing himself when the following conditions are experienced:

1. crosswinds more than 2/3rds limiting value.
2. runway is performance limited.
3. take-off and landing RVR's less than 1,000 m.
4. ceiling is less than 100' above decision altitude for a precision approach and 200' above minima (MDA) for a non-precision approach aid.
5. the runway is contaminated.
6. abnormal procedures are required due to system defects.
7. Inexperienced co-pilots shall not conduct the landing when the crosswind is in excess of 15 kt during normal operations. This restriction does not preclude the co-pilot from flying a monitored approach.
8. Visual Approaches.
9. Forecast or reported windshear.

# 8.0.10.1 Handover of Control

The handover of control from the Captain to the First Officer following a Captain’s only take-off shall not take place until after reaching the top of climb. Line Training Captain’s may exercise discretion on the hand over when engaged in Line Training.

8.1.0.1 Aircraft Pre-departure Checks and Inspections

The following manuals and publications shall be used for reference as appropriate:

- Airplane Flight Manual/CDL.
- Boeing Flight Planning and Performance Manual.
- FCOM Vol 1.2 and QRH and FCTM.

Other Web Based Providers Approved by Ryanair

LIDO Brief is an approved web based providers of Flight Plans. Additional features of the website include delivery of weather and NOTAMs. Crews will normally receive this documentation electronically. Jeppesen is the approved provider of OMC, airways and route manual information.

Security of Web Based providers: The Ryanair IT Department shall be responsible for the security of all approved electronic sources. The normal delivery of documentation and information required to complete assigned flight operations shall be electronic. Backup delivery systems shall be in place to ensure continuity of operations.

## 8.1.0.1.1 Onboard Performance Tool (OPT)

Onboard Performance Tool (OPT) is a Boeing software application which calculates a “real time” Performance and Mass and Balance data for the actual conditions existing at the time of departure. Variations in the aircraft configuration loading, ambient conditions, runway length and some common MEL/CDL downgrades can be accounted for.

Take-off analysis data in OPT reflects the AFM performance. The OPT software is administered by the Performance Department.

OPT software, software updates and a current airport database is installed on each portable EFB device. Normally operating crew will make all performance calculations using the Boeing OPT application. The Performance Department shall provide OPT solutions on request to operating crews where crew are unable to do so.

Crews shall complete the performance calculations in advance of each flight. When the OPT is used to calculate take-off performance, the result shall be emailed to the default address for archiving. There is no requirement to complete a manual RTOW calculation unless the OPT or email applications are unavailable. If the RTOW figures have been manually calculated, they must be attached to the EFL submission and recorded on the paper OFP or RTOW calculation table.

Where EFB is not available, an OPT request may be completed verbally between the Commander or a pilot designated by the Commander and the Duty Pilot or the Performance Department.

A hard copy of the OPT solution shall be delivered to the Commander prior to take-off and shall be retained in the returned flight envelope. If a soft (electronic) copy has been provided (i.e. via email), then the crew/Duty Pilot/Performance Department must forward the calculation to the default e-mail address “takeoffperformance@ryanair.com”.

OPT is the primary method of calculating take-off (including Weight and Balance) and landing performance.

## Operational Flight Plan General

All Ryanair flights are operated

- in accordance with the regulations laid down by EASA,
- in compliance with the specifications in the Company’s Air Operator’s Certificate,
- in compliance the limitations in the Aircraft Flight Manual, and
- in compliance with any restriction imposed by the IAA.

Ryanair specifies flight planning procedures to provide for the safe conduct of the flight and a LIDO Operational Flight Plan (OFP) is completed for each flight based on aircraft performance, operating limitations, relevant expected conditions on the route to be followed and the aerodromes concerned. The OFP shall include at least one alternate.

The flight crew shall complete the OFP as the flight progresses.

The Commander shall not commence a flight unless he is satisfied that:

1. the aeroplane is airworthy;
2. the aeroplane is not operated contrary to the provision of the Configuration Deviation List (CDL);
3. the instruments and equipment required for the flight to be conducted are available;
4. the instruments and equipment are in operable condition except as provided in the MEL;
5. the aircraft has been subject to a security check/security search;
6. those parts of the Operations Manual that are required for conduct of the flight are available, OMA and SEP, OMB and OMC;

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

7. the documents, additional information and forms required to be available, ref. section 8.1.12 are on board;
8. current maps, charts and associated documents or equivalent data are available to cover the intended operation of the aeroplane including any diversion which may reasonably be expected, OMC. Any conversion tables necessary to support operations where metric heights, altitudes and flight levels must be used and are available on board (operations in area U);
9. ground facilities and services required for the planned flight are available and adequate;
10. the provisions specified in the operations manual in respect of fuel, oil, oxygen, minimum safe altitudes, aerodrome operating minima and availability of alternate aerodromes, where required, can be complied with for the planned flight;
11. the load is properly distributed and secured;
12. the mass of the aeroplane, at the commencement of the take-off roll, is be such that the flight plan can be conducted in compliance with the performance and weight and balance limitations of the aeroplane;
13. any additional operational limitation can be complied with.

Reporting Directly to Aircraft/Gate:

Report Directly to Aircraft/Gate Bases can be identified in the base information guide located in the VPOS and the EFB.

## Briefings

- Safety Briefings are a vital part of the pre-departure sequence and ensure that all members of the crew establish and maintain a shared ‘mental model’ for the days operation. The Commander, or the CSS in the absence of the Commander, are required to ensure that all applicable safety briefings are completed prior to embarkation of passengers, that they are completed in full and are not subject to undue interruption. Under normal circumstances the briefing will be conducted in the aircraft cabin. Where privacy and control of the environment is assured. The yellow safety strap will remain in place until briefings are completed and the aircraft ready for boarding. If due to the delayed arrival of the aircraft the crew elect to use another suitable, alternate location for the briefing, the alternate location must satisfy the following requirements.

1. It must be sufficiently far away from members of the public, that the crew cannot be overheard.

page 8–20

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

2. It must be in a place where the crew are not subject to interruption from members of the public or ground staff.
3. If the weather is inclement then it must be conducted inside.

Note: If the above conditions cannot be met then the crew will wait for the aircraft to arrive or proceed to the base facilities where the briefing can be conducted.

## Flight Crew Briefing

As part of his introduction the commander shall confirm with the first officer that both EFBs are up to date and at the minimum accepted charge as per OMA 8.9, and that the eFPL is downloaded in EFL.

The Commander will then discuss with all members of the flight crew as a minimum the following.

- Aircraft defects, and status
- Weather conditions for destinations and chosen alternates
- NOTAMs/Co. NOTAMS if applicable
- Performance issues if applicable
- Fuel requirements
- Loading
- De-icing requirements if applicable
- Review appropriate TEM aspects for the planned operation

## Training Flights

In addition to the above the Commander will review the students training file and brief any training objectives for the duty consistent with the guidance containing in the Line Training Techniques Document (LTTD).

## Flight Crew Briefing of Cabin Crew

As part of the introduction the commander shall confirm with all members of the crew that all updates on EFB have been successfully completed. He will also confirm the report time of all members of the crew and establish if any member has been called from STBY or has had their report delayed.

Note: If for reasons of poor mobile network signal or airport WIFI a crew member reports that this has not been possible then the crew member will return to the base facilities to use the Company WIFI provided.

The Commander shall conduct a crew briefing in accordance with OMA 8.0.8.7, taking the opportunity to reinforce any current NPFO safety messages.

- Missing crew or crew changes must be notified to Crew Control by the commander prior to dispatch.
- Late crew changes require EFL to be updated prior to dispatch to ensure FTL compliance.

In the event of a delayed inbound aircraft or prolonged delay, crew should agree a time with Operations Control to return to the gate/aircraft.

## 8.1.1 Minimum Flight Altitudes

CAT.OP.MPA.145; CAT.OP.MPA.270

### General

Minimum flight altitudes are determined for all flights performed by the Company. These are indicated on the LIDO Operational Flight Plan. Corrections for wind, temperature and QNH are always the Commander’s responsibility except when under radar vectoring. A clearance issued under Radar control assures terrain and obstacle clearance in all temperature and allowable pressure conditions, in most states. AMC1 CAT.OP.MPA.145 (a).

**Note:** UK controllers will not correct for temperature

Operations are prohibited for take off, enroute, landing and selection of alternates when the QNH is not selectable on the altimeter sub-scale setting.

Where minimum flight altitudes established by national Authority are higher than those established by the Company, the higher values shall apply.

Ryanair flight crew shall notify below specified minimum altitudes except when necessary for take-off or landing or descending in accordance with procedures approved by the IAA.

### Procedure to Determine Terrain Clearance and Minimum Usable Flight Level (MUFL)

The cruising flight level/altitude shall be equal to or higher than the true minimum safe en-route altitude. When selecting cruising levels other than those specified in the OFP crews shall take into account that pressure and/or temperature below ISA require an increase of safety margins and that wind speed correction may have to be applied in addition:

- QNH: each 1 hPa below ISA requires an increase of 30 ft.

page 8–22

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

- OAT: when below 0deg the MSA shall be temperature corrected. Each minimum altitude below the corrected MSA shall be temperature corrected. The glare shield ‘handy dandy’ is available to calculate temperature corrections.

Example:

| Conditions: | MEA 12,000 ft
FL160, actual OAT -30°C at FL160
Lowest local QNH 977 hPa |
| --- | --- |
| Step 1: Temperature correction | ISA Temperature FL160 is -17 °C Deviation -13°C 13°C corresponds to 5% 12,000 ft + 5% = 12 600 ft Please follow the Handy Dandy provided on the Glare shield for Deviation Values. |
| Step 2: QNH correction (1013-local QNH) hPa × 30 ft | 1013 hPa-977 hPa = 36 hPa
12,600 ft + (36 × 30) ft = 13,680 ft |
| Step 3: MUFL | FL140 |

## 8.1.2 Criteria and Responsibilities for Determining the Adequacy of Aerodromes

### 8.1.2.1 Use of Aerodromes

CAT.OP.MPA.105

Only NPFO approved aerodromes adequate for the aircraft and the operations concerned are used. This is assured by the Performance Department aerodrome categorisation process which requires the sign-off of the NPFO for any approved aerodrome.

OMC Appendix A – Approved Aerodromes List details all approved aerodromes, with details of Aerodrome Category, RYR Restricted Status (if applicable), plus any pertinent additional information, such as Emergency Use Only.

Diversion to Emergency Use Only aerodromes may cause significant operational disruption, for example, due to the lack of ground/passenger handling, commercial contracts and runway/airport slots. Before diversion to an Emergency Use Only aerodrome, Flight Crew must consider such implications, making reference to the Airfield Brief, and where possible, contacting OCC (via ACARS).

Notwithstanding the above, in an emergency situation the Commander is authorised to use any aerodrome as required, but if time is available, he is expected to select an approved aerodrome.

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## Isolated Aerodrome

Isolated aerodrome operations not currently in use.

## 8.1.2.2 Adequate Aerodrome

CAT.OP.MPA.107

Ryanair considers an aerodrome as adequate if, at the expected time of use, the aerodrome is available and equipped with necessary ancillary services such as air traffic services (ATS), sufficient lighting, communications, meteorological reports, navigation aids and emergency services.

1. Diversion Distance is one hour flying time at an OEI cruising speed according to the AFM in still air standard conditions based on the actual take-off mass, being 427 nm (B737-800) and 416 nm (B737-8200).\*
2. The Diversion Distance is a maximum planning range, calculated at M.79/330 Kts.
3. The Commander must select an appropriate speed for diversion to the ‘Nearest Suitable Airport’ which may require up to M.79/330 Kts.

**Note:** \*In Accordance with CAT.IDE.A.285 for over water flights the Maximum Diversion Distance is limited to 400 NM unless Liferafts are carried.

## 8.1.2.3 Rescue and Fire Fighting Services (RFFS)

8.1.2.3.1 Aerodromes selected by Ryanair undergo airport categorisation process in compliance with Air Ops, OMC. The RFFS requirements shall comply with ICAO Annex 14, Section 9.2.

8.1.2.3.2 The following definitions apply for compliance:

**RFFS Category:**

Rescue and Fire Fighting Services as defined in ICAO Annex 14.

**Aeroplane RFFS Category:**

The category specified in ICAO Annex 14 Table 9-1 for a given aeroplane type.

**Aerodrome RFFS Category:**

The published RFFS category for a given aerodrome.

8.1.2.3.3 The Aeroplane RFFS category for B737-8200 series aircraft is Category 7.

The Aeroplane RFFS category for B737-800 series aircraft is Category 7.

When assessing the Minimum Aerodrome RFFS Category, the Company will consider the operational complexities for various operation types, such as aerodrome facilities and infrastructure, terrain, weather conditions and traffic density together with the availability of Fire Fighting Vehicles and Personnel ensuring the overall safety of the operation can be maximised.

In flight, the Commander may decide to land at an Aerodrome where the Aerodrome RFFS Category is lower than specified above, if in his judgment and after due consideration of all the prevailing circumstances, to do so would be safer than to divert.

## 8.1.2.4 Aerodrome Selection Policy

CAT.OP.MPA.182, AMC1 CAT.OP.MPA.182

1. At the planning stage, it must be ensured that once the flight has commenced, there is reasonable certainty that an aerodrome where a safe landing can be made will be available at the estimated time of use of that aerodrome.

2. At the planning stage, to allow for a safe landing in case of an abnormal or emergency situation after take-off, a take-off alternate aerodrome shall be selected and specified in the operational flight plan if either:

a. the meteorological conditions at the aerodrome of departure are below Ryanair’s established aerodrome landing minima for that operation; or
b. it would be impossible to return to the aerodrome of departure for other reasons.

3. The take-off alternate aerodrome shall be located within a distance from the departure aerodrome that minimises the risk of exposure to potential abnormal or emergency situations. In selecting the take-off alternate aerodrome, at least the following shall be considered:

a. actual and forecast meteorological conditions;
b. availability and quality of the aerodrome infrastructure;
c. navigation and landing capabilities of the aircraft in abnormal or emergency conditions, taking into account the redundancy of critical systems; and
d. approvals held (e.g. low visibility operation (LVO), etc.).

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

e. Where it is not possible to use the departure aerodrome as a take-off alternate aerodrome due to meteorological or performance reasons, Ryanair selects another adequate take-off alternate aerodrome that is no further from the departure aerodrome than maximum Diversion Distance in accordance with OMA 8.1.2

4. At the planning stage, for each Instrument Flight Rules (IFR) flight, one or more aerodromes shall be selected and specify in the operational and air traffic services (ATS) flight plans, so that two safe-landing options are available during normal operation when reaching the destination aerodrome.

Note: When planning the flight, two safe-landing options are expected to remain available until the flight reaches its destination, where a decision will be made to commit to land or divert. This will typically be a runway at the destination aerodrome itself and a runway at a destination alternate aerodrome.

The requirement may also be satisfied by two landing runways at the destination aerodrome, provided that the risk of a single event (such as an aircraft accident) or meteorological deterioration at that single aerodrome will not eliminate both options.

Ryanair is not approved for the use of an isolated aerodrome as destination aerodrome.

5. Appropriate safety margins to flight planning take into account a possible deterioration of the available forecast meteorological conditions at the estimated time of landing in accordance with OMA 8.1.5.4.

6. For each IFR flight, Ryanair shall ensure that sufficient means are available to navigate to and land at the destination aerodrome or at any destination alternate aerodrome in the event of loss of capability for the intended approach and landing operation.

## 8.1.2.4.1 Basic Fuel Scheme – Destination Aerodromes – PBN Operations

AMC1 CAT.OP.MPA.182(f)

To comply with point 8.1.2.4(6) when intending to use PBN, an aerodrome shall be selected as a destination alternate aerodrome only if an instrument approach procedure that does not rely on a GNSS is available either at that aerodrome or at the destination aerodrome.

GM1 CAT.OP.MPA.182(f)

OMA 8.1.2.4.1 applies only to destination alternate aerodromes in flights that require a destination alternate aerodrome. A take-off or an ERA aerodrome with instrument approach procedures that rely on a GNSS may be planned without

## 8.1.2.5 Basic Fuel Scheme – Take-off Alternate Aerodrome

AMC1 CAT.OP.MPA.182

The take-off alternate aerodrome should not be farther from the departure aerodrome than 1-hour flight time at a One-Engine-Inoperative (OEI) cruising speed according to the AFM in ISA and still-air conditions using the actual take-off mass in accordance with OMA 8.1.2.2.

## 8.1.2.6 Basic Fuel Scheme – Destination Alternate Aerodrome

1. For each IFR flight, at least one destination alternate aerodrome should be selected and specified in the operational and ATS flight plans.
2. For each IFR flight, two destination alternate aerodromes should be selected and specified in the operational and ATS flight plans, when the safety margins for meteorological conditions of OMA 8.1.5.4 (AMC5 CAT.OP.MPA.182), and the planning minima of OMA 8.1.5.5 (AMC6 CAT.OP.MPA.182) cannot be met, or when no meteorological information is available for the selected destination aerodrome.
3. Ryanair may operate with no destination alternate aerodrome when specific Flight Operations approval has been received from the company and when the following two conditions are met:

a. the duration of the planned flight from take-off to landing does not exceed 6 hours or, in the event of in-flight re-planning, in accordance with point OMA 8.1.7.3 (4) (CAT.OP.MPA.181(d)), the remaining flying time to destination does not exceed 4 hours; and
b. two separate runways are usable at the destination aerodrome and the appropriate weather reports and/or weather forecasts indicate that for the period from 1 hour before to 1 hour after the expected time of arrival, the ceiling is at least 2000 ft (600 m) or the circling height +500 ft (150 m), whichever is greater, and ground visibility is at least 5 km.

## 8.1.3 Use of Aerodromes

CAT.OP.MPA.105, 110

Ryanair only uses aerodromes that are adequate for the B737-800/8200 and for Commercial Air transport operations. The NPFO may approve flights to other aerodromes which are non CAT operations, eg maintenance positioning flights.

Ryanair has established minimum values for vertical and horizontal visibility for planning, take-off and landing. Jeppesen is the aeronautical Navigation Services Provider. The methods for determination of these minimum values are described in the following sections:

## 8.1.3.1 Take-off Conditions

CAT.OP.MPA.265

Before commencing take-off, the commander shall be satisfied that:

1. The meteorological conditions at the aerodrome and the condition of the runway/FATO intended to be used will not prevent a safe take-off and departure; and
2. The selected aerodrome operating minima are consistent with all of the following:
   a. the operative ground equipment;
   b. the operative aircraft systems;
   c. the aircraft performance;
   d. flight crew qualifications.

## Meteorological Conditions for Take-off – Runways

AMC1 CAT.OP.MPA.265(a)

1. The commander should not commence take-off unless the weather conditions at the aerodrome of departure are equal to or better than the applicable minima for landing at that aerodrome unless a weather-permissible take-off alternate aerodrome is available.
2. If the reported VIS is below the minimum specified for take-off and RVR is not reported, then take-off should only be commenced if the commander can determine that the visibility along the take-off runway is equal to or better than the required minimum.

page 8-30

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## 8.1.3.2 Approach and Landing Conditions

CAT.OP.MPA.300

Before commencing an approach operation, the commander shall be satisfied that:

1. The meteorological conditions at the aerodrome and the condition of the runway/FATO intended to be used will not prevent a safe approach, landing or go-around, considering the performance information contained in the operations manual; and

2. The selected aerodrome operating minima are consistent with all of the following:

a. the operative ground equipment;
b. the operative aircraft systems;
c. the aircraft performance;
d. flight crew qualifications;

Flight crew shall complete a ‘Landing Distance at Time of Arrival’ (LDTA) assessment, where required, in accordance with 8.1.3.2.2

## 8.1.3.2.1 Approach and Landing Conditions

AMC1 CAT.OP.MPA.300(a)

Landing Distance at Time of Arrival (LTDA) Assessment

1. The in-flight landing distance assessment should be based on the latest available weather report and runway condition report (RCR) or equivalent information based on the RCR.

2. The assessment should be initially carried out when the weather report and the RCR are obtained, usually around top of descent. If the planned duration of the flight does not allow the flight crew to carry out the assessment in non-critical phases of flight, the assessment should be carried out before departure.

3. When meteorological conditions may lead to a degradation of the runway surface condition, the assessment should include consideration of how much deterioration in runway surface friction characteristics may be tolerated, so that a quick decision can be made prior to landing.

4. The flight crew should monitor the evolution of the actual conditions during the approach, to ensure that they do not degrade below the condition that was previously determined to be the minimum acceptable.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## 8.1.3.2.2 In-Flight Check of the Landing Distance at Time of Arrival

CAT.OP.MPA.303

No approach to land shall be continued unless the landing distance available (LDA) on the intended runway is at least 115 % of the landing distance at the estimated time of landing, determined in accordance with the performance information for the assessment of the landing distance at time of arrival (LDTA).

Performance information for the assessment of the LDTA shall be based on approved data contained in the AFM.

## 8.1.3.3 Aerodrome Operating Minima

CAT.OP.MPA.110 (a)(b)

Ryanair establishes Aerodrome operating minima for each departure, destination or alternate aerodrome. These are published in the OMC. They are the lowest permissible minima taking into account various factors, such as:

1. the type, performance, and handling characteristics of the aircraft;
2. the equipment available on the aircraft for the purpose of navigation, acquisition of visual references, and/or control of the flight path during take-off, approach, landing, and the missed approach;
3. any conditions or limitations stated in the aircraft flight manual (AFM);
4. the relevant operational experience of Ryanair;
5. the dimensions and characteristics of the runways/final approach and take-off areas (FATOs) that may be selected for use;
6. the adequacy and performance of the available visual and non-visual aids and infrastructure;
7. the obstacle clearance altitude/height (OCA/H) for the instrument approach procedures (IAPs);
8. the obstacles in the climb-out areas and necessary clearance margins;
9. the composition of the flight crew, their competence and experience;
10. the IAP;
11. the aerodrome characteristics and the available air navigation services (ANS);
12. any minima that may be promulgated by the State of the aerodrome;
13. the conditions prescribed in the operations specifications including any specific approvals for low visibility operations (LVOs)
14. any non-standard characteristics of the aerodrome, the IAP or the environment

page 8-32

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

Heights used for calculation of Circling minima are related to the aerodrome elevation. Circling altitude is temperature corrected then rounded up to the nearest 100 ft.

Heights used for calculation of non-precision procedures may be related to aerodrome or threshold elevation. Refer to OMC for respective reference datum applied.

Decision Height (DH) for precision approaches are related to the threshold elevation of the landing runway.

Minimum Descent Altitude (DA/MDA) is temperature corrected if required then an additional 40 ft is added as required by the IAA.

CAT II Decision Heights are related to the threshold elevation.

CAT II Radio Altimeter setting heights are related to the highest terrain elevation underneath the approach path within a radius of 30 m around the point on centerline above which the nominal glide path height equals the DH.

CAT III Decision Height/Radio Altimeter setting heights are not calculated individually since the standard DH value is approved for the particular aeroplane type and is published in Table 8.14 and 8.15.

| • Decision Height/Altitude
• Minimum Descent Height/Altitude
• Ceiling | Feet |
| --- | --- |
| Visibility/RVR | Meters |

## 8.1.3.3.2 Determination of DH, DA, MDH, MDA and T/O Minima

CAT.OP.MPA.110

Jeppesen provides aeronautical charts as an external service provider. The values for DAs/DHs, MDAs/MDHs, RA setting heights, ceiling and RVR/VIS values published on Jeppesen charts, are determined by Jeppesen in accordance with prescribed calculation procedures taking into account:

1. The provisions of CAT.OP.MPA.110.
2. OCAs, OCHs, OCLs and/or official minima values published by state authorities.
3. Any other particular regulations prescribed by state authorities.

Landing minima may have to be modified on short term basis. State authorities will publish raised or lowered OCA and/or OCH values only.

If an OCA/OCH published in NOTAM is higher than DH/DA/MDH/MDA indicated on IAC this higher value shall be used.

## 8.1.3.4 Aerodrome Operating Minima – Take-off Operations

AMC1 CAT.OP.MPA.110

## 8.1.3.4.1 Take-off Minima

1. Take-off minima are expressed as visibility (VIS) or runway visual range (RVR) limits, taking into account all relevant factors for each runway planned to be used and aircraft characteristics and equipment. Where there is a specific need to see and avoid obstacles on departure and/or for a forced landing, additional conditions, e.g. ceiling, should be specified. This ceiling requirement will be annotated in the Airfield Brief when applicable.

## 8.1.3.4.2 Visual Reference

AMC1 CAT.OP.MPA.110

1. The take-off minima will be selected to ensure sufficient guidance to control the aircraft in the event of both a rejected take-off in adverse circumstances and a continued take-off after failure of the critical engine.
2. For night operations, the prescribed runway lights shall be in operation.

## 8.1.3.4.6 Aerodrome Operating Minima – MEANS TO DETERMINE THE REQUIRED RVR BASED ON DH AND LIGHTING FACILITIES

GM4 CAT.OP.MPA.110

The values in Table 9 are derived from the formula below:

Minimum RVR (m) = [(DH/MDH (ft) x 0.3048)/tana] — length of approach lights (m) where α is the calculation angle, being a default value of 3.00° increasing in steps of 0.10° for each line in Table 9 up to 3.77° and then remaining constant. An upper RVR limit of 2400 m has been applied to the table.

## 8.1.3.4.6.1 Aerodrome Operating Minima – USE OF DH FOR NPAs FLOWN USING THE CDFA TECHNIQUE

GMS CAT.OP.MPA.110

OMA 8.1.3.4.4 (AMC3 CAT.OP.MPA.110) provides that, in certain circumstances, a published MDH may be used as a DH for a 2D operation flown using the CDFA technique.

The safety of the use of MDH as DH in CDFA operations has been verified by at least two independent analyses concluding that the CDFA using MDH as DH without any add-on is safer than the traditional step-down and level-flight NPA operation. A comparison has been made between the safety level of using MDH as DH without an add-on with the well-established safety level resulting from the ILS collision risk model. The NPA used was the most demanding, i.e. most tightly designed NPA, which offers the least additional margins. It should be noted that the design limits of the ILS approach design, e.g. the maximum GP angle of 3.5 degrees, must be observed for the CDFA in order to keep the validity of the comparison.

Notwithstanding the text above, Minimum Descent Altitude (DA/MDA) is temperature corrected if required then an additional 40 ft is added as required by the IAA in accordance with OMA 8.1.3.3.

## 8.1.3.4.7 Aerodrome Operating Minima – Circling Operations

AMC7 CAT.OP.MPA.110

1. Circling minima

The following standards should apply for establishing circling minima for operations:

a. The MDH for circling operation should not be lower than the highest of:

i. the published circling OCH for the aeroplane category;

page 8–40

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

ii. the minimum circling height derived from Table 15; or
iii. the DH/MDH of the preceding instrument approach procedure (IAP);

b. The MDA for circling should be calculated by adding the published aerodrome elevation to the MDH, as determined by (1)(a); and
c. The minimum VIS for circling should be the higher of:
i. the circling VIS for the aeroplane category, if published; or
ii. the minimum VIS derived from Table 15.

Table 15 Circling
MDH and Minimum VIS vs. Aeroplane Category

|                 | Aeroplane Category |      |
| --------------- | ------------------ | ---- |
|                 | C                  | D    |
| MDH (ft)        | 600                | 700  |
| Minimum Vis (m) | 2400               | 3600 |

2. Conduct of flight

a. The MDH and OCH included in the procedure are referenced to aerodrome elevation;
b. The MDA is referenced to mean sea level;
c. For these procedures, the applicable visibility is the VIS; and
d. Table 15 provides tabular guidance of the relationship between height above threshold and the in-flight visibility required to obtain and sustain visual contact during the circling manoeuvre.

3. Instrument approach followed by visual manoeuvring (circling) without prescribed tracks

a. When the aeroplane is on the initial instrument approach, before visual reference is established, but not below the MDA/H, the aeroplane should follow the corresponding IAP until the appropriate instrument MAPt is reached.
b. At the beginning of the level flight phase at or above the MDA/H, the instrument approach track should be maintained until the pilot:
i. estimates that, in all probability, visual contact with the runway of intended landing or the runway environment will be maintained during the entire circling procedure;

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

ii. estimates that the aeroplane is within the circling area before commencing circling; and
iii. is able to determine the aeroplane’s position in relation to the runway of intended landing with the aid of the appropriate visual references.

c. If the pilot cannot comply with the conditions in (3)(b) at the MAPt then a missed approach should be executed in accordance with the IAP.
d. After the aeroplane has left the track of the initial instrument approach, the flight phase outbound from the runway should be limited to an appropriate distance, which is required to align the aeroplane onto the final approach. Such manoeuvres should be conducted to enable the aeroplane to:
i. attain a controlled and stable descent path to the intended landing runway; and
ii. remain within the circling area and in such way that visual contact with the runway of intended landing or runway environment is maintained at all times.
e. Flight manoeuvres should be carried out at an altitude/height that is not less than the circling MDA/H.
f. Descent below the MDA/H should not be initiated until the threshold of the runway to be used has been appropriately identified. The aeroplane should be in a position to continue with a normal rate of descent and land within the touchdown zone (TDZ).

4. Instrument approach followed by a visual manoeuvring (circling) with prescribed track

Note: Exceptionally Ryanair performs a circling manoeuvre with prescribed tracks in accordance with the standards below, and only when the procedure is described on the accompanying Airfield Brief.

a. The aeroplane should remain on the initial instrument approach procedure until one of the following is reached:
i. the prescribed divergence point to commence circling on the prescribed track; or
ii. the MAPt.
b. The aeroplane should be established on the instrument approach track in level flight at or above the MDA/H at or by the circling manoeuvre divergence point.

page 8-42

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

c. If the divergence point is reached before the required visual reference is acquired, a missed approach should be initiated not later than the MAPt and completed in accordance with the instrument approach procedure.

d. When commencing the prescribed circling manoeuvre at the published divergence point, the subsequent manoeuvres should be conducted to comply with the published routing and published heights/altitudes.

e. Unless otherwise specified, once the aeroplane is established on the prescribed track(s), the published visual reference does not need to be maintained unless:

i. required by the State of the aerodrome; or
ii. the circling MAPt (if published) is reached.

f. If the prescribed circling manoeuvre has a published MAPt and the required visual reference has not been obtained by that point, a missed approach should be executed in accordance with (5)(b) and (5)(c).

g. Subsequent further descent below MDA/H should only commence when the required visual reference has been obtained.

h. Unless otherwise specified in the procedure, final descent should not be commenced from MDA/H until the threshold of the intended landing runway has been identified and the aeroplane is in a position to continue with a normal rate of descent to land within the touchdown zone.

# 5. Missed approach

a. Missed approach during the instrument procedure prior to circling:

i. If the missed approach procedure is required to be flown when the aeroplane is positioned on the instrument approach track defined by radio-navigation aids RNAV, RNP, or ILS, MLS, and before commencing the circling manoeuvre, the published missed approach for the instrument approach should be followed; or
ii. If the IAP is carried out with the aid of an ILS, an MLS or a stabilised approach (SAp), the MAPt associated with an ILS, or an MLS procedure without glide path (GP-out procedure) or the SAp, where applicable, should be used.

b. If a prescribed missed approach is published for the circling manoeuvre, this overrides the manoeuvres prescribed below.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

c. If visual reference is lost while circling to land after the aeroplane has departed from the initial instrument approach track, the missed approach specified for that particular instrument approach should be followed. It is expected that the pilot will make an initial climbing turn toward the intended landing runway to a position overhead the aerodrome where the pilot will establish the aeroplane in a climb on the instrument missed approach segment.

d. The aeroplane should not leave the visual manoeuvring (circling) area, which is obstacle-protected, unless:

i. established on the appropriate missed approach procedure; or
ii. at minimum sector altitude (MSA).

e. All turns should be made in the same direction and the aeroplane should remain within the circling protected area while climbing either:

i. to the altitude assigned to any published circling missed approach manoeuvre if applicable;
ii. to the altitude assigned to the missed approach of the initial instrument approach;
iii. to the MSA; or
iv. to the minimum holding altitude (MHA) applicable to transition to a holding facility or fix, or continue to climb to an MSA;

or as directed by ATS.

When the missed approach procedure is commenced on the ‘downwind’ leg of the circling manoeuvre, an ‘S’ turn may be undertaken to align the aeroplane on the initial instrument approach missed approach path, provided the aeroplane remains within the protected circling area.

The commander should be responsible for ensuring adequate terrain clearance during the above-stipulated manoeuvres, particularly during the execution of a missed approach initiated by ATS.

f. Because the circling manoeuvre may be accomplished in more than one direction, different patterns will be required to establish the aeroplane on the prescribed missed approach course depending on its position at the time visual reference is lost. In particular, all turns are to be in the prescribed direction if this is restricted, e.g. to the west/east (left or right hand) to remain within the protected circling area.

page 8-44

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

g. If a missed approach procedure is published for a particular runway onto which the aeroplane is conducting a circling approach and the aeroplane has commenced a manoeuvre to align with the runway, the missed approach for this direction may be accomplished. The ATS unit should be informed of the intention to fly the published missed approach procedure for that particular runway.

h. The commander should advise ATS when any missed approach procedure has been commenced, the height/altitude the aeroplane is climbing to and the position the aeroplane is proceeding towards and/or heading the aeroplane is established on.

6. Circling at Night.

a. At night, the maximum reported surface crosswind component for a circling approach is 20 kts. Gust values shall not exceed 40 kts.

b. At night, the ceiling required before commencing an approach shall be Circling Minimums +300 ft,

c. “New” Captains may not conduct Circling approaches at night.

8.1.3.4.8 Aerodrome Operating Minima – Visual Approach Operations

AMC9 CAT.OP.MPA.110

Ryanair will not use an RVR of less than 800 m for a visual approach.

8.1.3.4.9 Aerodrome Operating Minima – Conversion of Visibility to CMV

AMC 10 CAT.OP.MPA.110

The following conditions apply to the use of converted meteorological visibility (CMV) instead of RVR:

1. If the reported RVR is not available, a CMV may be substituted for the RVR, except:

a. to satisfy the take-off minima; or

b. for the purpose of continuation of an approach in LVOs.

2. If the minimum RVR for an approach is more than the maximum value assessed by the aerodrome operator, then CMV should be used.

3. In order to determine CMV from visibility:

a. for flight planning purposes, a factor of 1.0 should be used;

b. for purposes other than flight planning, the conversion factors specified in Table 16 should be used.

8.1.3.4.10 Aerodrome Operating Minima – Effect on Landing Minima of Temporarily Failed or Downgraded Ground Equipment

AMC11 CAT.OP.MPA.110

Aerodrome operating minima

1. General

These instructions are intended for use both before and during flight. It is, however, not expected that the commander would consult such instructions after passing 1000 ft above the aerodrome. If failures of ground aids are announced at such a late stage, the approach could be continued at the commander’s discretion. If failures are announced before such a late stage in the approach, their effect on the approach should be considered as described in Table 17, and the approach may have to be abandoned.

2. Conditions applicable to Table 17:

a. multiple failures of runway/FATO lights other than those indicated in Table 17 should not be acceptable;

b. failures of approach and runway/FATO lights are acceptable at the same time, and the most demanding consequence should be applied; and

c. failures other than ILS, GLS, MLS affect the RVR only and not the DH.

| NALS                                                                                                   | Any other approach lighting system (HIALS, MALS or ALS <210 m) or no approach lights         |

8.1.3.4.12 Approach Flight Technique

CAT.OP.MPA.115

All instrument procedures, including NPAs, shall be flown using the Stabilised Approach technique (SAp) and from the FAF to the MAP or MDA the continuous descent final approach (CDFA) technique.

8.1.3.4.13 Aerodrome Operating Minima – Increments Specified by the Competent Authority

GM6 CAT.OP.MPA.110

8.1.3.4.13.1 Aerodrome Operating Minima – Low Temperature Correction

GM8 CAT.OP.MPA.110

When the aerodrome temperature is below 0 degrees celsius a correction should be applied to the DA/H. The OMB Quick Reference Guide may be used to determine the correction that should be applied. The calculations in the table are for a sea level aerodrome; they are therefore conservative when applied at higher level aerodromes.
