# 8.1.3.4.14 Approach Flight Technique – Continuous Descent Final Approach (CDFA)

AMC1 CAT.OP.MPA.115

The following criteria apply to CDFA:

1. The CDFA technique ensures that an approach can be flown on the desired vertical path and track in a stabilised manner, without significant vertical path changes during the final segment descent to the runway. While level flight is permitted prior to the FAF (e.g. V/S approaches), level flight is not permitted in the final approach segment, which commences at the FAF. This technique applies to an approach with no vertical guidance and controls the descent path until the DA/DH. Ryanair provides information allowing the flight crew to determine the appropriate descent path for each NPA procedure to be used. This information is either:

a. a descent path depicted on the approach chart including check altitude/heights against range;
b. a descent path coded into the aircraft flight management system; or
c. a recommended descent rate based on estimated ground speed.

2. The information provided to the crew observes human factors principles.

3. The descent path should be calculated to pass at or above the minimum altitude specified at any step-down fix. Jeppesen charts depict the appropriate cross check altitudes/heights with the corresponding appropriate range information. Generally, the MAPt is published on the chart.

4. The optimum angle for the descent path is 3 degrees and should not exceed 4.5 degrees.

5. Ryanair has established procedures that require:

a. the pilot monitoring to verbalise deviations from the required descent path;
b. the pilot flying to make prompt corrections to deviation from the required descent path; and
c. a call-out to be made when the aircraft is approaching the DA/H

6. A missed approach should be executed promptly at the DA/H or the MAPt, whichever is first, if the required visual references have not been established.

7. For approaches other than circling approaches, the lateral part of the missed approach should be flown via the MAPt unless otherwise stated on the approach chart.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## 8.1.3.4.15 Approach Flight Technique – NPA Operations Without Applying the CDFA Technique

AMC2 CAT.OP.MPA.115

Any Non CDFA approach to any runway end requires prior approval from the IAA.

RYR does NOT currently conduct non CDFA approaches.

Circling is not considered CDFA, however the instrument approach to the circling altitude is CDFA from the FAF.

## 8.1.3.4.15.1 Approaches Offset by More than 5 Degrees

When final approach track is offset by more than 5 degrees the approach shall:

1. be flown using Stabilised Approach techniques (SAp) and CDFA.
2. have the published RVR/CMV increased by 400 m (max 5000 m).

## 8.1.3.4.16 Approach Flight Technique – Operational Procedures and Instructions and Training

AMC3 CAT.OP.MPA.115

### Approach Flight Technique

It is Ryanair policy that the SAp and CDFA approaches will be flown including RNP APCH Operations. Maximum use of the AFDS applies. All approved and trained approach techniques are presented in FCOM Vol I and Boeing FCTM, including the duties of each crew member during the conduct of the approach

Ryanair has established procedures which prohibit level flight at MDA/H without the flight crew having obtained the required visual references. It is not the intention to prohibit level flight at MDA/H when conducting a circling approach, which does not come within the definition of the CDFA technique.

Initial and recurrent flight crew training required by ORO.FC includes the use of the CDFA technique.

# 8.1.3.4.17 Approach Flight Technique – Stabilised Approach Operations

CAT.OP.MPA. 115, AMC1 CAT.OP.MPA. 115(a)

## Approach Flight Technique

All approaches shall be flown as stabilised approach operations unless otherwise approved by the Competent Authority for a particular approach to a particular runway. The following criteria should be satisfied for all stabilised approach operations:

1. The flight management systems and approach aids should be correctly set, and any required radio aids identified before reaching a predetermined point or altitude/height on the approach.

2. The aeroplane should be flown according to the following criteria from a predetermined point or altitude/height on the approach:

a. the angle of bank should be less than 30 degrees; and
b. the target rate of descent should be that required to maintain the correct vertical path at the planned approach speed.

3. Variations in the rate of descent should normally not exceed 50% of the target rate of descent.

4. An aeroplane should be considered stabilised for landing when the following conditions are met (the below requirements are satisfied by complying with the LDA Stabilization Criteria for approaches, OMA 8.3.0.3.4):

a. the aeroplane is tracking within an acceptable tolerance of the required lateral path;
b. the aeroplane is tracking within an acceptable tolerance of the required vertical path;
c. the vertical speed of the aeroplane is within an acceptable tolerance of the required rate of descent;
d. the airspeed of the aeroplane is within an acceptable tolerance of the intended landing speed;
e. the aeroplane is in the correct configuration for landing, unless operating procedures require a final configuration change for performance reasons after visual reference is acquired; and
f. the thrust/power and trim settings are appropriate.

5. The aeroplane should be stabilised for landing before reaching 500 ft above the landing runway threshold elevation. Refer to OMA 8.3.0.3.4.

6. For approach operations where the pilot does not have visual reference with the ground, the aeroplane should additionally be stabilised for landing before reaching 1000 ft above the landing runway threshold elevation. In

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

the case of circling approaches flown after a CDFA, the aircraft will be stabilised in the circling configuration (Landing Gear Down, Flap 15, on speed) not later than passing 1000 ft above the runway elevation.

7. Ryanair specify the following in OMA 8.3.0.3.4:
   a. the acceptable tolerances referred to in (4);
   b. the means to identify the predetermined points referred to in (1) and (2) is described in OMA 8.3.0.3.8. This should normally be the FAF.

8. When an operator requests approval for an alternative to the stabilised approach criteria for a particular approach to a particular runway, the operator should demonstrate that the proposed alternative will ensure that an acceptable level of safety is achieved.

## 8.1.3.4.18 Noise Abatement Procedures – NADP DESIGN

AMC1 CAT.OP.MPA.130

1. For each aeroplane type two departure procedures are defined, in accordance with ICAO Doc. 8168 (Procedures for Air Navigation Services, ‘PANS-OPS’), Volume I:
   a. noise abatement departure procedure one (NADP 1), is designed to meet the close-in noise abatement objective; and
   b. noise abatement departure procedure two (NADP 2), is designed to meet the distant noise abatement objective.

2. For each type of NADP (1 and 2), a single climb profile is specified for use at all aerodromes, which is associated with a single sequence of actions.

The NADP procedures are described in detail in FCOM Vol I. Airfield Briefs will indicate which NADP profile is required but it is Ryanair policy to use NADP2 when possible. Where there is a discrepancy between Ryanair policy and a charted procedure Ryanair policy applies.

## 8.1.5.1 Planning Minima for IFR flights

CAT.OP.MPA.185

1. Ryanair has established procedures for in-flight fuel management that ensure:

a. continual validation of the assumptions made during the planning stage (pre-flight or in-flight re-planning, or both);
b. re-analysis and adjustment, if necessary;
c. that the amount of usable fuel remaining on board is protected and not less than the fuel that is required to proceed to an aerodrome where a safe landing can be made; and
d. relevant fuel data for the purpose of points (a), (b), and (c) shall be recorded.

2. Ryanair has procedures in place to require the commander to obtain delay information from a reliable source when unforeseen circumstances may result in landing at the destination aerodrome with less than the final reserve fuel plus any fuel to proceed to an alternate aerodrome, if required.

3. The commander shall advise air traffic control (ATC) of a ‘minimum fuel’ state by declaring ‘MINIMUM FUEL’ in accordance with OMA when the commander has:

a. committed to land at a specific aerodrome; and
b. calculated that any change to the existing clearance to that aerodrome may result in landing with less than the planned final reserve fuel.

4. The commander shall declare a situation of ‘fuel emergency’ by broadcasting ‘MAYDAY, MAYDAY, MAYDAY FUEL’ in accordance with OMA, when the usable fuel that is calculated to be available upon landing at the nearest aerodrome where a safe landing can be made is less than the planned final reserve fuel.

# 8.1.5.3 Basic Fuel Scheme – Reaching the Destination Aerodrome

AMC4 CAT.OP.MPA.182

In the context of the basic fuel scheme and basic fuel scheme with variations, ‘reaching the destination’ means the point at which the aircraft has reached the applicable DA/H or MDA/H at the destination aerodrome.

# 8.1.5.4 Basic Fuel Scheme – Safety Margins for Meteorological Conditions

AMC5 CAT.OP.MPA.182

1. An aerodrome should only be selected as a:

a. take-off alternate aerodrome; or
b. destination aerodrome

when the appropriate weather reports and/or forecasts indicate that during a period commencing 1 hour before and ending 1 hour after the estimated time of arrival at the aerodrome, the weather conditions will be at or above the applicable landing minima as follows:

i. RVR or VIS specified in accordance with point CAT.OP.MPA.110 (OMA 8.1.2.4); and
ii. for a type A or a circling operation, ceiling at or above MDH.

## 8.1.7.2 Ryanair Fuel Scheme

CAT.OP.MPA.180

Ryanair is approved to utilise a Basic Fuel Scheme with Variations.

When applicable, Ryanair may decide to deviate from the Basic Fuel Scheme, satisfying the AMC for Basic Fuel Scheme with Variations applicable to the specific deviation. Whenever a Basic Fuel Scheme with Variation is applied, this is specified in the OMA section accordingly (e.g. “Basic Fuel Scheme with Variation” is indicated in the title).

Ryanair is currently approved for Basic Fuel Scheme with Variations – Taxi Fuel and Contingency Fuel.

1. Ryanair has established, implemented, and maintains a fuel scheme that:

a. is appropriate for the type(s) of operation performed;
b. corresponds to the capability of Ryanair to support its implementation; and
c. is either:

i. a basic fuel scheme, which shall form the basis for a basic fuel scheme with variations and an individual fuel scheme; the basic fuel scheme derives from a large-scale analysis of safety and operational data from previous performance and

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

experience of the industry, applying scientific principles; the basic fuel scheme shall ensure, in this order, a safe, effective, and efficient operation of the aircraft; or

ii. a basic fuel scheme with variations, which is a basic fuel scheme where the analysis referred to in point (i) is used to establish a variation to the basic fuel scheme that ensures, in this order, a safe, effective, and efficient operation of the aircraft.

2. All fuel schemes shall comprise:

a. a fuel planning and in-flight re-planning policy;
b. an aerodrome selection policy; and
c. an in-flight fuel management policy.

3. The fuel scheme and any change to it shall require prior approval by the IAA.

## 8.1.7.3 Fuel Scheme – Fuel Planning and In-flight Re-planning Policy

CAT.OP.MPA.181

1. Ryanair:

a. has established a fuel planning and in-flight re-planning policy as part of the fuel scheme;
b. ensures that the aeroplane carries a sufficient amount of usable fuel to safely complete the planned flight and to allow for deviations from the planned operation;
c. has developed procedures for the fuel planning and in-flight re-planning policy that are contained in the operations manual.
d. ensures that the fuel planning of the flight is based on:

i. current aircraft-specific data derived from a fuel consumption monitoring system or, if not available;
ii. data provided by the aeroplane manufacturer.

2. Ryanair ensures that the planning of flights includes the operating conditions under which the flight is to be conducted; the operating conditions shall include at least:

a. aircraft fuel consumption data;
b. anticipated masses;
c. anticipated meteorological conditions;
d. the effects of deferred maintenance items and/or of configuration deviations;

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

e. the expected departure and arrival routing and runways; and
f. anticipated delays.

3. Ryanair ensures that the pre-flight calculation of the usable fuel that is required for a flight includes:

a. taxi fuel that shall not be less than the amount expected to be used prior to take-off;
b. trip fuel that shall be the amount of fuel that is required to enable the aeroplane to fly from take-off, or from the point of in-flight re-planning, to landing at the destination aerodrome;
c. contingency fuel that shall be the amount of fuel required to compensate for unforeseen factors;
d. destination alternate fuel:

i. when a flight is operated with at least one destination alternate aerodrome, it shall be the amount of fuel required to fly from the destination aerodrome to the destination alternate aerodrome; or
ii. when a flight is operated with no destination alternate aerodrome, it shall be the amount of fuel required to hold at the destination aerodrome, while enabling the aeroplane to perform a safe landing, and to allow for deviations from the planned operation; as a minimum, this amount shall be 15-minute fuel at holding speed at 1500 ft (450 m) above the aerodrome elevation in standard conditions, calculated according to the estimated aeroplane mass on arrival at the destination aerodrome;

e. final reserve fuel that shall be the amount of fuel that is calculated at holding speed at 1500 ft (450 m) above the aerodrome elevation in standard conditions according to the aeroplane estimated mass on arrival at the destination alternate aerodrome, or destination aerodrome when no destination alternate aerodrome is required, and shall not be less than (for turbine-engined aeroplanes) the fuel to fly for 30 minutes;
f. additional fuel, if required by the type of operation; it shall be the amount of fuel to enable the aeroplane to land at a fuel en route alternate aerodrome (fuel ERA aerodrome critical scenario) in the event of an aircraft failure that significantly increases the fuel consumption at the most critical point along the route; this additional fuel is required only if the minimum amount of fuel that is calculated according to points (3)(b) to (3)(e) is not sufficient for such an event;

page 8–58

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

g. extra fuel to take into account anticipated delays or specific operational constraints; and
h. discretionary fuel, if required by the commander.

4. Ryanair ensures that in-flight re-planning procedures for calculating the usable fuel that is required when a flight proceeds along a route or to a destination aerodrome other than the ones originally planned include points (3)(b) to (3)(g).

LIDO as the flight planning service provider complies fully with the Ryanair fuel scheme. No Ryanair revenue flight shall be planned to arrive at destination with less than 1800 kg of fuel. This means that the DEST Fuel in the PROGRESS page of the FMC shall indicate not less than 1800 kg at dispatch.

## 8.1.7.3.1 Basic Fuel Scheme – Pre-flight Calculation of Usable Fuel for Performance Class A Aeroplanes

AMC1.CAT.OP.MPA.181

For the pre-flight calculation of the usable fuel in accordance with OMA 8.1.7.3 (CAT.OP.MPA.181), Ryanair shall:

1. for taxi fuel, take into account the local conditions at the departure aerodrome and the APU consumption;

**Note 1:** Lido Taxi Fuel includes fuel for taxi, APU usage, engine start and anti-ice. Lido plans taxi times based on available airport/runway taxi time data. If no data is available, then the default taxi time of 15 mins is used.

**Note 2:** Ryanair may apply Basic Fuel Scheme with Variations - Taxi Fuel in accordance with OMA 8.1.7.3.1.2.

2. for trip fuel, include:

a. fuel for take-off and climb from the aerodrome elevation to the initial cruising level/altitude, taking into account the expected departure routing;
b. fuel from the top of climb to the top of descent, including any step climb/descent;
c. fuel from the top of descent to the point where the approach procedure is initiated, taking into account the expected arrival routing; and
d. fuel for making an approach and landing at the destination aerodrome;

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

3. for contingency fuel, calculate for unforeseen factors either:

a. 5% of the planned trip fuel or, in the event of in-flight re-planning, 5% of the trip fuel for the remainder of the flight; or
b. an amount to fly for 5 minutes at holding speed at 1500 ft (450 m) above the destination aerodrome in standard conditions, whichever is the higher;

Note: Ryanair may apply Basic Fuel Scheme with Variations – Contingency Fuel in accordance with OMA 8.1.7.3.1.3.

4. for destination alternate fuel, include:

a. when the aircraft is operated with one destination alternate aerodrome:

i. fuel for a missed approach from the applicable DA/H or MDA/H at the destination aerodrome to the missed-approach altitude, taking into account the complete missed-approach procedure;
ii. fuel for climb from the missed-approach altitude to the cruising level/altitude, taking into account the expected departure routing;
iii. fuel for cruising from the top of climb to the top of descent, taking into account the expected routing;
iv. fuel for descent from the top of descent to the point where the approach is initiated, taking into account the expected arrival routing; and
v. fuel for making an approach and landing at the destination alternate aerodrome; and

b. when the aircraft is operated with two destination alternate aerodromes, the amount of fuel that is calculated in accordance with point (4)(a), based on the destination alternate aerodrome that requires the greater amount of fuel;

5. for FRF, comply with OMA 8.1.7.3 (3) (CAT.OP.MPA.181(c));
6. for additional fuel, include an amount of fuel that allows the aeroplane to proceed, in the event of an engine failure or loss of pressurisation, from the most critical point along the route to a fuel en route alternate (fuel ERA) aerodrome in the relevant aircraft configuration, hold there for 15 minutes at 1500 ft (450 m) above the aerodrome elevation in standard conditions, make an approach, and land;

page 8–60

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

7. for extra fuel, include anticipated delays or specific operational constraints that can be predicted; and
8. for discretionary fuel, include a quantity at the sole discretion of the commander.

## 8.1.7.3.1.1 Basic Fuel Scheme – Pre-flight Calculation of Usable Fuel

AMC4 CAT.OP.MPA.181

The additional fuel required by the type of operation in the event of an aircraft failure that significantly increases fuel consumption at the most critical point along the route should be calculated according to the engine failure or loss of pressurisation, whichever requires a greater amount of fuel. This additional fuel is described in OMA section 8.3.2.4.2.5.1.

## 8.1.7.3.1.2 Basic Fuel Scheme with Variations – Taxi Fuel

AMC5 CAT.OP.MPA.181

To calculate taxi fuel for a basic fuel scheme with variations Ryanair may use statistical taxi fuel.

## 8.1.7.3.1.3 Basic Fuel Scheme with Variations – Contingency Fuel

AMC6 CAT.OP.MPA.181

1. Contingency fuel variations are methods of reducing the basic amount of contingency fuel based on established mitigating measures.
2. Ryanair has established and maintains a fuel consumption monitoring system for individual aeroplanes and uses valid data for fuel calculation based on such a system, and therefore may use any of the requirements in point (3) to calculate the contingency fuel.
3. The contingency fuel should be the fuel described in points (3)(a) or (3)(b) whichever is higher:

a. an amount of fuel that should be either:

i. not less than 3% of the planned trip fuel, or in the event of in-flight re-planning, 3% of the trip fuel for the remainder of the flight provided that a fuel en route alternate (fuel ERA) aerodrome is available; or
ii. an amount of fuel sufficient for 20-minute flying time based upon the planned trip fuel consumption;

b. an amount of fuel to fly for 5 minutes at holding speed at 1500 ft (450 m) above the destination aerodrome in standard conditions.

## 8.1.7.4 Basic Fuel Scheme with Variations – Location of the Fuel En Route Alternate Aerodrome to Reduce Contingency Fuel to 3%

AMC7 CAT.OP.MPA.181

The use of a fuel ERA allows contingency fuel to be reduced to 3% of the trip fuel.

The fuel en route alternate (fuel ERA) aerodrome should be located within a circle with a radius equal to 20% of the total flight plan distance; the centre of this circle lies on the planned route at a distance from the destination aerodrome equal to 25% of the total flight plan distance, or at least 20% of the total flight plan distance plus 50 NM, whichever is greater. All distances should be calculated in still-air conditions (see Figure 1). The fuel ERA aerodrome should be nominated in the operational flight plan.

## 8.1.7.6 Contingency Fuel

Contingency fuel is the amount of fuel required to compensate for unforeseen factors.

Unforeseen factors are those that could have an influence on the fuel consumption to the destination aerodrome, such as deviations of an individual aeroplane from the expected fuel consumption data, deviations from forecast meteorological conditions, extended unexpected delays in flight, extended unexpected taxi times, and deviations from planned routings and/or cruising levels.

Ryanair identifies each flight on which contingency fuel is used and the frequency of this and adjusts the LIDO OFP accordingly until such flights are conducted with contingency fuel remaining in compliance with the fuel monitoring programme.

## 8.1.7.6.1 Departure Runway/SID OFP Discrepancy

Contingency fuel is carried to account for deviations from planned routings (including departures and arrivals). Prior to pushback/engine start for the purpose of take-off, if it is established that the expected departure route is significantly longer than the departure depicted on the OFP, the commander may accept the FMC arrival fuel prediction at destination on condition that the FMC has been updated with actual ZFW, TOC and average winds. Crews shall evaluate the effect, if any, of the pilot input FMC SID stop altitude as this can cause an artificially high fuel burn in certain circumstances. If required, remove the restriction, check arrival fuel prediction and reinstate the restriction. The commander shall review the operational impact of the change of routing in deciding whether to accept the OFP or to request a revised OFP depicting the actual RWY/SID in use. Following a Runway/SID change, prior to engine start for the purpose of take off the FMC predicted fuel at destination shall be at least the sum of CONT + ALTN + FINRES + DEV + TAXI.

## 8.1.7.6.2

If during taxi for the purpose of taking off, delays/departure routing/Rwy changes are encountered such that the FMC predicted fuel at destination is less than ALTN + FINRES + DEV Fuel, the Commander, having updated the FMC with accurate TOC and average winds, shall return to stand unless the fuel required for alternate, final reserve and deviation fuel meet with flight plan requirements.

## 8.1.7.7 Fuel Policy Destination Alternate Aerodrome

The departure aerodrome may be selected as the destination alternate aerodrome. If an alternate is changed then the adjusted flight plan fuel required shall be on board.

## 8.1.7.8 Fuel and Oil Supply

CAT.OP.MPA.260

The commander shall only commence a flight or continue in the event of in-flight re-planning, when satisfied that the aircraft carries at least the planned amount of usable fuel and oil to safely complete the flight, taking into account the expected operating conditions.

## 8.1.7.9 Ryanair Fuel Policies

Ryanair’s fuel policy is to carry the correct amount of fuel necessary to safely and efficiently complete the flight while meeting regulatory requirements and maintaining operational flexibility. Every flight shall carry a sufficient amount of fuel, to complete the planned flight safely and to allow for deviations from the planned operation.

It is important to note, that the safe conclusion of any flight depends on the accuracy and completeness of initial planning as well as the intelligent use of on-board resources. Flight planning activities must be complemented by practical in-flight fuel management policies and procedures. The final authority and responsibility for fuel loads to be carried and the fuel management rests with the Commander.

Having ascertained the ZFW or estimated ZFW the Commander will determine the final fuel in accordance with the Ryanair fuel policy of Section 8.1.7. The required final fuel will then be passed to the Ramp Agent and to the Refueller. Fuel uplift shall be ordered in kilogrammes (kg) of fuel.

## Dispatch with over-fuelled aircraft.

Typically an over-fuel situation will arise from an aircraft swap whereby an aircraft fuelled for a long flight is swapped on to shorter flight which would result in arrival at destination heavier than the MLW.

It is prohibited to use high drag techniques for the purpose of increasing fuel burn during any phase of flight after take-off with the exception of Gear Down in a holding pattern having consulted PI-QRH, and having received an ATC clearance to hold or otherwise delay arrival.

page 8-64

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR
PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

In order to meet with Performance Dispatch requirements a new flight plan is required to reflect the swopped aircraft TOW. To ensure the flight plan reflects a landing at or below Maximum Landing Weight, Flight Planning will consider the following options:

- Reducing zero fuel weight (ZFW) by offloading cargo (consult with Ops).
- Extended taxi or ‘Push and Hold’ with engines running, (fuel burn attributable to this recorded as Planned Taxi Fuel Burn in OPT W&amp;B or in the paper Loadsheet).
- Flight plan at a lower level.
- Flight plan with a longer routing.

Note: The new flight plan produced by Flight Planning will not plan holding with the gear extended in order to satisfy a landing at or below Maximum Landing Weight.

The landing must be completed at or below MLW unless a non-normal situation dictates otherwise.

The OFP will have the following company notam:

“This flight plan route/burn has been adjusted to take account of over-fuelled condition. Ensure MLW limitation is not exceeded at touchdown”.

## 8.1.7.9.1 PIC Discretionary Fuel

It is the Company's policy to achieve optimum fuel economy by means of fuel uplift management together with fuel saving techniques in operation.

In keeping with this policy:

Flight crew shall load PIC Discretionary Fuel only if, in the Commander’s opinion, the extra fuel is required due to forecast meteorological or operational requirements. PIC Discretionary Fuel should be considered in terms of endurance (time) and not quantity of fuel (for example 15 minutes is half final reserve fuel). PIC Discretionary Fuel is carried at the discretion of the Commander for operational reasons. This Discretionary fuel is additional to the required OFP fuel.

PIC Discretionary Fuel should not be carried unless there are sound operational or economic reasons for doing so. If potential disruption is not evident during pre-flight planning, then it is acceptable practice for the commander to dispatch with OFP Block Fuel or adjusted Block Fuel. To avoid first wave delays, GOPS will load OFP Block Fuel rounded up to the nearest $100\mathrm{~kgs} + 100\mathrm{~kgs}$ when pre-fueling aircraft.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

Additional fuel will be included on the OFP where experience on a route has shown that flight level restrictions or destination holding are common this fuel will be added to the ATC or OPN fields of the OFP.

The penalty for carriage of extra fuel is approximately 2.5% of extra fuel per hour of flight. (i.e. on a 4 hour flight up to 10% of the extra fuel uplifted will be burned off due to the increased aircraft weight).

Even with the best planning information and practices, not all factors can be foreseen. If the flight is well planned and well executed, a diversion is not considered a failure. The occasional weather related diversion is preferable and less expensive than an overly conservative fuel carriage philosophy. Commanders shall anticipate flight disruption and arrival delays when the destination forecast indicates conditions approaching limiting values. When limiting weather conditions are forecast at airports crews will consider the implications of multiple flight disruptions when assessing the PIC Discretionary fuel or diversion fuel requirements, which may be more than 15 minutes.

When widespread disruption is forecasted (several airports in the same geographic region subjected to similar weather phenomena such as snow, gale force winds or fog) crews will consider selecting an ‘out of area’ alternate at the planning stage. As the flight progresses more accurate information can be gathered and a closer alternate can be selected. Selection of a closer alternate while enroute will allow the commander to consider using some of the extra fuel for the planned alternate for holding or other disruption at the planned destination before proceeding to the new closer alternate.

In addition to ensuring legal dispatch requirements are met crews must also give consideration to the primary objective of completing the planned flight. For example, an airport which is forecast PROB30 CB’s or PROB40 CB’s may only require 1 alternate provided the other required minima are met. In these circumstances, it may be prudent to take some holding fuel in order to increase the chances of completing the flight to the planned destination.

The following guidelines will be used in helping to determine when to load PIC Discretionary Fuel:

- Volcanic Ash Event in progress or operations in close proximity to areas known to contain increased levels of volcanic ash.
- Thunderstorms are forecast;
- Operational landing limits are forecast or reported to be 2/3 or less of the forecast RVR, for example landing RVR 600 m and forecast is 900 m RVR or less.

page 8–66

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

- An unscheduled runway closure, affecting the arrival rate, for example: Freezing precipitation (runway treatment) – Moderate to heavy intensity snowfall (snow removal);
- Crosswinds in excess of 2/3 of limiting value;
- Contaminated runway limitations (it may be desirable to reduce landing weight and not increase by loading extra fuel in these conditions);
- Forecast en-route adverse meteorological conditions.

When risk of diversion is high i.e. weather approaching limits, curfew etc. the Commander shall ensure that; weather and NOTAM permitting, the highest ranked commercial alternate (C1, C2, C3 etc.) is planned. In this situation if the commercial alternate is also listed on the OFP as a suitable alternate, the Commander may simply uplift the fuel required for the chosen commercial alternate.

If the commercial alternate is not listed as a suitable alternate the Commander shall contact Operations to request a new OFP with the agreed commercial alternate. The revised fuel figure shall be recorded as BLOCK FUEL in the Electronic Flight Log, or FPF FUEL on the paper Voyage Report. Where OPS are not informed of the change of alternate ATC will anticipate a diversion to the planned alternate unless advised otherwise. Commercial alternates are listed in order of preference as C1, C2 and C3, etc. If the commercial alternate is not listed as a suitable alternate on the OFP (failed suitability check at the planning stage etc.) the Commander may still choose to divert to the commercial alternate subject to:

1. A review of NOTAMs.
2. FMC predicted arrival fuel at the alternate is greater than final reserve fuel after entering the expected route, Long Range Cruise (LRC), expected cruise altitude, arrival and expected approach.
3. Negotiating ATC approval to divert to an alternate that may not be included in the OFP.

The final decision on the required fuel rests with the Commander.

When Total Fuel is greater than the Block Fuel rounded up to the nearest 100 kgs + 100 kg, the Commander shall record on the Electronic Flight Log (or paper Voyage Report) the reason for the difference between the Total Fuel and the Block Fuel. Such explanations will include reference to relevant supporting documentation, including but not limited to weather, Notams etc.

## 8.1.7.9.2 Fuel Saving is Effected at the Planning Stage by:

Using the computerised plan which accurately accounts for enroute winds and estimated aircraft weight.

Using ECON speeds for the flight plan.

## 8.1.7.10 Fuel Icing

The LIDO flight planning system will not plan a flight through areas of extremely low temperature. If an en-route segment has an average temperature of -70 °C or below, the flight will either be rerouted or the cruising flight level will be limited to minimize the aircrafts exposure to extremely low temperatures.

### Tankering and Environmental Icing

On Tanker sectors, if the crew considers that de-icing is expected at destination, then round-trip fuel may be carried subject to possibly restrictive landing performance, and the aircraft will be de/anti-iced as per Ryanair de/anti-icing procedures using holdover tables in the Technical Log.

### Tankering Limitations

The commercial decision to tanker fuel will be made automatically by LIDO. Trip fuel is calculated to include the fuel used for carriage and will be included as TANKERING on the LIDO Flight Plan. To maximise the benefits of fuel tankering, captains are required to tanker the fuel recommended by LIDO unless there are sound operational reasons for not doing so. Flight crew shall adjust the LIDO tankering recommendation when take-off or landing is anticipated on a contaminated runway. Tankering in excess of fuel required on the subsequent sector is uneconomical, the carriage of any such extra fuel shall be explained on the Electronic Flight Log.

Tankering is subject to landing performance, and CSFF considerations.

LIDO calculates optimum tanker fuel. Do not uplift for multi-sector tankering unless specifically directed.

On tanker sectors the Next Leg fuel requirement is indicated under the Planned Fuel section on the OFP. This information is used to calculate the uplift when tankering subject to limitations described above.

When tankering is planned and included in the OFP, LIDO will consider the additional trip fuel required to carry this fuel. The flight plan trip fuel will be automatically adjusted to reflect this. Where tankering has not been planned or included in the OFP crews shall correct the trip fuel using the operational impacts section of the OFP or the formula below.

page 8–68

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

The following formula may be used to calculate the additional Trip Fuel burn when ATOW exceeds FPL TOW on non-tankering sectors:

2.5% of the additional weight (fuel in this example) carried in kg, multiplied by the flight time in hours. For the B737-NG, 2.5% of the additional fuel carried in kg, multiplied by the flight time in hours, e.g. 1500 kg additional weight (fuel) carried for a 2 hr flight = 0.025 × 1500 × 2.0 = 75 kg.

## 8.1.8 Mass (Weight) and Center of Gravity

Ryanair has established a Mass and Balance (MAB) Programme derived from data from Boeing including the AFM. This policy is contained in the OMB, FCOM Vol 1, QRH and OMA. The Ryanair Mass and Balance programme is managed and maintained in regulatory compliance by the HEAD OF FLIGHT OPERATIONS PERFORMANCE ENGINEERING who reports to the NPFO. In this section the word weight and mass are interchangeable.

## 8.1.8.1 Mass and Balance, Loading CAT.POL.MAB.100

1. Ryanair ensures that during any phase of operation, the loading, mass and Centre of Gravity (CG) of the aircraft complies with the limitations specified in the AFM, or the operations manual if more restrictive.
2. Ryanair establishes the mass and the CG of each aircraft by actual weighing prior to initial entry into service and thereafter at intervals of nine years as fleet masses are used. The accumulated effects of modifications and repairs on the mass and balance are accounted for and properly documented. Aircraft are reweighed if the effect of modifications on the mass and balance is not accurately known.
3. The weighing is accomplished by the manufacturer of the aircraft or by an approved maintenance organisation.
4. Ryanair determines the mass of all operating items and crew members included in the aircraft dry operating mass by using standard masses. The influence of their position on the aircraft’s CG is also determined.
5. Ryanair establishes the mass of the traffic load in accordance with standard passenger and baggage masses.
6. In addition to standard masses for passengers and checked baggage, Ryanair can use standard masses for other load items, if it demonstrates to the IAA that these items have the same mass or that their masses are within specified tolerances.
7. Ryanair determines the mass of the fuel load by using the actual density or, if not known, a fuel density of .79.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

8. Ryanair ensures that the loading of
   a. its aircraft is performed under the supervision of qualified personnel; and
   b. traffic load is consistent with the data used for the calculation of the aircraft mass and balance.

9. Ryanair Mass and Balance Policy complies with additional structural limits such as the floor strength limitations, the maximum load per running metre, the maximum mass per cargo compartment and the maximum seating limit.

10. Ryanair specifies in OMB – Mass and Balance, the principles and methods involved in the loading and in the mass and balance system that meet the requirements contained in this section which covers all types of intended operations.

## 8.1.8.2 Mass and Balance Data and Documentation CAT.POL.MAB.105

1. Ryanair establishes mass and balance data and produces mass and balance documentation (OPT W&amp;B/Loadsheet) prior to each flight specifying the load and its distribution. The loadsheet may be in paper or electronic format. The OPT Weight and Balance App and/or the paper loadsheet enable the commander to determine that the load and its distribution is such that the mass and balance limits of the aircraft are not exceeded. The paper Loadsheet contains the following information:
   a. Aircraft registration and type;
   b. Flight identification, number and date;
   c. Name of the commander;
   d. Name of the person who prepared the document;
   e. Dry operating mass and the corresponding CG of the aircraft;
   f. Mass of the fuel at take-off and the mass of trip fuel;
   g. Mass of consumables other than fuel, if applicable;
   h. Load components including passengers, baggage, freight and ballast;
   i. Take-off mass, landing mass and zero fuel mass;
   j. Applicable aircraft CG positions; and
   k. The limiting mass and CG values.

8.1.8.16 When the Commander considers that it maybe be necessary to leave bags behind the Commander shall:

- Establish the limiting RTOW.
- Choose the closest suitable destination alternate subject to weather conditions to minimise the fuel uplift. Advise Ryanair Dublin Operations and enter relevant remarks in EFL Captains Remarks.
- Establish the actual ZFW prior to fuel uplift.
- Personally check all OPT W&amp;B/loadsheet entries for accuracy.
- Consider a tech stop in consultation with Ryanair Dublin Operations.
- Do not uplift any fuel in excess of OFP Operation Flight Plan Fuel resulting in bags being left behind.
- Where fuel in excess of OFP has been loaded, it is preferable to extend taxi time and burn excess fuel in order to reduce the take-off weight to RTOW. The maximum taxi weight is published in the fleet weight schedule by fleet.
- “Push and Hold”: the aircraft pushes back, starts engines or powers off and proceeds to a designated hold area and remains there with engines running.

The weight reduction to be accomplished by fuel burn during “push and hold” shall be included in the taxi fuel on the loadsheet. The taxi limiting weight shall apply when the aircraft is taxied for take-off. It is preferable to burn 700 kg of fuel during an extended taxi/holding period, rather than leave 54 bags behind. ATC permission to accomplish this “push and hold” procedure is required.

8.1.8.17 Loadsheet Discrepancies and LMCs

Should a paper loadsheet discrepancy arise, or a LMC be required, the aircraft will be closed up as normal providing the paper loadsheet has been signed by the Commander. Any loadsheet discrepancy shall be resolved over the headset with the doors closed.

Procedure flow:

- Completed paper Load Sheet with the Captain.
- The Captain will sign the paper loadsheet and return a copy together with a copy of the Technical Log to the dispatcher.
- The Captain will sign the load sheet and return a copy together with a copy of the Technical Log to the dispatcher.

- The dispatcher will immediately leave the aircraft.
- The No.1 will select the flight deck access switch to NORM, lock the flight deck door and close the L1 door.
- The Cabin Crew will then assist the passengers to take their seats in the cabin.
- When the aircraft is on stand, entering “I” and “ENT” on the flight deck door access panel gives an audible tone twice in the flight deck this signifies all passengers are seated.
- In the event that a paper loadsheet amendment or LMC is required the paper loadsheet will be lowered to the ramp agent.
- Once the corrected paperwork is returned to the flight deck, the Before Start checklist is completed to the line and push and start is requested.

## 8.1.8.18 Verification of Mass and Balance Documentation Used

To determine the Allowed Traffic Load:

1. The maximum weight for take-off shall be the Regulated Take-off Weight (RTOW) for the conditions and runway in use and not a default maximum certified or structural take-off weight (MTOW), as used previously. This value shall be calculated for every take-off, regardless of the length of runway in use, and shall not exceed the placarded MTOW.
2. The maximum weight for landing under normal conditions shall be the Regulated Landing Weight for the expected runway in use and forecast or actual conditions as appropriate. This value shall not exceed maximum certified landing weight. Paper loadsheets must show the maximum weights as calculated and notified by you.

## Newspapers

When newspapers are carried on board, refer to the dispatcher or OMB – Mass and Balance for instructions on Paper Loadsheet completion and storage of newspapers on board.

The Commander is required to examine the OPT W&amp;B/paper loadsheet to ensure that the maximum structural weights and the regulated take-off weight for the expected flight are not exceeded and that the centre of gravity is within the aircraft limits.

## Paper Loadsheets

In order to minimise the chance of single or cumulative errors, here is a short “checklist” of items to look out for:

- Have an up-to-date weight certificate to hand.

## 8.1.8.19 Acceptance/Certification of OFP

The OFP No is automatically recorded when the Commander accepts the eLID by entering their PIN code in the EFL App. Where paper LID/Loadsheet are in use, the OFP request No. will be recorded by the commander in the S.I. box on the paper loadsheet or the designated OFP number box on the LID.

![img-2.jpeg](img-2.jpeg)

![img-3.jpeg](img-3.jpeg)

## 8.1.8.20 Load Information Document (LID)

The LID may be in paper or electronic format (eLID). The Load Information Document (LID) is prepared by the handling agent or the flight crew at gate closure. The policy of LID completion following gate closure is designed to minimise LMCs. The flight crew will use the passenger and cargo distribution information contained in the LID/eLID to complete the electronic OPT W&amp;B prior to flight.

## 8.1.8.20.1 LID Discrepancies and LMCs

In the event that a LID amendment or LMC is required after the aircraft doors have been closed, the eLID may be edited by the handling agent or the commander and electronically submitted. For a paper LID amendment, the document will be lowered to the ramp agent, amended by the ramp agent and returned to the flight deck. Alternatively, the flight crew may amend the paper LID when contact with the ramp agent is available on the headset.

Note: The handling agent must confirm that any amendment to the eLID is acknowledged by the commander.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## 8.1.9 ATS Flight Plan

### Submission of the ATS Flight Plan

Ryanair Operation is responsible for ensuring that every Ryanair flight has a flight plan submitted and accepted by ATC prior to departure.

A flight plan may be submitted by Boeing or Ryanair Operations for delivery flights.

Contents of ATS flight plan can be found OMC, Jeppesen General Airway Manual. A detailed description of LIDO flight plan and associated terminology is presented in OMA 8.1.10.

A Ryanair flight plan is automatically cancelled in the ATS system when a delay of 15 minutes after filed departure time has occurred. Operating crews and dispatchers must manage the communication of revised departure times to Ryanair Operations to prevent or minimise Flight Ops delays for cancelled flight plans.

A flight plan may be refiled in flight with ATS.

## 8.1.9.1 In-Flight Changes to Flight Plans

### Operations

Ryanair operations shall ensure that the in-flight operational instructions involving a change to the air traffic flight plan shall, when practicable, be coordinated with the appropriate Air Traffic Service unit before transmission to an aeroplane.

### Flight Crew

When crew receive a rerouting, upon reaching the last cleared point crew should confirm next point with ATC. Do not clear FMC route discontinuities without ATC confirmation.

## 8.1.9.2 Calculated Take-off Time (CTOT) Slot Allocation

If a flight is delayed or is expecting a delay beyond the STD/CTOT the crew shall advise handling agents to contact Dub Ops by the most expeditious means, indicating when they expect to be ready to depart, and Dub Ops shall secure a new CTOT. If it is not possible to contact Dub Ops then ATC shall be advised that you will miss your STD/CTOT.

8.1.9.3 Mode S

Flight Crews shall accurately operate their Mode S transponders. The flight callsign on the OFP shall be entered correctly in the data field provided on the RTE page and the format is the three letter IATA designator for the aircraft operating agency, followed by the flight alphanumeric (e.g. RYR212 or RYR2G etc).

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## 8.1.10 Operational Flight Plan (OFP)

AMC1 CAT.OP.MPA.175, 180

## 8.1.10.1 Requirements

The Operational Flight Plan provides for the determination of the fuel to be carried in accordance with Section 8.1.7.2, Ryanair Fuel Scheme (CAT.OP.MPA.180). The Lido plan can be calculated up to 10 hours before departure. At the time the plan is calculated, a suitability check will be performed on destination and alternate airports based on the latest weather forecast and Notam information. If the suitability check is disabled by operations, a remark will appear on the flight plan to advise crew. The commander must always review the most up to date forecast information for departure, destination and alternate aerodromes before deciding to accept or reject the OFP.

## 8.1.10.1.1 Operational Flight Plan

AMC1 CAT.OP.MPA.175(a)

All entries on the operational flight plan will be made concurrently and be permanent in nature.

The flight plan requirements are specified below.

## Route to Alternate (RTA)

The Flight Level from DEST to ALTN displayed on the OFP indicates the highest FL as planned by Lido along the route to alternate. Fuel requirements for the route to alternate are calculated at the flight level displayed in the RTA section of the OFP.

The missed approach of the destination runway is calculated, followed by a DCT segment to the end point of the departure procedure at the destination aerodrome. Thereafter the already calculated route between departure and arrival procedure is used to proceed to the alternate aerodrome.

On short routings to alternate where the missed approach procedure intersects the STAR, a low FL may be planned by Lido (see example above: EGCC to EGNM @ FL050). This does not prevent the crew from requesting a higher level from ATC if required.

## Commercial Alternate

A commercial alternate is an aerodrome selected and approved by the company. It may not be the nearest airport to the destination, but it may be more desirable to use for commercial reasons. Commercial alternates are listed in the RTA section of the OFP. Commercial alternates are labelled in order of preference as C1, C2 and C3, etc. The sequence of alternates may change based on fuel requirements, suitability checks, or if manually sequenced by the flight planning department.

When risk of diversion is high, i.e. weather approaching limits, curfew etc. the Commander shall ensure that; weather and NOTAM permitting, the highest ranked commercial alternate (C1, C2, C3 etc.) is planned.

## Fuel Alternate

A fuel alternate is an aerodrome selected and approved by the company. It is usually the nearest airport to the destination. It may not be desirable to use for commercial reasons (e.g. inadequate ground transport) but may be included for flight planning purposes. The letter F will appear alongside a fuel alternate to advise the crew.

Fuel alternate shall only be planned when the risk of diversion is low.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

When risk of diversion is low, i.e. weather is good, no curfew etc., the Commander may expect that; weather and NOTAM permitting, the fuel alternate is planned.

## Unsuitable Alternate

The commercial alternates, in a small number of cases, are deemed ‘US’ (unsuitable) by Lido during the airport suitability check. This anomaly occurs when no weather forecast is available or the weather is deemed to be outside of limits for that airport at the time of flight plan calculation.

If a commercial alternate (C1 or C2) fails a weather suitability check, the alternate information (Track, Distance, Route, Flight Level, MORA, Wind Component, Time and Fuel) will still be displayed in the Route to Alternate section of the OFP.

The designator ‘US’ will appear beside the ‘unsuitable’ alternate to highlight the fact that it has failed a suitability check at the planning stage.

## Unsuitable Alternate Procedure

If the commercial alternate is listed as ‘US’ on the OFP, the commander may elect to divert to this alternate following a review of NOTAMs, weather and fuel requirements.

Weather and NOTAMs for the ‘unsuitable’ alternate will be automatically included in the briefing package.

## Expanded Route to Alternate (RTA)

The expanded route to alternate is displayed at the end of the flight plan. RTA will show the full routing from the destination to each alternate.

Alternate fuel calculations are based on the Lido Block Fuel. The fuel required for the flight planned commercial alternate is included in the OFP Block Fuel. Additional listed alternates may have higher fuel requirements.

**Note:** Alternates with higher fuel requirements may display an EFOB figure less than FINRES, or a negative EFOB figure in the expanded RTA section on the OFP. Crew must manage fuel requirements in accordance with sections 8.1.7.9.1, PIC Discretionary Fuel and 8.3.7.1, In-flight Fuel Management Policy when selecting and planning alternates.

## 8.1.10.1.2 Shear Value on OFP

Theoretically there is no upper limit to the value but generally it will appear as a single digit. The wind shear value is calculated from wind speed and direction samples taken 2,000 ft above and 2,000 ft below the datum level. The lower the number the less the chance of turbulence.

Moderate turbulence can be encountered whenever the shear value is equal to or greater than 3. Severe turbulence can be anticipated whenever the shear value is equal to or greater than 5.

There is no exact correlation between the shear value and clear air turbulence (CAT). For example, a shear value of zero just means that the winds above and below the datum are the same; it gives no indication of the wind speed and direction at the datum level which could be a 100 kt jetstream (with high turbulence) or 20 kt light winds (with no turbulence).

Commanders shall review this section of the OFP at the pre-flight stage, compare it with forecast areas of turbulence and brief the SCCM on any anticipated areas of turbulence en route. Be aware that the predictions are based on forecast winds and should be used for guidance in conjunction with the information derived from Significant Weather charts and Sigmets.

## 8.1.10.1.3 Fuel ERA and 3% Contingency OFP

The use of a fuel ERA allows contingency fuel to be reduced to 3% of trip fuel, providing that 3% contingency fuel is greater than an amount of fuel to fly for 5 minutes at holding speed at 1500 ft (450 m) above the destination aerodrome in standard conditions.

The Fuel ERA location is automatically generated by LIDO in compliance with the formula in 8.1.7.4.

The same in-flight fuel checks, fuel management monitoring and re-planning in-flight considerations apply whether with 5% or 3% contingency as described in 8.3.7.1.

‘FUEL EN ROUTE ALTERNATE (ERA) AERODROME’ Fuel ERA aerodromes could be used in the following cases:

1. ‘fuel ERA aerodrome critical scenario’: that aerodrome is used when additional fuel is required at the most critical point along the route to comply with point (3)(f) of OMA 8.1.7.3 (CAT.OP.MPA.181)
2. ‘fuel ERA aerodrome 3%’: that aerodrome is used when Ryanair reduces the contingency fuel to 3%.

## LIDO Suitability Check 3% Contingency

Lido automatically performs suitability checks when selecting any alternate aerodrome. The Fuel ERA suitability check establishes that the Fuel ERA qualifies firstly as an alternate, and secondly that it is located in the geographical area required by the Fuel ERA formula. The suitability check includes:

- That the alternate is an adequate aerodrome on which the aircraft can be operated taking account of the applicable performance requirements and runway characteristics.
- That the weather forecast for -/+1 hour of expected time of use indicates minima enabling an instrument approach, or in the absence of an instrument approach a visual approach to be completed, and is therefore weather permissible.
- Check of NOTAMS to ensure that the aircraft can land safely at the FUEL ERA if required.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

If an aerodrome fails the LIDO Fuel ERA suitability check it cannot be a Fuel ERA and LIDO will seek the next aerodrome which complies with Fuel ERA requirements. If LIDO cannot find a qualifying Fuel ERA in the area specified in the formula, LIDO will NOT generate a 3% Contingency OFP.

## Identification of a 3% Contingency OFP

A 3% contingency OFP will be annotated in the Dispatch Remarks Text box on the OFP and have CONT 3% in the fuel section instead of CONT 5% and Fuel ERA IATA code on the OFP.

DISP RMK:

“THIS OFP IS PLANNED 3% CONTINGENCY FUEL. THE FUEL ERA HAS BEEN SELECTED IN COMPLIANCE WITH OMA 8.1.7.4. LIDO AUTOMATICALLY CONDUCTED A SUITABILITY CHECK WHICH INCLUDED WEATHER AND NOTAM CHECK AND VALID FOR +/- 1HR OF POTENTIAL TIME OF USE TO IDENTIFY THE FUEL ERA”

| FUEL    | ARPT | FUEL  | TIME |
| ------- | ---- | ----- | ---- |
| TRIP    | STN  | 11706 | 0444 |
| CONT 3% | BOH  | 351   | 0009 |
| ALTN    | LTN  | 1111  | 0025 |
| FINRES  |      | 1130  | 0030 |
| ADD RES |      | 0     | 0000 |
| ETP     |      | 0     | 0000 |

## 3% Contingency Planning requirements

At flight planning, crews do not have to verify that the Fuel ERA automatically generated by LIDO complies with the formula in OMA 8.1.7.4. This is a required element of the LIDO certification.

Crews do have to establish that the briefing pack contains weather forecast and Notams for the Fuel ERA and verify that the Fuel ERA is weather permissible for the period of potential use, (OMA 8.1.5. refers), to enable crew acceptance of the 3% Contingency OFP.

## 3% Operational Impacts

When operating on a 3% OFP the standard in-flight fuel checks, in-flight re-planning considerations and continue/divert considerations apply. If a diversion is required en-route any en-route alternate may be used, including the Fuel ERA, as deemed appropriate by the aircraft commander.

## 8.1.10.2 Computerised OFP

The briefing pack may be paper or electronic format. All OFPs will be prepared by the OCC and presented on EFL/LIDOBRIEF. As required it may be electronically transmitted to another station or station office for hard copy delivery to the Commander. OFPs should not normally be downloaded (or printed) prior to STD-45 mins on the first sector of the rostered duty to prevent an invalid OFP on the first sector, or subsequent sectors.

The OCC frequently change flight plans after they have been posted to EFL/LIDOBRIEF. If the briefing pack has been downloaded/printed before STD-45, it is imperative that the flight plan be rechecked at STD-45 mins to confirm its validity prior to departure.

- Electronically: For EFL, crew should open the EFL App and select ‘Download’ to check for the latest flight plan. If the EFL update is not available, then crew must contact the OCC or check the Lidobrief portal to verify validity of the OFP.
- Paper: For Lidobrief, check the printed OFP number against the onscreen OFP number. If the numbers do not match, then you must print the updated flight plan.

Note: Each OFP is assigned a random OFP Number. Should your OFP be updated by the OCC, the latest assigned OFP number may be higher or lower than the original OFP Number.

![img-4.jpeg](img-4.jpeg)

When a flight plan change is made after STD-45 mins, the OCC will contact the crew by phone/dispatcher/handling agent or any other appropriate means.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## OFP Changes after STD-45

When there is a change to an OFP after reporting for duty, the OCC e-mails the OFP change notification to the Handling Agent. The OFP Change notification e-mail is copied to the operating crews’ EFBs solely to alert them of the OFP change notification, i.e. that a new OFP is required to enable completion of the flight preparation required for dispatch.

EFBs shall be checked for communications from Ryanair during all ground operations. Specifically the EFBs should be checked for an OFP change notification when commencing the RIBETS – Route Check. Prior to route entry, select ‘Download’ in EFL to check for the latest OFP. Where a check of EFB emails or EFL App indicates an OFP update, then the latest flight plan must be downloaded for flight. If unable to download electronic flight plans, then the crew should contact the handling agent and request the hard copy plans at first available opportunity.

All electronic OFP change notifications on EFBs will be acknowledged by the flight crew member by replying to the OFP change notification email. There is no text required, simply open the e-mail and press REPLY – SEND as the acknowledgment.

The OCC will cancel the flight plan for a sector where an updated OFP has not been downloaded by the operating crew at STD-10 mins. In this case check with the OCC for the new flight plan and new flight number/call sign.

## 8.1.10.3 OFP as a Navigation Log

The Operational Flight Plan as well as recording pertinent details of the Flight operated will be used as a navigation log for;

1. Recording fuel and time checks at the first convenient waypoint in the cruise, and thereafter at or abeam OFP waypoints at no more than 60 minute intervals. Fuel checks are made in accordance with OMA 8.3.7.1 Inflight fuel checks and fuel management.
2. Enroute and destination diversions routings are to be logged.
3. All ATC clearances must be recorded accurately when the aircraft is on the ground and in flight in the Cruise, usually by the PM.

Total Fuel figure must always be entered. The requirement to tanker is reflected in the calculated departure fuel, i.e. the ETOW of 69,990 would represent booked payload plus trip fuel plus tankered fuel to 69,990 kgs.

The corrected amount less taxi shall also be shown at the top of the EFOB column and subsequent figures altered accordingly. AFOB will be completed in tandem with ATOs.

The weather for the aerodrome of departure and landing shall be recorded on the OFP. Record alternate actuals and/or other en-route wx reports when required.

## 8.1.10.4 Flight Preparation Conversion Tables

Any conversion tables necessary to support operations where metric heights, altitudes and flight levels are required will be provided by Ryanair as a quick reference laminated checklist on the flight deck. These tables are in OMC Jeppesen General Airway Manual.

## 8.1.10.5 Ditching CAT.GEN.MPA.150, CAT.IDE.A.285

Ryanair aircraft will always be flight planned to remain within 400 nms from an adequate aerodrome unless Liferafts are carried.

## 8.1.10.6 ORO.MLR.110 Journey log

Voyage Reports

AMC1 ORO.MLR.110 Journey log

General

1. The aircraft journey log, or voyage report will include the following items, where applicable:

a. Aircraft registration,
b. Date,
c. Name(s) of crew member(s),
d. Duty assignments of crew member(s),
e. Place of departure,
f. Place of arrival,
g. Time of departure,
h. Time of arrival,
i. Hours of flight,
j. Nature of flight (scheduled or non-scheduled),
k. Incidents, observations, if any,
l. Signature of person in charge.

2. The information, or parts thereof, may be recorded in a form other than on printed paper. This is the Electronic Flight Log or crewlink in Ryanair and accessibility, usability and reliability is assured by Ryanair IT.

3. ‘Series of flights’, means consecutive flights, which begin and end:

a. Within a 24 hour period;

b. At the same aerodrome or operating site or remain within a local area specified in the operations manual; and
c. With the same pilot-in-command/commander of the aircraft.

Paper Voyage Reports must be completed correctly and signed by the Commander on completion of the series of sectors which constitute his assigned flight duty. The Paper Voyage Report contains details of flight times, delay reports, fuel data, FDT and DT. The data fields on the VR shall be completed as follows:

- Fuel Uplift LTS: Exact fuel uplifts in litres as indicated on the fuel receipt(s) e.g. 7856 ltr.
- FPL Fuel: The exact Block Fuel on the OFP to the kg, e.g. 8456 kg.
- Dep Fuel: Fuel onboard to nearest 10 kg, as per fuel gauges and tech log, e.g. 7240 kg.
- Arr Fuel: Fuel onboard to nearest 10 kg, as per fuel gauges and tech log e.g. 2340 kg.
- Burn Off: Difference between Dep Fuel and Arr fuel to nearest 10 kg, e.g. 4270 kg.
- PLOG BURN: APU + TRIP: The total of APU + OFP TRIP + TAXI. (TRIP+ TAXI in LIDO as APU included in Taxi fuel)

Do not include contingency fuel in this calculation.

## 8.1.10.6.1 Electronic Flight Log (EFL)

The Electronic Flight Log must be completed correctly and verified by the Commander on completion of the series of sectors which constitute the assigned flight duty. The EFL enables crew to download and view the electronic briefing pack including the electronic OFP for their flight. The user may also view Crew and Flight Information, HIL, Weather data and eLID information. EFL allows crew to electronically submit Fuel, Payload, Times, Delays and GOPs data. ECDR reports are also submitted through the EFL App.

Expanded guidance for use and completion of EFL is provided in the EFB User Guide.

### EFL Preflight Procedures

Crew must log-in to the EFL App at the start of each duty.

Crew must download the electronic briefing pack for each sector within EFL. If the EFL is unavailable or crew are unable to download the electronic briefing pack, then the full Lido briefing pack must be printed and used for flight.

### EFL Normal Cockpit Procedures

Crew should enter the relevant information for the flight.

### EFL Post Flight Procedure

The Electronic Flight Log data may be submitted by either the Captain or First Officer. Once all flight information has been submitted by the First Officer the data must be verified by the Captain.

Crew should ensure that they log out of the EFL App once all data has been successfully submitted.

## 8.1.10.6.2 Paper Voyage Report

Where Electronic Flight Log is not available, the paper voyage report must be completed correctly and signed by the Commander on completion of the series of sectors which constitute his assigned flight duty. The Voyage Report contains details of flight times, delay reports, fuel data, FDT and DT. The data fields on the VR should be completed as follows:

## 8.1.11.2 Certification of Technical Log ORO.MLR.100

Commanders shall ensure that the following tasks are completed:

1. The recording, and rectification of defects, together with certification;
2. The recording and certification of servicing with fuel and oils and of de-icing;
3. Certification of completion of the Pre-flight/Transit inspection; and
4. Certification of acceptance of the aircraft for flight by the Commander;
5. If flight crew encounter a possible defect during flight operations the Commander shall, at the next shutdown use the MAINTROL/FLIGHT CREW COMMUNICATIONS FLOW CHART below which is also contained in the aircraft Technical Log to determine the appropriate action required.

## 8.1.11.3 Aircraft Acceptance by Commander CAT.GEN.MPA.105

The Commander is required to review and certify the aircraft Technical Log. By signing the Technical Log the Commander is certifying compliance with the requirements of Air OPS with respect to:

1. The aircraft is airworthy, and the Certificate of Release to Service is current, if such Certificate is required to be contained in the Technical Log (see below).
2. The aircraft configuration is in accordance with the Configuration Deviation List (CDL).
3. The instruments and equipment required for the flight to be conducted are available; Air OPS
4. The instruments and equipment are in operable condition except as provided in the MEL.
5. The manuals, documents, additional information and forms as required to be available and carried on board by CAT.GEN.MPA.180, AMC1 CAT.GEN.MPA.180 are on board;
6. The aircraft has been de-iced and inspected as required by CAT.OP.MPA.250;
7. The aircraft carries at least the planned amount of fuel and oil to complete the flight safely, taking into account the expected operating conditions.

For the purposes of compliance with respect to (1) above the Commander is deemed to have properly satisfied himself if the Technical Log has been duly completed and presented to him showing:

1. That defects from the previous flights have been rectified and signed off or deferred, and that any deferred defects and equipment found inoperative during the pre-departure inspection are acceptable as inoperative for flight in accordance with the MEL; and

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

2. That a daily check has been duly certified within the requisite period. The Commander is not required to check the running total of flying hours nor to verify the validity of the Certificate of Release to Service or to have knowledge of the next scheduled maintenance where this is controlled by other means acceptable to the IAA.

Flight Crew as a privilege of their licence, having been duly instructed, are entitled to conduct transit and pre-departure inspections, carry out or supervise the servicing with fuel, oils and hydraulic fluid or the de-icing of the aircraft they intend to operate, complete specified maintenance tasks as trained, and to certify the satisfactory completion of these activities in the Technical Log.

## 8.1.11.3.1 Pre-departure Inspections

### Pre-flight/Transit Check

CAT.GEN.MPA.105

The Transit Check is the external check of the aircraft, or “walkaround”, completed by a qualified crew member prior to commencement of a flight. All Ryanair pilots are qualified to conduct a Transit Check. The conduct of the transit check is described in FCOM Vol 1. When the transit check has been completed the Captain will strikes through “Pre-flight” and sign the Pre-Flight/Transit data field in the Technical Log.

The forward and rear hold shall always be opened by the Flight Crew member during the walkaround to preclude the possibility of inbound bags/freight not being offloaded.

He will pay particular attention to flight controls and gear and tyre condition; the removal of locks and covers, the high risk of damage by ground service vehicles and the possibility of fluid leaks from engine, hydraulic and fuel systems.

Ryanair Flight Crew members are qualified to perform the external transit checks. This check is certified completed by the Captain in the Technical Log.

## 8.1.11.4 Completion and Certification of Technical Log after Flight

The various sections of the Technical Log require the following to be completed by the Flight Crew and certified by the Commander after any flight:

## 8.1.11.4.1 Use of Aircraft Torches

Aircraft torches are emergency equipment and shall therefore be used in an emergency. They shall NOT be used for aircraft “walkaround”. Pilots are required to have their own serviceable torch. Use of aircraft torches shall be recorded in the Technical Log.

page 8–118

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

# 8.1.11.4.2 Radio Ahead Codes

When calling maintenance on VHF the crew should refer only to the code describing the affected system on the list of Radio Ahead Codes, e.g. “Station this is RYR 8TG, please advise Ryanair Engineering that we have a CODE 59”.

The Radio Ahead Code is in the Technical Log.

## 8.1.11.6 Recording of Defects

CAT.GEN.MPA.105 (a) (14)

The Commander shall record, at the termination of the flight, utilisation data and all known or suspected defects of the aircraft in the aircraft technical log of the aircraft to ensure continued flight safety.

page 8–124

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

The accurate recording of defects that have occurred during the flight is of paramount importance. Historically, fatal accidents have occurred as a result of an Aircraft Commanders failure to record the full details of a technical event allowing engineering to make an informed decision as to the airworthiness of the aircraft.

The following types of event shall be recorded in detail in the aircraft technical log.

- Any event that resulted in a PAN or Mayday as a result of technical issue.
- Any event where safety equipment was used.
- Any event where crew oxygen was used.
- Any stick shaker event that was not attributable to environmental conditions.
- Any exceedance as prescribed in the FCOM limitations section.
- Any fire warning.
- Any overheat warning.
- Any Failure of the pressurisation system (even if re-settable).
- Any Event where the crew were required to reference the Boeing QRH for a technical reason.
- Any event where flight control issues were experienced.
- Any Severe turbulence encounter.

The above list is not exhaustive Commander’s shall record any technical event deemed appropriate which has the potential to impact the safe operation of the aircraft due to a technical deficiency.

Commanders must ensure that all symptoms, indications and effects of the failure are recorded in the technical log and if necessary, communicated by phone to maintenance control.

Commanders should note it is not permissible to defer the recording of aircraft defects. The decision rest with maintenance control as to whether a HIL may be raised or the fault rectified before the next flight.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## 8.1.11.6.1 Dispatch with Defects at an Outstation

Ryanair Engineering has approval from the IAA (Airworthiness) for the Commander to release an aircraft for service at an outstation without a Ryanair engineer licensed on type subject to the conditions below:

- The captain shall always in the first instance advise MAINTROL in DUB of the defect.
- If the defect can be deferred with reference to the MEL, Maintrol will instruct the Captain how to complete the relevant work order and HIL item.
- Maintrol will record verbatim the HIL description electronically.
- The crew shall take no maintenance action other than to reset a circuit breaker, insert a cockpit placard\*, or conduct a BITE reset in the cockpit (not the E&amp;E bay) on the instructions of maintrol.
- The defect is to be reviewed by an appropriately approved engineer on return to base, and either rectified or formally raised as a deferred defect by the engineer.

* A placard shall take the form of an INOP sticker unless specified. Pilots are NOT authorised to alter the CAT I/II/IIIA status.

This procedure is only intended for use when maintrol cannot make available a suitable engineer and the defect meets all the requirements for dispatch according to the relevant MEL.

## 8.1.11.6.1.1 Technical Delays – Boarding

Crews shall embark the passengers on to the aircraft unless a technical defect renders it unsafe to board, reduces passenger comfort beyond acceptable levels, or is not possible due to fuelling and boarding regulations.

In general wheel changes, seat repairs, brake changes, birdstrike inspections, minor system failures, flight deck cautions etc are acceptable in terms of boarding unless engineering specifically directs that boarding is delayed.

All boarding delays shall be noted on the Electronic Flight Log/Paper Voyage report (Refer to Section 8.1.10.6).

## 8.1.11.7 Stowage of Technical Log

The CORRECT AIRCRAFT TECHNICAL LOG will be stowed behind the

Captain’s seat, or in a bracket provided on the flight deck for take-off and landing in such a way that it cannot become a hazard in the flight deck.

page 8–126

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## 8.1.11.8 Cabin Defects Log

To ensure airworthiness related items are transferred to the Aircraft Technical Log the Commander shall at the end of each flight, receive the Cabin defects Log from the SCCM. The Commander shall transfer any airworthiness or safety related items in the Aircraft Technical Log, (e.g. defective/missing safety equipment, cabin or emergency lighting deficiencies, missing/torn decals, damaged/malfunctioning seats, etc.). Engineering shall clear/defer defects in accordance with MEL/DDG.

## 8.1.11.9 Section Not In Use

### 8.1.11.10 Engineering Tasks Permitted to be Carried Out by Commanders

There are a limited number of tasks normally carried out by engineers that may be carried out by Commanders who have received adequate approved training. The tasks are carried out under supervision of the Chief engineer and are derived from the MOE 2.16.10.

Flight Crew authorised in accordance with MOE 3.9.7 may perform the below set of tasks:

- Pre-flight Inspection in accordance with CAME 1.11
- Birdstrike Inspection in accordance with MOE L2.2.6
- Deferral of Defects in accordance with CAME 1.8.6.

## 8.1.11.10.1 Section Not In Use

### 8.1.11.10.2 Bird Strike Inspection

Training Requirements: Aircraft commanders have completed the bird strike inspection training as described in MOE 3.9.

Maintenance Control must be contacted to determine if the aircraft is at unsupported station.

Commander may complete a bird strike inspection of an aircraft in accordance with Ryanair Engineering procedure RA 210 if instructed to do so by engineering.

### 8.1.11.10.3 Raising of a HIL IAW CAME 1.8.6

In the event of an aircraft crew discovering that they have a defect at a station where there is no engineering support on site, the aircraft commander will contact Maintrol and discuss the extent of the defect as evidenced by cockpit indications and/or BITE messages available to the crew.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## 8.1.11.10.4 Affixing a Placard (Inoperative Sticker)

Commanders may affix inoperative stickers to indicate a system is unserviceable upon the instruction of an engineer, they may not change Aircraft autoland category placards.

## 8.1.11.10.5 Resetting of a Circuit Breaker(s)

Commanders may upon the instructions of an Engineer pull/reset circuit breakers on the ground for diagnostic purposes, they may not collar or inhibit systems by pulling circuit breakers.

## 8.1.11.10.6 Issue a CRS

Commanders may only issue a certificate of release to service for Bird-strike inspections.

## 8.1.11.10.7 Ground Running of Engines for Diagnostic Purpose

Commanders may at the request of Engineering with an Engineer present run an engine at idle thrust to enable check to me made Engineer Procedure Notice 18.03 refers.

**Note:** This is not the same as the engine fuel leak procedure defined in FCOM VOL 1 NP 21.50. NP 21.50 is a flight crew procedure not an engineering procedure.

## 8.1.11.10.8 Exercising of Flight Control or System

The Commander may at the request of Engineering exercise any control or operate any switch on the flight deck for the purposes of validating whether or not a system is inoperative or to reproduce a fault with the aircraft on the ground.

Should a commander be unsure as to whether he is authorised to carry out a procedure requested by Engineering then he shall in the first instance contact the duty pilot. (+353 1248 0557 Option as per AOC then Option 9).
