## 8.2.2.10 Multiple Occupancy of Aircraft Seats

An infant may be carried by an adult in a single seat and secured for take-off and landing by a supplementary loop belt to the adult's seat belt. They will not be allocated, nor occupy seats where they would obstruct access to emergency equipment, or impede the emergency evacuation of the aircraft. An adult may carry only one infant in this context.

Only one infant will be carried by an adult in any seat row unit (triple), as the number of drop down oxygen masks in any PSU is limited. There will be a minimum of 4 masks, sometimes more depending on the rows.

The different specifications of infant/child car seats and harnesses is specified on the Ryanair website and procedures for cabin crew to assure correct use is included in the OMA-SEP.

## 8.2.3 Refusal of Embarkation

CAT.GEN.MPA.170

Ryanair personnel and service providers shall take all reasonable measures to ensure that no person under the influence of psychoactive substances to the extent that the safety of the aircraft or its occupants is likely to be endangered, is permitted to board, to enter or to be on board its aircraft.

The Commander shall not allow a person to be carried in the aircraft who appears to be under the influence of alcohol or drugs to the extent that the safety of the aircraft or its occupants is likely to be endangered.

The CSS shall inform the Commander if a passenger appears to be under the influence of alcohol or drugs and may cause a safety problem. If the safety of the aircraft or its occupants is likely to be endangered the Commander shall off load the passenger.

## 8.2.4 De-icing and Anti-icing on the Ground CAT.OP.MPA.250

A Commander shall not take-off unless the external surfaces are clear of any deposit which might adversely affect the performance and or controllability of the aircraft except as permitted in the Aircraft Flight Manual and set out in the FCOM Volume 1, Supplementary Procedures, Adverse Weather Operation (SP 16). This is also known as a clean aircraft operating policy.

If contamination is expected or observed then the aircraft shall be de-iced and/or anti-iced in accordance with Ryanair procedures.

It is Ryanair policy to delay flap selection until reaching the holding point in Freezing Conditions as defined in FCOM Vol 1 SP 16 or following aircraft de icing. This policy assures the longest hold over times available.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

If, for whatever reason, the flaps are extended prior to the commencement of taxi in Freezing Conditions or following anti-icing and remain in that position, the holdover time is no longer valid or applicable.

## 8.2.4.1 Proprietary or Commercial Names

The De-Icing Manual maintained by the Director of Engineering (DENG) contains the proprietary or commercial names of all de-icing or anti-icing fluids approved for use on Ryanair aircraft. The holdover tables approved and the names of fluids are also in the Technical Log and available to operating crews.

## 8.2.4.2 Characteristics of De-icing/Anti-icing Fluids

The basic function of De/Anti-icing fluids is to lower the freezing point of water. This will delay the accumulation of frost, slush, snow or ice on critical surfaces or and/or will melt contamination already present.

Fluids used for de-icing and/or anti-icing shall be acceptable to Ryanair and the aircraft manufacturer. These fluids will conform to specifications such as SAE AMS1424 (Type 1), SAE AMS1428 (Types II, III and IV). Use of non-conforming fluids is not permitted due to their characteristics being unknown. The anti-icing and aerodynamic properties of thickened fluids may be seriously degraded by, for example, inappropriate storage, treatment, application, application equipment and age and in case they are applied on top of non-chemically compatible de-icing fluids.

## Fluids

1. ISO Type I (unthickened)

Type I fluid: Due to its properties, Type I fluid forms a thin, liquid-wetting film on surfaces to which it is applied which, under certain weather conditions, gives a very limited HoT. For anti-icing purposes the fluid/water mixture should have a freezing point of at least 10°C below OAT; increasing the concentration of fluid in the fluid/water mix does not provide any extension in HOT.

2. ISO Type II and IV (thickened)

Type II and Type IV fluids contain thickeners which enable the fluid to form a thicker liquid-wetting film on surfaces to which it is applied. Generally, this fluid provides a longer HoT than Type I fluids in similar conditions.

3. ISO Type III (thickened)

Type III fluid is a thickened fluid especially intended for use on aircraft with low rotation speeds.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## Early Fluid Failure on Extended Slats and Flaps

Winter 2009-2010 anti-icing fluid testing confirmed that fluid failure occurs much more rapidly on the highly sloped critical surfaces of the airplane such as the flaps and slats when extended in the take-off position than it does on the less severely sloped other critical surfaces. The degree of degradation of the holdover and allowance times on these surfaces is affected by many elements; the amount of slope of the surface, the type of fluid, the airplane skin and ambient temperature, the type of precipitation, the amount of fluid applied, and the effective wind. Under conditions typical of normal operation in ground icing condition and flap angles fluids were observed to fail in as much as half of the published holdover time for the fluid and precipitation conditions present.

Ryanair has approved the risk mitigation strategies below:

- Delay flap and slat extension until just prior to take-off, minimizing the time that the slat and flap surfaces are in the highly sloped configuration.

## 8.2.4.3 Effects of Icing and Other Contamination on Aircraft Performance

## 8.2.4.3.1 Aerodynamics and the Contaminated Wing

Any type of ice accretion is an obstacle to smooth airflow and will slow the airflow down causing flow separation, introduce turbulence thereby degrading lifting performance of the wing. Furthermore, freezing deposits may cause moving parts, such as elevators, ailerons, flap actuating mechanism, etc., to jam and create a potentially hazardous condition.

## 8.2.4.3.2 Weather-related Conditions

Icing conditions on the ground can be expected when air temperatures fall below freezing and when moisture or ice occurs in the form of either precipitation or condensation. Precipitation may be rain, sleet or snow. Frost can occur due to the condensation of fog or mist.

## Cold Soaked Fuel Frost (CSFF)

The CSFF (Cold soaked Fuel Frost) relief as described in the AFM applies to Ryanair operations when the ambient temperature is $+4^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$ or above. The presence of cold soaked fuel frost on the upper surface of the wing, within the confines of the defined CSFF area, and on the lower surface of the wing within the equivalent defined CSFF area is acceptable for departure in accordance with FCOM procedures.

FCOM Vol 1, SP.3 'Cold Soaked Fuel Frost' shall be used to enable dispatch with CSFF within the limits specified.

Dispatch with CSFF within the limits and using the procedure described in these documents is approved by the IAA.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

Note 1: Remind the CSS that any passenger reports of wing contamination must be reported to the Captain. If CSFF relief is being applied, the Captain shall inform the CSS before departure.

Note 2: A Flight Crew Member must verify that the frost on the upper surface of the wing is within the confines of the defined CSFF area and there is no evidence of clear ice formation due to refreezing of melted CSFF.

## Clear Ice Phenomenon

Under certain conditions, a Clear Ice layer or frost can form on the wing upper surfaces when the aircraft is on the ground. In most cases this is accompanied by frost on the underwing surface.

Severe conditions occur with precipitation when sub-zero fuel is in contact with the wing upper surface skin panels. During walk-around Clear Ice accumulations are very difficult to detect from ahead of the wing or behind it, especially in poor lighting and when the wing is wet. The leading edge may not feel particularly cold. The Clear Ice may not be detected from the cabin either because wing surface details show through.

The following factors contribute to the formation intensity and the final thickness of the Clear Ice layer:

- low temperature of fuel that was added to the aircraft during the previous ground stop and/or the long airborne time of the previous flight resulting in a situation that the remaining fuel in the wing tanks is below 0°C.
- abnormally large amount of remaining cold fuel in wing tanks causing the fuel level to be in contact with the wing upper surface panels as well as the lower surface, especially in the wing tank area.
- temperature of fuel added to the aircraft during the current ground stop, adding (relatively) warm fuel can melt dry, falling snow with the possibility of re-freezing. Drizzle/rain and ambient temperatures around 0°C on the ground is very critical. Heavy freezing has been reported during drizzle/rain even at temperatures of 8 to 14°C. The use of thermal leading edge Anti-icing may melt falling dry snow that re-freezes later.

The areas most vulnerable to freezing are:

- the wing root area between the front and rear spars.
- any part of the wing that will contain unused fuel after flight, the areas where different structures of the wing are concentrated (a lot of cold metal), such as areas above the spars and the main landing gear doubler plate.

## Aircraft-related Conditions

The concept of icing is commonly associated only with exposure to inclement weather. However, even if the OAT is above freezing point, ice or frost can form if the aircraft structure is below 0° C and moisture or relatively high humidity is present.

With rain or drizzle falling on sub-zero structure, a clear Ice layer can form on the wing upper surfaces when the aircraft is on the ground. In most cases this is accompanied by frost on the underwing surface.

## 8.2.4.3.3 Icing on the Ground

### Types of Ice

**Frost and Hoar Frost** – Ice crystal deposits forming directly on cold surfaces with a temperature below freezing when the dew point is also below zero.

**Rime Ice** – White or opaque deposits of crystalline and granular ice formed by supercooled water droplets impinging and freezing on impact with the exposed surfaces of the aircraft. Typical of freezing fog with the deposits generally thicker than frost.

**Clear Ice** – Essentially transparent and adhering closely with almost 100% bonding to the aircraft surfaces, and formed by freezing of water caused by condensation or precipitation including freezing rain onto the surface, or by larger supercooled water droplets which do not freeze directly on impact. Also called glaze ice when it occurs in flight.

**Dry Snow** – Small flakes of snow with no liquid moisture content at temperatures below freezing. As a consequence does not tend to stick to surfaces which are also below zero and can be removed blowing or brushing off.

**Wet Snow** – Snow close to freezing point and composed of generally larger flakes with a higher water content than dry snow, some of which is in liquid form. Generally sticks to aircraft surfaces, engines and exposed system components and if the surfaces are below zero or temperatures drop below freezing point can freeze onto the surface greatly increasing the bonding to the surface.

**Snow Pellets** – The snow column for all fluid types has been updated to include snow pellets. Recent testing has indicated that snow holdover times are appropriate for snow pellet conditions.

**Ice Pellet Allowance Times** – During the winter of 2009-2010 wind tunnel research conducted with a newer generation type airfoil showed that propylene glycol (PG) and Ethylene Glycol (EG) fluids behave differently under certain temperature and ice pellet conditions. Specifically, higher aircraft rotation speeds are required to effectively remove PG fluid contaminated with light or moderate

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

ice pellets at temperatures less than -10°C. The range of Boeing 737 rotation speeds is above the minimum worst case rotation speed to remove fluid with Ice Pellets.

Sleet – A mixture of rain and snow or hail which can adhere to aircraft surfaces as with wet snow.

## 8.2.4.3.4 Ice Accretion on the Ground

Aircraft on the ground, while parked or during ground operations, are susceptible to accumulation of ice accretions in conditions peculiar to ground operations in addition to many of the conditions that can be encountered in flight. Precautions are necessary to avoid re-contamination of the aircraft structure and systems after anti-icing/de-icing. These precautions are given in FCOM Vol 1 Supplementary Procedures, Adverse Weather. The conditions giving rise to ice accretion on the ground include:

1. All forms of precipitation – snow, sleet, hail or rain adhering to and freezing onto aircraft structure because of a fall in ambient temperature or due to structural cold soak;
2. Conditions of high relative humidity that may produce frost accretions on aircraft surfaces having a temperature at or below the frost point. Frost accumulations are common during overnight ground parking and after landing where aircraft surface temperatures remain cold following descent from higher altitudes. This is a common occurrence on lower wing surfaces in the vicinity of fuel cells. Frost accretions can also occur on upper wing surfaces in contact with cold fuel;
3. Freezing fog;
4. Operation on ramps, taxiways, and runways containing moisture, slush, or snow;
5. Blown snow from snow drifts, other aircraft, buildings, or other ground structures;
6. Snow blown by winds, other aircraft or ground support equipment; and
7. Snow, slush and ice re-circulated by engine, propeller, or rotor wash.
8. Operation of jet engines in reverse thrust is a common cause of such contamination and ingestion damage.

Airframe icing may occur on the ground with ambient temperatures (OAT) above freezing point, if the precipitation temperature is below 0°C (supercooled droplets) and/or the aircraft wing is cold soaked (either due low temperatures for an extended period on the ground or in flight).

## 8.2.4.4 Hold over Times (HoT)

The responsibility regarding Hold-over Time and subsequent re-application of de-ice/anti-ice (if applicable) rests with the Commander from the time he has received the final report about the treatment from the Anti/De-icing person in charge.

The Hold-over Time starts at the beginning of the last treatment. ‘Holdover time (HOT)’ means the period of time during which an anti-icing fluid provides protection against frozen contamination to the treated aircraft surfaces. It depends among other variables, on the type and intensity of the precipitation, OAT, wind, the particular fluid (or fluid Type) and aircraft design and aircraft configuration during the treatment.

HoT will in effect have run out when frozen deposits starts to form/accumulate on the treated surfaces irrespective of the tabulated Hold-over Times, or at the commencement of the take-off roll due to aerodynamic shedding of fluid.

Hold-over Times are only guidelines because a variety of factors can change the effectiveness of the fluid. These factors include high winds, jet blasts, wet snow heavy precipitation, aircraft skin temperature lower than outside air temperature, direct sunlight, etc. Monitoring of the environmental conditions is therefore essential.

Holdover tables indicating the hold over times are published annually by FCI before the commencement of the Winter period. These constitute the hold over tables approved by DENG for use in Ryanair operations.

## Lowest Operational Use Temperature (LOUT) Table

Lowest Operational Use Temperature (LOUT) information for Type I, II, III and IV fluids has been added to the holdover time guidelines. This information has been derived by the FAA based on data provided by the fluid manufacturers. The Lowest Operational Use Temperature, or LOUT is the lowest temperature at which a fluid has been tested and certified as acceptable in accordance with the appropriate aerodynamic acceptance test whilst still maintaining a freezing point buffer of not less than:

1.  $10^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$ for a Type I fluid; or
2.  $7^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$ for Type II, III or IV fluids.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

There are two aerodynamic fluid flow off test protocols for fluids; the low speed test is for aircraft with rotation speeds less than 100 knots and the high speed test for aircraft with rotation speeds greater than 100 knots. Type II, and Type IV fluids generally do not pass the low speed test.

In the case of Types II, III, and IV fluids there can be multiple LOUTs to account for the neat (100/0) and the 50/50 and 75/25 dilutions. The LOUTs for these fluids are accounted for in the temperature bands which are an integral part of their holdover tables. If a fluid has a LOUT which is warmer than the lowest temperature in a particular band, this information will be noted on the appropriate table.

The LOUTs for Type I fluids are provided in Tables as well as the manufacturer specified fluid/ water concentration used to establish the LOUT for each fluid. This concentration shall not be exceeded.

The LOUTs for undiluted Type II, Type III, and Type IV fluid (100/0) are provided in Tables. The LOUTs for the 50/50, and 75/25 dilutions are shown only in the brand name holdover tables if the temperature is warmer than the lowest temperature in a given band.

8.2.4.4.1 Account is taken of the wing skin temperature versus OAT, as this may affect:

a. The need to carry out aeroplane de-icing and/or anti-icing; and
b. The performance of the de-icing/anti-icing fluids.

Wing skin temperatures may be lower than OAT. A stronger mix may need to be used to ensure a sufficient freeze point buffer. The de-ice service provider shall be informed about the wing skin temperature (as indicated by the fuel temperature) and fluid concentration will be based on the lower of OAT and wing skin temp.

8.2.4.5 De-icing and Anti-icing Procedures and Precautions During use

8.2.4.5.1 General

Depending on the facilities available at the aerodrome, and on the aeroplane type, removal may be achieved by brushing, the application of fluids, infra-red heat or forced air, or a combination of these methods. Flight crews shall familiarise themselves with the methods locally available.

## 8.2.4.5.2 Commanders Initiation of Procedure

On arrival at the aeroplane the Commander will decide whether or not the aeroplane is aerodynamically clean (including the flight controls).

The Commander is responsible for selecting an effective De/Anti-icing treatment and to conform to FCOM Vol I procedures, techniques and legal requirements. The Commander’s request for such treatment and the fluid mixtures used, will always take precedence over locally recommended procedures.

A Commander’s personal check for the correct treatment may not be practicable and, in this case, the person in charge representing the Anti/De-icing Company is responsible for verification of the Anti/De-icing results, (post treatment check).

The result of the Anti/De-icing procedure will be communicated to the Commander including the time for start of treatment, type of fluid(s) and mixture ratio.

## 8.2.4.5.2.1

At all Ryanair bases Engineers carry out early morning pre-flight/daily checks. These include a check as to whether the aircraft requires de-icing or not. They supervise the process, ensure the necessary fluid application time details are recorded in the Technical Log and carry out tactile checks as required. The de-icing crew do not need supervision but they must provide the fluid application details.

At other airfields, contracted ground handling agents complete de-icing. The Flight Crew shall complete the ‘Exterior Inspection’ as normal. The Captain shall request de-icing when required. It is imperative he/she does so as soon as possible. The de-icing crew do not need supervision but they must provide the fluid application details.

The Technical Log must be filled out and signed. This provides a written record to support any subsequent holdover time calculation.

Hoar Frost (overnight): Engineering/Ground Handling agents use a company checklist to determine if early morning de-icing due frost is likely. They may then “early Anti ice/De-ice” the aircraft with approved fluid to avoid a delay to the first departure. They are required to furnish the relevant Anti-icing details. Ensure you refer to the Hold over time (listed in the Technical Log) before dispatch.

Anti-icing against Hoar Frost has a long Hold Over time. Doing so overnight generally avoids the requirement to De/Anti ice prior to departure.

## 8.2.4.5.2.2 De-icing Admin: DAR Form

DAR is “De-icing/Anti-icing Request form”. This is a GOPS form. Spare copies may be carried in the aircraft library locker.

DAR form is divided into the following sections:

- Flight Details: normally completed by the dispatcher/handling agent before the aircraft arrives on stand.
- Contamination Check: this section establishes which parts of the aircraft need de-icing/anti icing. Normally completed by the Captain or Engineer.
- De-icing/Anti-icing Procedure (Anti-icing code): Outlines details of the required treatment including number of treatments/steps used, fluid type, mix and quantity. Normally completed by the service provider.
- Post Treatment Check: Normally completed by the Service provider. This certifies that the above procedure has been completed to the required standard and that the areas outlined in the Contamination Check are now clear of all contamination. The service provider will only certify in the post treatment check areas which have been requested in the contamination check section of this form. It remains the responsibility of the Commander to ensure the contamination check highlights all areas requiring treatment. The Commander shall sign the form and insert the relevant Technical Log page reference.
- Bottom Copy: The bottom copy is returned to the handling agent/service provider.
- Top Copy: The top copy is retained by the Commander who will clip this to the left hand control column where it will serve as a reminder when calling for the “Before Take-off Checks ‘Below the Line’” that (1) the holdover time is still valid, and (2) have the conditions changed. Having reviewed these items the green copy is removed from the Control Column PRIOR to Take-off roll.

## 8.2.4.5.2.3 De/Anti-icing with Engines Running

- Ensure the Flight Details and Contamination Check sections of the form are completed. Sign the form and insert the Technical Log page reference.
- Provide a copy of the Technical Log to the handling agent/service provider PRIOR to starting engines.
- Start engines, taxi and de/anti-ice the aircraft. When this procedure is complete confirm over the headset the Anti-icing Code (section 2) and the name of the person responsible for the procedure and post Treatment Check. Write the name(s) on the form (top blue copy).
- Sign the form and clip to the control column as described above.

## 8.2.4.5.2.5 Service Provider Declines to Use DAR Form

Pending standardisation of this issue by EASA many airlines and service providers have designed their own forms. Some providers insist on not using the Ryanair DAR form, using their own documentation instead. This is acceptable, and a copy of the service providers form shall be attached to the EFL submission or returned in the flight envelope.

## 8.2.4.5.2.6 Alternative Methods of Removal of Dry Snow

As an alternative to the application of fluid, service providers may use long handled soft brooms and squeegees have been approved for snow clearance from wings and stabilisers. Soft cotton rope and fabric hoses may be used for clearing the upper fuselage surfaces.

Under certain conditions (e.g. temperature below +3°C light snow) it may be advisable not to spray the aircraft surfaces with de-icing fluid if the structure is dry. Wetting the skin will cause the new snow to stick and turn to slush, with the possibility of these deposits re-freezing during take-off.

## 8.2.4.5.2.7 First Aid Measures

- Ingestion: Give large quantities of water to drink. Consult medical personnel.
- Skin contact: Wash off in flowing water. Launder contaminated clothing before reuse.
- Eye contact: Irrigate for five minutes. Obtain medical assistance if irritation persists.
- Inhalation: Remove to fresh air if feeling unwell. Consult medical personnel if symptoms persist.

## 8.2.4.5.2.8 Special Considerations Due to Residues of Dried Fluids

### Dried fluid residues:

Dried fluid residue could occur when surfaces have been treated but the aircraft has not subsequently been flown and not been subject to precipitation. The fluid may then have dried on the surfaces.

### Re-hydrated fluid residues:

Repetitive application of thickened de-icing/anti-icing fluids may lead to the subsequent formation/build up of a dried residue in aerodynamically quiet areas, such as cavities and gaps. This residue may re-hydrate if exposed to high humidity conditions, precipitation, washing, etc., and increase to many times its original size/volume. This residue will freeze if exposed to conditions at or below 0° C. This may cause moving parts such as elevators, ailerons, and flap actuating mechanisms to stiffen or jam in flight.

Re-hydrated residues may also form on exterior surfaces, which can reduce lift, increase drag and stall speed. Re-hydrated residues may also collect inside control surface structures and cause clogging of drain holes or imbalances to flight controls. Residues may also collect in hidden areas: around flight control hinges, pulleys, grommets, on cables and in gaps refer to Section 3 of the De-ice/Anti-icing Manual.

## 8.2.4.5.3 Step Anti/De-icing

Anti/De-icing can be performed in one step or in two steps:

The “One Step” procedure means that De-icing and Anti-icing are carried out at the same time, using a mixture of De-icing/Anti-icing fluid and water. This assumes that the applied fluid will provide an adequate protection period (HoT), in the prevailing meteorological conditions, against re-freezing.

In the “Two-Step” procedure the aeroplane is first de-iced to ensure that it is clean of all frozen contaminants, followed immediately by the application of anti-ice fluid. The second step will be applied before the first step fluid freezes, typically within three minutes and, if necessary, area by area.

- With a “one-step” Anti/De-icing procedure the HoT begins at the start of the Procedure.
- With a “two-step” procedure the HoT begins at the start of the second (Anti/De-icing) step.

## 8.2.4.5.3.1 Removal of Local Area Contamination

Local area de-icing is the process of removing of frost and/or ice which is limited to localised areas on the surfaces of the aeroplane. This type of contamination will generally be found on the wing and/or Horizontal stabilizer leading edges or in patches on the wing and/or stabilizer upper surfaces for example CSFF.

The following conditions are applied:

- Freezing conditions do not exist or are forecasted before departure
- Application of the treatment must be symmetrical
- The same type of fluid, heated with a fluid/water mixture suitable for a One-Step Procedure

If crews are unable to determine the extent of contamination (i.e. on horizontal stabilizer during hours of darkness), the required action is to revert to standard de-icing procedures.

**Note:** Both sides of the aeroplane must be treated identically (same areas, same amount and type of fluid, same mixture strength), even if the contamination is only present on one side.

After this check has confirmed that the treated areas are clean, the following statement shall be given to the Commander:

“Local Area De-icing only. Holdover times do not apply”

The extract below from the DAR form indicates to the crew that Local are de-icing has taken place.

1(a). Local Area Contamination/ Cold Soaked Fuel Frost

HOLD OVER TIMES DO NOT APPLY

Indicate areas to be treated on the diagram to the right. With this type of contamination only affected areas need to be treated. De-ice fluids must be applied symmetrically on both sides of the aircraft.

![img-15.jpeg](img-15.jpeg)

Crews shall use the TEM techniques to ensure they are aware of the type of de-icing that has taken place.

Crew shall be aware that should the environmental conditions change then they will consider that the aircraft is not de-iced and return to stand for a de-icing/anti-icing treatment.

## 8.2.4.5.4 External Pre Take-off Contamination Check

The Commander shall continually monitor the environmental situation (e.g. precipitation, wind and OAT) after the performed Anti/De-icing treatment. Prior to take-off the Commander shall assess whether the applied HoT is still appropriate.

When the HoT has been exceeded or if doubt exists regarding the continued effectiveness of the applied Anti-icing treatment, the Commander shall arrange for a pre take-off contamination check to be performed externally by a qualified person\* in order to verify that the aircraft's surfaces are free of contamination. When required, advise ATC/Dispatch that “an external pre take-off contamination check is required” and they will co-ordinate to facilitate the local procedure. If this external check cannot be performed just before take-off, the aircraft shall return to a place where re-treatment shall be applied.

Note: \* A qualified person is one who has received training in aircraft de-icing techniques normally de-icing staff or an engineer.

## 8.2.4.5.5 General Precautions

Aeroplane surfaces treated with Anti/De-icing fluids should appear glossy, smooth and wet. An indication that protection is deteriorating is the loss of gloss or dulling of the surfaces as the fluid gradually turns to slush. If, at any time prior to take-off, the Commander requires confirmation of the ice-free state of the aeroplane, he must have a visual inspection carried out or return to the ramp.

Unless the aeroplane is being anti/de-iced by an approved anti/de-ice contractor, the Flight Crew or other authorised personnel shall supervise the Anti/De-icing operation being carried out by, or on behalf of, a handling agency.

In this case, care will be taken to ensure that whether removed by broom, or the application of fluid spray, deposits are swept away from control surface hinge areas and system intakes, and that the sprays themselves are not directed to these areas. Since the melting deposits, which the Anti/De-icing is supposed to remove, may further dilute the Anti/De-icing fluid, re-freezing may occur if the solution runs onto other parts of the aeroplane, and close attention shall be paid to this possibility.

When Anti/De-icing operations have been completed, ideally as close to the scheduled departure time as possible, a careful inspection of the aeroplane is to be completed in order to confirm that wing, tailplane and control surfaces have been cleared of deposits, and that intake and drain holes are free of any obstruction. Control surfaces shall be moved over their full range, and jet engine compressors rotated by hand to ensure that they have not become frozen in position.

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

Note: Before reconfiguring or moving the aircraft, the Flight Crew shall receive a confirmation from the personnel involved in the operation that all De-icing and/or Anti-icing operations are complete and that all personnel and equipment are clear of the aircraft.

**CAUTION:** Under no circumstances may an aircraft that has been anti-iced receive a further coating of Anti/De-icing fluid directly on top of the contaminated film. If an additional treatment is required before flight, a complete Anti/De-icing/Anti/De-icing process must be performed to ensure that any residues from the previous treatment have been removed.

The aircraft must always be treated symmetrically – the left hand and right hand sides must receive the same and complete treatment.

## 8.2.4.5.6 Information to the Commander

The person carrying out or monitoring the correct Anti/De-icing of the aeroplane at the Anti/De-icing platform is responsible for the visual check on completion even though the final responsibility rests with the Commander. The Flight Crew may perform the visual check (if possible).

The code is then transmitted to the Flight Crew to confirm satisfactory treatment and the Commander will decide whether the HoT is adequate for taxiing and take-off.

The code defines the treatment (Anti/De-icing) applied to the aeroplane. It includes the fluid classification, concentration and time of starting the treatment. Protection period (HoT) is measured from the time of start of the last treatment.

Example 1: TYPE I 75/25 0500 (Local Time)

Example 2: TYPE II 100/0 0600 (Local Time)

Note 1: The mix ratio is by volume with the Anti/De-icing fluid quoted first, e.g. a 75/25 mix contains 75% Anti/De-icing fluid and 25% water.

Note 2: When the mix used differs from those tabulated, use the next lower concentration code, e.g. an 80/20 mix is coded as 75/25.

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## 8.2.4.5.7 Taxi-through De/Anti-icing

Anti/De-icing with engines running may be performed in taxi-through facilities at particular airports. The OMB FCOM SP16 prescribes procedures and check-lists for use under these circumstances.

Pre-briefing must be conducted between the Flight Crew and the ground personnel performing the Anti/De-icing to ensure that they are aware of any specific requirements called for by the Flight Crew, VHF contact must be maintained with the Anti/De-icing supervisor throughout the operation. The Anti/De-icing supervisor is responsible for confirming that the aeroplane is completely free of ice and snow before releasing it.

The lowest possible power setting shall be used, flaps retracted or extended, (according to OMB FCOM SP 16 procedure), and the air-conditioning switched OFF to prevent fluid from entering the cabin.

Before takeoff, perform a flight control check.

As soon as the aeroplane is clear of the facility, the air-conditioning may be switched ON again. Ensure that taxi and take-off checklists are completed. Prior to releasing brakes for take-off, applicable engine De-icing procedures will be applied.

## 8.2.4.5.8 Technical Log

The Commander is to confirm that, whenever Anti/De-icing has taken place, an appropriate entry has been made and signed in the technical log. In particular, the fluid type/mix and start/completion times\* shall be recorded.

If there is any subsequent departure delay, or further deterioration in the weather conditions, the Commander shall use this information, together with the HoT, to form a realistic idea of whether further Anti/De-icing may be required.

**Note:** \*Times passed to the flight crew by the deicing team will be in local time, Commanders will need to convert this to UTC for entry into the Technical Log.

## 8.2.4.5.9 Take-off Prohibition

Take-off is prohibited if any of the following conditions exist:

- Snow, ice or frost deposits are adhering to the wings tail plane, control surfaces or engines.
- Heavy fall of wet snow, slush and rain with ambient temperature around freezing point unless adequately protected by anti-ice treatment.
- Freezing rain or drizzle unless adequately protected by anti-ice treatment.

CAUTION: Under certain meteorological conditions, de-icing and/or anti-icing procedures may be ineffective in providing sufficient protection for continued operations. Examples of these conditions are freezing rain, ice pellets and hail snow exceeding certain intensities, high wind velocity, and fast-dropping OAT. No HOT guidelines exist for these conditions. Where HOT guidelines do not exist for these conditions, or if in doubt, take-of shall not be commenced.

## 8.2.4.6 Icing

CAT.OP.MPA.255, AMC1 CAT.OP.MPA.255

Ryanair aeroplanes are certified for flights in icing conditions. However, if actual icing exceeds the intensity of icing for which the aircraft is certified, the Commander shall exit the icing conditions without delay, by a change of level and/or route, and if necessary by declaring an emergency to ATC. The intensity of aeroplane icing is defined in accordance with definitions provided in the Table below:

Table Definition of Aeroplane Icing

| Light:    | Ice accumulation which does not necessitate a change of heading or altitude                         |
| --------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Moderate: | Ice accumulation which makes a change of heading and/or altitude desirable                          |
| Severe:   | The rate of accumulation is such that De/Anti-icing equipment fails to control or reduce the hazard |

For procedures in flight refer to FCOM Vol 1 NP and SP 16.

## 8.2.5 Crew Inspections

### 8.2.5.1 Pre-flight

CAT.GEN.MPA.105(a)(12)

The Pre-flight Check is the external check of the aircraft, or "walkaround", completed by a qualified crew member prior to commencement of a flight in compliance with Annex I (Part-M) to Regulation (EU) No 1321/2014; pre-flight check. The Pre-flight check is described in FCOM Vol 1. All Ryanair Flight Crew members are qualified to perform the Pre-flight checks.

The forward and rear hold shall always be opened by the Flight Crew member during the walk around to preclude the possibility of inbound bags/freight not being offloaded. Where bags or cargo are found in the hold they shall be brought to the attention of the ramp agent.

The Flight Crew will pay particular attention to flight controls, gear and tyre condition; the removal of gear pins and covers (if used), the high risk of damage by ground service vehicles and the possibility of fluid leaks from engine, hydraulic and fuel systems.

The captain’s signature in the aircraft Technical Log is certification that all aspects of Pre-flight, including flight deck door access system (required for the first sector of each flight duty) have been completed as per approved procedures.

## 8.3 Flight Procedures

## 8.3.0 Flight Procedures – Ryanair Guidance for Various Flight Phases

All Ryanair revenue flights shall be conducted under Instrument Flight Rules, IFR.

### Instrument Departure and Approach Procedures

1. Ryanair shall ensure that instrument departure and approach procedures established by the State in which the aerodrome is located are used.
2. Notwithstanding sub-paragraph above, a Commander may accept an ATC clearance to deviate from a published departure or arrival route, provided obstacle clearance criteria are observed and full account is taken of the operating conditions. The final approach must be flown visually or in accordance with the established instrument approach procedure.
3. Different procedures to those required to be used in accordance with sub-paragraph (1) above may only be implemented by an operator provided they have been approved by the State in which the aerodrome is located, if required, and accepted by the Authority.

## 8.3.0.1 Take-off and Departure

## 8.3.0.1.1 Take-off Briefing

The Ryanair policy for, and content of briefings is presented in FCOM Vol 1, Normal procedures. Refer section 8.0.4.

If the departure circumstances change then a new briefing or update must be given prior to take-off at or before the checklist item “Take-off brief...Review”.

The object of briefing is to ensure that both Flight Crew members are aware of and agree with a proposed plan of action. Briefings will be as short as possible but give a clear understanding of the intentions. It is normally unnecessary to reiterate Standard Operating Procedures, but it is necessary to cover any special requirements.

If the recipient of a briefing is unsure of the intentions to any degree he must make sure the points are clarified.

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

The statement “Standard Calls and Procedures” for take-off (also applicable for landing) will apply only when both Flight Crew members are familiar with the procedures for the airfield concerned and when further elaboration is clearly not required. “Standard Calls and Procedures” cannot be applied to Non Precision or Visual approaches, both of which require a comprehensive briefing.

The take-off briefing shall be initiated by the pilot flying (PF) and shall always be the full briefing for the first flight of the day.

## 8.3.0.1.2 Taxiing

Ryanair policy for taxiing is presented in FCOM Vol 1, Normal procedures. If a technical problem manifests itself crews having completed the applicable Non Normal checklist, shall check circuit breakers to establish if this is the primary cause.

If at any time the clearance between the wing tip and stationary or moving objects cannot be assured then the Commander shall STOP the aircraft and ensure that there is a Marshaller guiding the wing tip before proceeding further.

Additionally crews shall follow taxi markings meticulously during the power off procedure to ensure that no part of the aircraft presents a hazard to other aircraft parked on the ramp.

1. The Taxi phase of operations shall be considered a ‘Critical Phase of Flight’. A sterile cockpit is mandatory and only essential safety PA’s are allowed.
2. Expected taxi routings will be briefed for prior to push back for taxi out and prior to descent for taxi in, for airports both crew members are not intimately familiar with. Always brief temporary taxi routings/restrictions.
3. Write down all taxi clearances. The RHS pilot must ensure the Captain has acknowledged the clearance before reading back.
4. The ‘Sterile Cockpit’ and ‘Sterile Comms’ applies to all taxi phases and climb and descent. This forbids non-operational chatter.
5. Maximum taxi speeds must never be exceeded.

a. 50 kts on a runway when backtracking to vacate or take-off
b. 30 kts on a taxiway
c. 15 kts on the apron
d. 10 kts when executing a turn of 45 or greater
e. 5 kts on contaminated runways or taxiways

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

If unsure of a taxi routing, stop the aircraft and ask ATC for clarification. If unsure of your position when taxiing, stop and immediately notify ATC (including the last known position). If you inadvertently enter a runway, immediately notify ATC (an ASR for runway incursion is required). If visibility enables you to safely vacate a runway, vacate the runway as expeditiously as possible, unless otherwise instructed by the air traffic services unit, and then once vacated, stop the aircraft. If in LVPs, and it is not safe to vacate a runway, include this in your notification to ATC. When requested, give the taxi clearance you believed you were following.

6. Strobe Lights will be turned ON when entering or crossing an active or non active runway.

7. Prior to crossing or entering a runway, either active or non active, crews must make a ‘Two Phase Call’ (inter cockpit). FCOM Vol 1 Normal Procedures detail this call. This ‘Two Phase Call’ requires the Commander to establish with the RHS pilot that:

a. That a clearance has been received to cross or enter the runway.
b. The approach is checked clear as much as is possible.

This must be responded to by the RHS pilot, acknowledging that the approach on their side (right) has been checked as per the Captain.

8. Taxi and Braking on Slippery Surface

The FCTM advises using reverse thrust as required to taxi speed – This is 5 kts on slippery surfaces. The FCTM cautions against using prolonged use of reverse thrust at taxi speeds due to possible FOD and engine surge, but this must be balanced against the risk of a runway/taxiway excursion. The only braking available when the aircraft is skidding is reverse thrust, up to and including max reverse.

Promptly select reverse thrust at any stage that the aircraft is, or appears to be compromised.

Exercise extreme caution:

- On runways promulgated as wet when the OAT is 0°C or colder.
- At runway ends where it is difficult to perform friction tests.
- When crossing painted runway or taxiway markings.
- On clearways or stopways which may not be de-iced as not actual runway.

The Ryanair policy for external aircraft lighting configurations is presented in OMA, FCOM Vol 1, Normal procedures available on DocuNet. These policies are incorporated in the Checklist and Standard Operating Procedures, FCOM Vol 1.

An announcement is made on the P.A. before each start and before each take-off, the contents of which are promulgated in the PA checklist on the flight deck.

1. The take-off shall not take place until the Cabin Crew have made the cabin secure report, nor shall the crew accept a line-up clearance until this report is received, and the aircraft is actually ready to effect a take-off clearance.

He shall minimise the time off the operational frequency. When the announcement is complete he must positively establish from the PM any instructions received from ATC.

## 8.3.0.1.3 Logging of ATC Instructions and Clearances

The airways clearance and other instructions will always be logged by the F/O on the ground or the PM when airborne and in the cruise.

All ATC instructions must be repeated back in full.

Standard phraseology as presented in OMC, Jeppesen Publications (Pubs) &gt; Air Traffic Management (Doc 4444) &gt; Section 12 Phraseologies shall be used.

All communications, except greetings in local language, shall be in the English language.

Ideally both the Commander and co-pilot are required to monitor ATC clearances and have a responsibility to query any clearance that is not understood, incorrectly received, or incomplete. Any uncertainty must be resolved by asking ATC to repeat the clearance. It is recognised that certain events preclude both pilots being in the flight deck simultaneously.

On stand, ATC departure clearances may be requested/confirmed via datalink or voice. When utilising voice, both pilots must be present in the flight deck and on headset. When conducted via datalink, the PM may request and accept the clearance at the appropriate time during the preflight procedure. During the RIBETS briefing, both the Captain and FO must confirm the clearance via reference to the ATC LOG in the FMC and the OFP.

**Note:** As per AFM, when obtaining departure clearance via ACARS, pilots shall manually verify (compare) the filed flight plan versus the digital pre-departure clearance and shall initiate voice contact with Air Traffic Control if any question/confusion exists between the filed flight plan and the digital pre-departure clearance.

Particular attention is needed to ensure that the SID corresponds to that for which the take-off briefing was given. It is a typical human factor mistake to believe that the clearance received corresponds with the clearance filed and expected.

On the ground, the altitude alert shall be pre-set up to an expected altitude clearance plus 100 ft, and selected to the actual altitude cleared on receipt of the clearance.

If you receive either a different departure clearance to that shown on the OFP or a change of SID after receipt you shall:

- Ask for confirmation of the clearance given
- If confirmed, ask for the flight plan route
- Exit Airplane mode on EFB. Open the Summary tab within the EFL App and select ‘Download’ to check for the latest flight plan. If the EFL flight plan update is not available, then crew must contact Ryanair Ops
- If taxiing ask for clearance to stop and hold clear of the runway and re-brief the new SID and route
- Re-programme the FMC and conduct a route check
- Be aware that the new route may be significantly longer than the original and an additional fuel uplift may be necessary.

In circumstances where the OFP does not agree with the flight plan held by ATC an ASR must be filed at the earliest opportunity to ensure that any systemic flaw in operational communications is corrected immediately.

## 8.3.0.1.4 Radio Aids

The Ryanair policy for navigation and tuning and identification of Nav Aids is presented in FCOM Vol 1, Normal procedures.

Nav Aids shall be selected to enable the departure/SID, arrival/STAR and instrument approach to be flown in raw data. Enroute Nav aid tuning may be accomplished for situational awareness, but is subservient to RNP 5.

Loss of RNP 5 or RNP 1 shall be reported to ATC immediately to enable resumption of conventional navigation.

## 8.3.0.1.5 Take-off

Aircraft management procedures specific to aircraft type are given in the FCOM Vol 1 and/or the QRH for all take-off variations.

The take-off shall be abandoned for a take-off configuration warning. If this results in loss of line up allowance the aircraft shall reposition to assure line up allowance integrity.

Before commencing take-off, a Commander must satisfy himself that, according to the information available to him, the weather at the aerodrome and the condition of the runway intended to be used will not prevent a safe take-off and departure. This information is available in the OMB – Performance.

## Take-off Warning Report Faults – Fault Isolation

When a Take-off Config warning is triggered and the aircraft is returning to stand for maintenance investigation do not advance the thrust levers beyond 40% N1 during taxi to stand.

This procedure ensures that the cause of the TOC warning is preserved in the PSEU BITE control.

Advancing the thrust levers beyond 40% risks a thrust lever angle which cancels the fault that caused the TOC Warning.

Similarly observe 40% N1 when taxiing after a RTO caused by a TOC warning. Loose LE Slat connector and stab trim connectors disturbed by vibration on take-off roll typically cause high speed TOC warning. You are free to use reverse thrust as required to stop the aircraft as it is forward thrust lever angle which cancels the cause of the warning.

## Weather Radar, Thunderstorms

Aircraft shall not take-off or follow a path which takes the aircraft into a thunderstorm cell. The weather radar shall be switched on for take-off and the Captain shall monitor the take-off path for cell activity. Thunderstorm activity and wind shear are covered under adverse weather in Part A, Guide to AWO.

## Wake Turbulence Categories

Wake turbulence categories are presented in OMC, Jeppesen Publications (Pubs) &gt; Air Traffic Management (Doc 4444) &gt; ATC Section and OMA 8.3.9.

## Bird Hazard

Crews shall carefully consider initiating take-off if flocks of birds are seen on the runway or close to the runway edge.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

# Take-off Flaps

Flap 5 is the normal departure flap setting, flaps other than Flap 5 shall be used when operationally necessary. If crosswind component is in excess of 10 kt fixed derate is permitted, however assumed temperature thrust reduction is not permitted.

All Flap 1 departures shall be flown by the Captain as PF.

# Intersection Take-off

Intersection take-offs are encouraged and policy summary is as follows:

1. Performance plan for intersection take-off when appropriate.

Appropriate means when the intersection is en route to the threshold, use intersection numbers. If the threshold occurs before an intersection then you use the threshold numbers. It is not intended that a pilot prepares for Q3 at STN when 99% of the time departures are initiated from the threshold of RWY 22. It is however intended that pilots plan L1 when departing RWY 04 in STN as the outbound taxi route passes L1 on the way to the RWY 04 threshold. This issue is a combination of expediency, efficiency and airmanship.

2. Subject to performance limitations it is preferable to utilise intersection take-offs when available, permissible and safe to do so, rather than consume extra taxi fuel and time taken to depart from the full length. Up to maximum thrust may be considered to achieve this policy.

3. If you must use full length so advise ATC on start up.

4. On transfer to tower frequency reconfirm that you can take intersection take-off.

# Starter Extension Take-off

Starter Extensions are an area of the runway prior to the threshold used for increasing TORA/TODA.

In most European airports starter extensions are more commonly marked with solid white lines the same as runway edges, other airports use dashed lines to define the starter extension area.

Crew should remain vigilant to the possibility of confusing dashed edge marking with the centerline marking at airports that conform to the dashed marking layout, e.g. BGY.

When lining up on a runway using a starter extension confirm the aircraft is aligned with the centerline not the runway edge.

In Low Visibility conditions ensure that the ILS is tuned to the departing runway where available.

It is permissible to follow center line markings from the taxiway leading on to the runway but crews must give consideration to line up distances on performance limiting runways.

## 8.3.0.1.6 Rejected Take-off

The decision to reject a take-off will be made by the Commander, unless incapacitated. The F/O shall indicate to the Commander if he disagrees with a course of action during any flight phase, e.g. a continued take-off with a CONFIG WARNING.

1. The rejected take-off procedures are presented in FCOM Vol 1, Normal procedures.
2. Additional guidance material and best operating practise is provided in the Boeing Flight Crew Training Manual for Rejected take-off.
3. RTO &gt; 80 kt and runway vacated:

- Ask ATC for parking on taxiway/return to stand
- Call Maintrol and report a high energy RTO
- Do not take-off again until released by Maintrol
- Calculate the turnaround time required from the brake cooling schedule
- File a ASR on completion of duty.

## 8.3.0.1.7 Initial Climb-out

The after take-off and climb procedures are presented in FCOM Vol 1, and additional guidance and best operating practise is provided in the Boeing Flight Crew Training Manual.

Normal profiles are presented in the FCOM Vol 1, and non normal profiles in the QRH.

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## 8.3.0.1.7.1 Minimum Turn Altitude

A turning altitude is specified in a SID if this altitude is less than 398 ft. Crews shall comply with these turn instructions. Where no turning altitude is specified turns shall not commence below 400 AAL.

Departures with an initial track up to 15° offset from the runway track are not considered to be turning departures. The published departure track shall be followed and requires selection of LNAV or an appropriate HDG SEL prior to 400 ft.

No turns may commence below 59 ft above aerodrome level.

No turns may commence before the Departure End of Runway unless following a published SID or Omnidirectional Departure Procedure.

## 8.3.0.1.7.2 Track Deviation: Prevention

Flight Crew members shall follow departure routings accurately. The primary causes of track deviations are non selection of LNAV, slow turning speed for initial turns not entered in FMC to contain the turn radius until the turn is completed, and inaccurate practice hand flying.

Standard ICAO speeds shall be flown until advised by ATC that no speed limit exists, and the aircraft is operating in Class A, B or C airspace, and is in the climb. Speed should be constrained to not more than 250 kt climb speed until the aircraft is heading in the direction of the on course routing to avoid flying extra mileage. This speed is comfortably within the max speed for initial turns in a SID of 265 kt for B737-800/8200. When LNAV is not engaged the lesser of rate one turns for the speed or 25° of bank shall be flown until established enroute, and the aircraft shall be operated in a manner that enables the crew to establish that the intended flight path is clear of traffic/obstacles. In the descent, 250 kt is recommended &lt; FL 100 regardless of airspace classification, and crews shall decline offers from ATC of higher speeds. If ATC instructs a higher speed it shall be flown.

## 8.3.0.1.8 Noise Abatement

NADP2: Bug UP at 1,000 ft AAL, N1 at 1,500 ft AAL, retract flaps on schedule, maintain UP speed to 3,000 ft AAL, then accelerate.

When this Noise Abatement procedure does not provide adequate obstacle clearance an amended procedure shall be indicated in the RTOW charts in the same manner as ETs, and used by the crew.

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

Some destinations may still make reference to NADP A, or NADP B etc. The term NADP A or B no longer exists. The Noise Abatement reference literature currently used at many RYR destinations predate NPA 53. Therefore in the absences of a specific instruction to fly NAPD 1, Ryanair Boeing 737 aircraft will fly NADP 2. All other noise references are immaterial.

NADP1: N1 @ 1,500 feet AAL, Bug up @ 3,000 AAL, flap retraction on schedule

The THR RED Alt in the FMC may be preprogrammed to 3,000 ft AAL or may be manually programmed to 3,000 ft AAL, or as required.

A turning altitude is specified in a SID if this altitude is less than 398 ft. Crews shall comply with these turn instructions. Where no turning altitude is specified turns shall not commence below 400 AAL. The minimum altitude limitation for commencement of any turn is specified in FCOM Vol 1, limitations.

Noise abatement procedures do not take precedence over safe operation and emergency procedures when required, and may be cancelled during certain meteorological conditions.

Standard noise abatement departure procedures (FCOM Vol 1), shall be carried out for all normal departures. Crews shall follow the departure instructions presented on the OFP/AFB if different from the Standard RYR procedure. Speed in excess of that promulgated in the procedure will significantly degrade the effectiveness of noise abatement.

Strict adherence to routing and other procedures specified by the Airport Authority is important. These are specified in the Jeppesen Publications (Pubs).

There are noise measuring terminals points at many of the larger airports and noise violations will be filed by the airport authority, usually with a fine when noise limits are exceeded.

## 8.3.0.1.9 Systems Operation

The Ryanair standard operating procedures for systems operation, and area of operation concepts in the cockpit are presented in the FCOM Vol 1, Normal Operating Procedures, Supplementary Procedures, and QRH for non normal procedures.

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## 8.3.0.1.10 Look Out

TCAS is fitted to all Ryanair aircraft. Crews shall respond to all TCAS RA instructions at all times. TCAS is not Secondary surveillance radar and shall not be used in this way. Standard Operating Procedure for TCAS system operation is presented in the QRH, and system description is in Vol 2. TCAS is not a substitute for “Lookout”.

Flight operations are IFR but the principle of "see and be seen" is deemed to apply. Crews must exercise increased levels of awareness when operating in the vicinity of airports and during climb and descent, particularly in destinations located in Class E, F and G airspace, or destinations requiring transit through Class E, F and G airspace.

In general, one Flight Crew member will always be in a position to maintain a look-out. Flight Crew members will be aware that while relative motion helps to detect another aircraft, an aircraft on a collision course will remain in the same position within the window frame and is consequently more difficult to detect. This also applies when airports are conducting simultaneous take-offs from intersecting runways.

Be particularly alert for GA aircraft and gliders in particular, many of which do not have transponders.

MAC (Mid Air Collision) has been identified in the RYR SMS as a key operational risk area (KORA) in the current Ryanair safety strategy.

## 8.3.0.1.11 ATC Communications

### Stuck Mike Protection

The Ryanair fleet has a stuck mike modification programme completed. This modification means that after 35 seconds of continuous transmission the VHF comm. transceiver will revert to the receive mode. Ryanair aircraft radio comply with the requirements of CAT.IDE.A.330, CAT.IDE.A.335 and AMC1 CAT.IDE.A.325.

ATC communication is covered in OMC, Jeppesen Publications (Pubs) &gt; Air Traffic Management (Doc 4444). Both the PF and the PM have a responsibility to monitor ATC transmissions and to query any transmission that is not received fully and/or understood. Any uncertainty must be resolved by confirmation with ATC.

Both pilots shall react independently and simultaneously to an ATC instruction. Both pilots have defined actions to do on receipt of a clearance and there is a cross check built into the process. The reason for independent simultaneous action is to ensure that both pilots have heard the instruction, have actioned the instruction

page 8–196

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR
PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

and the crosscheck will identify any error by an individual pilot. If the response is not independent and simultaneous one pilot tends to follow the other and there is no opportunity to capture an error if one is made at this point.

All ATC communications shall be conducted in the English language.

Both Flight Crew members shall normally maintain a listening watch on the appropriate ATC frequency, including on the ground during pre-flight. If a Flight Crew member goes off frequency he must inform the other Flight Crew member.

Flight Crew members shall maintain good R/T discipline and make every effort to reduce speech traffic, particularly on congested frequencies. The following general rules apply:

1. Always listen out before transmitting.
2. The full call sign is to be used preceding the message. Enunciate clearly and avoid clipped responses or replying without giving a call sign.
3. On first contact after changing frequency during the departure, give call sign, SID, passing altitude/FL, cleared altitude/FL. Thereafter passing FL for cleared FL or current FL as appropriate, e.g. " RYR 3015 FL 380".
4. Read back all clearances followed by your call sign. The word "CLEARED" shall only be used by ATC for an airways clearance, a take-off and a landing clearance.
5. A Take-off clearance shall be issued separately from any other clearance message. The expression "TAKE-OFF" shall only be used in radiotelephony when an aircraft is cleared for take-off or when cancelling a take-off clearance.
6. When requested to advise that you have vacated altitude or flight level, do not say you have vacated it until you have actually have.
7. NATS UK has produced a booklet addressing the most common ATC issues.

## Change of Radio Frequency

It is essential to preset and crosscheck the new frequency before changing and contacting ATC. The following procedure shall apply to frequency changes:

# Awareness of FIR Boundaries

It is important to note the FIR boundaries on the OFP as a point where ATC handover can be expected:

- The EETs for all the FIR boundaries are given in the ATC flight plan section of the briefing package.
- FIR boundaries are also displayed in the appropriate positions throughout the OFP. Using this information, it is a simple task to identify the waypoints corresponding to the FIR boundaries. The time given to each boundary is the elapsed time from take-off. An example is shown below on a HHN-LPA OFP where MADRID (LECM0133) FIR boundary is expected 1hr 33m after take-off.

-N0367F220 DIK1Y DIK UN857 TOLVU/N0370F230 UN857 RAPOR/N0436F330 UN857 GIMER/N0445F350 UZ912 PON/N0450F370 UN872 ERIGA UZ218 BATAX DCT BABEX DCT DIGAL DCT MAGUM DCT ATECA DCT BAROK UN873 SAMAR

-GCLP0420 GCFV

-PBN/A1B1C1D1O1 COM/TCAS DOF/130326 REG/EIEMA EET/EBUR0011 LFFF001/LECM0133LPPC0200 GMMM0256 GCCC0352 OPR/RYANAIR RVR/200

- These boundaries will be identified and underlined on the OFP in anticipation of an ATC frequency change.
- For any direct routings the only abeam points required are those relating to these FIR boundaries. In the absence of OFP waypoints during an extended DCT routing an appropriate FIR boundary waypoint shall be created in the FMC. The lat/long coordinates for the boundary can be found on the OFP. A user waypoint and abeam point where applicable shall be created in the FMC fix page using this lat/log. Such waypoints should be named FIR1, FIR2 etc.
- Crews shall anticipate a frequency change prior to reaching an FIR boundary. If none is forthcoming then a radio check with ATC shall be initiated. For extended routings between FIR boundaries crews shall (in the absence of any other RT instructions) ensure that radio contact is maintained by performing

page 8–198

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

a radio check at intervals not exceeding 25 minutes and corresponding to a point identified along the route and highlighted on the ND from the FMC FIX page.

In summary, establish where the boundaries are, enter them sequentially as a FIX in the FMC, exercise R/T and CPDLC vigilance when approaching, and monitor 121.50 at all times in the cruise.

In addition to the MORA/altitude information contained on the flight plan, the low level Navigation Chart and/or airport area chart giving the minimum safe altitude shall be readily available for consultation in case of re-routing or if further amplification on safety heights is needed.

## Checking Enroute Weather

During periods of general poor weather, enroute weather should be recorded during the cruise to satisfy the requirement to land at the nearest suitable airport in accordance with stated QRH procedure. Preference will be to get weather for enroute Ryanair bases as these are the most likely enroute diversion airports. You may use the ND to pick the airports that you want and then check the ATIS frequency on the E(LO) chart (above the airport name). Spending a lot of time listening to a VOLMET is not recommended. Never try and get weather when monitoring the ATC frequency. Always hand over the radio.

![img-18.jpeg](img-18.jpeg)

## Monitoring 121.5

121.5 shall be monitored above FL100/10,000 ft. This frequency should be active on VHF 2, except when VHF 2 is used for other purposes. After 30 mins over water listening watch on 121.5 MHz is mandatory. Once you have passed FL100/10,000 ft climbing confirm 121.5 selected on VHF 2, with the volume adjusted to hear transmissions on this frequency.

# R/T Discipline

Correct ATC terminology is essential. Accurate readback of all clearances, especially when using the terms “Heading” and “Altitude”, will ensure that there is no confusion with ATC and therefore reduce the amount of time spent on the frequency.

Before leaving a frequency it is important to pause in order to allow ATC the chance to correct your frequency readback. On selecting a new frequency it is important to pause before speaking in order not to block ATC or another aircraft transmission.

Remember how we sound on air will greatly influence others impression of our professionalism.

It is not necessary to maintain a listening watch while operating on HF provided the aircraft is Selcal equipped and this facility has been checked out on the ground or on initial contact with the radio station.

Position reports may be omitted under radar control if it is clearly understood from ATC that such reports are not required.

8:3,0.1.12 ATC Airspace and Speed Policy

| FL 100 and below | Class A, B, C                         | Class D, E, F, G                      |
| ---------------- | ------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------- |
| Climb            | 250, faster if released by ATC        | 250 max                               |
| Descent          | 250 max, decline ATC offers of faster | 250 max, no ATC discretion for faster |

In Class A, B and C airspace, ATC have the discretion to cancel speed control. Where ATC advise “no speed restriction” or “high speed approved” in the climb the aircraft may, at the Captain’s discretion, be operated at the ECON climb speed.

Speed control instructions remain in effect unless explicitly cancelled or amended by the air traffic controller. Crew should comply with published SID and STAR speed restrictions unless the restrictions are explicitly cancelled or amended by the air traffic controller. Cancellation of any speed control instruction does not relieve the flight crew of compliance with the speed limitations associated with airspace classifications. Some SID and STAR speed restrictions ensure containment within RNAV departure or arrival procedure (e.g., maximum speed associated with a constant radius arc to a fix (RF) leg).

page 8-200

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

When descending in Class A, B and C airspace, crews are restricted to 250 kt below FL100 unless otherwise instructed. Thus, crews shall decline ATC offers of discretionary high speed below FL 100, and request standard speeds. If a higher speed than 250 kt below FL100 is instructed by ATC whilst in A, B, or C airspace, crews shall fly this speed instructed until reaching FL60 or 6,000' AAL, whichever occurs first, where crews shall advise ATC, and reduce to standard SERA speeds.

The mandatory SERA speed restriction of 250 kt when operating in Class D, E, F and G airspace below FL100 remains unchanged. This restriction is to provide an acceptable level of safety when operating in airpace that is shared with VFR traffic. Unless instructed otherwise by ATC the 250 kt limit must be strictly observed. Consider reducing to 220Kts or minimum manoeuvre speed to improve ability to "See &amp; Avoid" in airspace with significant presence of VFR traffic.

It is Ryanair policy to accept the highest standard radar service available while, regardless of airspace classification.

As OMC enroute charts do not always depict airspace classification/structure, refer to Ryanair Airfield Briefings for associated airspace and TEM guidance for airports where VFR traffic prevails.

Most importantly maintain lookout particularly in Class E, F and G airspace. At airports with dedicated glider sectors, crews must be alert to VFR activity which can take place inside or outside of charted areas. Utilize TEM to anticipate VFR traffic probability, presence, and intensity (i.e., consider Daylight VMC, higher cloud bases, airstrips in the vicinity, time of the day, etc.).

When VFR traffic conflicts with published or ATC directed routings, proactive TEM and coordination with ATC is essential. If necessary, consider delaying takeoff if uncontrolled VFR traffic impedes or interferes with departure routings transiting E, F, G airspace. When airborne, proactively request avoidance vectors if available, take avoiding action and advice advise ATC or enter a published holding pattern until well-clear of VFR traffic.

Visual approaches or departure/arrival runway changes are prohibited when glider/VFR activity is promulgated by ATIS/ATC unless doing so clearly reduces exposure to VFR traffic (e.g., enable aircraft to operate with a higher standard of radar service, airspace classification, etc.) or is otherwise explicitly specified in the Airfield Briefing.

It is good airmanship to take avoiding action (heading change) whenever radar provides conflicting traffic information, thereby ensuring that the conflict does not develop to a point where a TCAS warning is received.

Ryanair aircraft are fitted with Enhanced Mode S transponders and ATC can view:

- the flight number,
- current heading, altitude and speed selected on the MCP,
- the current V/S

Is is considered best practice to request "standard speeds" from ATC when higher speeds are offered. These speeds are:

- 220 kt at IAF
- 180 kt on base
- 180 kt on Final approach track
- 160 kt usually from 8 nm to 4 nm

## 8.3.0.2 Climb, Cruise and Descent

## 8.3.0.2.1 Terrain Clearance

Particular care must be exercised when operating out of or into airports, close to mountains and other significant terrain:

1. Take-off Briefing and Descent and Approach Briefing shall identify terrain hazards and the minimum safe altitude.
2. After take-off when any emergency or compulsory turn has been completed the best gradient climb speed shall be flown until the safety altitude is achieved;
3. Direct To clearances shall not be requested until at or above the current MSA.
4. In the CLIMB, only an unconditional “DIR TO” clearance issued by ATC may be accepted as ATC is responsible for terrain/obstacle clearance. When other conditions are attached to the “DIR TO”, e.g. “with terrain in sight”, pilots shall reject these clearances until at or above the MSA during climb. These issues do not arise in the Descent.
5. “Radar Vectors” during departure, at or below the MSA, in compliance with 8.3.0.2 may be accepted. A commander may accept an ATC clearance to deviate from a published departure or arrival route, provided obstacle clearance criteria are observed and full account is taken of the operating conditions.

page 8-202

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

In addition to the safety height/altitude information contained on the flight plan, the low level Navigation Chart and/or airport area chart giving the minimum safe altitude shall be readily available for consultation in case of re-routing or if further amplification on safety heights is needed.

All altitudes require cold weather compensation correction when not under radar control, when self positioning, or when levelling off after an emergency descent.

Crews shall respond to EGPWS as specified in the QRH. Flight Crew members must not descend below minimum safe altitudes for the route or the sector at any time until they have positively identified and cross checked their position.

## 8.3.0.2.2 Climb Phase

The climb speed shall be limited to 250 kt below FL 100 as sound operating practice in class D, E, F and G airspace. ECON climb speeds are approved below FL 100 with ATC authorisation in A, B and C airspace.

Flight at ECON speed is Ryanair policy above FL 100. It is recognised that variations from ECON will be required and these are approved.

Changes to airspeed required by turbulence shall be notified to ATC immediately.

## 8.3.0.2.3 Bat

ATC has the altitude readout from mode S. ATC in some locations has the ability to identify incorrect altimeter setting (QNH) from this data. When this is detected ATC will reissue the QNH and ask crews to confirm given QNH is actually set.

### What is BAT?

- BAT = Barometric Pressure Setting (BPS) Advisory Tool.
- Tool to identify significant QNH setting errors.

### What does it do?

- Provide an advisory warning on the radar to London Terminal Control Approach controllers where there is a significant difference (greater than 5 mb) from the London QNH.
- It only supports arrival aircraft for aircraft at or below the transition level.

### What does it not do?

- Alert on departure aircraft.
- Alert above the transition level (e.g. aircraft entering a terminal hold).

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

Is there any new phraseology?

- Where controllers choose to query the discrepancy, the phraseology to be used is:

“(Callsign), Check Altimeter Setting, QNH xxx” – where xxx is the local QNH being utilised.

- Where sufficient time exists, the use of the following phraseology may be used to query the Altimeter Setting used by the aircrew:

“(Callsign) Report QNH”

- This will enable flight crew to check selected pressure setting, irrespective of any BAT Advisory or QNH data displayed in the Mode S Data Window.

  8.3.0.2.4 Altitude Awareness

Altitude awareness is achieved by the active attention of the Flight Crew. This awareness with associated continuous monitoring is the primary means of ensuring that cleared altitudes are adhered to and clearances are not infringed.

The standard operating procedure mandates altitude checks which confirm altitude awareness, and mandate vertical speeds to be flown to reduce closure rates. These policies and standard calls are presented in FCOM Vol 1, Normal procedures.

Climbing

It is mandatory to select VNAV after all MCP Altitude/Flight Level changes during climb.

Below FL300 it is prohibited to select V/S unless the ROC approaching the cleared Altitude/Flight Level exceeds the following limits:

- At 3000 ft to level off, if ROC exceeds 3000 ft per min, select V/S and reduce ROC to a maximum of 3000 ft per min.
- At 2000 ft to level off, if ROC exceeds 2000 ft per min, select V/S and reduce ROC to a maximum of 2000 ft per min.
- At 1000 ft to level off, if ROC exceeds 100 ft per min, select V/S and reduce ROC to a maximum of 1000 ft per min.

Only select V/S when VNAV is providing a sustained climb rate in excess of 3000 ft, 2000 ft or 1000 ft per minute.

Above FL300 crew should remain in VNAV to avail of the system protections that include stall protection, minimum/maximum operating speed limits, thrust limits, and manoeuvre margins at high altitudes.

page 8-204

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR
PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

Above FL300 if sustained rates of climb in excess of 1500 ft per minute are encountered when approaching 1000' to the cleared level, both PF and PM must actively monitor aircraft performance and traffic display to prevent avoidable TCAS RAs. Where traffic is a consideration, adjust V/S as required and revert to VNAV as soon as traffic is no longer a potential conflict.

Aircraft performance must be closely monitored when using V/S, especially at high altitudes. Minimal and sudden environmental changes such as temperature and/or wind can severely deteriorate aircraft performance. Requests/Restrictions from ATC shall be managed through VNAV.

## Descending

The preferred descent mode is VNAV. Other descent modes may be utilized to accommodate CDA’s, level restrictions, ATC restrictions and requests. The ROD must be reduced unless a restriction is set by ATC, whenever the ROD approaching the cleared Flight Level/Altitude exceeds the following limits:

- At 3000 ft to level off, if ROD exceeds 3000 ft per min, select V/S and reduce to a maximum of 3000 ft per min.
- At 2000 ft to level off, if ROD exceeds 2000 ft per min, select V/S and reduce to a maximum of 2000 ft per min.
- At 1000 ft to level off, if ROD exceeds 1000 ft per min, select V/S and reduce to a maximum of 1000 ft per min.

Step descents are often a reflection of potential traffic below. ATC stepped descents present a threat of avoidable TCAS RAs if the ROD is not effectively managed. If ATC instruct a stepped descent (altitude changes of 2000 ft or less), crew should initially select V/S to manage these requirements.

- At 2000 ft to level off, select a maximum of 2000 ft per min.
- At 1000 ft to level off, select a maximum of 1000 ft per min.

Both PF and PM must actively monitor aircraft performance when utilising V/S, particularly at high altitudes. Do not engage VNAV or LVL CH when performing stepped descents.

The altitude alert system should only be considered as a back-up aid. Its operation must not be permitted to replace the requirement to make the standard altitude calls.

The Ryanair Standard Operating Procedure in this area is presented in FCOM Vol 1, Normal procedures.
