Incorrect setting of the QNH in the descent is an insidious mistake frequently made. An altitude crosscheck on final approach path is an essential crew action to detect this.

## 8.3.0.2.5 Systems Monitoring

Flight and navigation instruments and engine indications will be monitored on a routine on-going basis, but additionally the proper functioning of aircraft systems will be monitored regularly throughout the flight.

Whereas the aircraft checklists will provide a reminder for setting up the various systems, the orientation checks should be used as a trigger to periodically scan those panels and systems outside the regular forward instrument panel scan and not tied in to a central warning system. Typical items requiring re-selection or adjustment might include landing lights at FL 100, seat belt sign, anti-icing systems, and air conditioning systems etc.

## 8.3.0.2.6 Cruise

Company policy is to operate at the FliteDeck Advisor (FDA) recommended Cost Index or fixed speed/Mach. FDA will generate a tail specific Cost Index based on ambient conditions and therefore may differ to the OFP Cost Index.

Note: Occasionally, OCC will issue a CI 70 or CI 100 OFP to assist with delay recovery. In this case, crew shall operate at the OFP Cost Index.

Crews shall operate at ECON speeds generated by the FMC. The only exception to this is for diversions where the route to alternate is always calculated at and shall always be flown using LRC.

The flight levels on the OFP are optimum flight levels as determined by the LIDO flight planning optimization tool. These flight levels shall be flown as follows:

- When the flight plan cruise altitude is lower than the FMC OPT altitude crews shall fly the flight plan cruise altitude (See Note below).

page 8-206

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

- When the flight plan cruise altitude is higher than the FMC OPT altitude crews shall fly the FMC OPT altitude.

Note: Some flights may be planned at a lower level due to route restrictions. If the operational Impacts section of the OFP indicates a fuel saving at a higher level, a climb should be considered if deemed appropriate by the commander.

| OPERATIONAL IMPACTS |            |      |        |     |             |     |
| ------------------- | ---------- | ---- | ------ | --- | ----------- | --- |
| FL CHANGE           | 2000 ABOVE | TRIP | M 0031 | KGS | TIME M 0001 |     |
| FL CHANGE           | 2000 BELOW | TRIP | P 0067 | KGS | TIME P 0002 |     |
| WEIGHT CHANGE       | UP 1.0     | TRIP | P 0028 | KGS | TIME M 0000 |     |

Crew should reference the block wind information at the end of the OFP to determine if it is appropriate to climb to levels not displayed in the Operational Impacts section.

| 350              | 035/011 | -59 | 230 | 057/013 | -27 |     | 230 | 060/013 | -27 | 230   | 056/012 |

However, the aircraft may be operated safely up to and including maximum altitude indicated in the FMC. Crews shall carefully consider the following prior to electing to operate above the FMC optimum altitude:

- Identify enroute areas of moderate turbulence and carefully consider reducing operating altitudes accordingly.
- Review the shear indications on the OFP.
- Avoid flight in severe turbulence (greater than 1.5 G).
- Not select wing anti-ice on with the SAT less than -40 degrees C (including when ice is visible on wipers and L1 and R1 windows).
- Select 10 degree bank angle on the MCP passing FL300 climbing (to provide over-bank protection in HDG SEL) and 25 degrees passing FL300 descending.
- For operations other than LNAV, fly at least 10 knots above the minimum manoeuvre speed (lower amber band).
- If speed drops below the lower amber band, immediately increase speed by reducing bank and/or increase thrust to max continuous (N1 page FMC) and/or descend.

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

Operations above the FMC MAX ALT are not permitted. The flight plan flight levels are calculated using 5% MAC. This is the most restrictive C of G position for generating cruising levels. Crews shall use a combination of the Operational Impacts information on the OFP and FMC information if a cruising FL above FMC MAX ALT is generated by the flight plan and a lower level is required.

Step climbs and descents shall be requested in accordance with the Operational Flight Plan. For the avoidance of doubt, it is only permitted to fly at speeds greater than ECON CLIMB, CRZ OR DESC in order to operate a Fixed speed/Mach flight plan, for profile adjustment, to comply with an ATC speed request or if directed by MEL, QRH or supplementary procedure for example, turbulence penetration.

The acceptance of any cruise altitude either enroute or during diversion indicates that the crew is aware that obstacle clearance is achieved. Accurate navigation and judicious requests for direct clearances can reduce the nautical airmiles (nam) flown and so reduce flight time and cost. Possible areas of clear air turbulence will be identified during flight preparation to provide prior warning to flight deck and Cabin Crew:

1. Severe CAT if identifiable shall be avoided.
2. When turbulence is experienced or anticipated the passenger seat belt sign shall be switched on. Best practice is that crews advise ATC of hazardous meteorological conditions enroute, which has the effect of notifying other traffic. It may be advisable also to limit cabin service.
3. Speed shall be adjusted at the Commanders discretion, having regard to the expected severity of the turbulence. It is not expected to reduce to the max severe turbulence penetration speed for light turbulence, it is expected to reduce to this speed for severe turbulence. Any speed reduction shall be notified to ATC, and Commanders shall advise ATC as to the continuing efficacy of RVSM in these conditions.
4. Crews shall follow FCOM Vol 1, SP 16 guidance, in-flight, in severe turbulence and mountain wave activity to maintain control of the aircraft.

Weather radar scanning shall be used to identify and avoid Cb build-up and thunderstorm activity. The Commander shall have WXR selected for take-off, climb, cruise and descent/approach in IMC and at night. This does not preclude the First Officer (when PF) from using the weather radar during the cruise to identify and avoid Cumulonimbus build-up and thunderstorm activity. Permission should be requested from ATC to detour around thunderstorm activity, preferably to upwind rather than downwind of the build-up. If the thunderstorm activity appears to be exceptionally severe and there is no apparent

page 8-208

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

path through or around the storm area/line then consideration should be given to diverting the flight. Crew’s primary objective is to conduct our air transport activities safely.

## Destination and primary alternate weather

Best practise is to obtain what weather you need to complete the flight, minimising the time the PM is off the operational ATC frequency. This is particularly important when the destination and alternates are just south of the alps. This weather is only available from VHF on line of sight transmission, which is too frequently coincident with ATC descent and arrival clearances. Proper planning should avoid this conflict of both pilots being on different frequencies.

Ideally crews shall avoid getting weather in the descent. It is recognised that occasionally actual destination weather is required in the descent/approach, and this is allowed. This shall be accomplished expeditiously, preferably by listening to ATIS, or if below 5,000 ft and within 25 miles, from the destination tower frequency. The PF shall be particularly vigilant on the live ATC frequency when the PM is getting this weather, and ALL ATC instructions received by the PF shall be briefed to the PM on his return to the ATC frequency.

## Recording Weather on the OFP

The Wx pack on report includes up to 9 hr forecasts which complies with the requirements for destination and alternate aerodromes. The weather supplied on turnaround also supports these requirements.

When the crew decides to record weather during flight, this shall be recorded on the OFP ideally in the cruise. Best practise is to obtain what additional weather you need to complete the flight, minimising the time the PM is off the operational ATC frequency.

Use Wx information locations, e.g. Zurich, Vienna, to avoid the conflict of receiving weather reports coincident with descent planning or descent activities. Proper planning will minimise the threat of both pilots being on different frequencies.

The weather for the aerodrome of departure and landing shall be recorded on the OFP.

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## 8.3.0.2.6.1 Management of ATC Clearances/MCP with One Pilot on Operational VHF Freq.

Where the PM is off the radio to get weather or operational information the PF will monitor the Operational VHF frequency. Where the PF gets an ATC instruction to change ALT or HDG, the PF will make the MCP change but will not execute the change until the PM is back on frequency and the change of HDG or ALT has been verified with the appropriate ATC centre.

Where ATC requires an immediate change of ALT or HDG the PF will interrupt the PM and point out the change to the MCP. The PM will immediately return to the operational frequency and ask for confirmation of the instruction. "XXX Control, confirm the HDG/ATL for RYR 123".

Where the PF gets an ATC instruction to change ALT or HDG via CPDLC, the PF will Respond WILCO/UNABLE or STANDBY. When the PM is back on frequency PF will brief all new CLOSED instructions on ATC log page.

## 8.3.0.2.7 Pre-descent Considerations

The top-of-descent point shall be determined taking into account the standard descent distance adjusted for wind component, anticipated ATC routing and possible holding, icing, safety heights, and runway in use. This is computed by the FMC based on routings and constraints entered.

The descent will be conducted in such a way as to achieve fuel economy. This is best achieved by VNAV and ECON speed.

FCOM Vol 1, Normal procedures describes the descent phase, including timings and content of descent and approach briefing. Flight Crew members shall be conscious of terrain under the descent path and aware of safety heights/altitudes along the arrival route and for the approach.

1. Flight Crew members must not descend below the MEA (minimum enroute altitude) or MORA (minimum off route altitude) or the minimum sector altitude (MSA) for the approach facility until they have positively identified and cross checked their position.
2. Flight Crew members shall not descend below the MSA to the minimum procedural altitude until the aircraft has passed over the approach fix or unless specific descent instructions are given in the approach chart except for paragraph. (3) below. The extended ILS glidepath is not approved as an en-route descent aid and all aids shall be used only within their certified limits.
3. Where the aircraft is being vectored by radar, the radar controller is responsible for ensuring proper terrain clearance, including in cold weather/temperature compensation conditions. In these circumstances

page 8-210

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

clearance and descent to the cleared altitude below the MSA is authorised. When clearance for the approach is issued, the aircraft is expected to maintain the last assigned level until intercepting the specified or nominal glide path of the approach procedure. If radar contact is lost, the aircraft shall climb to the MSA without further delay.

4. STARs take the aircraft safely from the enroute phase to the initial approach fix and assure terrain/obstacle clearance. STARS are referenced to ground based navaids.

5. RNAV stars are not necessarily referenced to ground based navaids. RNAV descents below the MSA require a RNP1 approval. Ryanair has a PRNAV approval. (Note that self positioning on RNP1 approval must not take the aircraft outside the areas used to design the approach to ensure proper sequencing of the RNP during the descent approach (see OMC RNP1, ATC Section) Self positioning relieves the radar controller of responsibility for terrain clearance.

6. Additionally accepting “Direct to” may take the aircraft over higher terrain/obstacles than the SH on the OFP indicates. Pilots shall establish that the minimum altitude in these circumstances is higher than the highest altitude in the current MORA and all MORAs enroute to the cleared waypoint/fix.

The Descent and Approach Briefing shall be completed as SOP prior to commencement of the descent, see FCOM Vol 1 for content.

## MLW Consideration:

Crews shall establish that the aircraft shall not be above the Max Landing Weight on landing, particularly if it is close to the MLW at descent briefing. It is considered good airmanship to verify MLW will not be exceeded on landing prior to commencement of final approach.

## 8.3.0.2.8 Descent and Approach Briefing

The descent briefing is presented in FCOM Vol 1, Normal procedures, and the Flight Crew Training Manual provides additional operational guidance. The statement “Standard Calls and Procedures” will apply only when both Flight Crew members are familiar with the procedures for the airfield concerned and when further elaboration is not required.

The Airfield brief if published shall be consulted and briefed prior to top of descent. The airfield brief for an alternate if published shall be reviewed if a diversion is likely prior to top of descent, or as soon as practicable once a diversion is initiated. Airfield briefs are located within Docunet and FDF.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

If a diversion is contemplated, the Commander will ensure that the co-pilot is aware of:

1. The nominated alternate airfield, and the expected routing to this alternate;
2. The fuel requirements for diversion bearing in mind air distance, weather and anticipated ATC delay;
3. The descent and approach procedures at the alternate airfield; and
4. If a definite decision has been made to divert after a particular approach, then a specific request will be made to ATC stating that in the event of a missed approach a clearance to the alternate airfield is required. The prior indication given should eliminate any ATC delays in setting course for the alternate.

## 8.3.0.2.9 Correcting Flight from above the Descent/Approach profile

The only lateral manoeuvring that is acceptable to regain the vertical approach profile is as follows:

- Request entry to a published intermediate holding fix in order to lose altitude in a clearly defined and controlled manner to achieve the platform or step altitude at the correct speed and aircraft configuration.
- Request an "orbit" that will be executed as a racetrack pattern for stability which is subject to the following:

1. Not less than 10nms remaining to touchdown.
2. Descent to not below the MSA or 3,000 ft whichever is higher.
3. Max Flap 10, with speedbrake if operationally necessary.
4. Met conditions VMC.

- If a visual approach has been requested and approved by ATC that is not straight in then consider extending the downwind or widening the base leg, subject to other traffic.
- If required conduct a full published go-around to another approach.
- It is not acceptable to join a visual circuit from an incomplete go-around.

If there is any doubt about the successful outcome of the approach a go-around must be the prime consideration and is preferred over any other method.

## 8.3.0.2.10 Orbits

It is only permitted to perform an orbit inside 10 nm from touchdown subject to the following restrictions:

- Requested by ATC for traffic separation

page 8-212

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

- Under Radar Control
- Not below 1500 ft AGL
- Max Flap 15
- Day VMC

Any other condition requires a go-around.

Ryanair does not permit orbits inside 10 nm from touchdown for the purpose of losing altitude or speed.

## 8.3.0.3 Approach and Landing

### 8.3.0.3.1 Landing Conditions

The destination meteorological information shall be obtained and evaluated before commencing an approach. This information together with the runway surface conditions is likely to determine whether an approach can or should be made, the type of approach to be made and the runway in use. Flight crew shall plan and coordinate the lowest available aerodrome approach minima in order to avoid any unnecessary go arounds due to deteriorating or marginal weather conditions. E.g. an ILS CAT II/III shall be requested when marginal CAT I conditions exists provided it is readily available.

Before commencing an approach to land, the commander shall adhere to the requirements of Section 8.1.3.2.

Crosswind component and limits shall be taken into account as it is noted that cross winds approaching limiting values can present significant handling challenges. In these circumstances it is prudent for the aircraft commander to act as pilot flying.

The weather minima to be used for the different types of approaches are displayed on the Approach Chart. The effect of downgraded equipment and meteorological terminology with regard to the determination and application of Airfield Operating Minima is presented in this manual.

In adverse weather conditions, and for landing on contaminated runways; see FCOM Vol 1, and QRH for procedures. The Flight Crew Training Manual provides additional guidance for descent, approach and landing techniques, including explanation of the consequences of errors.

An aircraft shall avoid making an approach if a thunderstorm is active over the airfield or there are cells on the final approach centre line within 3 nautical miles of the field. Crews shall be particularly aware of windshear conditions, and shall review the windshear escape manoeuvre if such conditions exist.

# 8.3.0.3.1.1 Landing Lights and Night Time Operations

CAT I ILS/NPA – (Manual Landing) Landing Lights – ON

CAT II/IIIA – Landing Lights – Commander's Discretion

# 8.3.0.3.2 Spurious Predictive Windshear (PWS) Alerts

The Honeywell Predictive Windshear (PWS) alert system as fitted to the Boeing 737 fleet is prone to spurious PWS alerts in certain atmospheric conditions. Spurious PWS alerts are most likely to occur in calm conditions where the takeoff or approach path is over an area of open water. Certain airports in the route network are known to suffer from this phenomenon and are identified in FCOM Boeing Bulletins or in the AFB.

Predictive Windshear Alerts are identified by the following Aural Alerts:

- “WINDSHEAR AHEAD, WINDSHEAR AHEAD”,
- “GO AROUND WINDSHEAR AHEAD”, or
- “MONITOR RADAR DISPLAY”

When operating in areas of known spurious PWS activity, and briefed in advance, a takeoff or approach may be continued at the discretion of the Commander after a spurious PWS alert, provided there are no weather conditions existing or forecast associated with windshear.

If a windshear escape maneuver or go-around is performed following a spurious PWS Alert, a subsequent approach may be flown provided that no associated weather conditions exist.

Note: PWS Alerts should not be confused with a GPWS windshear warning (Two-tone siren followed by “WINDSHEAR, WINDSHEAR, WINDSHEAR”) which indicates that the aircraft is in windshear.

An ASR shall be filed in all cases of activation of PWS, spurious or otherwise.

# 8.3.0.3.3 Conventional Approach (Non CAT II or III) Monitored Approach (MA) Policy

A conventional approach implies that either trained Flight Crew member may perform any type of approach and land at the Commander’s discretion. Profiles for such approaches are presented in FCOM Vol 1, Normal procedures, and profiles for non normal approaches are presented in the QRH. Further limitations are specified for inexperienced co-pilots. When available all descents and approaches shall be conducted in LNAV/VNAV.

# Monitored Approach (MA)

Ryanair procedure for MA is presented in FCOM Vol 1, QRH and is applicable to Precision Approaches and Non Precision Approaches (NPA) including LNAV/VNAV when the visibility is reported as less than specified minima. A Monitored Approach must be carried out when the weather conditions are below the following limits:

| Approach | Minima: vis/ceiling | Exceptions |
| -------- | ------------------- | ---------- |

| Type B
(3D DH < 250 ft, >= 200 ft) | <= 1,000 m, 300 ft AAL | Nil |
| Type A
(2D or 3D DH >= 250 ft) | <= 3,000 m, 1,000 ft AAL | Circling approach
Visual approach (No published procedure) |

Nothing prevents a crew conducting a Monitored Approach in better weather conditions.

Inexperienced co-pilots may fly a Monitored Approach (including when the crosswind is in excess of 15 kt) as the landing will be flown by the Captain. The Captain shall call ‘continue’ at or before minima when he has the required visual references for landing.

# Competence – Conduct of Approaches

The conduct of approaches and the areas of responsibility for each crew member, the standard operating procedure, and the approach profiles are presented in FCOM Vol 1, Normal procedures.

# Go-Around

The conduct of the GO-Around and the areas of responsibility for each crew member, the standard operating procedure, and the Go-Around profiles are presented in FCOM Vol 1, Normal procedures, and QRH for non normal Go-around procedures. Additional guidance is provided in the Boeing Flight Crew Training Manual.

## 8.3.0.3.4 Stabilised Approach

### Stabilised Approach Procedure

The objective of a stabilised approach is to cross the runway threshold at a safe altitude, aligned with the runway centreline, with the aircraft in trim and thrust at appropriate approach settings, to arrive at the flare point at the required airspeed, and to touchdown as close as possible to the aiming point (1,000 feet for jet aircraft).

To achieve this with consistency in various conditions of wind and weather requires:

1. Timely deceleration to final approach speed, adjusted for wind, matched with configuration change so that the landing configuration is achieved at 500 ft above airfield level (AAL) in VMC and 1,000 ft AAL in IMC.
2. Adherence to a constant angle of descent/flight path angle, e.g., the ILS glide slope, a visually assessed path, VNAV path on non-ILS/GLS approaches with the current V/S proportional to the aircraft current ground speed.

Crews are reminded that any aircraft that proceeds beyond the 500 ft VMC Landing Gate or 1000 ft IMC Landing Gate without being stabilised (parameters below) is required to submit an ASR detailing the circumstances behind the decision to continue the approach.

### Stabilised Approach Criteria

The following stabilised approach criteria must be achieved by the respective landing gate, and maintained until touchdown.

1. Target Approach speed $V_{REF} + 5$ to $V_{REF} + 15$ (minimum speed $V_{REF}$, maximum speed $V_{REF} + 20$)
2. On the correct vertical and lateral path, where only small changes in heading and pitch are required to maintain the correct flight path. FCOM NP refers.

**Note 1:** Lateral manoeuvring to align with the runway extended centreline must be completed by 300 ft AAL during all Circling or Side-Step Approaches.

**Note 2:** Exceptions to this policy, where lateral manoeuvring below 300 ft AAL is permitted, shall be detailed in the relevant OMC AFB (CAT C aerodromes), and as approved by the Competent Authority.

3. Appropriate thrust set.

page 8-216

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

4. Vertical speed proportional to the approach design and ground speed, but not more than 1000 fpm, unless briefed prior to the approach.
5. Landing Checklist complete (except landing lights).

## 8.3.0.3.4.1 CDA/LDA

Continuous descent approach (CDA) and Lo Drag approach (LDA) is considered best practise for CAT I ILS approach.

Additional guidance is provided in the Boeing Flight Crew Training Manual.

Coupled with the control of the aircraft descent profile on final approach is the need to maintain aircraft tracking along the aircraft centreline to:

1. permit easier recognition and handling of windshear;
2. assess the crosswind;
3. avoid large lateral deviation corrections necessitating excessive bank angles close to the runway; and
4. facilitate touchdown on the runway centreline and ensure that the aircraft is tracking the runway centreline after touchdown.

These criteria apply to both instrument and visual approaches even though the cues used for assessment and judgement differ. The stabilised approach facilitates the transfer from instrument to visual conditions, in which:

1. care is required to avoid “duck-under” after breakout into visual conditions at low altitude following an instrument approach;
2. care is also required to maintain the proper descent profile in conditions of decreasing visibility, and with the decreasing visibility of shallow fog, it is particularly important that the visual approach is fully stabilised before entering the fog layer;
3. Approach profile and path shall always be monitored by reference to all the nav aids, VNAV path and visual aids available. In addition, the PM must specifically direct attention to speed, rate-of-descent and glidepath throughout the final descent.

Utilising the Stabilised Approach concept with adherence to electronic and visual glide slope indicators, 1,000 ft visual runway aiming points, constant angle non-precision approaches, and “duck-under” avoidance constitute the essential operational procedures designed to ensure that an aircraft crosses the threshold by a safe margin, with the aircraft in the landing configuration and attitude. FCOM Vol 1 refers.

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## 8.3.0.3.5 Visual Approaches

1. A visual approach may be carried out or continued only if it is clearly determined that the meteorological conditions are adequate for the approach, the aircraft will remain in visual contact with the ground and that the aircraft can be positioned for a landing by visual reference to the runway, and most importantly after prior double briefing of the procedure.

2. Verification of aircraft position before commencing a visual approach is particularly important and shall be cross checked by all available means. Radio aids will be used for this purpose and will in any case be selected, tuned and identified as for an instrument approach onto the landing runway and/or to facilitate monitoring of the approach when available.

3. A visual approach is a non-precision approach (NPA) by Ryanair standards and is therefore subject to the Double Brief. A crew may not elect to fly a visual approach without completing the Double Brief. The briefing must include the position of all configuration changes and autopilot/FD handling for the approach and the complete go-around manoeuvre. The crew must be alert for airfields, disused runways or roads that may be mistaken for the runway. Use the map display to back up the visual picture. A visual approach may only be flown in conditions where constant contact with the runway and its environment can be maintained throughout the manoeuvre.

4. Aircraft shall not fly below glidepath, or follow a descent path lower than the approach path normally indicated by the glidepath/PAPI.

5. The latest position to select Flap 1 on a visual approach is 10 nm from touchdown. Additional configuration changes must be made to allow the aircraft to be fully stable (as defined in FCOM Volume 1) with the landing checklist complete to the landing clearance by 500 ft AAL. Minimums therefore for Visual Approaches are 500 ft AAL.

6. Autopilot limitations (Boeing FCOM) must be considered when flying a visual approach without a LOC or GS. Crews should avoid the temptation to immediately disconnect all automatics in order to fly the approach and passenger comfort must not be compromised in the execution of the manoeuvre.

7. Visual approaches are required to utilise the stabilised approach technique and follow a constant angle electronic or VASI/PAPI glide path.

8. When manoeuvring for a visual approach, care must be taken to avoid and remain well clear of all terrain and obstruction.

page 8–218

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

9. This means that pilots shall not conduct self manoeuvring/positioning below the MSA at night. Pilots can conduct a visual landing at night only from a published approach procedure.

Where a night time circling approach is necessary the initial approach shall be conducted as an instrument approach with a break at MDA to a circling approach. Night landings from a circling approach require a PAPI/VASI

10. Night landings from a NPA to a runway less than 2000 m LDA without PAPI/VASI require NPFO approval.

11. ATC must be informed and have approved the visual manoeuvre prior to executing it.

12. The go-around is potentially the most difficult manoeuvre to execute from a visual approach, however, a well briefed and thought out manoeuvre that includes touch drilling the go-around is essential. FD's shall be used when available and the PF must push TOGA to activate GA mode if a go-around is required below the GA Alt.

13. In the event that a go-around is required the published missed approach procedure must be flown either to completion, or to level flight under radar vectors and after take-off checks completed.

14. Ryanair shall not use an RVR of less than 800 m for a visual approach.

15. Visual approaches shall be conducted by Commanders and experienced co-pilots (&gt;500 hrs on type in Ryanair Group and 1500 flight hours on a jet aircraft with a MTOW of greater than 20 tonnes).

## 8.3.0.3.5.1 Continuous Descent Approach (CDA)

The purpose of the Continuous Descent Approach is to reduce the environmental impact of the aircraft while improving passenger comfort and reducing overall fuel burn.

Crews should avoid any level segment in the flight path prior to Final Approach Point intercept (i.e. no ALT HOLD). Vertical speed should be adjusted to intercept the final approach path as early as possible during the initial approach phase.

When CDA procedure is published crews may use any V/S to achieve it, including V/S &lt; 500 fpm

## 8.3.0.3.5.2 Low Drag Approach (LDA)

The purpose of the Low Drag Approach is to reduce the environmental impact of the aircraft while improving passenger comfort and reducing overall fuel burn.

Subject to ATC, crews will plan for a LDA.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

Plan to fly the aircraft to intercept the final approach path at 180 kt, and flaps as required. Thereafter the aircraft is flown in the gear-up configuration to 4 nm (Precision approach) and 5/4 nm (NPA) from touchdown. Passing 4 nm or (5/4 nm NPA) but not later than 3.5 nm the PF should call for gear down, F15 and landing checklist and commence re-configuration for landing. The aircraft must be fully configured and stabilised by 500 ft AAL VMC or 1,000 ft AAL IMC.

## 8.3.0.3.5.3 Low drag Approach (Non-precision Approach)

Low drag non-precision approaches are not normally applicable to V/S NPA.

## 8.3.0.3.5.4 Low Drag Approach (Visual Approach)

Straight in arrivals with the runway in sight are approved for low drag technique.

## 8.3.0.3.5.5 Sidestep Approach

The criteria set out shall be observed unless associated AFB specifies otherwise. Collision avoidance, terrain and obstacle clearance is the responsibility of the aircrew upon commencement of the sidestep.

A sidestep manoeuvre can be commenced providing;

1. The sidestep has been requested by ATC and Missed Approach Instructions are confirmed;
2. The landing runway threshold is equal to or further away than the threshold of the instrument approach runway being flown;
3. Daylight VMC;
4. PAPI in sight;
5. Visual with the landing runway;
6. The manoeuvre must be commenced prior to 1000 feet above landing altitude indicated (Landing Altitude Reference Bar);
7. F30/40 selected prior to commencement of the manoeuvre;
8. The stabilised approach criteria can be met;
9. Landing performance must be calculated in advance for the most limiting runway.

## 8.3.0.3.6 Visual Company Coded RNV V

The availability of a VISUAL COMPANY CODED CIRCLING RNV V, at suitable Airports/Runways, will be detailed in the Airfield Brief (AFB).

The Performance Department will generate Circling (visual) profiles and waypoints for VISUAL COMPANY CODED CIRCLING RNV V to ensure compliance with the following standards in accordance with standard Company Circling SOPs:

1. The VISUAL COMPANY CODED CIRCLING RNV V will remain within the PANS-OPS 4.2 nm radius protected area.
2. All waypoints shall be based on the WGS84 standard and accurate to 0.01 seconds.
3. All tracks shall be Great Circle tracks.
4. Conventional IAPs only will be used to CIRCLING MDA.
5. Company generated waypoints are uniquely designated as XXXZ1 through to XXXZn, where XXX comprises the IATA aerodrome designator.
6. Waypoints defining the procedure are published on the associated VISUAL COMPANY CODED CIRCLING RNV V RWY XX.

Each procedure design achieves the following:

- The first waypoint in the procedure is at the start of the downwind leg,
- An intermediate waypoint is provided along the downwind leg,
- The base leg is coded as an RF turn terminating to coincide with the Runway Extended Centerline and PAPI angle,
- RF Base Turns are designed at 20°AOB and 168 kt,
- Descent will commence from a point representing the intersection of the final approach descent angle and the circling MDA which may be on the base turn RF leg to assure containment within 4.2 nm protected area,
- Descent gradient will be identical to the PAPI angle,
- Initial MAP will be coded straight ahead to CIRCLING MDA,
- MAP is a straight climb to CIRCLING MDA. Crew will follow the original published procedure in conventional navigation.

VISUAL COMPANY CODED CIRCLING RNV V will be applied only to suitable Airports/Runways where a circling MDA is published. Coded circling procedures are **flown visually**, with AFDS engaged.

## Procedure Sim Check

VISUAL COMPANY CODED CIRCLING RNV V has been extensively tested in a Company Level D Sim to ensure efficacy and safety of the procedure.

## Instrument Approach Minima

The minima for the instrument approach preceding the VISUAL COMPANY CODED CIRCLING RNV V is the published circling altitude (height) and the meteorological visibility. The associated visibility required for any instrument approach procedure is determined from table 9 in OMA 8.1.3.4.5. and dependent on the approach lighting available to the landing runway.

## FCOM

FCOM Vol 1, Normal procedures presents the flight profile and duties of each crew member during circling approaches. The QRH presents the flight profile and duties of each crew member for normal and Non-normal circling procedures.

## Landing Gate

The landing gate for a circling approach/sidestep is 500 ft AAL. Lateral manoeuvring to align with the runway extended centreline must be completed by 300 ft AAL during all Circling or Side-Step Approaches. The aircraft must be configured in the landing configuration by 500 ft AAL. Exceptions to this policy, where lateral manoeuvring below 300 ft AAL is permitted, shall be detailed in the relevant OMC AFB (CAT C aerodromes).

## Night Circling

A VISUAL COMPANY CODED CIRCLING RNV V will not be flown at night unless VASI/PAPI or electronic glideslope is available to the landing runway.

## Approach Chart and FMC descriptors VISUAL COMPANY CODED CIRCLING RNV V Approach Procedure

VISUAL COMPANY CODED CIRCLING RNV V charts are provided for approved aerodromes.

## VISUAL COMPANY CODED CIRCLING RNV V

R is the nomenclature required by ARINC 424 for coding procedures in FMCs regardless of FMC manufacturer and represents the letter for Area Navigation (RNAV) approach. As the B737 fleet is certified for RNP 0.3 operations the navigation specifications for RNP 0.3 are applied for all visual coded circling procedures. These procedures are listed as RNV in the FMC.

## VISUAL COMPANY CODED CIRCLING RNV V

The VISUAL COMPANY CODED CIRCLING indicates that this is a Visual, Company proprietary, (Flight Operations Performance Engineering Department) coded circling procedure produced by Jeppesen.

VISUAL COMPANY CODED CIRCLING RNV V RWY XX

XX is the runway identifier.

## Navigation Specification and Temperature Limits

The navigation specification required is indicated on the chart and Company aircraft are certified to Navigation Specification RNP 0.3. Each coded circling procedure has a base turn which is a Radius-to-fix (RF) leg, also relying on RNP 0.3 navigation specification. Upper and lower temperature limits as applicable are indicated on the chart available in the EFB.

## RNAV APPROACH LIMITATIONS

Company Coded Circling procedures are subject to the Double Brief limitations in QRH NC.12.

## AFDS

The AFDS is used during the visual coded circling procedure. LNAV provides lateral navigation to RNP 0.3 navigation specification. RF Legs are also to RNP 0.3 and executed visually. VNAV provides vertical guidance to within 75 ft above or below the FMC computed descent path and may remain engaged in vertical mode to MUH during the execution of the procedure visually.

## Waypoint Integrity

Waypoints published on VISUAL COMPANY CODED CIRCLING RNV V charts have the equivalent integrity as waypoints created in any procedure design and comply with ANNEX VII PART-DAT (DAT.OR.100) thereby providing an equivalent level of safety. When cleared for the circling approach by ATC and a VISUAL COMPANY CODED CIRCLING RNV V is published and detailed in the AFB, crews are expected to execute the VISUAL COMPANY CODED CIRCLING RNV V procedure visually with AFDS engaged until continuing to land or executing a MAP.

## Air Traffic Service (ATS)

ATS will have been briefed by the Company on the design and advantages of these procedures in locations where these procedures exist including the tracks and corresponding location of the aircraft during these procedures.

VISUAL COMPANY CODED CIRCLING RNV V procedures reduce crew workload, increase AFDS usage, promote stabilised approaches and are the preferred method over conventional circling where only 2D AFDS guidance is

available. These approaches are utilised when the aircraft cannot be flown on a straight course from the approach aid to the runway in use and visual manoeuvring is required.

The VISUAL COMPANY CODED CIRCLING RNV V commences after the IFR approach procedure where the final approach track intercepts the circling MDA. This is the point on the instrument procedure where the pilot must decide if the meteorological conditions are suitable to continue Visually following the VISUAL COMPANY CODED CIRCLING RNV V procedure with sufficient visual references, otherwise the missed approach of the Instrument Procedure will be flown.

Visual contact with the runway environment is required during the VISUAL COMPANY CODED CIRCLING RNV V and the more stringent requirement of maintaining visibility with the landing runway threshold applies to permit descent below circling altitude. Once required visual contact has been established to continue circling, the VISUAL COMPANY CODED CIRCLING RNV V should be activated. Downwind waypoints are provided followed by a Radius to Fix (RF) leg to a point on final approach to the landing runway coincident with the descent angle. The VISUAL COMPANY CODED CIRCLING RNV V is flown visually with AFDS engaged in LNAV/VNAV. Descent point from MDA is calculated by the FMC and is coded to the landing runway threshold.

It is not permitted to route direct to a RF Leg waypoint. It is permitted to route direct to any waypoint preceding the first RF Leg waypoint. Aircraft configuration must be GEAR DOWN, FLAPS 15, match speed at this waypoint.

## Missed Approach Procedure

Standard Circling missed approach procedures apply.

This may require Gear Down, Flap 15 with associated gear warning.

The missed approach procedure is potentially a high workload, critical phase of the procedure, and must be fully briefed.

The Missed Approach Procedure can be split into three categories:

1. Missed approach on final to instrument approach runway.
   a. Ensure MAA is set on the MCP before pressing TOGA.
   b. LNAV is available and the FDs can be followed.
   c. Follow the published missed approach procedure.

Once on crosswind with LNAV, VNAV PTH, SPD INT engaged the PM will select DEP/ARR, and re-select the original instrument approach procedure. This enables the utilisation of LNAV for the missed approach. Do not execute. This is a contingency plan for in the event of a missed approach.

1. Missed approach on crosswind or downwind with LNAV, VNAV PTH, SPD INT engaged:
   a. Turn towards the runway in HDG SEL.
   b. Intercept the missed approach procedure of the initial IAP in HDG SEL.
   c. When time permits the PM will select the appropriate missed approach waypoint to L1 in LEGS and execute. Care must be taken that the turn direction is correct when executing ‘direct-to’ waypoints. LNAV is now available to provide guidance for the missed approach procedure of the initial IAP.

1. If a missed approach is required from final:
   a. Climb ahead to circling MDA in HDG SEL.
   b. Commence a turn on the circling side leaving flaps at 15.
   c. Intercept the standard circling missed approach in HDG SEL.

The missed approach procedure is potentially a high workload, critical phase of the procedure, and must be fully briefed.

## 8.3.0.3.7 Circling/Sidestep Approach

After an IMC-letdown, a circling approach must be made when the aircraft cannot be flown on a straight course from the approach aid to the runway- in-use and the Flight Crew member must use visual guidance to position the aircraft on final approach. Such approaches require an accurate approach pattern and utmost attention:

1. At the circling minimum altitude a terrain clearance of at least 300 ft above the highest spot elevation within 4 nm of the runway system of the aerodrome is provided;
2. The PF shall, after having contact with the runway, proceed at an altitude not lower than the applicable circling minimum. This altitude must be maintained, keeping visual contact with the runway, until the final approach path is being intercepted on base leg or final. During descent, a normal safe final approach path shall be followed taking into account the spot-elevations in the approach path; and
3. A left downwind/base leg shall be flown unless cleared otherwise or when stipulated in the Terminal chart.
4. A circling approach will not be flown at night unless VASI/PAPI or electronic glideslope is available on the landing runway.

FCOM Vol 1, Normal procedures presents the flight profile and duties of each crew member during circling approaches. Additional guidance is provided in the Boeing Flight Crew Training Manual.

The QRH presents the flight profile and duties of each crew member for non normal circle to land procedure.

The landing gate for a circling/sidestep approach is 500 ft AAL. Lateral manoeuvring to align with the runway extended centreline must be completed by 300 ft AAL during all Circling or Side-Step Approaches. Exceptions to this policy, where lateral manoeuvring below 300 ft AAL is permitted, shall be detailed in the relevant OMC AFB (CAT C aerodromes).

## 8.3.0.3.8 NPA and Visual Landing in Marginal Conditions

8.3.0.3.8.1 Before electing to conduct a straight in approach on an NPA runway (rather than extend the track miles to the ILS runway) crews will carefully consider the current weather conditions.

As a general rule when the ceiling is &lt; 1,000 ft and/or the visibility is &lt; 3,000 m it is preferable to opt for the precision approach where the landing can be assured.

8.3.0.3.8.2 Extreme caution must be exercised when landing in reduced runway visibility just above the ground such as shallow fog, smoke, blowing snow or sand.

## 8.3.0.3.9 Instrument Approaches

The procedures to be used for an instrument approach are those published in the Jeppesen manual, or procedures specifically approved for use by Ryanair.

1. An instrument approach procedure is defined as a series of predetermined manoeuvres by reference to flight instruments with specified protection from obstacles from the initial approach fix, or where applicable from the beginning of a defined arrival route to a point from which a landing can be completed and thereafter if a landing is not completed, to a position at which holding or en-route obstacle clearance criteria apply.

2. Initial and intermediate approach procedures are used to identify segments of the approach prior to the commencement of the final approach.

3. In the case of NPA, the final approach is that part of the procedure commencing at the final approach fix or when no FAF is published, when the aircraft is established on the final approach course, and ends at a point in the vicinity of the airfield from which a landing can be made, or a missed approach initiated. The Final Approach Fix (FAF) for an NPA published in the FMC database, with flight path angle displayed in FMC, and when no Final Approach Fix is specified in the procedure, shall be 5/4 nm from the threshold when this approach is conducted in VNAV. When it is not practicable to identify a 5/4 nm point the FAF in VNAV shall be 2,000 ft AAL.

4. In some countries the FAF is a position identified by Radar controller, from which a constant angle approach is conducted. In some countries the distance from the threshold and the altitude corresponding to this point is published in the AIP for that country, e.g. Sweden.

**Note:** Where approaches are approved from fan markers, descents to the relevant altitudes specified are only authorised when both aural and visual marker signals have been received and identified.

Airports in some countries may be officially closed under certain conditions which obviously preclude the use of discretion by the Commander to make an approach.

Following a NPA the Missed Approach shall be executed when reaching the decision altitude or MAP whichever occurs first. The intention is that there shall be no level flight prior to the MDA or MAP, whichever occurs first. The RYR Missed Approach Procedure which requires ALT HOLD to be achieved before the MCP is set to the Missed Approach altitude complies with this requirement.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

The Missed Approach/Circling shall commence at the circling minimum unless the required visual references exist to conduct the circling approach. If the required visual references are lost thereafter, a Missed Approach shall be executed.

Once the go-around has been initiated, resulting in de-stabilization of the approach, no attempt to reverse the decision and land shall be made even if the visual cues suddenly improve.

On non-precision approaches (NDB, SRA, VOR or other approaches without glide path information) it is vitally important that published procedures are rigidly adhered to. The correct descent profile must be followed in order to meet both safety and noise abatement requirements; consequently:

1. Final descent must not be commenced until “distance to go” information, locator or marker beacon, VOR cross-cut, DME or radar information, etc., associated with the approach has been correctly established, or until passing the final descent point in VNAV;
2. Normal descent procedures, correct rate of descent (R.O.D.), selection, shall ensure that the aircraft does not fly below the published approach descent profile and ensure that the aircraft does not reach its MDA at an undesirable distance from the runway or in a hazardous area. A decision to go-around or continue an adjusted descent shall be made on reaching MDA.
3. The preferred LNAV/VNAV NPA procedure ensures lateral and vertical integrity during the approach, deviation scales and various alerts are presented to the crew to indicate system malfunction.
4. Step-down non-precision technique is prohibited after the FAF.
5. Some non-precision approaches may be timed from the final approach descent point. If sufficient visual reference to permit a safe landing has not been established before the MDA then the missed approach procedure will be immediately initiated.
6. The standard operating procedure, duties of each crew member and flight profile for NPAs is presented in FCOM Vol 1, Normal procedures, and additional guidance is provided in the Boeing Flight Crew training manual. The equivalent Non Normal procedures are presented in the QRH.

## 8.3.0.3.9.1 Commencement and Continuation of Approach “Approach Ban”

### “Approach Ban”

CAT.OP.MPA.305

1. If the reported visibility (VIS) or controlling RVR for the runway to be used for landing is less than the applicable minimum, then an instrument approach operation shall not be continued:

a. past a point at which the aeroplane is 1000 ft above the aerodrome elevation; or
b. into the final approach segment (FAS) if the DH or MDH is higher than 1000 ft.

2. If the required visual reference is not established (OMA 8.3.0.3.11), then a missed approach shall be executed at or before the DA/H or the MDA/H.

3. If the required visual reference is not maintained after DA/H or MDA/H, then a go-around shall be executed promptly.

4. Notwithstanding point (1), in the case where no RVR is reported, and the reported VIS is less than the applicable minimum, but the converted meteorological visibility (CMV) is equal or greater than the applicable minimum, then the instrument approach can be continued to the DA/H or MDA/H.

### Application of RVR or VIS Reports

GM1 CAT.OP.MPA.305

1. There is no prohibition on the commencement of an approach based on the reported RVR or VIS. The restriction in OMA 8.3.0.3.9.1(1) applies only if the RVR or VIS is reported and applies to the continuation of the approach past a point where the aircraft is 1000 ft above the aerodrome elevation or in the FAS, as applicable.

2. If a deterioration in the RVR or VIS is reported once the aircraft is below 1000 ft or in the FAS, as applicable, then there is no requirement for the approach to be discontinued. In this situation, the normal visual reference requirements would apply at the DA/H.

3. Where additional RVR information is provided (e.g. midpoint and stop end), this is advisory; such information may be useful to the pilot in order to determine whether there will be sufficient visual reference to control the aircraft during roll-out and taxi. For operations where the aircraft is controlled manually during roll-out, OMA 8.4.1 Table 2 (AMC1 SPA.LVO.100(a)) provides an indication of the RVR that may be required to allow manual lateral control of the aircraft on the runway.

The touchdown zone RVR shall always be controlling. The minimum RVR value for the midpoint shall be $125\mathrm{m}$ and $75\mathrm{m}$ for the stop end.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

# Minimum RVR for Continuation of Approach

AMC1 CAT.OP.MPA.305(a)

1. The touchdown RVR should be the controlling RVR.
2. If the touchdown RVR is not reported, then the midpoint RVR should be the controlling RVR.
3. Where the RVR is not available, CMV should be used except for the purpose of continuation of an approach in LVO in accordance with OMA 8.1.3.4.9 Table 16.

# 8.3.0.3.10 Go-Around (GA)

A go-around shall be initiated if the required visual reference for landing has not been established on reaching the MDA or DA. Go-around policy is to fly the published MAP (missed approach procedure) to the specified altitude, and the go-around is NOT complete until the “after take-off checklist” is completed. Improvised/abbreviated go-arounds, e.g GA from one approach abbreviated into downwind to the opposite runway) are not recommended. Thereafter further options may be considered:

## A Go-Around shall also be made:

1. If any of the Stabilised approach criteria will not be met by the landing gate refer to OMA 8.3.0.3.4.
2. Engine Failure on final approach when IMC and below 1,000 ft AAL.
3. If below 1,000 ft AAL, any significant departure from the normal approach path occurs and corrective action is not immediately effective.
4. If the approach aid in use or the respective aircraft installation is suspected to be malfunctioning, unless VMC.
5. Upon instruction of ATC; or
6. At the discretion of the Captain.
7. Flap load relief activation after the applicable landing gate – 1,000 ft AAL IMC, 500 ft AAL VMC.
8. TOO LOW GEAR, TOO LOW FLAP in normal operations.
9. Following Non-Routine RAAS/ORW Aural alerts requiring a Go-Around:

a. 737-NG RAAS

i. “UNSTABLE, UNSTABLE”
ii. “CAUTION TAXIWAY, CAUTION TAXIWAY”

page 8–230

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR
PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

iii. “LONG LANDING, LONG LANDING”

A. When operating to a short\* or performance limited\*\* runway, a go-around is mandatory. Refer to OMC AFB for short runway operational guidance.

B. When alert observed during landing after reverse thrust initiation, the pilot flying shall utilise increased levels of reverse thrust and braking, as appropriate.

- Short runway: runway LDA &lt; 1800 m.

\*\* Performance limited: calculated enroute landing performance requires MAX AUTO or MAX MANUAL braking selections to achieve LDR ≤ LDA.

b. 737-8200 RAAS/ORW

i. “OVERRUN, GO AROUND”

All go-arounds shall be executed according to the procedures laid down for the aircraft. Once the decision has been made to go-around during final approach no decision to abandon this go-around shall be taken.

An ASR shall be filed for all go-arounds initiated below 1,000 ft AAL. It is not necessary to file ASR for a go-around above 1,000 ft AAL unless there are additional reportable circumstances.

## Weather Related Missed Approaches

If a missed approach has been executed for meteorological reasons, another approach shall only be commenced if the Commander has reason to believe that a second approach will lead to a successful landing.

In the event that two weather related missed approaches have been carried out, The Commander shall divert to his alternate unless he receives weather information which indicates that an improvement to double the previously given weather has occurred. In this context, visibility would need to be increased by a factor of 2 and ceiling to twice the previously reported height.

The Company is aware that weather conditions are seldom stable and that a particular flight may encounter below limits weather where other flights approaching at the same time are able to land. Any economic penalty involved in complying this instruction is acceptable to the Company.

Our primary operations objective is to conduct our air operations activity safely.

8.3.0.3.10.1 It is not permitted to conduct a third approach following two abandoned approaches due to windshear.

## 8.3.0.3.11 Visual References for Instrument Approach Operations

AMC1 CAT.OP.MPA.305(c)

The change-over from flying on instruments to visual flying is the most difficult phase of the approach and needs good co-ordination and liaison between both Flight Crew members.

For instrument approach operations Type A and CAT I instrument approach operations Type B, at least one of the visual references specified below should be distinctly visible and identifiable to the pilot at the MDA/H or the DA/H:

1. Elements of the approach lighting system;
2. The threshold;
3. The threshold markings;
4. The threshold lights;
5. The threshold identification lights;
6. The visual glide slope indicator;
7. The TDZ or TDZ markings;
8. The TDZ lights;
9. The runway edge lights; or
10. Other visual references specified in the operations manual.

**Note:** \*OMA 8.3.0.3.5,

Night landings from a NPA to a runway less than 2000 m LDA without PAPI/VASI require NPFO approval.

Night landings from a circling approach require a PAPI/VASI.

This visual reference is required to allow Flight Crew member assessment of aircraft position and rate of change of position in relation to the desired flight path. An approach may not be continued below the DA/MDA unless the minimum visual reference required has been established as sufficient to continue visually, i.e. as at least equal to or better than the specified visibility minimum for the procedure being used.

## 8.3.0.3.11.1 Visual Descent After IMC Final Approach (CAT I, NPA)

The Flight Crew procedures and work distribution for the IMC approach are outlined in FCOM Vol 1, Normal procedures.

Care is needed to stay in the proper approach path by maintaining the stabilised aircraft condition and continuously monitoring the flight progress on flight instruments. Specifically:

1. Having directed his attention outside the cockpit during the last 100 feet above DA/MDA in order to pick-up and identify the approach lights or other visual reference, the PM must then redirect his scan on the flight instruments – in particular on deviations from glide slope and localiser, on aircraft pitch and roll, and on rate of descent;
2. The most common fault after transferring to visual flight in poor visibility is to lower the aircraft nose/pitch down and drop below the glide path (“duck under”). The PM must be expected to identify this deviation from the glide slope and “call” immediately; and
3. The illusion of pitch-up in rapidly decreasing visibility exacerbates this “duck under” tendency and must be monitored and guarded against by reference back to the flight instruments.

If visual reference is lost, an immediate go-around is mandatory as a matter of urgency:

1. In such circumstances, the instrument scan maintained by the PM becomes a vital monitoring function, with intervention if a critical condition should develop, until the PF's flight instrument scan has been re-established; and
2. The landing gear must not be retracted until a positive rate of climb is established. The go-around shall be continued even when an inadvertent touchdown on the runway occurs.

Under low visibility conditions near the minimum, dimming of the approach/runway lights shall not be requested as it results in reduced visual range for the lights:

1. Flight Crew members shall be aware of the intensity at which the approach/runway lights are operating, and to what intensity they will be reduced to if dimming is requested. This is particularly important if the approach and runway lights are on separate dimming circuits at certain airports, and Flight Crew members shall be familiar with the procedure for requesting individual dimming so as to avoid losing runway visibility if only the approach lights need to be dimmed.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

2. Though very unusual in Europe some approach/runway lighting systems (Pilot Controlled Lighting – PCL, e.g. Dinard) may be dimmed from the cockpit by microphone “keying”.

The utmost caution is necessary in shallow fog conditions where the lighting is clearly visible from higher altitudes. However, in the top of the ground fog the visibility may suddenly drop to a very low value which then increases rather slowly on descending further.

There are a range of similar cases of such conditions in which the height of the layer of maximum fog density can vary from a few feet above the runway up to DA or higher which need to be carefully assessed in deciding how they should be handled.

Sequence flashing lights (SFL's) incorporated into some approach lighting systems are good for establishing visual contact with the full approach light system. However, by themselves, SFL’s do not provide sufficient visual reference to assess aircraft position nor to allow correction of a lateral centre line correction as the roll reference is entirely missing.

Crews shall be familiar with the Ryanair Low Visibility operations document, which is part of the Ryanair SOP for low Visibility Operations.

## De-tuning ILS

Crews shall not de-tune the ILS frequency during instrument approaches, including after becoming visual, unless required by the discontinued approach procedure or an ATC requested orbit.

## 8.3.0.3.12 Landing

## 8.3.0.3.12.1 “Land After” Clearances

"Land After" clearances are authorised for Ryanair flight operations. Usually ATC will advise you prior to "MINIMUMS" to expect a late landing clearance. This is your cue to expect the actual landing clearance after "MINIMUMS". Execution of this approved clearance requires completion of the "LANDING LIGHTS" item on the Landing Checklist after the "MINIMUMS" call. The issuance of a LAND AFTER clearance authorises the aircraft to “cross the runway threshold” (OMC, ATC 7.10)

Crews shall be aware that Go-Around may be required after acceptance of the "land after" clearance up to a baulked landing.

## 8.3.0.3.12.1.1 Loss of Communications on Approach

1. If communications are lost with ATC on approach and clearance to land has not been received, the crew shall;
   a. Check frequency selection and volume controls.
   b. If frequency and volume are correctly set and no reply received, revert to previous frequency.
   c. If still unable to establish communications attempt to establish communications on 121.5.
   d. If no radio contact established or no signal received from the tower to land a go around shall be accomplished.

## 8.3.0.3.12.2 Threshold Crossing Altitude/Speed

CAT.OP.MPA.310

Ryanair has established operational procedures designed to ensure that an aeroplane conducting 3D instrument approach operations crosses the threshold of the runway by a safe margin, with the aeroplane in the landing configuration and attitude.

The standard prerequisites for a safe landing require the runway threshold to be crossed at the correct height and airspeed, and to land on the centreline within the prescribed touchdown area.

A stabilised approach is the means by which this effected.

The tower reported wind (10 m AGL) is the information on which Flight Crew member decisions are predicated.

Excess threshold height, speed and touchdown float will significantly increase landing/stopping distance. Specifically:

1. The target threshold height for all aircraft is 50 feet;
2. The maximum allowable target airspeed over the threshold is $V_{REF} + 15$ kt for the Boeing 737; and
3. The fly speed which contains increments for wind speed and gust shall be flown accurately on the final approach and the additional speed increments reduced as the threshold is crossed so as to land at $V_{REF}$ plus gust increment. For a tail wind the fly speed shall be $V_{REF} + 5$ kt to touchdown at $V_{REF}$.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

4. With a 3° approach glide path and a target runway threshold height of 50 ft the touchdown aiming point can be identified from the standard runway markings.

Electronic and visual glide slopes may intersect the runway surface beyond the 1,000 foot point and some runways may have a displaced threshold. These shall be taken into account when determining the target touchdown point.

A shallower approach path of less than 3° and a down slope on the runway make an accurate touchdown progressively more difficult.

If for any reason the approach path is not maintained and it is likely that the touchdown will occur too short or too far beyond the touchdown area, or the threshold speed is excessive then a go-around must be initiated.

The Boeing Flight Crew Training Manual provides detailed guidance for the landing and roll out phase of flight.

1. The calculation of crosswind components is presented in the FCOM Vol 1, Normal procedures.
2. The recommended Boeing procedures for crosswind landings is presented in the FCTM where four options are given.

## 8.3.0.3.12.3 Runway Turn-off

Runway turn-offs are built to different designs. Flight Crew members will be prudent about aircraft turn-off speed until they are familiar with the turn-offs on specific runways:

1. Turn-offs at high speed can generate high side loads on the landing gear and increase tyre wear.
2. Turning forces on the aircraft are higher than experienced on the runway and special care needs to be taken where there is a possibility of ice or frost on the turn-offs and taxiways even though none exists on the runway.
3. Where ice or frost is present on runways, turn-offs or taxiways the aircraft shall be brought to a virtual standstill on the runway before turning-off to allow careful assessment of the surface conditions.

## 8.3.0.3.12.4 Reverse Thrust Policy

### a. All Landings, a Serviceable Aircraft, No Applicable NNCs

The PF, with the agreement of the Commander shall decide on the Landing Flap, Autobrake and Reverse Thrust settings having consulted the QRH, PI section. When a OFP note or Airfield Brief specifies a particular landing configuration and/or technique these configurations/settings shall be used.

A minimum of Idle Reverse Thrust shall be used for all landings along with Autobrake 0-1-2-3-Max as appropriate. Where the use of higher Autobrake brake settings causes turnaround brake cooling issues, Reverse Thrust settings greater than idle shall be used.

Idle Reverse Thrust therefore shall be used in normal flight operations with RWYCC 5 (GOOD) or better as follows:

- On last flight of the day at all Ryanair bases.
- On the last flight of a series of flights when there is at least a 60 minute turnaround.
- Last landing prior to aircraft going out of service.
- Any landing when the turnaround schedule will not be adversely affected by the sole use of Autobrake.

The Commander is encouraged to use a combination of Flap/REV THR/AUTO BRK in order to exit the runway at a particular exit to minimise the taxi time to stand.

**Note:** The operational objective is to minimise runway occupancy times consistent with safety of operations.

### b. Safety

Our primary operations objective is to conduct our air transport activities safely. Use of idle reverse thrust as described in this section is not considered detrimental to safety.

When required, up to and including MAX REV THR may be used.

## 8.3.0.3.13 Taxi-in

Taxiing comprises a relatively large proportion of engine running time for a short haul operation. While the Boeing 737 is normally taxied on both engines, the shutting down of an engine for taxi to the ramp/gate is acceptable and will generate a considerable fuel saving.

Care must be taken to avoid excessive jet blast when taxiing on one engine, particularly if the aircraft has had to stop or when turning at low speed.

It is considered best practise and crews shall have the airport map available for all operations on the ground at all times.

At many airports “Follow Me” vehicles are provided. Otherwise follow ATC instructions or ask for instructions if any doubt exists.

## Arrival on Stand (Flight Deck)

Some stands require either electronic signalling or marshalling. It is not permitted to self position onto stands for which either electronic guidance or marshalling is required.

Some stands require neither. It is permitted to self position onto stands which do not require electronic guidance or marshalling.

Acknowledge all hand signals. Be aware that the “cut-throat” signal to shut down engines may be an urgent instruction rather than the last signal in a routine sequence of marshalling. If this is the case, do so even if you will be temporarily without AC power.

Crews shall not turn off the anti-collision light until N2 has fallen below 20% on both engines.

## Parking

## Stand Alignment and Stopping Guidance Systems

Stand alignment and stopping guidance systems using visual reference geometry are based on the Commanders/left seat eye position. When parking on a stand using such a system, the Flight Crew member operating from the left seat is required to taxi the aircraft, as only he can judge the correct positioning of the aircraft using these systems.

When clearances are judged not to be adequate marshalling assistance must be requested.

On non-guidance system stands a marshalling service is normally provided. However, a Flight Crew member may manoeuvre onto a self-parking stand at the Commanders discretion.

In either case, if there is any doubt about safety and clearance from an obstruction the Commander must stop short until the obstruction is removed, a marshaller is available, or the aircraft is towed onto the stand.

When the aircraft has reached its final parking position and the parking brake has been confirmed set, the flight crew shall provide the brakes set signal, which will be acknowledged by the ground crew.

## Use of Parking Brake

The pilot monitoring (PM) will accomplish the exterior inspection at the earliest opportunity following completion of the shutdown procedure/checks (full or transit). The PM will connect the RFS headset and confirm with the pilot flying (PF) that the chocks are in-place/properly fitted and that all ground servicing equipment is properly in position.

The PF will then release the parking brake with caution and advise the PM when the parking brake is fully released. The PF and PM must monitor aircraft movement and pay particular attention during the walk around to ensure servicing equipment and aircraft (including air-stairs) has not been displaced and/or damaged. The confirmation of the proper placement of chocks/servicing equipment and release of the parking brake must be accomplished at the earliest opportunity to ensure optimum brake cooling. Report any abnormalities and damaged observed to the pilot flying, base engineers, and/or MAINTROL as appropriate.

Prior to securing the aircraft, notify ENG/MAINTROL if the aircraft will be left in a non-standard configuration (e.g., flaps not fully retracted).

## 8.3.0.3.14 Use of Ground Power

It is Ryanair policy to maximise the use of Ground Power when available for all turnarounds. Ideally the APU shall be started just prior to closing the L1 door. Without prejudice to Airport bylaws/Directors Notices APU is not normally required between temperatures of 5°C and 25°C and may be used for air-conditioning outside these temperature ranges during turnarounds.

## 8.3.0.3.15 Aircraft Changes, Crew Changes

The aircraft you are leaving shall be secured as described in the base airfield brief. This ensures regulations in that particular country/base are complied with.

The F/O on the inbound aircraft shall complete the shutdown checklist and immediately proceed to the outbound aircraft and commence pre-flight preparations.

The Captain shall complete the Technical Log, secure the aircraft, and proceed to the outbound aircraft.

When the outbound crew awaits your aircraft it is sufficient for the off going crew to vacate their aircraft having completed the shutdown checks and the Technical Log. The on coming crew shall complete a full pre-flight preparation.

## Aircraft Changes

Planned aircraft changes are identified to operating crew in the EFL App.

The Electronic Flight Log/Paper Voyage report/Journey Log will have both registrations indicated where an aircraft change is planned.

Exercise caution with aircraft changes. The registration of the aircraft must be correctly reflected on the OFP. In the case of an unscheduled aircraft change the flight may not be commenced until a new OFP has been generated.

## Boarding of Passengers During Aircraft or Crew Changes

Boarding of passengers on an aircraft that will have a crew change post-boarding is permitted subject to the following:

- The off-going crew shall remain on the aircraft and preserve the integrity of the Security Check/Search until the relieving crew arrives.
- The off-going Commander or SCCM shall brief the ongoing Commander or SCCM on the aircraft that passengers have been boarded and Security Check/Search has been completed on the aircraft in accordance with Ryanair procedures.

and:

For Security Checks:

- Either the off-going Commander or the ongoing Commander shall sign on the prescribed form that the Security Checks have been completed.

For Security Search:

- The off-going Commander shall sign on the prescribed form that the Security Search has been completed.

Commanders shall request a copy of the RAMP inspector’s report if available, regardless of findings. This is to maintain the Ryanair Group database of reports and if findings are made to enable Ryanair Group to appeal such findings it considers unfair or inaccurate.

Refer to OFP CO-NOTAM for guidance.

1. What type of RAMP inspection is this (SAFA, SACA, SANA, etc.)?
2. What are the inspectors’ names and identification numbers?

4. For all technical findings, call MAINTROL before departure.

## 8.3.0.5 Post Flight Duties

A “Transit Checklist” or full Shut-down Checklist shall be completed as appropriate after each sector.

Upon leaving the aircraft at the end of a duty, at a base/airport where there is no engineer present, the Aircraft commander will perform a walk around inspection.

This inspection is to check for evidence of any defect that will affect the serviceability of the aircraft. In particular the aircraft commander will be checking for evidence of Bird strike and/or tyre damage.

Should the commander discover any defect or evidence of bird strike this will be reported to Maintrol on the telephone numbers listed in the Technical Log, but shall not leave the aircraft until maintrol has been contacted and the defect entered in the Technical Log.

The aircraft will be secured in accordance with current RYR security policies. Guidance for cold weather operations are provided in FCOM Vol 1.

Prior to securing the aircraft, notify ENG/MAINTROL if the aircraft will be left in a non-standard configuration (e.g., flaps not fully retracted).

The Commander will ensure that the crew adhere to all Customs clearance.

The Electronic Flight Log/Paper Voyage Report/Journey Log, Electronic Voyage Report/Journey Log (Crewlink) and associated electronic documentation, Captain’s Special Report, ASR and any other necessary reports, (see Chapter 11) shall be Completed, normally as part of 30 min post flight duty.

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

These reports together with the LID/Loadsheet and other specified flight documentation must be filed at the Base of Operations in accordance with Ryanair procedures below and in Chapter 11:

Electronic Flight Log: Submitted and Verified post flight.

## 8.3.0.5.1 Ryanair Connect App/Crewlink

The Ryanair Connect App is the primary source for roster information. The Roster App within Ryanair Connect provides ‘live’ duty details for the active week and displays rostered duty details for 3 weeks following the active week.

All operating crew are required to check-in via EFL at commencement of duty.

Crewlink reverts to back up as an electronic communication and reporting system. It is hosted electronically and accessible on base computers at workstations via Ryanair Connect “CHECKIN/CHECKOUT”. If EFL is unavailable, all operating crew are required to sign in and log out on Crewlink at commencement and completion of duty. This option exists for 7 days post flight.

Where crew are unable to submit Electronic Flight Log data, the Commander is responsible to ensure that the data fields presented in Crewlink data input (effectively electronic voyage report) are completed correctly as part of post flight duties. If this is not possible this option remains for seven days to enable completion of data input. The same accuracy criteria as the Electronic Flight Log/Paper Voyage report applies. If the data input is not completed by this time, it causes disruption of Company account reconciliations, a non-compliance report is generated, and non-compliance file notes will be notified to Commanders.

Crew rosters are accessible via the Roster App. It is compulsory for crew members, on Friday, to check their roster for the subsequent week. This is because this is final notification of your actual duty plan for the following week. If there are changes after the Friday you will receive a notification from crew control via the Roster App, SMS, Email, Crewlink or phone.

There are ancillary functions available which are not flight operations functions in the context of this manual.

## 8.3.1 VFR/IFR Policy

CAT.OP.MPA.100

All flights shall be operated in accordance with Instrument Flight Rules. A flight plan shall be submitted prior to operating. Air Traffic Services (ATS) appropriate to the airspace and the applicable rules of the air shall be used for all flights

page 8–242

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

whenever available. In-flight operational instructions involving a change to the ATS flight plan, when practicable, shall be coordinated with the appropriate ATS unit before transmission to an aircraft.

Commanders shall not cancel an IFR flight plan, or proceed VFR at any stage of a flight.

Aircraft operating under IFR may be cleared to conduct part of a flight in ‘VMC’, e.g. a visual approach. Such a clearance does not cancel the IFR plan, but for the duration of such a clearance, collision avoidance, terrain and obstacle clearance is the responsibility of the aircrew. The aircrew must be in visual contact with the runway to conduct a visual approach.

The NPFO may approve a portion of a flight under VFR. For CAT operations this is typically a departure from a location requiring the initial part of the departure to be flown VFR before resuming IFR. Occasionally a VFR departure may be authorised because the infrastructure to support a SID is temporarily unserviceable.

Clearances to maintain ‘own separation in VMC’ may be accepted by the Commander for limited portions of climb, descent or approach under the following conditions:

- Relevant traffic can be clearly identified and kept in sight.
- It is assured that sufficient separation will exist at all times.
- VMC can be maintained.
- ATC has coordinated the clearance with both aircraft.

When flights are conducted within class F and G airspace crews shall refer to the ‘Air Traffic Services Outside Controlled Airspace’ in the Jeppesen General Airway Manual &gt; ICAO Rules of the Air &gt; Rules Applicable to IFR Flights Outside Controlled Airspace and request the highest service available. Additional information may be available on the Airway Manual sections of the country overflown.
