## 8.3.2 Navigation Procedures

### 8.3.2.1 ‘Conventional Navigation’

All operating crews are trained to navigate using ‘conventional navigation’. Any navaid being used shall be identified (IDENT) and proper indication verified. ‘Conventional navigation’ is rarely used en-route as flight plans are RNAV5. The convention for being unable to remain RNAV5 is radar vectors not least because any ground based NAVAID probably has no relevance to your current filed en-route routing and any deviation towards a ground based NAVAID could compromise separation without an ATC clearance.

## Procedures for Aircraft Equipped with Flight Management Systems (FMS)

All Ryanair Boeing 737 aircraft are equipped with dual FMS. The procedures for its operation and navigation, including independent cross check of keyboard entries utilising the FMS are detailed in FCOM Vol 1, Normal procedures, Supplemental procedures and system description in Vol 2.

Additional procedures for operations in NAT HLA are described in the ICAO NAT Doc 007 (North Atlantic Operations and Airspace) and the ‘Oceanic Guide and Checklist’. All charted procedures used shall be verified in the FMC against the Jeppesen charts and backed up with ground-based navigation aids when required. No charted procedure shall be flown without the requisite chart being available on the flight deck.

All FMC navigation alerts shall be assessed for their operational significance depending on the navigation procedure in use and the considerations indicated in FCOM Vol 2 shall be applied.

The Area of Operations for Ryanair is indicated on the Operations Specifications Certificate which is available on the EFB.

### 8.3.2.2 Performance Based Navigation (PBN) excl RNP(AR)

CAT.OP.MPA.126

AMC1 CAT.OP.MPA.126, GM1 CAT.OP.MPA.126

Ryanair PBN navigation specifications are stated in the AFM and all PBN operations are conducted in accordance with these navigation specifications.

For both, RNP X and RNAV X designations, the ‘X’ (where stated) refers to the lateral navigation accuracy (total system error) in NM, which is expected to be achieved at least 95 % of the flight time by the population of aircraft operating within the airspace, route or procedure. For RNP APCH and A-RNP, the lateral navigation accuracy depends on the segment.

## 8.3.2.2.2 Monitoring and Verification

AMC2 CAT.OP.MPA.126

## Performance-based Navigation

1. Pre-flight and general considerations

a. At navigation system initialisation, the flight crew confirms that the navigation database is current and verifies that the aircraft position has been entered correctly.

b. The active flight plan is checked including by comparing and confirming the charted procedures to be flown with the FMC and ND. Verify the departing runway and the waypoint sequence. Correct waypoint sequencing and the resultant ‘picture’ on the ND identifies reasonableness of track angles and distances. Verify any altitude or speed constraints and note which waypoints are flyby and which are fly-over. Where relevant, the RF leg arc radii should be confirmed.

c. Check that the navigation aids critical to the operation of the intended PBN procedure are available, if any.

d. Identify and confirm the navigation aids that should be excluded from the operation, if any.

e. An arrival, approach or departure procedure should not be used if the validity of the procedure in the navigation database has expired.

f. Verify that the navigation systems required for the intended operation are operational.

g. It is a requirement of RNP APCH operations that prior to dispatch, a prediction of the number of satellites available within the constellation must be carried out. The RAIM check is applicable to 15 min before Estimated Time of Arrival (ETA) until 15 min after ETA. In the event of a predicted outage of more than five (5) minutes, the flight planning should be revised (e.g. delaying the departure or planning a different approach procedure).

h. When intending to use PBN, the pilot-in-command should only select an aerodrome as a destination alternate aerodrome only if an instrument approach procedure that does not rely on GNSS is available either at that aerodrome or at the destination aerodrome.

**Note:** It is required to conduct a RAIM check for destination and alternate airports at the planning stage, however, if as a result of emergency or en-route diversion, an RNP approach may be

page 8-248

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR
PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

carried out at an airport that was not considered at the planning stage as any loss of RNP will be highlighted by the Aircraft systems. MEL restrictions for RNP APCH apply.

## Flight Planning

For departures from an aerodrome which is served only with an APV BARO-LNAV/VNAV approach, a take-off alternate with NPA or CAT I minima is required. Crews intending to conduct operations on RNP APCH procedures shall ensure the aircraft is equipped with a dual FMC. Crews shall verify appropriate flight plan suffixes. The flight plan suffix R in item 10a S1 or S2 in item 18 indicates RNAV/RNP APCH capability.

The FMC navigation database must be current and must include the appropriate procedures. For dispatch with an expired navigation database Flight Ops must be contacted in advance to make required changes to the operational flight plan.

The instrument approach chart will clearly identify the RNP APCH operation as RNAV(GNSS), RNAV(GPS) or equivalent (e.g.: RNAV(GNSS) RWY 27,...). The RNP APCH procedures which may be used for the intended flight (including alternates aerodromes) must be selectable from a valid FMC navigation database.

Ryanair Ops and flight crews must take account of any NOTAMs or briefing material that could adversely affect the aircraft system operation, or the availability or suitability of the procedures at the airport of landing, or any alternate airport.

If the missed approach procedures are based on conventional means (VOR, NDB), the appropriate airborne equipment required to fly this procedure must be installed in the aircraft and must be operational. The associated ground based navaids must also be operational.

If the missed approach procedure is based on RNAV (no conventional or dead reckoning missed approach available), the appropriate airborne equipment required to fly this procedure must be available and serviceable on board the aircraft. The contingency procedure shall be briefed in advance of conducting the approach to address possible failure of RNAV system components.

## 2. Departure

a. Prior to commencing a take-off on a PBN procedure, check that the indicated aircraft position is consistent with the actual aircraft position at the start of the take-off roll, including verifying the RWY designator on the ND or RAAS.

b. Where GNSS is used, the signal must be acquired before the take-off roll commences.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

c. Ryanair aircraft have automatic position updating including when TOGA is selected.

3. Arrival and Approach Preparation

Use DALTA and the double brief to check the procedure as loaded in the FMC. Prior to commencing the approach operation (before the IAF), the flight crew must verify the correctness of the loaded procedure by comparison with the appropriate approach charts.

This check includes:

a. the waypoint sequence;
b. reasonableness of the tracks and distances of the approach legs and the accuracy of the inbound course; and
c. the vertical path angle, if applicable.
d. any published altitude and speed constraints will be observed.

Verify aircraft is dual FMC equipped. Select or verify Approach RNP 0.3 in the FMC.

The crew must verify during the approach that a GNSS sensor is used for position computation. This is achieved operationally by verifying that the 'UNABLE REQ'D NAV PERF –RNP’ message is not displayed.

4. Altimetry settings for RNP APCH operations using Baro VNAV

a. Barometric settings

i. Set and confirm the correct altimeter setting and check that the two altimeters provide altitude values that do not differ more than 100 ft at or before the final approach fix (FAF).

ii. Fly the procedure with:

A. a current local altimeter setting source available — a remote or regional altimeter setting source must not be used; and
B. the QNH set on the aircraft’s altimeters.

b. Temperature compensation

i. For RNP APCH operations to LNAV/VNAV minima using Baro VNAV:

A. Do not commence the approach when the aerodrome temperature is outside the promulgated aerodrome temperature limits for the procedure;
B. when the temperature is within promulgated limits, do not make compensation to the altitude at the FAF;

page 8–250

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

C. since only the final approach segment is protected by the promulgated aerodrome temperature limits, make cold weather altitude corrections as appropriate in other phases of flight.

ii. For RNP APCH operations to LNAV minima, make cold weather temperature corrections to all published altitudes and minima at or under the temperature corrected MSA, in particular on any step-down fix.

5. Sensor and lateral navigation accuracy selection

a. Verify, prior to approach, that the GNSS sensor is used for position computation.

b. Confirm that the indicated RNP value is appropriate for the PBN operation.

## 8.3.2.2.3 Management of the Navigation Database

AMC3 CAT.OP.MPA.126

1. For RNAV 1, RNAV 2, RNP 1, RNP 2, RNP4, and RNP APCH, the flight crew should neither insert nor modify waypoints by manual entry into a procedure (departure, arrival or approach) that has been retrieved from the database. User-defined data may be entered and used for waypoint altitude/speed constraints on a procedure where said constraints are not included in the navigation database coding.

Note: It is permissible to edit altitude restrictions on arrivals XXXX replaced with XXXA in order to maintain a SAp/CDA approach prior to the FAF.

2. For RNP 4 operations, the flight crew shall not modify waypoints that have been retrieved from the database. User-defined data (e.g. for flex-track routes) may be entered and used.

3. The lateral and vertical definition of the flight path between the FAF and the missed approach point (MAPt) retrieved from the database shall not be revised by the flight crew.

The navigation data products used by Ryanair are compliant with these requirements and copies of the relevant LoAs are maintained and available for inspection in Ryanair.

## 8.3.2.2.4 Displays and Automation

AMC4 CAT.OP.MPA.126

**Equipment to be Used:**

Aircraft must be equipped with dual FMC, at least 1 flight director must be available and used. Use of autopilot when available is recommended.

VNAV is the recommended vertical mode for the initial and intermediate approach segments. Where “LNAV” minima is published the crew may use VNAV (Ref; Instrument Approaches using VNAV, FCOM Vol 1 and QRH MAN) or V/S (Ref; Instrument approaches using V/S, QRH MAN) on the final approach segment. VNAV is the preferred vertical AFDS mode. Where

page 8–252

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

"LNAV/VNAV" minima are published the crew must use LNAV and VNAV on the final approach segment. (Ref; Instrument approaches using VNAV, FCOM Vol 1 and QRH MAN.)

1. For RNAV 1, RNP 1, and RNP APCH operations, use Navigation Performance Scale (as installed) or Progress page for lateral deviation indication, and where available, flight director and/or autopilot in lateral navigation mode.

2. The appropriate displays should be selected so that the following information can be monitored:

a. the computed desired path;

b. aircraft position relative to the lateral path (cross-track deviation) for FTE monitoring;

c. aircraft position relative to the vertical path (for a 3D operation).

3. Lateral deviation indicator scaling when available must be suitable for the navigation accuracy associated with the various segments of the procedure.

4. Maintain procedure centrelines unless authorised to deviate by air traffic control (ATC) or demanded by emergency conditions.

5. Cross-track error/deviation (the difference between the area-navigation-system-computed path and the aircraft-computed position) is limited to $\pm .5$ x RNAV/RNP value associated with the procedure. Brief deviations from this standard (e.g. overshoots or undershoots during and immediately after turns) up to a maximum of 1 x RNAV/RNP value are allowable.

6. For a 3D (LNAV/VNAV) approach operation, a vertical deviation indicator must be used and where required by AFM limitations, a flight director or autopilot in vertical navigation mode.

7. Deviations below the vertical path should not exceed 75 ft at any time, (or half-scale deflection where angular deviation is indicated), and not more than 75 ft above the vertical profile, (or half-scale deflection where angular deviation is indicated), at or below 1000 ft above aerodrome level. Execute a missed approach if the vertical deviation exceeds this criterion, unless the visual references required to continue the approach are in sight.

## 8.3.2.2.5 Vectoring and Positioning

AMCS CAT.OP.MPA.126

The manual entry of coordinates into the FMC by the flight crew for operation within the terminal area is not permitted.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

ATC tactical interventions in the terminal area may include radar headings, ‘direct to’ clearances which bypass the initial legs of an approach procedure, interceptions of an initial or intermediate segments of an approach procedure or the insertion of additional waypoints loaded from the database. In complying with ATC instructions, the flight crew must be aware of the implications for the navigation system.

‘Direct to’ clearances may be accepted to the IF provided that it is clear to the flight crew that the aircraft will be established on the final approach track at least 2 NM before the FAF.

‘Direct to’ clearance to the FAF is not acceptable. Modifying the procedure to intercept the final approach track prior to the FAF is acceptable for radar-vectored arrivals or otherwise only with ATC approval. e.g. extending the centerline from the FAF/FAP, or a waypoint further out, to intercept the final approach course prior to the FAF/FAP for radar vectored arrivals.

The final approach trajectory must be intercepted no later than the FAF in order for the aircraft to be correctly established on the final approach track before starting the descent (to ensure terrain and obstacle clearance).

The lateral and vertical (for APV BARO-LNAV/VNAV operation) definition of the flight path between the FAF and the Missed Approach Point (MAPt) must not be revised by the flight crew under any circumstances.

1. ‘Direct to’ clearances to a fix that immediately precede an RF leg are not permitted.
2. For parallel offset operations en-route, transitions to and from the offset track should maintain an intercept angle of no more than 45° unless specified otherwise by ATC.

## 8.3.2.2.5.1 Alerting and Abort

AMC6 CAT.OP.MPA.126

1. Unless the flight crew has sufficient visual reference to continue the approach operation to a safe landing, an RNP APCH operation must be discontinued if:

a. navigation system failure is enunciated (e.g. warning flag);
b. lateral or vertical deviations exceed the tolerances;
c. loss of the on-board monitoring and alerting system.

2. Discontinuing the approach operation may not be necessary for a multi-sensor navigation system that includes demonstrated RNP capability without GNSS in accordance with the AFM. In this case the ANP/RNP continues to be indicated to the crew after the loss of GNSS.

page 8-254

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR
PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

3. Where vertical guidance is lost while the aircraft is still above 1000 ft AGL, the flight crew may decide to continue the approach to LNAV minima, when supported by the navigation system.

## 8.3.2.2.6 Contingency Procedures

AMC7 CAT.OP.MPA.126

1. The flight crew will make the necessary preparation to revert to a conventional arrival procedure where appropriate. If there is no conventional procedure expect radar vectors on notifying ATC of the loss of GNSS capability. The following conditions are considered in the presentation of minimum equipment required for conduct of PBN approaches, (LNAV and LNAV/VNAV):

a. failure of the navigation system components including navigation sensors, and a failure affecting flight technical error (e.g. failures of the flight director or autopilot);
b. multiple system failures affecting aircraft performance;
c. coasting on inertial sensors beyond a specified time limit; and
d. RAIM (or equivalent) alert or loss of integrity function.

2. In the event of loss of PBN capability, apply the contingency procedures indicated and navigate using an alternative means of navigation.

3. Notify ATC of any problem affecting PBN capability.

4. In the event of communication failure, the flight crew should continue with the operation in accordance with published lost communication procedures.

## RNP APCH Operations, Contingency Procedures

The flight crew must notify ATC of any loss of the RNAV and/or RNP APCH capability, together with the proposed course of action as soon as possible. The loss of RNAV and/or RNP APCH capability includes any failure or event causing the aircraft to no longer satisfy the RNAV and/or RNP APCH requirements of the procedure. A clearance for a non-RNP approach must be requested. If no other procedure is available and acceptable and if below MSA/MOCA climb to a safe altitude along the prescribed track or be radar vectored.

In the event of communications failure, the flight crew shall continue with the procedure in accordance with lost communication procedures.

The procedure must be discontinued and a Go-around/missed approach is required if the ‘UNABLE REQ’D NAV PERF-RNP, FMC DISAGREE’, ‘GPS INVALID’ FMC Amber alert or VERIFY POSITION alert is displayed unless suitable visual reference is established and maintained.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

The missed approach must be flown in accordance with the published procedure.

Use of the FMC during the missed approach is acceptable provided:

1. The FMC is operational.
2. The whole procedure (including the missed approach) is loaded from the navigation data base.

In the event of failure of one FMC during a procedure where two systems are necessary, the crew shall abort the procedure if the failure occurs before the FAF but may continue the approach if the failure occurs after FAF.

## 8.3.2.3 GLS (Ground Based Augmentation System) Landing System

GLS is a Type B, 3D approach and is a Category 1 (CAT I) Precision Approach

The GLS consists of three major elements:

- The satellite sub-system (space based constellation), which provides both the aircraft GNSS receiver and the ground GBAS station with ranging information;
- The ground station sub-system, which monitors the satellite signals and calculates/broadcasts pseudo-range corrections, integrity parameters, various locally relevant data such as atmospheric data and Final Approach Segments (FAS) defining the path in space to enable Precision Approach (PA) operations;
- The aircraft sub-system, which receives both the satellite signals and the GBAS signals, supplying navigation output/guidance to the flight instruments and to the autopilot.
- GLS equipped aircraft are certified to conduct GLS operations using GNSS (GPS) signals for approach guidance.

## 8.3.2.3.1 Augmentation

Augmentation refers to the corrections to satellite signals made by the GBAS ground station, called pseudo-range corrections. The GBAS calculates pseudo-range corrections for all the satellites that meet the specified in-view criteria for both the aircraft and GBAS ground station. These corrections are applied to the GLS approach which is broadcast to the aircraft.

page 8–256

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## 8.3.2.3.2 Operations using GLS

### GBAS Ground Station

Each runway approach direction requires the definition of a final approach segment (FAS) to establish the desired reference path for an approach, landing, and rollout. The FAS data for each approach is determined by the GBAS service provider. Each approach is given a unique five-digit channel number and four-character identifier for a particular FAS, glideslope, and missed approach combination. FAS data for approaches supported by the particular GBAS facility are transmitted to the airplane through the VDB data link.

GBAS performs the following functions:

1. provides locally relevant pseudo-range corrections;
2. provides GBAS related data;
3. provides final approach segment data when supporting precision approach;
4. provides predicted ranging source availability data; and
5. provides integrity monitoring for GNSS ranging sources.

### iMMR (Integrated Multi Mode Receiver)

The iMMR functionality is described in the FCOM and training module.

The iMMRs process the pseudo-range correction and FAS data to produce an ILS-like deviation indication from the final approach path. These deviations are then displayed on the Primary Flight Display [PFD] and are used by airplane systems such as the flight guidance system (e.g. autopilot and flight director) for landing guidance.

The iMMR tunes the VHF receiver to a frequency based on the selected GLS channel. When the Ground Based Augmentation System (GBAS) VHF signal strength is sufficient, the iMMR is capable of decoding digital data broadcast by the GBAS. When the GBAS broadcast is received, the approach data block shows data as received from the GBAS, i.e. Ident/front course; DME to RWY threshold; Navigation Source Reference (GLS.) The iMMR can show distance to threshold when greater than the service volume established for GLS.

### Threat:

The iMMR continues to show selected GLS identifier, runway ID, and distance to threshold after a GBAS failure. This means a continuous display of the GLS identifier does not necessarily mean a reliable GLS signal. The primary indication of a reliable GLS signal is the presence of localizer and glideslope pointers on the deviation scale (NPS).

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

# iMMR Failure

In the event of an iMMR failure, localizer or glideslope pointers are not shown or are removed, and the amber LOC or G/S failure flags are shown. The absence of pointers prevents approach capture, or if LOC and G/S have already been captured, flight directors are removed from view and the autopilot disengages.

# GLS Failure

Flight crew should not rely on the presence of an amber LOC failure flag, G/S failure flag, or GLS identifier to determine reliability of the GLS signal. In the event of a GLS ground station failure, localizer or glideslope pointers are not shown or are removed. The absence of pointers prevents approach capture, or if LOC or G/S have already been captured, flight directors are removed from view and the autopilot disengages.

If either of these two events occur execute a Go-Around and Missed Approach Procedure unless suitable visual reference is established and maintained to enable a safe landing.

# GPS Failure

While GLS offers high levels of accuracy, integrity and continuity of service, events such as intentional or unintentional GPS jamming must still be considered. In the event of a loss of GPS signal, execute the Go-Around and Missed Approach Procedure unless suitable visual reference is established and maintained to enable a safe landing.

The AFM contains GLS approvals. GLS systems and procedures are described in the FCOM and equipment failures are addressed in the MEL/DDG.

## 8.3.2.3.3 GLS Training and E-learning

The ILS-like implementation of the GLS was selected to support common flight deck and airplane systems integration for both safety and economic reasons. This implementation provides an optimal pilot and system interface with FMAs and deviation indications identical to those utilized during ILS approaches.

## 8.3.2.3.4 GLS Security

The accuracy, integrity, and continuity of service for the GLS have been specified to be the 'same as or better' than ICAO standards for an ILS ground station supporting Category I approaches.

page 8–258

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## 8.3.2.3.5 Reportable Events of GLS Operations

A reportable event is an event that adversely affects the safety of the operation and may be caused by actions or events external to the functioning of the aircraft navigation system.

Technical defects and the exceedance of technical limitations are reportable events, including:

1. significant navigation errors attributed to incorrect data or a database coding error;
2. unexpected deviations in lateral/vertical flight path not caused by flight crew input or erroneous operation of equipment;
3. significant misleading information without a failure warning;
4. total loss or multiple navigation equipment failure;
5. loss of integrity, e.g. RAIM function, whereas integrity was predicted to be available during pre-flight planning,
6. any GLS system failure (refer FCOM)

An ASR shall be used to report all GLS anomalies. Safety Reporting System records ASRs GLS filed.

## 8.3.2.4 SPA

SPA.GEN.100, 105, 110, 115, 120, AMC1 SPA.GEN.105(a)

The Irish Aviation Authority (IAA) is the responsible authority for issuance of specific approval(s) as listed in the Ryanair AOC. Ryanair SPAs are compliant with ORO.GEN.105.

Ryanair will document the use of each Specific Approval including all non-compliances observed and make such documentation available to the IAA on request. The operation of each approval shall be in compliance with the OMA and Part-ORO (Operator Requirements) of Annex III. There is an annual SRA for each approval which ensures resilience of Ryanair application of such approvals and the SRA is updated with Ryanair experience over the previous 12 months.

Ryanair will only carry out operations with those specific approvals which have been authorised by the authority and listed in the OP Spec. Specific approvals will remain in force until either amended or revoked by the authority

## 8.3.2.4.2 NAT HLA

CAT.IDE.A.345, SPA.MNPS.100, 105, AMC1 SPA.GEN.105(a)

Aircraft shall only be operated in NAT HLA airspace in accordance with regional supplementary procedures if the operator has been granted an approval by the competent authority to conduct such operations.

Ryanair’s Operations Specification certificate, as issued by the Competent Authority, details all operator approvals, including NAT HLA.

Operations within NAT HLA (North Atlantic High-Level Airspace), within the extent of the Area of Operations as detailed in the current Operations Specification Certificate, must comply with regulation and operator approvals, as outlined in OMA 0.2.6.1.1, and remain within the applicable diversion distance for the aircraft, 427 NM (B737-800)_ or 416 NM (B737-8200)_ of an adequate aerodrome at all times.

**Note:** _In accordance with CAT.IDE.A.285 for over water flights the Maximum Diversion Distance is limited to 400 NM unless life rafts are carried. The following documents, available on EFB, are applicable to NAT HLA operations in Oceanic Airspace:_

- Oceanic Guide and Checklist
- The HF Management Guidance Material (NAT Doc 003)
- Jeppesen Publications (Pubs) &gt; Airway Manuals
- The North Atlantic Operations and Airspace Manual (NAT Doc 007).

## NAT Doc 007 – North Atlantic Operations and Airspace Manual

North Atlantic Operations and Airspace Manual (NAT Doc 007) has been produced with the approval and on behalf of the North Atlantic Systems Planning Group (NAT SPG) which is the North Atlantic regional planning body established under the auspices of the International Civil Aviation Organisation (ICAO). This Group is responsible for developing the required operational procedures, specifying the necessary services and facilities, and defining the aircraft and operator approval standards employed in the NAT Region.

Regulatory material relating to North Atlantic aircraft operations is contained in relevant ICAO Annexes, PANS/ATM (Doc.4444), Regional Supplementary Procedures (Doc.7030), State AIPs and current NOTAMs, which should be read in conjunction with the material contained in this Manual.

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## Jeppesen Publications (Pubs) &gt; Airway Manuals

The Jeppesen Publications (Pubs) (e.g., General Airway Manual, Africa, Atlantic, Europe/Asia, North America, Pacific, etc.) extracts procedures, information and guidance from the North Atlantic Operations &amp; Airspace Manual, PANS OPS 4444 and the Regional Supplementary Procedures, AIPs and other relevant documentation.

## The Oceanic Guide and Checklist

The Oceanic guide and checklist extracts the most significant information from both of the above and includes Ryanair SOP to be a more user friendly document for immediate access as required electronically for use in CAT operations in NAT HLA.

## Ryanair HF Guidance Material

This document explains in detail the principles of High Frequency radio propagation, how to use and contingency procedures.

## Flight Crew Training and Qualification

The flight crew training requirements are specified in OMD and include associated e-learning modules. This training in conjunction with this section of OMA and current NOTAMs ensures familiarity with the regulatory requirements for operations in NAT HLA.

## Operations Controller Training and Qualification

OCC personnel engaged in NAT HLA operations are trained in compliance with Operations Controller Training, including NAT HLA, as stated in OMD. This training ensures such personnel understand the NAT HLA regulatory material relating to North Atlantic aircraft operations contained in relevant ICAO Annexes, PANS/ATM (Doc.4444), Regional Supplementary Procedures (Doc.7030), State AIPs and current NOTAMs.

## Normal and Contingency Procedures

Normal and contingency operating procedures for operations in NAT HLA are described in great detail in the North Atlantic Operations and Airspace Manual (NAT Doc 007). Procedures are tailored for operational use in the 'Oceanic Guide and Checklist'. These include, but are not limited to, carrying out independent cross-checks of keyboard entries, RCL procedures, turn back and weather avoidance contingencies etc. reflecting the TEM concept of operations in NAT HLA.

## Records of Operations in NAT HLA

Records of operations in NAT HLA are retained as applicable in compliance with OMA 2.0.3 and training records in an electronic record keeping system.

## Safety Reporting

Any crew errors or omissions which did or could have compromised safety in NAT HLA shall be reported in accordance with the requirements of OMA Chapter 11.

## Monitoring of Operations in NAT HLA

Ryanair monitors operations in NAT HLA to measure compliance and to have insight into failures and non-compliances. Non-compliances, crew errors and equipment failures shall be reported in compliance with normal operating procedures and as appropriate in the technical log records. The ATS also monitors operations in NAT HLA and non-compliances are reported to the IAA thence to Ryanair.

## 8.3.2.4.2.1 NAT HLA Equipment

CAT.IDE.A.345, AMC4 CAT.IDE.A.345, AMC1 SPA.MNPS.105

Aircraft authorised to operate within oceanic airspace or NAT HLA airspace shall comply with SPA.MNPS.105 NAT HLA and AIP requirements. Refer to MELDDG Annex 1 for NAT HLA Dispatch Requirements

## 8.3.2.4.2.1.1 Long Range Communications

### HF

Aircraft operating in NAT HLA require at least one long range communication system. This is High Frequency (HF) radio on Ryanair aircraft, and is in addition to two independent radio communications systems, VHF1 &amp; 2. This enables communication with an appropriate ground station from any point on the route, including diversions.

Whilst Ryanair aircraft have one HF, the Ryanair MNPS approval is not restricted to specific routes due to the relatively short duration of these flights outside VHF coverage. Contingency for HF blackout or loss of HF is presented in detail in the NAT Doc 007, Jeppesen Publications (Pubs) &gt; Atlantic and the Oceanic Guide.

### SATCOM

SATCOM voice, when equipped, shall only be used to comply with route-specific operational procedures, when directed by ATC, or if communication system failure and/or safety of flight dictates.

As per ICAO guidance, SATCOM voice is not a replacement for ADS, CPDLC, or HF communications, but rather reduces the risk of communications failure, improves operational safety and efficiency, and alleviates HF channel congestion. Dedicated SATCOM voice access numbers (short codes) for aeronautical stations and air traffic service units (ATSUs) are published in national AIPs and aeronautical charts. ATSUs may contact specific aircraft utilising the aircraft’s unique SATCOM code, as annotated on the operational flight plan.

## 8.3.2.4.2.1.2 Long Range Navigation System (LRNS)

The Long Range Navigation System (LRNS) on board Company aircraft is an FMC with GPS and IRS inputs. Dual FMC aircraft have two LRNS capable of filed oceanic route specification. It is Company policy to assign dual FMC aircraft to operations in NAT HLA. However, a Company aircraft may operate on T9 or T290 with one LRNS only (HF required).

## 8.3.2.4.2.1.3 RVSM

All Ryanair aircraft are RVSM compliant and all flight crew are RVSM qualified.

## 8.3.2.4.2.1.4 TCAS

All Ryanair aircraft are TCAS compliant and all flight crew are trained accordingly.

## 8.3.2.4.2.1.5 PBCS

Ryanair aircraft planned on NAT HLA routings comply with PBCS and ADS route specification requirements.

## 8.3.2.4.2.1.6 Datalink

Ryanair aircraft planned on NAT HLA routings comply with route datalink requirements.

## 8.3.2.4.2.1.7 Underwater Locator Devices

Ryanair aircraft planned on NAT HLA routings comply with ULD requirements.

## 8.3.2.4.2.1.8 Liferafts

For over water flights, the Maximum Diversion Distance is limited to 400 NM Unless Liferafts are carried.

## 8.3.2.4.2.2 NAT HLA Crew Qualification

To operate flights in NAT HLA, both pilots shall be NAT HLA qualified by completing the training specified in OMD, and so recorded on an electronic record keeping system.

A crew pairing of a ‘new captain and a ‘new’ co-pilot is prohibited.

## 8.3.2.4.2.3 NAT HLA Organised Track System (OTS)

Ryanair flights are not planned on the OTS but the location of the OTS is useful for crew on any NAT HLA flight to have insight on the location of other NAT HLA traffic in case of contingencies or diversions. Therefore, the organised track message and relevant amendments to it are provided in the NAT HLA preflight briefing pack from LIDO.

The OTS is described in detail in the ICAO NAT DOC 007 and the ‘Oceanic Guide and Checklist’. Ryanair operations in NAT HLA are not filed on the OTS, but on occasions may cross a track(s) on the OTS. Level-capping and fuelling considerations in these instances are explained in the ‘Oceanic Guide and Checklist’ on Docunet.

## 8.3.2.4.2.4 Other Routes and Route Structures Within or Adjacent to NAT HLA Shannon Oceanic Transition Area (SOTA)

Part of the Shanwick OCA is designated as the Shannon Oceanic Transition Area (SOTA) and since 2009 this is not designated NAT HLA. SOTA has the same vertical extent as the Shanwick OCA, and is bounded by lines joining successively the following points:

N5100 W01500 – N5100 W00800 – N4830 W00800 – N4900 W01500 – N5100 W01500

Air Traffic Service is provided by Shannon ACC using the call sign SHANNON CONTROL. Full details of the service provided and the procedures used are contained in OMC, Jeppesen Publications (Pubs) &gt; Atlantic &gt; North Atlantic Crossing Clearance Procedures and Frequencies and Europe Airway Manual &gt; Air Traffic Control Data &gt; North Atlantic (NAT) High Level Airspace (HLA).

## Brest Oceanic Transition Area (BOTA)

Part of the Shanwick OCA is designated as the Brest Oceanic Transition Area (BOTA). NAT HLA requirements are still applicable from FL285 to FL420. BOTA has the same vertical extent as the Shanwick OCA, and is bounded by lines joining successively the following points:

N4834 W00845 – N4830 W00800 – N4500 W00800 – N4500 W00845 – N4834 W00845

Air Traffic service is provided by the Brest ACC, call sign BREST CONTROL.

## 8.3.2.4.2.5 NAT HLA Flight Plan Requirements Random Routes

A route segment in NAT HLA that does not follow a published OTS track, in its entirety, is known as a “Random Route”. All flights which generally route in an eastbound or westbound direction should normally be flight planned so that specified ten degrees of longitude (20°W, 30°W, 40°W, etc.) are crossed at whole degrees of latitude. All generally northbound or southbound flights should normally be flight planned so that specified parallels of latitude spaced at five degree intervals (65°N, 60°N, 55°N etc.) are crossed at whole degrees of longitude.

All flights should plan to operate on great circle tracks joining successive significant waypoints.

OCC personnel planning a random route, should normally plan flight level(s) appropriate to the direction of flight. OCC flight planners are aware that the AIPs specify some exceptions to the use of “Appropriate Direction Levels” both during the OTS time periods and outside them. The current usage allocation of flight levels in the NAT HLA is published in the UK and Canadian AIPs as the NAT Flight Level Allocation Scheme (FLAS). Therefore, OCC planners shall always consult the current AIPs and any supporting NOTAMs when flight planning random routes through NAT HLA.

## Plotting:

In NAT HLA, a plot of the filed routing and any re-clearances enroute is maintained on a suitable plotting chart. The Jeppesen FD Pro enroute map may be used as a plotting chart. Routes planned always remain within the diversion distance of one hour flying time at an OEI cruising speed of an adequate aerodrome. Any re-clearance enroute shall be declined if any part of the re-cleared route segment is outside the of the applicable diversion distance range ring when plotted.

When required to plot, it is good practice to plot your Present Position regularly on your chart because the present position output cannot normally be interfered with and its calculation is independent of the waypoint data. It is the one output which can be relied upon to detect gross tracking errors. A position should be checked and preferably plotted approximately 10 minutes after passing each waypoint, and, if circumstances dictate, midway between waypoints. e.g. if one system has failed.

However the following exceptions apply:

On T9, 213 &amp; 16 – Do not plot.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

These are airways programmed into the FMC so a plot is not required. However tracks and distances must be checked against the OFP in case of a programmed error by the service provider. If however you are routed ‘off-airways’ you must PLOT. Examples of this are:

- From one airway to another (LASNO on T9 to GONAN on T16) – plot required
- OMOKO on T16 to ‘47N11W’ then GONAN, back to T16 (i.e. a ‘dogleg’) – plot required
- A random LAT/LONG, which may not be on any of these three airways – plot required.

To/from Azores via Santa Maria OCA and Lisbon FIR – Do not plot.

The Portuguese Authority has confirmed that plotting is not required on these routes. However tracks and distances must be checked against the OFP in case of FMC insertion errors.

Note 1: All company flights are planned to be within a limit of an adequate airport at any point during the flight. SOPs ensure that crew operating in NAT HLA enter the nearest enroute alternate on FIX page and circle with the applicable range ring for situational awareness. ETPs are computed for contingencies.

Note 2: \*In Accordance with CAT.IDE.A.285 for over water flights the Maximum Diversion Distance is limited to 400 NM unless Liferafts are carried.

## 8.3.2.4.2.5.1 NAT HLA (ETP) Critical Fuel

There is a Critical Fuel calculation on NAT HLA flights. CRITICAL FUEL is fuel required for a scenario to divert to a suitable airport at the most critical point. Three Critical Fuel scenarios are considered.

1. A rapid decompression at the most critical point followed by descent to a 10,000 ft or a higher altitude if sufficient oxygen is provided in accordance with the applicable operational requirements.
2. Flight at the approved one-engine-inoperative cruise speed assuming a rapid decompression and a simultaneous engine failure at the most critical point followed by descent to a 10,000 ft or a higher altitude if sufficient oxygen is provided in accordance with the applicable operational requirements.

page 8–266

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

3. Flight at the approved one-engine-inoperative cruise speed assuming an engine failure at the most critical point followed by descent to the one-engine-inoperative cruise altitude. Upon reaching the alternate, fuel to hold at 1,500 ft above field elevation for 15 minutes and then conduct an instrument approach and landing is included.

Note: Critical Fuel includes ICE FUEL.

## 8.3.2.4.2.5.2 MNPS (ETOPS/ETP) Ice Fuel

There is an Ice Fuel calculation on NAT HLA flights. Ice Fuel is the expected fuel usage by anti-icing systems during a critical fuel situation. ICE FUEL is included in Critical Fuel.

Critical Fuel is corrected to include the greater of:

1. The effect of airframe icing during 10% of the time during which icing is forecast (including ice accumulation on unprotected surfaces, and the fuel used by engine and wing anti-ice during this period), or
2. Fuel for engine anti-ice, and if appropriate wing anti-ice for the entire time during which icing is forecast.

## 8.3.2.4.2.6 NAT HLA Request for Clearance (RCL) General

There is a requirement to send a Request for Clearance (RCL) prior to Oceanic entry. An RCL is a voice or data link message via ACARS used to provide ETA at Oceanic Entry Point (OEP), requested flight level, and speed.

Flight Crew procedures for RCL are addressed in the 'Oceanic Guide and Checklist'. Contingencies including the Flight Level contingency rules, are extensively detailed in the North Atlantic Operations and Airspace Manual (NAT Doc 007), and Jeppesen Publications (Pubs) (e.g., Atlantic Reference and Airway Manuals). Enroute aircraft shall enter Oceanic Airspace in accordance with their current flight plan or as amended by ATC. Any change to the flight plan routing will be transmitted as part of the RCL.

## 8.3.2.4.2.7 LIDO NAT HLA Planning

Ryanair is not authorised to conduct ETOPS operations. LIDO will generate ETPs (Equal Time Points) and will identify adequate enroute alternate aerodromes when a NAT HLA flight plan is calculated. **_ETP FLIGHT_** will appear on the OFP. LIDO generated NAT HLA routes are always planned to remain within 60 mins of an Enroute Alternate Aerodrome.

## Planning Minima – NAT HLA

Ryanair will select an aerodrome as a NAT HLA fuel ERA only when the appropriate weather reports and/or forecasts indicate that the weather conditions will be at or above the planning minima of OMA 8.1.5.5 Table 2.

## 8.3.2.4.3 Reduced Vertical Separation Minima (RVSM)

SPA.RVSM.105, 100, 110, AMC1 SPA.RVSM.110(a)

Ryanair aircraft may be operated in designated airspace where a reduced vertical separation minimum of 300 m (1000 ft) applies between FL290 and FL410 inclusive on the basis of the RVSM approval granted to the Ryanair by the IAA in the Operations Specifications in the AOC.

## RVSM Operational Approval

SPA.RVSM.105, AMC1 SPA.RVSM.105

The Ryanair has provided to the IAA the items listed below to achieve an RVSM approval:

1. The RVSM airworthiness approval for each aircraft from the OEM;
2. Procedures for monitoring and reporting height-keeping errors have been established;
3. A training programme for the flight crew members involved in these operations has been established;
4. Operating procedures have been established specifying:
   a. The equipment to be carried, including its operating limitations and appropriate entries in the MEL;
   b. Flight crew composition and experience requirements;
   c. Flight planning;
   d. Pre-flight procedures;
   e. Procedures prior to RVSM airspace entry;
   f. In-flight procedures;
   g. Post-flight procedures;
   h. Incident reporting;
   i. Specific regional operating procedures.

Ryanair has a RVSM Approval as stated in the Operations Specifications in the AOC. RVSM is represented by “W” on the filed flight plan in item 10. The operating procedures for operation in RVSM airspace, including the processes whereby an aircraft ceases to be RVSM compliant are presented in Operations Manual Part A and Part B including the MEL.

## MEL RVSM Equipment Requirements (MEL Annex I)

(SPA.RVSM.110)

All Ryanair aircraft operating in RVSM airspace shall be equipped with:

a. Two independent altitude measurement systems;
b. An altitude alerting system;
c. An automatic altitude control system; and
d. A Secondary Surveillance Radar (SSR) transponder with altitude reporting system that can be connected to the altitude measurement system in use for altitude keeping.

## RVSM Equipment Requirements

(AMC1 SPA.RVSM.110(a))

Two independent altitude measurement systems:

All Ryanair aircraft comply with the following equipment requirements and each system has the following components:

a. Cross-coupled static source/system, with ice protection if located in areas subject to ice accretion;
b. Equipment for measuring static pressure sensed by the static source, converting it to pressure altitude and displaying the pressure altitude to the flight crew;
c. Equipment for providing a digitally encoded signal corresponding to the displayed pressure altitude, for automatic altitude reporting purposes;
d. Static Source Error Correction (SSEC), if needed to meet the performance criteria for RVSM flight envelopes; and
e. Signals referenced to a flight crew selected altitude for automatic control and alerting. These signals are derived from aircraft altitude measurement systems meeting the performance criteria for RVSM flight envelopes.

Regional Monitoring Agency (RMA) Verification/monitoring programmes Ryanair participates in Monitoring Programmes in the Area of Operations operated by Regional Monitoring Agencies. The data for all Ryanair aircraft is collated and available to Ryanair. An altitude violation

by a Ryanair aircraft is communicated by the RMA to the OCC. This violation is notified by the Manager OCC to the Safety Manager, NPFO, Maintrol and NPCA.

When height-keeping errors are recorded or received, the Ryanair shall take immediate action to rectify the conditions that caused the errors. Once rectified, this aircraft achieves RVSM capability, and the RVSM airworthiness approval is restored. RVSM violation notification shall be notified to the Competent Authority by the Safety Manager within 72 hrs from the moment the company becomes aware of the occurrence.

Ryanair has a Maintenance Programme and Continuing Airworthiness Procedures in support of RVSM operations.

Note: The ADS-B capability is extensively used to compute aircraft altitude compliance in addition to Height Monitoring terminals. This captures a greater altitude monitoring sample more frequently by the Regional Monitoring Agencies.

## 8.3.2.4.3.1 RVSM Altitude Error Tolerance

(SPA.RVSM.115)

Occurrences of height-keeping errors caused by malfunction of aircraft equipment or of operational nature, equal to or greater than:

- A Total Vertical Error (TVE) of ± 90 m (± 300 ft);
- An Altimetry System Error (ASE) of ± 75 m (± 245 ft); and
- An Assigned Altitude Deviation (AAD) of ± 90 m (± 300 ft)

## 8.3.2.4.3.2 RVSM Airspace Procedures

(AMC2 SPA.RVSM.105), (CAT.OP.MPA.101, 105)

### Operating Procedures

#### 1. Flight planning

During flight planning the flight crew shall pay particular attention to conditions that may affect operation in RVSM airspace. These include reported and forecast weather on the route of flight noting areas of turbulence in particular, minimum equipment requirements pertaining to height-keeping and alerting systems and any airframe or operating restriction related to RVSM operations.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR
PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## 2. Pre-flight procedures

The following actions shall be accomplished during the pre-flight procedure:

a. Review the Technical Log to determine the condition of equipment required for flight in the RVSM airspace.

Ensure that maintenance action has been taken to correct defects to required equipment.

b. During the external inspection of aircraft, particular attention will be paid to the condition of static sources and the condition of the fuselage skin near each static source and any other component that affects altimetry system accuracy.

c. Before take-off, the aircraft altimeters shall be set to the QNH of the airfield. Prior to departure, the two primary altimeters shall be crosschecked and must not exceed those limitations outlined in the FCOM ‘Altitude Display Limits for RVSM Operations’.

d. Before take-off, equipment required for flight in RVSM airspace shall be operative and any indications of malfunction will be resolved.

## 3. Prior to RVSM airspace entry

The following equipment must be operating normally at entry into RVSM airspace:

a. Two primary altitude measurement systems.

i. A cross-check between the two primary altimeters must be made and must agree within ± 60 m (± 200 ft). Failure to meet this condition will require that the altimetry system is reported as defective and Air Traffic Control (ATC) notified.

The altimetry checks performed during the climb comply with this requirement.

ii. One automatic altitude-control system;

- One altitude-alerting device; and
- Operating transponder.

b. Should any of the required equipment fail prior to the aircraft entering RVSM airspace, request a new clearance to avoid entering RVSM airspace.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

# 4. In-flight procedures

The following practices are included in Crew Training and Standard Operating Procedures for RVSM:

a. Flight crew shall comply with any aircraft operating restrictions given in the RVSM airworthiness approval.

b. The sub-scale on all primary and standby altimeters must be set to 1013/29.92 in Hg when passing the transition altitude and rechecked for proper altimeter setting when reaching the initial cleared flight level.

The maximum in-flight difference between the Captain and First Officer altitude displays for RVSM operations is 200 ft which is also the trigger for the ALT DISAGREE alert.

c. In level cruise it is essential that the aircraft is flown at the cleared flight level. This requires that particular care is taken to ensure that ATC clearances are fully understood and followed. The aircraft shall not intentionally depart from a cleared flight level without a positive clearance from ATC unless the crew are conducting contingency or emergency manoeuvres.

d. When changing levels, the aircraft shall not be allowed to overshoot or undershoot the cleared flight level by more than 45 m (150 ft). Level off shall be accomplished using the altitude capture feature of the automatic altitude-control system, i.e. with an autopilot engaged.

e. An automatic altitude-control system should be operative and engaged during level cruise. In any event, adherence to cruise altitude will be done by reference to one of the two primary altimeters. Following loss of the automatic height-keeping function, any consequential restrictions will need to be observed.

f. Ensure that the altitude-alerting system is operative.

i. At intervals of approximately 1 hour, cross-checks between the primary altimeters will be made. Both altimeters must agree within ± 60 m (± 200 ft). Failure to meet this condition will require that the altimetry system be reported as defective and ATC notified, or contingency procedures applied.

ii. Monitoring of the Autopilot Flight Director System and flight deck instruments suffices for altimeter cross-checking with one automatic altitude control system, one altitude alerting device and an operating transponder serviceable. The hourly check of the No.1 and No.2 altimeter during flight in RVSM airspace is not recorded.

page 8–272

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

iii. In normal operations, the altimetry system being used to control the aircraft will be selected for the input to the altitude reporting transponder transmitting information to ATC.

g. If the pilot is notified by ATC of a deviation from an assigned altitude exceeding ± 90 m (± 300 ft) then the pilot shall take action to return to the cleared flight level as quickly as possible.

h. Contingency procedures after entering RVSM airspace are as follows:

i. Notify ATC of contingencies (equipment failures, weather) that affect the ability to maintain the cleared flight level and coordinate a plan of action appropriate to the airspace concerned.

ii. Examples of equipment failures that shall be notified to ATC are:

- Failure of all automatic altitude control systems aboard the aircraft;
- Loss of redundancy of altimetry systems;
- Loss of thrust on an engine necessitating descent; or
- Any other equipment failure affecting the ability to maintain cleared flight level.

iii. Notify ATC when encountering greater than moderate turbulence.

iv. If unable to notify ATC and obtain an ATC clearance prior to deviating from the cleared flight level, follow any established contingency procedures for the region of operation and obtain ATC clearance as soon as possible.

5. Post-flight procedures

In making technical log entries against malfunctions in height-keeping systems, the commander will provide sufficient detail to enable maintenance to effectively troubleshoot and repair the system. Include the following information when reporting the defect:

a. Primary and standby altimeter readings;
b. Altitude selector setting;
c. Subscale setting on altimeter;
d. Autopilot used to control the aircraft and any differences when an alternative autopilot system was selected;
e. Differences in altimeter readings;

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

f. Use of air data computer selector for fault diagnosis procedure; and
g. The transponder selected to provide altitude information to ATC and any difference noted when an alternative transponder was selected

6. Reporting RVSM Altimetry error after Tech Log entry

If a Tech Log entry has been made regarding altimeter performance outside RVSM tolerances, an ASR must be filed on completion of duty. This is to enable Ryanair to report the RVSM altimetry non-compliance to the Competent Authority within 72 hrs from the moment the company becomes aware of the occurrence. Reports shall include an initial analysis of causal factors and measures taken to prevent repeat occurrences. When height-keeping errors are recorded or received, the operator shall take immediate action to rectify the conditions that caused the errors and provide follow-up reports, if requested by the competent authority.

SPA.LVO.100

1. take-off operations with visibility conditions of less than 400 m RVR; and
2. instrument approach operations in low visibility conditions.

8.3.2.5 Performance Based Specification Operations

8.3.2.5.1 Performance Based Communications and Surveillance (PBCS)

PBCS standards were introduced in the NAT HLA for aircraft that meet the ‘Required Communication Performance’ (RCP) and ‘Required Surveillance Performance’ (RSP) specifications which ensure the ongoing safety and efficiency of ATM operations.

Performance-based operations and monitoring have been implemented in the NAT HLA to ensure the ongoing safety and efficiency of ATM operations. The performance of FANS equivalent, CPDLC, and ADS-C aircraft are monitored in the NAT HLA against RCP 240 and RSP 180 specifications.

page 8-274

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

RCP 240 reflects the maximum transaction time of 240 seconds between when controller sends an uplink message to the aircraft and when they receive the downlinked response. RCP 240 time allocations provide for the pilots to respond within 60 seconds after receiving an uplinked message.

RSP 180 reflects the maximum transaction time of 180 seconds for ADS contract processing, beginning from when the avionics generates a contract and when displayed to ATC.

Within approved airspace, RCP 240 and RSP 180 operational specifications enable ATC to optimize airspace efficiency by providing 30 nm, 50 nm, or 5-minute longitudinal separation minima and 23 nm lateral separation minimum between aircraft which are also RCP 240 and RSP 180 capable.

Separation of PBCS aircraft remains an air traffic control function and responsibility.

When an aircraft does not meet RCP 240 and RSP 180 specifications, ATC shall provide standard separation criteria.

OMC Flight Supplement, ATC Section 23 provides PBCS guidance associated with NAT HLA operations.

Aircrew must report any failure or malfunction of GNSS, ADS-C, or CPDLC equipment to ATC as soon as it becomes apparent.

ANSPs and operators will participate in monitoring performance programmes and report PBCS deficiencies via established channels.

## 8.3.2.5.1.1 PBCS Flight Planning

For aircraft planning to operate in the NAT HLA, PBCS capabilities, when operational, are reflected in the ICAO flight plan as follows:

Item 10a: FANS designator (e.g., J7)
FANS CPDLC RCP 240 compliance designator (e.g., P2)

Item 10b: FANS ADS-C compliance (D1 or G1 for ATN systems)

Item 18: FANS ADS-C RSP 180 compliance (SUR/RSP180)
RNP 4 compliance (PBN/L1)

When noted prior to dispatch, the PIC must advise Operations Control of any defective PBCS related equipment and revise the ICAO OFP Item 10a/10b/18 codes and flight plan route accordingly.

## 8.3.2.5.2 RNP4

RNP4 is a navigation specification developed for operations in oceanic and remote airspace. GNSS is the primary navigation sensor to support RNP4, either as a stand-alone navigation system or as part of a multi-sensor system.

## 8.3.2.5.3 RNP2

RNP2 is a Navigation Specification applicable in Domestic airspace, also called Continental or European airspace and ‘Domestic Offshore’ airspace designated for T9 and T290. GNSS is the primary navigation sensor to support RNP2, either as a stand-alone navigation system or as part of a multi-sensor system. All Company aircraft are RNP2 compliant.

## 8.3.2.5.4 Data-Link Mandates (DLM)

Company equipped with ‘FANS2 VDL (VHF Data Link) MODE 2 + Iridium SATCOM’ aircraft are compliant with the HLA DLM Mandate. This will be indicated by a Technical Log Notice to Crew and ‘J7’ in Item 10 of the OFP. This equipage includes compliance with PBCS requirements.

Depending on the equipage fitted to the aircraft to enable compliance with this mandate either J1, J4 or J7 will be indicated in Item 10 of the OFP.

## 8.3.2.5.5 Operational Impact NAT HLA

If RNP4 is not indicated in the FMC when entering NAT HLA airspace, the crew must manually enter RNP4 at least 10 mins prior to entry to ensure the onboard alerting is compliant with the Navigation Specification required. The crew must delete RNP4 when departing NAT HLA airspace. The aircraft RNP is then 2 for the remaining enroute segment.

- Crews must verify that the RNP value displayed in the FMC is applicable to the intended area of operation.
- Flight crew should not modify waypoints that have been retrieved from the database. User-defined data (e.g. for flex-track routes) may be entered and used.
- Strategic Lateral Offset Procedure (SLOP) is mandatory for all Tango routes except T9/T290.

The following ICAO Flight Plan annotations in Items 10 are required for operations in NAT HLA:

Item 10a (Radio communication, navigation and approach-aid equipment and capabilities):

- ‘J7’ indicates FANS 2 VDL MODE 2 + SATCOM (Iridium)

- ‘M3’ indicates SATCOM VOICE (Iridium) – only available on specific 737-8200 aircraft

Item 10b (Surveillance equipment and capabilities):

- ‘D1’ indicates ADS-C (FANS) equipage and operation, and/or
- ‘G1’ indicates ADS-C (ATN) equipage and operation

## 8.3.2.5.6 NAT HLA Exempted Routes – T9, T290

Tango 9 (T9) and Tango 290 (T290) require RNP2 specification for operations from FL285 to FL410. T9 is southbound, T290 is northbound. HF is required. VHF frequency 128.360 is assigned as a required monitoring frequency (in addition to 121.5) and used for intervention and emergency only. Crews will monitor and only transmit during emergency or contingency.

## 8.3.2.7.1.3.1 ADS Contracts

Periodic: Onboard avionics sends updated position report which will include level, time and NEXT and NEXT+1 waypoints as per active flight plan.

Event: Event position/reports will be automatically triggered based upon the following conditions:

Waypoint Change Event – when passing active flight plan waypoint or crew amends NEXT or NEXT+1 waypoint.

Lateral Deviation Event: when aircraft deviates from cleared route beyond Strategic Lateral Offset provisions.

Level Range Deviation Event: aircraft deviates from cleared level ≥ 300 ft.

Vertical Rate Change Event: rate of descent exceeds 5000 FPM.

Demand: ATC function to trigger instantaneous aircraft position report.

Emergency: FMC selection available to advise ATC of a MAYDAY or PAN situation. Emergency report page accessible via FMC MCDU ATC INDEX menu.

## 8.3.2.7.1.3.2 ADS-C Operations

ICAO OFP Item 10b should reflect D1 to indicate ADS-C equipage and operation.

Prior to entering ADS-C airspace, verify CPDLC (VHF and SATCOM) and ADS-C serviceability.

Whilst operating in ADS-C airspace, should:

- avoid selecting ADS OFF (FMC ATC LOGON/STATUS 2/2 LSK 2L) unless instructed by ATC.
- maintain listening watch on assigned HF, VHF frequencies while in the NAT region.
- proactively monitor UIR/FIR boundaries and apply PLOC mitigation TEM, SOPs.

## 8.3.2.7.3 DLM Airspace Contingency Procedures

Prior to Departure – Contact Operations Control Centre to refile flight plan to remain clear of DLM airspace (e.g. NAT HLA).

After Departure But Prior to DLM Airspace – Advise ATC. Expect revised clearance to remain clear of, or if tactically available, to remain within DLM airspace.

After Entering DLM Airspace – Immediately advise ATC. Expect revised clearance to exit, or if tactically available, to remain within DLM airspace.

## 8.3.2.7.4 Aircraft Communications, Addressing and Reporting System (ACARS)

When installed, Technical Log Notice to Crew and the Operational Flight Plan, Item 10a identifies ACARS configuration.

On ACARS operational aircraft, system operation is restricted to obtaining departure clearance (DLC), pushback clearance, and Airline Operational Control (AOC) transmissions associated with safety/security alerts, diversions, and RA808 Radio Ahead Codes.

ACARS AOC messages to/from OCC shall comply with standardised formats and content. Refer to the ACARS Supplementary Guide in the EFB for guidance.

When noted prior to dispatch, the PIC should advise Operations Control of any defective ACARS related equipment and revise OFP Item 10a accordingly, e.g.:

- E3 – PDC ACARS: ATC Pre-Departure Clearance received via ACARS.

## 8.3.3 Altimeter Setting Procedures

AMC1 CAT.POL.A.225, CAT.OP.MPA.101

The following altimeter setting procedures apply for flight operations:

### On Ground

All Altimeters shall be set to QNH.

### During Climb

When cleared to a flight level and above 3000AGL both pilots shall set primary altimeters to standard.

### Cruise Level

When passing the transition altitude the standby altimeter shall be set to standard when above the MSA and remain set to standard during cruise and descent until cleared to an altitude in the descent.

page 8-280

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

During Descent

When cleared to an altitude both pilots shall set primary altimeters to QNH as well as the standby altimeter.

8.3.3.1 Checking of Barometric Altimeters

After each setting of altimeters their readings shall be compared. This shall include the metric and standby altimeters when they are used (e.g. Area U).

The altimeter indications shall be cross-checked against the elevation of the aerodrome during preflight instrument check.

8.3.3.1.1 Temperature Error

Pressure altimeters are calibrated to indicate true altitude under International Standard Atmosphere (ISA) conditions. Any deviation from ISA will therefore result in an erroneous reading on the altimeter. The altimeter error may be significant under conditions of extremely cold temperature and appropriate corrections shall be applied.

8.3.3.1.2 For procedures on how to correct for temperature error refer to FCOM vol 1.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## 8.3.3.1.3 Minimum Flight Altitude Correction

When converting minimum flight altitude to minimum flight level, corrections shall be applied when actual QNH differs from the standard value. For each 1 hPa below 1013 hPa subtract 28 ft from your flight level to establish the true altitude of your flight level.

E.g. FL = 160

MEA = 15,000 ft

Local QNH 975 hPa = Minus 1,080 ft

True altitude = FL 16000 – 1,080 ft = 14,920 ft

MEA is 15,000 ft and true altitude of aircraft is 14,920 ft, therefore the minimum required obstacle clearance is not assured.

## 8.3.3.2 Types of Altimeter Settings

With regard to the ICAO standard atmosphere, each type of altimeter setting indicates a vertical distance above the corresponding reference datum shown in the table:

| Type of Altimeter Setting | Reference Datum                                         | Altimeter Indication              |
| ------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------- |
| Standard                  | 1 013.2 hPa/29,92 in Hg Level                           | Flight Level                      |
| QNH                       | QNH pressure level (approx. Mean Sea Level).            | Altitude                          |
| QFE                       | QFE reference datum (aerodrome or threshold elevation). | Height above QFE reference datum. |

Note: QFE settings are not allowed.

## 8.3.3.3 Metric Altimetry

Use of metric altimetry settings is not normally required for Ryanair operations. Metric Altimetry shall only be used as prescribed in the relevant Airfield Brief. Jeppesen charts provide a conversion table on the approach chart which cross references feet and meters, and QNH/QFE altitudes for all the charted altitudes of the procedure depicted. In addition, conversion tables are published on the Jeppesen Flight Level Transition Procedures section of the relevant region’s Airway Manual, where metric altimetry is required.

## 8.3.4 Policy for Altitude Alert

CAT.IDE.A.140

The aircraft altitude alert system shall be considered as a back-up aid. Its operation must not be permitted to replace the requirement to make the standard altitude calls as presented in FCOM Vol 1, Normal procedures. Incorrect setting of the QNH in the descent is an insidious mistake frequently made. An altitude crosscheck on final approach path is an essential crew action to detect this. VSD when available also identifies blunder error.

## 8.3.5 Policy for Ground Proximity Detection

CAT.OP.MPA.290, CAT.IDE.A.150

When undue proximity to the ground is detected by a Flight Crew member or by a ground proximity warning system (GWPS/EGWPS), the pilot flying shall take corrective action immediately in accordance with procedures listed in the OMB to establish safe flight conditions.

8.3.5.1 The GPWS may not be deactivated/by pulling the circuit breakers or use of the flap or gear inhibit switch, except for approved checklist procedures where use of flaps at less than normal landing flap positions or landing gear up is specified.

## 8.3.5.2 Enhanced Ground Proximity Warning System (EGPWS)

EGPWS is a mandatory system and offers two advanced features, Terrain Awareness and Warning System (TAWS) based on an integral database, and Windshear Warning System.

Although there are few areas of known degradation of GPS signal, the consequences of interrupted GPS signal to some aircraft is a “TERRAIN, TERRAIN, PULL UP, PULL UP” command, EGPWS, or other look ahead terrain alerts. This will occur any time the signal is interrupted, regardless of the phase of flight of the aircraft, e.g., in the cruise. Ryanair flight planning requires assured terrain clearance, and by definition, a cruise altitude/flight level clears all obstacles enroute by the prescribed amounts.

Crews shall only inhibit EGPWS functionality when instructed to do so by a Boeing Checklist or Company procedure.

Unless specifically permitted by QRH or company procedure, and briefed in advance, crew reaction to an EGPWS ‘PULL UP’ warning shall be instantaneous. Operations into destinations not contained in WGS-84 database will cause GPWS warnings as the system does not recognise the destination and believes that the aircraft is too low. The warning is triggered as a result of system pre-programmed parameters relating aircraft altitude and distance from the nearest airport the GPWS system has in its database as distinct from the current non WGS-84 destination.

## 8.3.5.2.1 Management of the Enhanced Ground Proximity Warning System (EGPWS) Terrain Database (TDB)

CAT.IDE.A.355(B)

The Engineering Department ensures the timely distribution and upload of Terrain Data Bases (TDB) on all aircraft within eight weeks of the TDB release date. Each TDB will remain valid for a maximum of sixteen weeks.

The Performance Department reviews all TDB releases, cross-referencing the database updates with the specific Area of Operation (as per AOC Ops Spec). A Notice to Crew is raised in the Technical Logbook by Engineering, referring to the TDB update process. Safety or operationally critical TDB issues will be promulgated to Flight Crew by Company NOTAM and/or AFB. This structured process ensures that the Flight Crew operates with accurate and relevant information regarding obstacles and terrain in the intended Area of Operation.

The TDB version can be displayed on the Navigation Display by pressing the Ground proximity SYS TEST switch, which might be requested during SAFA/SACA Ramp Inspections.

## 8.3.6 Policy for Use of TCAS

CAT.OP.MPA.295, CAT.IDE.A.155

Ryanair aircraft are fitted with TCAS as required by current regulation. TCAS equipage is documented in FCOM/QRH and/or Aircraft Technical Log Notice to Crew/HIL as appropriate. The operating policy, procedures and call-outs are presented in the FCOM and QRH non-normal manoeuvres.

If an ATC clearance or instruction contradictory to the TCAS RA is received, the Flight Crew shall follow the RA and inform ATC directly: “[Callsign], Unable, TCAS RA”.

## 8.3.6.1 Traffic Advisory (TA)

The response to a TA is to immediately attempt to establish visual contact with the intruder. Pilots shall be aware that an aeroplane sighted may not necessarily be the intruder or the sole intruder TCAS is responding to.

On receipt of a TA, pilots shall use all available information to prepare for appropriate action if an RA occurs. While the PF shall continue to fly the aeroplane, the PM shall provide updates on the traffic location shown on the TCAS traffic display and use this information to help visually acquire the intruder.

**Note:** TCAS is not able to provide RAs for intruders without altitude reporting capability. Therefore, TAs without relative altitude data on the display shall be closely monitored.

page 8–284

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## 8.3.6.2 Resolution Advisory (RA)

The PF must respond immediately to RA, according to the procedures outlined in the QRH Chapter MAN. The maneuver must be limited to the minimum required to satisfy the RA. The PF must strictly follow the TCAS RA even if:

- Conflicting instructions have been issued by ATC.
- The RA response capability is limited by aircraft performance.
- A traffic information has been given by ATC.
- There is a visual contact to other traffic. Pilots must be aware that an aeroplane sighted may not necessarily be the intruder or the sole intruder TCAS is responding to.

The PM shall monitor the response to the RA and shall provide updates on the traffic location by checking the traffic display.

When a RA occurs, the Flight Crew must notify the appropriate ATC unit as soon as possible, as permitted by Flight Crew workload, in the following manner:

&gt; [Callsign], TCAS RA«

If the pilot is unable to comply with an ATC clearance or instruction because there is a RA, the appropriate response is:

&gt; [Callsign] – Unable – TCAS RA«

The ATC response in both cases should be:

“Roger”.

This relieves the controller of the responsibility to provide separation and he will not try to modify the aircraft flight path until the pilot reports “CLEAR OF CONFLICT”.

When TCAS announces “CLEAR OF CONFLICT” the PF must return the aeroplane promptly but smoothly to the ATC clearance. Announce the TCAS “CLEAR OF CONFLICT” message when the conflict is over using the phraseology:

“[Callsign] – CLEAR OF CONFLICT, RETURNING TO (assigned clearance)”, or

“[Callsign] – CLEAR OF CONFLICT, (assigned clearance) RESUMED”.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

8.3.6.2.1 TCAS ‘TA only’ shall be used in accordance with any QRH procedure, and/or MEL procedure. This includes, but is not limited to, EMERGENCY DESCENT and ENGINE FAILURE. TCAS will not track aircraft with vertical rates in excess of 10,000 fpm. Therefore, in an emergency descent, TCAS TA will be tracked by other TCAS II. As you are in TCAS TA mode you will not receive a RA, while an RA will be issued to an intruder, thereby not jeopardising collision avoidance.

TCAS climb can be generated at MAX ALT and shall be followed as the aircraft will have residual rate of climb capability, and if unable to climb (do not descend in this case) the system will generate another resolution in response.

Flying with an inoperative TCAS is permitted, including in RVSM airspace provided it is done in accordance with the applicable MEL. An aircraft that has a TCAS failure during flight can continue to its destination, and thereafter IAW MEL. There is no requirement to declare TCAS failure to ATC, and there is no requirement to annotate in the flight plan that TCAS II is unserviceable.

## 8.3.7 Fuel and Oil Supply

CAT.OP.MPA.260

The commander shall only commence a flight or continue in the event of in-flight re-planning, when satisfied that the aircraft carries at least the planned amount of usable fuel and oil to safely complete the flight, taking into account the expected operating conditions.

## 8.3.7.1 In-flight Fuel Management Policy

AMC1 CAT.OP.MPA.185(a)

## 8.3.7.1.1 Basic Fuel Scheme – Procedures for In-flight Fuel Management

1. In-flight fuel checks

a. Ryanair have established a procedure to ensure that in-flight fuel checks are carried out at regular intervals or at specified points indicated in the operational flight plan (one check at least every 60 minutes) in accordance with OMA.

b. The remaining usable fuel should be evaluated to:

i. compare the actual consumption with the planned consumption;

ii. check that the remaining usable fuel is sufficient to complete the flight, in accordance with point (2); and

iii. determine the usable fuel that is expected to remain upon landing at the destination aerodrome.

page 8-286

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR
PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

c. In relation to the recording of relevant data, Ryanair should:
i. agree with the IAA on what constitutes relevant data for the purpose of recording;
ii. use the relevant data as safety performance indicators (SPIs) of the current fuel scheme; and
iii. ensure that the recorded data is stored for at least 2 years.

Ryanair have established a procedure for the data to be de-identified to a level that ensures the implementation of a ‘just culture’.

2. In-flight fuel management

a. The flight should be conducted to ensure that the usable fuel expected to remain upon landing at the destination aerodrome is not less than:
i. the required alternate fuel plus the FRF; or
ii. the FRF if no alternate aerodrome is required.

b. If an in-flight fuel check shows that the usable fuel expected to remain upon landing at the destination aerodrome is less than:
i. the required alternate fuel plus the FRF, the commander should request delay information from the ATC, and take into account the prevailing traffic and operational conditions at the destination aerodrome, at the destination alternate aerodrome, and at any other adequate aerodrome, to decide whether to proceed to the destination aerodrome or to divert in order to perform a safe landing with not less than the FRF; or
ii. the FRF, if no destination alternate aerodrome is required, the commander should take appropriate action and proceed to an aerodrome where a safe landing can be made with not less than the FRF.

c. The use of fuel after flight commencement for objectives other than the ones originally intended during pre-flight planning should require reanalysis and, if applicable, adjustment of the planned operation.

8.3.7.2 Re-planning In-flight

Re-planning in-flight may be conducted when planned operating conditions have changed or other reasons make further adherence to the original flight plan unacceptable or impractical, for example:

- Poor weather conditions or runway conditions at the planned destination and alternate.

The Commander shall declare a situation of fuel emergency by broadcasting “MAYDAY, MAYDAY, MAYDAY, FUEL, callsign”, when the calculated usable fuel on landing, at the nearest adequate aerodrome where a safe landing can be performed, is less than final reserve fuel.
