## 8.3.7.6 Minimum Fuel

When an impending fuel condition is identified for the current planned flight to intended aerodrome of landing the Commander shall take the following action.

The Commander shall request delay information from ATC when unanticipated circumstances may result in landing at the destination aerodrome with less than the final reserve fuel plus any fuel required to proceed to an alternate aerodrome. “ATC, request EAT, RYR 123”.

## ‘Minimum Fuel’ Declaration

GM1 CAT.OP.MPA.185

When committed to land at a specific aerodrome, the commander should take into account any operational factor that may cause a delay to landing, and thus determine whether the aircraft will land with less than the planned FRF, even after receiving clearance from ATC. A change that may cause a delay to landing could be other than ATC, e.g. a change of weather conditions, etc. If any such factor is likely to result in landing with less than the planned FRF, the commander should declare ‘MINIMUM FUEL’ to ATC.

**Note 1:** The ‘MINIMUM FUEL’ declaration informs ATC that all planned aerodrome options have been reduced to a specific aerodrome of intended landing. It also informs the ATC that any change to the existing clearance may result in landing with less than the planned FRF. This is not an emergency situation but an indication that an emergency situation is possible, should any additional delay occur.

**Note 2:** The pilot should not expect any form of priority handling as a result of a ‘MINIMUM FUEL’ declaration. However, ATC should advise the flight crew of any additional expected delays, as well as coordinate with other ATC units when transferring the control of the aeroplane, to ensure that the other ATC units are aware of the flight’s fuel state.

It is important to note that a common element in every scenario is that each time MINIMUM FUEL is declared, the Commander has already committed to land at a specific aerodrome and is concerned that a landing may occur with less than final reserve fuel in the tanks. It is equally important to note that, although the coordinated escalation process (with ATC) related to the protection of final reserve typically occurs in 3 steps, each situation is different, and may be resolved at any stage in the process. The 3 steps in the escalation process are:

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

# PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

| Step 1
Request delay information when required. | Seek information from ATC concerning any expected delays. “ATC, request EAT, RYR 123”. |
| --- | --- |
| Step 2
Declare MINIMUM FUEL when committed to land at a specific aerodrome and any change in the existing clearance may result in a landing with less than planned final reserve fuel. | Declare “ATC, MINIMUM FUEL, RYR 123” which should represent the last lines of defence in a multilayered strategy designed to ensure the protection of final reserve fuel and safe flight completion. Practically speaking, the Commander should declare “MINIMUM FUEL” when, based on the current ATC clearance, the anticipated amount of fuel remaining upon landing at the aerodrome to which the aeroplane is committed is approaching the planned Final Reserve Fuel quantity. |
| Step 3
The commander shall declare an emergency when calculated usable fuel on landing, at the nearest adequate aerodrome where a safe landing can be performed, is less than final reserve fuel. | Declare Fuel Emergency using “MAYDAY, MAYDAY, MAYDAY, FUEL, RYR 123”. The last in a series of procedural steps to ensure the safe completion of a flight is the declaration of an emergency. The “MAYDAY” declaration is used when all opportunities to protect final reserve fuel have been exploited and in the judgment of the Commander the flight will now land with less than final reserve fuel remaining in the tanks. The word FUEL is used as part of the declaration simply to convey the nature of the emergency to ATC. It is also important to note an emergency declaration not only opens all options for pilots (e.g. available closed runways, military fields, etc.) but it also allows ATC added flexibility in handling an aeroplane. |

Note: Minimum Fuel declaration is required by the Standardised Rules of the Air SERA.11012 Minimum Fuel and Fuel Emergency. Crew must submit an ASR following a ‘Minimum Fuel’ call

# Ensuring a Safe Landing – Final Reserve Fuel Protection

The objective of the FRF protection is to ensure that a safe landing is made at any aerodrome when unforeseen circumstances may not allow to safely complete the flight, as originally planned.

The commander should always consider first planning a safe landing option and estimating whether this landing can be performed with more than the FRF. When this estimation indicates that the FRF can no longer be protected, then a fuel emergency should be declared and any landing option explored (e.g. aerodromes not assessed by Ryanair, military aerodromes, closed runways), including deviating from rules, operational procedures, and methods in the interest of safety (in accordance with OMA 1.4.3.1)

## 8.3.8 Adverse and Potentially Hazardous Atmospheric Conditions

The following definitions are listed:

- Thunderstorms
- Icing Conditions
- Turbulemce
- Windshear
- Jet Stream
- Volcanic Ash Clouds
- Heavy precipitation
- Sand Storms
- Mountain Waves
- Significant temperature Inversions

## 8.3.8.1 Thunderstorms

1. In the case of detection of a significant cell or storm, the flight crew should observe the following recommendations:

- To avoid a large storm, the flight crew should make decisions while still 40 nm from it;
- Deviate upwind instead of downwind of a cell (less probability of turbulence or hail);
- For storm-avoidance planning, consider the height of the storm:

- Avoid all yellow, red, or magenta weather radar returns by at least 20 nm
- Avoid all green, yellow, red, and magenta weather radar returns of cells taller than 28,000 ft by at least 20 nm
- Cells exceeding 35,000 ft should be considered extremely hazardous and further separation (in addition to the 20 nm) should be used

- If the top of cell is at or above 25,000 ft, do not overfly it to minimise the possibility of encountering turbulence;
- Do not attempt to penetrate a cell or clear its top by less than 5000 ft vertically, otherwise the aircraft may encounter severe turbulence;

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

- Avoid flying under a thunderstorm because of possible windshear, microbursts, severe turbulence, or hail.

![img-25.jpeg](img-25.jpeg)

If the Commander finds himself committed to flying through an area of thunderstorm activity, the following procedures are recommended.

2. Approaching the Thunderstorm Area

- Ensure that Crew Members’ and passengers’ safety belts or harnesses are firmly fastened and any loose articles are secured.
- One pilot shall control the aeroplane and the other monitor the flight instruments and electrical supplies continuously.
- Agree with ATC an altitude for penetration whilst ensuring adequate terrain clearance.
- Check the operation of all Anti-icing and De-icing equipment and operate all these systems in accordance with Boeing and/or instructions.
- Follow Boeing and/or Ryanair recommendations on the use of the flight director and autopilot.
- Continue monitoring the weather radar in order to select the safest track for penetration.
- Avoid flying in close proximity to a thunderstorm whenever possible.

3. Air Traffic Control Considerations

A pilot intending to detour around observed weather should obtain clearance from or notify ATC so that separation from other aircraft can be maintained. If for any reason the pilot is unable to contact ATC to inform the controller of his intended action, any manoeuvre shall be limited to the extent necessary to avoid immediate danger and ATC must be informed as soon as possible.

4. Within the Storm Area

Maintain control of the aeroplane whilst concentrating on maintaining a constant pitch attitude appropriate to climb, cruise or descent, by reference to the attitude indicators and avoid harsh or excessive control movements. Do not be misled by conflicting indications on other instruments. Do not allow large attitude excursions in the rolling plane to persist. Attempt to maintain the original heading.

Do not correct for altitude gained or lost through up and down draughts unless absolutely necessary.

Maintain the trim settings and avoid changing the power setting except when necessary to restore margins from stall warning or high speed buffet.

If trim variations due to the autopilot (auto-trim) are large, the autopilot shall be disengaged. Movement of the mach trim, where it occurs, is however necessary and desirable. Check that the yaw-damper remains engaged.

8.3.8.2 Icing Conditions

8.3.8.2.1 General

Boeing Aeroplanes are certified to all applicable airworthiness regulations regarding flight in icing conditions.

The definitive holdover times for approved fluids are those published from the Manual of Aircraft De-icing, (Engineering), which are presented in the Technical Log.

A Commander shall not commence a take-off unless the external surfaces are clear of any deposit which might adversely affect the performance and/or controllability of the aircraft except as permitted in the AFM. A Commander shall not commence a flight under known or expected icing conditions unless the aircraft is certified and equipped to cope with such conditions.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## 8.3.8.2.2 Icing Conditions

Icing conditions exist when the OAT on the ground or TAT in-flight is 10 degrees C (50 degrees F) or below and visible moisture in any form is present (such as clouds, fog with visibility of one mile or less, rain, snow, sleet, ice crystals), or standing water, slush, ice or snow is present on the ramps, taxiways or runways.

Aircraft in flight can encounter a variety of atmospheric conditions which will individually or in combination produce ice accretions on various components of the aircraft. These conditions include:

1.  Supercooled Clouds

Clouds containing water droplets (below 0°C) that have remained in the liquid state. Supercooled water droplets will freeze upon impact with another object. Water droplets can remain in the liquid state at ambient temperatures as low as -40°C. The rate of ice accretion on an aircraft component is dependent upon many factors such as droplet size, cloud liquid water content, ambient temperature, and component size, shape, and velocity;

2.  Ice Crystal Clouds

Clouds existing usually at very cold temperatures where moisture has frozen to the solid or crystal state;

3.  Mixed Conditions

Clouds at ambient temperatures below 0°C containing a mixture of ice crystals and supercooled water droplets;

4.  Freezing Rain and Drizzle

Precipitation existing within clouds or below clouds at ambient temperatures below 0°C where rain droplets remain in the supercooled liquid state.

5.  Freezing Fog (FZFG)

Fog (horizontal visibility reduced to less than 1,000 m) consisting mainly of super-cooled droplets that usually deposit rime or glaze on objects or surfaces with below freezing temperatures. The Outside Air Temperature range for freezing fog is -0.1°C to -30.0°C.

In freezing fog, reported as FZFG in a METAR or ATIS, or when the horizontal visibility is less than 1,000 m with an OAT below 0°C, the aircraft must be anti-iced (or de-iced if such conditions exist) prior to departure.

A cold soaked aircraft with a wing skin temperature below 0°C may in reduced visibility be exposed to similar conditions as in FZFG. The Commander must carefully consider the requirement for

page 8-294

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

de-icing/anti-icing to ensure that the external surfaces are clear and remain clear of any deposit which might adversely affect the performance and/or controllability of the aircraft.

## 6. Severe Icing Associated with High Ground

Some incidents of very severe aircraft icing have been experienced in proximity to high ground (the Alps, and Pyrenees); Vigorous developed frontal systems particularly warm fronts (high water vapour concentration) which become slow moving or stationary due to the influence of high ground, shall be treated as capable or generating icing. Fronts that stagnate in mountainous areas may be subject to orographic lifting which develop areas of freezing rain, embedded cumulo-nimbus clouds, intense icing and severe turbulence;

Such icing may be difficult to forecast and when encountered can build-up to several inches extremely rapidly. It may be encountered at low temperatures and higher altitudes where icing of such seventy would not be expected;

Heavy icing has also been experienced in mountains wave conditions.

Thrust settings need to be increased during descent in order to ensure adequate protection by maintaining sufficiently high temperature in the engine and wing anti-icing systems.

Types of ice deposits are detailed in 8.2.4.

Aircraft on the ground, while parked or during ground operations, are susceptible to accumulation of ice accretions in conditions peculiar to ground operations in addition to many of the conditions that can be encountered in flight (8.2.4). It is necessary to avoid re-contamination of the aircraft structure and systems after anti-icing/de-icing.

During the pre-flight inspection, the Commander is responsible for ensuring that the aircraft structure is clear of all snow, ice accretion and frost which could affect the safety of the aircraft in flight. The Pre-flight Inspection for contamination by frost, snow or ice prior to or after the de-icing/anti-icing process is set out in Section 8.2.4.

## 8.3.8.2.3 Procedures in Icing Conditions

The applicable procedures are presented detail in FCOM Vol 1.

## 8.3.8.2.4 Effects of Ice/Frost Accretion on Aircraft Performance and Flight Characteristics

### General

During flight operations ice will form on leading edges of various components, within forward-facing air intakes (e.g. engine inlets) and frontal areas of the airframe. During ground storage or operations, ice will form on other portions of aircraft components such as upper surfaces of wings, fuselages, engine nacelles, horizontal stabiliser surfaces, and control surfaces. The effects that in-flight or ground ice accretions will have on aircraft performance and flight characteristics are many, varied and highly dependent upon aircraft design, ice surface roughness, ice shape, and areas covered. These effects will generally be reflected in the form of decreased thrust, decreased lift, increased drag, increased stall speed, trim changes, and altered stall characteristics and handling qualities. Slight weight increases will also occur, however, the effect of weight increase (with the exception of heavy snow and freezing rain deposits) is usually insignificant in comparison to aerodynamic degradation.

Wind tunnel and flight testing conducted in the past under research, development, and certification efforts, as well as operational experience, has shown that ice accretions on various aircraft components can have very significant and sometimes devastating effects on aircraft equipment operation, aircraft performance, and flight characteristics.

The effects of ice accretions on some of these components and the contribution to degradation of aircraft performance and flight characteristics are itemised on the following list:

1. Slight surface roughness can have significant effects on stall speed, stall behaviour and power required to achieve or to sustain flight;
2. Increased surface roughness, due to ice accretion, on wing leading and trailing edges will produce additional drag and further reduce lift. Leading edge surface roughness is more significant on most aerofoils.

This effect of surface roughness on the leading edge tends to be under estimated generally and is very great on modem aerofoil sections;

3. Stall characteristics will change and, depending upon aircraft design, the nature of ice accretions can cause either violent stall or a slower progression of stall. In some aircraft, pitch-up tendencies may be greater and roll-off tendencies can be exaggerated.
4. Controllability may be reduced requiring more stick deflection for manoeuvres or stall recovery.
5. Power available may be reduced due to ice accretions on propellers or jet engine inlets.

page 8-296

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

# PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

6. Ice or excessive quantities of FPD fluid have been known to cause control surface flutter.
7. Trim effectiveness can deteriorate with the accumulation of ice on unprotected surfaces.
8. Power failures may occur due to intake duct icing, carburettor icing, ingestion of ice particles into jet engines or clogging of fuel tank vents and fuel caps.
9. Severe vibrations may occur due to asymmetric shedding of ice from propellers or rotor blades. Helicopter auto-rotation capability can be significantly changed or lost.
10. Control surfaces such as ailerons, elevators, and wing spoilers can freeze in place if water deposits, snow, and FPD fluids are not properly cleaned or drained from critical areas. Movement of flying and trim control cables, rods or jacks can be impaired by freezing moisture in unpressurised areas, making control surfaces immovable. Attention to sealing and the use of anti-freeze lubricants can ensure protection.
11. Wing flaps may be damaged if retracted with ice accretions adhering to critical areas.
12. Landing gear mechanisms may be damaged or frozen in place if not properly cleared of ice accretions.
13. Forward visibility may be lost or significantly reduced if windscreen anti-icing systems are not available or are not properly utilised.
14. Radio, radar, and other communications and navigation antennae may be damaged or efficiency reduced due to ice accretions.
15. Ventilation, air conditioning, and other air inlets can be blocked or flow restricted.
16. Ice dislodged from fuselage or wing sections, antennae and other components forward of engine inlets and other critical components can produce damage.
17. Ice accretions, under certain conditions, may not have noticeable effects on aircraft performance and flight characteristics, however, these effects may become quite apparent in the event of engine failure or other emergencies.
18. Flight, engine, and other instruments are subject to error if ice accretions exist on external probes, in pressure lines, or on areas forward of or adjacent to external probes. Operational experience indicates that typical sources of error are icing of pitot-static probes used for airspeed, altitude, and engine pressure ratio measurements.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

19. Automatic systems that utilise signal sources from external sensors such as Automatic Flight Control Systems, autothrottle speed command systems, or stability augmentation systems may be adversely affected by ice accretions on or in the vicinity of the sensors.

Residual moisture on door and cargo hatch seals may freeze under certain conditions causing leaks or seal damage, and the inability to open doors in an emergency.

## 8.3.8.2.5 Extended Operations in Icing Conditions

Although the process of certifying jet transport aircraft for operation in icing conditions involves many conservative practices, these practices have never intended to validate unlimited operations in severe icing conditions.

The safest course of action is to avoid prolonged operations in moderate to severe icing conditions.

## 8.3.8.2.6 CSFF

This is a condition whereby a thin deposit of hoar frost forms on surfaces which have been cold-soaked. In particular this hoar frost coating forms on the lower wing surface and on the upper wing surface above the fuel tanks, which contain very cold fuel (cold-soaked) as a result of extended operation in very cold temperatures.

It is a major safety issue that crews are aware of the effects of any precipitation falling on surfaces already cold-soaked, and the de/anti icing procedures contained in the FCOM Vol 1, in conjunction with the published holdover tables SHALL be applied.

## 8.3.8.2.7 Operation from Contaminated Runways and Taxiways

## 8.3.8.2.7.1 General

This section covers the general precautions and operating procedures that shall be taken and adhered to when operating from contaminated airport surfaces. Specific limitations, performance reductions, and operating procedures specific to aircraft type are contained the respective Flight Manual, and FCOM Vol 1, and QRH. Definitions of Contaminated Runways, Runway Surface Conditions including contaminant types and guidance material for Take-Off and Landing Performance Assessments are contained in OMB – Performance. The operational issues arising are detailed in the Ryanair Winter Operations Manual.

## 8.3.8.2.7.2 SNOWTAMs

Information on runway and taxiway state is provided by a SNOWTAM message transmitted by AIS.

Details of the SNOWTAM message format and the codes used are contained in the Winter Operations Guide.

## 8.3.8.2.7.3 Braking Action

A subjective description of airplane stopping capability used by flight crew to characterise the deceleration associated with the wheel braking effort and directional controllability of the aircraft.

Maximum manoeuvring speed on the ground with RWYCC 3 (MEDIUM) or below shall be 5 kt.

## 8.3.8.3 Turbulence

No flights shall be planned through areas with severe turbulence. For procedures refer to FCOM Vol 1 and SEP.

**Light:**

- Light Turbulence: Slight changes in attitude or altitude or changes in IAS of 5-15 kts.
- Light Chop: Slight bumpiness without changes in attitude, altitude or IAS. Occupants may feel a slight strain against seat belts though no difficulty is encountered in walking.

**Moderate:**

- Moderate Turbulence: Turbulence that is similar to light turbulence but of greater intensity, changes in attitude and or altitude occur, IAS fluctuates 16-25 kts but the aircraft remains in positive control at all times.
- Moderate Chop: Turbulence that is similar to light chop but of greater intensity causing rapid bumps or jolts without appreciable changes in attitude or altitude. Occupants feel definite strain against seat belts or shoulder harness. Unsecured objects are dislodged.

Food service and walking are difficult.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

# Severe:

- Severe Turbulence: Turbulence that causes large, abrupt changes in attitude or altitude. Aircraft may be momentarily out of control. IAS fluctuates by more than 25 kts. Occupants are forced against seat belts or shoulder harness. Unsecured objects are tossed about. Food service and walking are impossible.

The continuity of turbulence will be described as:

- Occasional – less than 1/3 of the time.
- Intermittent – 1/3 to 2/3 of the time.
- Continuous – more than 2/3 of the time.

Severe turbulence must be reported to ATC giving position, time (GMT), FL/Altitude, A/C type, intensity, in or near cloud and duration. If severe turbulence is encountered an ASR must be filed and a Technical Log entry made. The Technical Log page reference shall be included in the ASR to facilitate Engineering admin.

# 8.3.8.4

## Windshear

Wind shear is a variation in wind velocity (direction and/or speed) with distance in the vertical or horizontal plane. It can affect airspeed whenever an aircraft transverses from one wind velocity condition to another in less time than it takes for the aircraft to become adjusted in speed to the new environment.

## Windshear

Windshear encountered on take-off or approach must be reported to ATC immediately by R/T if considered a hazard, giving details of height and severity. Severe windshear is considered to be uncontrollable changes from normal steady flight conditions below 1000 AGL, in excess of the following:

- 15 kts indicated airspeed.
- 500 fpm vertical speed.
- 5° pitch attitude
- 1 dot displacement from the glideslope/glidepath.
- Unusual thrust levels for a significant period of time.

If severe windshear is reported or forecast – DO NOT TAKEOFF.

page 8-300

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

Full thrust must be used if the nature or severity of the windshear cannot be determined.

Reduced thrust may be used if any windshear expected during or after take-off is caused by strong winds not associated with CBs or frontal activity.

## Windshear Escape Maneuver

If a GPWS windshear warning (Two-tone siren followed by “WINDSHEAR, WINDSHEAR, WINDSHEAR”) is received, the windshear escape maneuver must be carried out as per the QRH MAN Windshear Escape Maneuver.

It is not permitted to conduct a third approach following two abandoned approaches due to windshear.

## 8.3.8.5 Jetstream

Jet streams are fast flowing, narrow air currents found in the Earth’s atmosphere. The main jet streams are located near the altitude of the tropopause, the transition between the troposphere and the stratosphere.

Jetstreams are indicated on SIG WX charts. Avoid flying along the edge of jetstreams due to the possibility of associated turbulence. Pilots will be aware of the effect of increased fuel consumption due to unexpected significant head wind components that can be experienced. It may be possible to avoid jetstreams by changing route and/or altitude.

## 8.3.8.6 Volcanic Ash Cloud

ORO.GEN.200

### Volcanic Ash

Flight in visible/discernible ash must be avoided. When operating in areas where volcanic activity may occur pilots shall be vigilant and aware of their responsibilities to report any activity immediately. Flights planned near or through any areas of forecast contamination will comply with the Ryanair Safety Risk assessment accepted by the IAA. The operating crew will be provided with the latest information. The responsibility to avoid flight in visible/discernible ash remains with the operating Commander.

## 8.3.8.6.1 Operations in the Context of Volcanic Ash; Overflight and Underflight of Ryanair NFZs

Ryanair will not plan or intentionally conduct operations in areas known to contain visible or discernible Volcanic Ash (VA).

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## Threat and Error Management (TEM)

The generic threat is unintentional operations in airspace or aerodromes with known or predicted contamination by a volcanic ash cloud with characteristics harmful to the airworthiness and operation of the Boeing 737.

Arising from this generic hazard are specific risks to Ryanair’s operation. These occur in maintenance, flight planning/dispatch and flight operations. A list of assessed hazards is located in the Hazard/Risk Identification &amp; Assessment Register maintained by the NPFO.

The Ryanair approach to managing its operations during a VA event is based on the TEM concepts of **Anticipation, Recognition** and **Recovery**. Anticipating potential hazards and risks associated with a future VA event builds vigilance at all levels in the company. Recognition of the existence of a threat allows FOPS and flight crew to deploy procedures and utilize their training to avoid the error of unintentional flight in levels of VA deemed to be harmful to the aircraft and its occupants.

The following paragraphs are grouped so that the activity undertaken by Flight Operations (FOPS) and Flight Crew are stated in each of the above categories.

In all cases, the fundamental principle underlying operations in the context of VA is to avoid flight in visible or discernible volcanic ash. Discernible ash is ash that can be sensed by human sight or smell or other senses.

## 8.3.8.6.2 Anticipation

## 8.3.8.6.2.1 Ryanair No Fly Zones

From the commencement of the Eruptive Stage of a VA event Ryanair will impose the following limits to its area of operations:

- A Ryanair No Fly Zone will be applied where the dimensions of a VAA/VAG overlap those of the WSI VA SIGMET. This combined graphic will be promulgated on a Ryanair No Fly Zone Chart. See below.

- Where a WSI VA SIGMET is not overlapped by the VAG Ryanair will regard this as a NFZ until the following has been established following discussion with a WSI forecaster:

- The basis for the existence of the area not coinciding with the VAG,
- When the forecast was made,
- Whether any new information has been considered (PIREPS, etc.) since the forecast,
- The level of confidence that WSI have in the accuracy of their forecast,
- An assessment of the risk of operating into that area.

page 8-302

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR
PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

- Following this analysis with WSI, the NPFO will conduct a risk assessment of operations onto this area of the WSI SIGMET. If the risk assessment results in a Tolerable or Acceptable category, then the operation may be planned and conducted. Otherwise this area of the WSI VA SIGMET will be regarded as a Ryanair No Fly Zone.

- The area in the VAG that does not coincide with the WSI SIGMET will be available to Ryanair operations for the planning and execution of flights. However, given that the VAAC has forecasted that some unspecified concentration of ash might be present in this area the following extra measures will be taken:

- Ryanair will use the Safety Reporting System to analyse Ryanair ASRs for reports of ash throughout its network.
- Ryanair will send an SMS message and e mail to all Ryanair Base Captains that will require reports of any VA activity (or the absence of any VA activity) in their base to be submitted as a PIREP.
- Industry reported PIREPs and other actual sightings of VA will be reviewed. Reports of No ash in areas where it had been forecast will be reviewed.
- The Volcanic Ash Advisory Group daily web conference will be used to inform flight operations of the conditions within this area.
- Flight crew will be provided with the following information.

- The Ryanair No-Fly Zone as depicted on the Ryanair VA NOTAM.
- Volcanic Ash Advisories and Graphics.
- Intervened modelled ash concentration charts (if available, and for supplementary reference only).

Ryanair is prohibited from operating in any Initial Danger Area as defined by an Area Control Centre (ACC) in accordance with ICAO Doc 019 – Volcanic Ash Contingency Plan EUR and NAT Regions, Section 1 Alerting Phase, Paragraph 1.2.1.

## 8.3.8.6.2.2 Accepted Safety Risk Assessments (SRA)

The IAA has accepted SRA proposals from Ryanair which permit:

- Ryanair to operate in areas forecast to be contaminated with VA contamination indicated on the applicable VAA/VAG that do not overlap with a WSI VA SIGMET.

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

- Ryanair to fly under and to overfly a NFZ when required, e.g. the vertical extent of the applicable zone may be from FL200 to FL350, and operations below FL200 and above FL350 are approved. The requirements of OMA Section 8.1.1 – Minimum Flight Altitudes apply to all flights conducted under the NFZ.

## 8.3.8.6.2.3 Training

OMD Section 3.1.4.3 contains a requirement for Ryanair flight crew to be trained in the emergency Volcanic Ash procedure as detailed in the Boeing 737 QRH during the recurrent three year simulator training cycle. The training can be either theoretical or simulator based at the discretion of the HT in consultation with the NPFO.

FOPS controllers are trained in the use of CFMU, the associated WSI FUSION airspace management tool and procedures specifically designed to manage operations in the context of no-fly zones during a VA event.

## 8.3.8.6.2.4 FOPS Control

A detailed VA Event Response Procedure has been prepared that commences with the ICAO defined Pre-Eruption stage. See FOPS Volcanic Ash Event Response Procedure. The NPFO has overall responsibility for ensuring the safety of flight operations. For the duration of a VA event, a Suitably Qualified (SQ) Captain will be available to OCC planning to assist in data interpretation and dissemination.

## 8.3.8.6.2.5 Pre Flight

Volcanic Ash Advisories and Graphs are those issued by Met Office VAAC which are published for various vertical blocks of airspace. Volcanic Ash information for pilots are available through Volcanic ASHTAMs and advisories as well as NOTAMs. These and the VA SIGMET are available through the LIDO website or company publication available to pilots.

A Ryanair VA NOTAM and RYR No Fly Zone Chart will be published by the OCC to the Pilots EFB via email within hours of a significant eruption. All charts must be downloaded, studied and included in flight briefing material carried on board.

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## 8.3.8.6.2.6 Escape Routes

Ryanair policy is to circumnavigate any VASH NFZ. Flight Operations Planning will implement this policy during any VASH event. Overflight may be planned on a case-by-case basis, OCC flight planners shall ensure that a 2000 ft vertical clearance above the NFZ in the event of driftdown. Where avoidance of NFZ requires delayed climb or early descent, this will normally be reflected in the OFP. In all cases verify that the revised FL assures clearance above MSA.

Ryanair will endeavour to minimize flight over NFZ zones and flight plan accordingly. Ideally crews shall avoid or minimize flight operations over a NFZ where possible. To minimize time under/over a NFZ in an emergency or atmospheric event an ETP shall be calculated and displayed on the FMC and ND. This process shall be accomplished as described below for each transit of a NFZ en-route.

Airports in the NFZ will not be considered as suitable destinations or alternates at the planning stage with this in mind all flights shall be planned to be the diversion distance of one hour flying time at an OEI cruising speed of suitable Airports at any point during the flight.

If weather avoidance is anticipated in the area below a NFZ do not plan to overfly weather to the extent that the NFZ would be penetrated. Consider 180 turn, reroute or divert. Do not climb into a NFZ.

Where turbulence Shear Value 5 or greater is predicted in the section of flight above the NFZ and performance requirements cannot be assured, plan to re file clear of the area of turbulence. In the event of an inadvertent CAT encounter, follow manufacturer's procedures including descent if required. Where possible, avoid flight in NFZ unless no other option is available.

Crew shall refer to ‘Drift-down’ tables from OMB – Performance Section 2.6.1 which are provided for all engine and airframe configurations used by Ryanair. These tables provide guidance on the ‘Distance to Drift-down in Nautical Miles’ and ‘Maximum Ash Cloud with Nautical Miles’

## 8.3.8.6.2.7 Flight Crew Threat and Error Management (TEM)

Crews must have heightened vigilance and apply good TEM techniques, especially Anticipation and Recognition to ensure a safe operation. Procedures designed to mitigate the effect of predicted concentrations of VA can themselves become a hazard with associated risk. An example of this occurs when aircraft may have been sealed to prevent penetration of VA while parked.

The engineering department may have sealed all ports and orifices of aircraft that have been parked in areas where VA may have fallen to the ground. These seals will be removed by engineers using an approved procedure. When conducting an

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

Exterior Inspection, Flight Crew must adhere to the requirements of FCOM VOL 1 NP Exterior Inspection to ensure that their responsibilities are fulfilled and that any omissions by an engineer will be trapped and rectified.

## 8.3.8.6.2.8 Hazard Location

While crews can be confident that their route and flight level are safe and free from hazardous levels of VA, VA levels may be higher than normal. Accordingly, crews must apply TEM and be vigilant complying with related checklists and be fully briefed on the advisory information provided by FOPS. Airborne weather radar systems used on commercial aircraft are not designed to detect volcanic dust and in general will not and cannot be relied on to do so.

## 8.3.8.6.2.9 Avoidance

Crews are reminded to be vigilant against the possibility of an inadvertent encounter with volcanic ash. Maintain a close listening watch for PIREPs or reports from ATC. Flight operations in a RYR NFZ or in known areas of VA are prohibited except in non-normal situations.

## 8.3.8.6.2.10 Reporting

- Crew members are required to advise ATC of any volcanic ash encounter (PIREP).
- Operations in areas of predicted volcanic ash must be reported by ASR-Volcanic Ash – detailing the extent of the time spent in the relevant zone.
- Operations which are the subject of an actual volcanic ash encounter must be reported by ASR- Volcanic Ash. The encounter must be recorded in the aircraft Technical Log and MAINTROL advised.
- Operations outside a RYR NFZ but within an area of predicted VA by the VAAC where no volcanic ash encounter has occurred shall be reported as set out above.

**Note:** It is important to establish where ash has been predicted but has not been encountered.

- Even if an aircraft enters a NFZ and no volcanic ash encounter has been established, a Technical Log entry shall be made to initiate post flight maintenance action.

## 8.3.8.6.2.11 Legalities

Flight crew must exercise increased diligence with regards to the status of the aircraft’s Pre Flight Inspection Status and the Hold Item List in the Aircraft Technical Log. If there is any doubt regarding the status of the aircraft’s airworthiness or compliance with routine maintenance procedures then Maintrol must be contacted immediately.

If crews are disrupted and/or are located away from base for any period of time they must exercise increased diligence with regards to the status of their Checks as required by regulation. These include, but are not limited to Medical, Line Check, LPC, OPC and recurrent Safety Training. If there is any doubt regarding the status of your Checks you must contact Crew Control immediately prior to operating.

## 8.3.8.6.2.12 Review

The review process will operate as follows:

- At least twice a day the NPFO and the Deputy Director Flight Operations (or nominated deputies) will review the following prior to deciding on the extent of schedule change to the next “wave”:
- The Volcanic Ash Advisories and Graphs
- The WSI VA SIGMET
- The plot of the RYR NFZs
- ASRs and VA Reports
- PIREPS from other operators.
- Input from the IAA, EACCC conference calls, VAAG Webex conferences and other such bodies.
- Ryanair will conduct further risk assessments as warranted by the situation or a change to the circumstances underpinning the current approval.

## 8.3.8.6.3 Recognition

### 8.3.8.6.3.1 Visible or Discernible Ash

Flight in visible or discernible volcanic ash must be avoided. At night and in IMC the Inboard Landing Lights must be selected ON in all areas predicted to contain VA.

Discernible ash is ash that can be sensed by human sight or smell or other senses. Ash may not be visible during darkness or daytime IMC. However, ash can be discerned by odour, haze, changing engine conditions, airspeed, pressurization and static discharges.

8.3.8.6.3.2 Low and Medium Levels of VA

- Static discharges. A phenomenon similar to St. Elmo’s fire or glow can occur. In these instances, blue-colored sparks can appear to flow up the outside of the windshield or a white or orange glow can appear at the leading edges of the wings or at the front of the engine inlets.
- Smoke/dust/haze. Most flight crews, as well as cabin crew or passengers, see a haze develop within the airplane. Dust can settle on surfaces.
- Odor. When encountering a volcanic ash cloud, flight crews usually notice a smoky or acrid odor that can smell like electrical smoke, burned dust, or sulphur.
- Frosting of windows. Caused by the abrasive action of the ash on the windshields.

  8.3.8.6.3.3 Active Ash Cloud

- An active ash cloud will be visible from a considerable distance.
- Changing engine conditions. Surging, torching from the tailpipe and flameouts can occur. Engine temperatures can change unexpectedly, and a bright orange glow can appear at the engine inlet.
- Airspeed. If volcanic ash fouls the pitot tube, the indicated airspeed can decrease or fluctuate erratically.

- Pressurization. Cabin pressure can change, including possible loss of cabin pressurization.

## 8.3.8.6.3.4 Post Flight

Ryanair has implemented CFM56-7B S/B 72-0789, Revision 02, December 14, 2011/LEAP-1B-72-00-0004-01A-930A-D. Accordingly, Ryanair has identified selected engines to their CFM representative for inclusion in a volcanic ash ingestion leading indicator program. Aircraft fitted with these selected engines can be identified by means of a Notice to Crew in the Aircraft Technical Log.

For the selected engines, the Commander must ensure that the following visual inspections after each flight in concentrations of volcanic ash are completed. Visually inspect the fan blades, inlet cowl, Outlet Guide Vanes (OGVs), spinner cone, splitter fairing, acoustical panels, and exhaust cowl for unusual damage or evidence of volcanic ash associated with erosion on the powerplant. Report unusual findings to Maintrol.

Pilots are also required to pay particular attention to windscreens and landing lights when performing exterior inspections, to look for any new signs of abrasion and to seek maintenance advice if they suspected that VA was encountered on the inbound flight.

## 8.3.8.6.3.5 FOPS Control

FOPS Control will be informed of a VA event by any or all of the agencies specified in the VA Event Response Procedure which will be actioned immediately under the supervision of the Deputy Director Flight Operations.

## 8.3.8.6.4 Recovery

## 8.3.8.6.4.1 VA Encounter

Whenever an ash encounter is confirmed when airborne, the QRH Volcanic Ash checklist shall be completed in its appropriate sequence. The following procedures, compatible with the B737 QRH, have been communicated to the IAA:

## 8.3.8.6.4.2 Commence the Manoeuvre (PF)

1. MCP HDG: Select reciprocal heading
2. HDG SEL: Engage
3. MCP ALT: Select Lower Altitude
4. LVL CHG: Engage
5. A/T: Disengage
6. THR Levers: Close

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

Exit the ash cloud as quickly as possible. Executing a 180-deg turn out of the ash cloud using a descending turn is the quickest exit strategy. Many ash clouds extend for hundreds of miles, so assuming that the encounter will end shortly can be false. Climbing out of the ash could result in increased engine debris build up as a result of increased EGT. The increased engine build up can cause total thrust loss.

## 8.3.8.6.4.3 Communicate (PM)

- Make a Mayday call. Inform ATC of intended heading and level. (PM)
- Set 7700 and select TA on the transponder mode selector. (PM)

## 8.3.8.6.4.4 Pan or Mayday

- The least level of distress declared will be a Mayday call. The MAYDAY can be downgraded to a PAN call if the aircraft’s condition or its impact on other traffic as a result of the above manoeuvre does not endanger it or other traffic.

## 8.3.8.6.4.5 Heading and Flight Level

- When the encounter is momentary in nature and there are sufficient visual indications and it can be confirmed that that the aircraft has cleared the VA, the Commander may elect to continue flying a course which is deemed to be free of VA.
- The crew are directed by the QRH to consider flying 180 degrees from the heading that existed at the time of the VA encounter. Ryanair crew will state the reciprocal heading that has been selected and activated. A crew shall initially select a heading 180 degrees away and remain on the new heading (terrain permitting) until it can be confirmed that the aircraft is clear of ash or other manoeuvring is required.
- Ryanair crew will confirm with ATC when they are clear of the ash, their intentions and when they can accept vectors again.
- Ryanair crew will state the required flight level/altitude that has been selected and activated. ATC may treat this manoeuvre as an Emergency Descent. In this case controllers may have to initiate emergency separation between following aircraft on the same track in order to provide separation between these aircraft and the aircraft turning and descending away from the ash. This is a matter for ATC to resolve and Ryanair crew will proceed as described above.
- The rate of descent will be very much less than that associated with a pressurisation failure induced emergency descent. This is because retaining altitude to increase time available is crucial to the success of the VA QRH checklist. Therefore the aircraft will descend at idle thrust, and at the speed that obtained at the time of the VA encounter. In order to reduce rate of descent and retain altitude, this speed may be reduced. This will give a variable rate of descent and no standard rate of descent can be predicted.

## 8.3.8.6.4.6 QRH Volcanic Ash Checklist

- Do the Volcanic Ash emergency checklist in the Boeing 737 QRH. Below is background information to the steps in the NNC intended to give crews a better understanding of the required actions. The main objective is to exit the ash cloud as quickly as possible and restart engines if needed.
- QRH Item 1: If volcanic dust fills the flight deck, the crew may need to use oxygen. Use flight deck oxygen at the 100 per cent setting.
- QRH Item 3: Turn the autothrottle off. This prevents the engines from increasing thrust above idle. Ash debris in the engine can result in reduced surge margins, and limiting the number of thrust adjustments improves the chances of engine recovery. If conditions allow, reduce thrust to idle immediately. By reducing thrust, engines may suffer less build up of molten debris on turbine blades and hot - section components. This reduces possible engine damage or flameout, or both, by decreasing EGT. Idle thrust allows engines to continue producing electrical power, bleed air for pressurization, and hydraulic power for airplane control.
- QRH Item 5: Turn ignition to FLT. In the event that the engines flame out or stall, use appropriate procedures to restart the engines. During restart, the engines may take longer than normal to reach idle thrust due to the combined effects of high altitude and volcanic ash ingestion. If an engine fails to start, try restarting it again immediately.
- QRH Items 7 &amp; 8: Turn on engine and wing anti-ice and set air-conditioning packs to HIGH flow. These actions improve the engine stall margins by increasing the flow of bleed air.
- QRH Items 9: If possible, start the Auxiliary Power Unit (APU). The APU can power systems in the event of a multiple-engine power loss. It can also be used to restart engines through the use of APU bleed air.
- QRH Item 10: Monitor engine Exhaust Gas Temperature (EGT). Engines may accelerate to idle very slowly, especially at high altitudes. Slow acceleration may be incorrectly interpreted as a hung start or an engine malfunction. If N2 is steadily increasing, and EGT stays within limits, the start is progressing normally.

## 8.3.8.6.4.7 TCAS

- Follow QRH procedure and select TA.

## 8.3.8.6.4.8 NATS Policy

### Safety and Procedural Considerations

- Within the various contamination areas it is for the aircraft operator and aircraft commander to determine that it is safe to operate the aircraft in such airspace. ATS providers are not required to question a pilot’s approval to fly through a Volcanic Ash Temporary Danger Area (TDA).

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

- ATS providers are to ensure that ATCOs and FISOs are aware that, should an aircraft unexpectedly encounter a significant concentration of volcanic ash, the pilot may (possibly without prior warning):

- Execute a 180° turn;
- Descend;
- Reduce engine power; and
- Disconnect auto-throttle.

- Pilots operating within an area of contamination may decline a vector or climb/descent instruction if that would take the aircraft into an area of higher contamination and if that would be contrary to the aircraft type and engine pairing tolerance level. A pilot may also request to leave an area if the aircraft is unable to sustain flight within the area. ATCOs and FISOs should accommodate any such request as expeditiously as operational safety considerations allow.

If the windscreen is obscured, and auto land capability is not available, it may be necessary to fly what is effectively an IMC approach and open the left or right Number 2 window to assess the landing flare.

8.3.8.6.4.10

## Non Normal or Emergency Procedures

In the event of a depressurization on a flight operating over a NFZ, an emergency descent may penetrate the NFZ. It is important to understand that flight in a NFZ may, or may not, result in a volcanic ash encounter. The only circumstances in which a Ryanair aircraft would intentionally be in a NFZ is as a result of a prescribed action to address a current emergency situation. In the case of depressurization an emergency descent in accordance with manufacturer’s procedures must be performed. Statistical data would indicate that the probability of a depressurization is very low.

Equally, statistical data would indicate that the probability of a VA encounter is very low. Therefore the possibility of a VA encounter combined with a depressurization is statistically highly unlikely.

In summary, various threat and associated recovery techniques are explained in the table below:

## 8.3.8.7 Heavy Precipitation

Heavy precipitation can quickly lead to high levels of runway contamination so runway clearance/drainage rate must be closely monitored in order to assess if a diversion is necessary.

When encountered whilst in flight, heavy precipitation can be associated with significant downdrafts and windshear. The specific procedures in the FCOM Vol.1 Limitations apply.

## 8.3.8.8 Operation in a Sandy or Dusty Environment

### Sandstorms

Dust or sand is raised from the ground by the wind and carried upwards to a height depending on the meteorological conditions and the size of the particles. Whenever the deterioration in visibility is serious the phenomenon is generally known as a dust storm or sand storm.

Large sand particles are too heavy to be lifted far and some sandstorms consist merely of this surface sand, no more than three or four feet deep. To an aircraft coming in to land the surface may be blanketed but hangars/buildings stand out.

Small sand/dust particles can be carried up through the whole turbulent layer to some 3,000 ft and remain in suspension as long as the wind blows with sufficient strength. This type of storm can be prolonged and widespread, or it may occur in patches in association with minor squalls of wind. The diurnal variation of windspeed is usually marked in the regions affected and such storms rarely occur at night.

Dust/haze exists when fine particles raised during dust storms are gradually dissipating in the atmosphere. This gives a general haziness but rarely results in visibility less than 1,000 m.

A HABOOB is a severe sand/dust storm. The term is now restricted to summer dust storms originating with CB clouds. These storms can be seen approaching, usually at considerable distances airborne, as a solid wall of dust. Its arrival is characterised by a sudden increase of wind and a rapid deterioration of visibility.

A more widespread type of dust storm occurs with strong winds over a long area of desert. Both the strength of the wind and instability of the lower layers contribute to the dust laden atmosphere. Such storms can continue for many hours and cover a considerable area, visibility being reduced to hundreds, sometimes tens of metres, while they may extend upwards several thousand feet. These may continue well into the night although they are then generally less severe.

Dust Devils and sand-pillars are small whirlwinds formed by convection. They are usually quite small and can exceed gale force. These shall be avoided.

### Sandstorms/dust storms constitute serious hazards to aircraft, and shall be avoided.

Operation in dust/sand storm poses a high risk of aircraft damage and may not be obvious to operating crew. Damage to engines, electronic equipment, pitot tubes and air conditioning systems is not unusual following a dust or sand encounter.

page 8-314

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR
PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

Should inadvertent aircraft exposure to Dust/Sandstorms occur enroute or whilst on the ground, contact Maintrol and the Duty Pilot prior to operating, file an ASR at the end of the duty.

The following dispatch and enroute planning criteria apply:

**Dispatch:**

OMA 8.1.2 applies. If an airport is forecasting Sandstorms (SS) or Dust storms (DS) ±1 hour of ETA, the aircraft may dispatch to the airport so long as two suitable alternates are available. Crew should select the furthest suitable alternate for the OFP and uplift fuel accordingly.

If conducting ground operations at an airport experiencing moderate to heavy DS/SS:

1. Confirm local disembarkation/embarkation restrictions with dispatcher. DO NOT commence boarding if boarding will necessitate the use of APU for Air conditioning.
2. Avoid APU usage if possible. If required, operate APU as per FCOM SP 16 Operation in a Sandy or Dusty Environment.
3. Do not start engines or attempt dispatch during active +DS/+SS or when visibilities reduce below 600 m.
4. Do not taxi on taxiways which are contaminated with drifting sand.
5. Contact MAINTROL and the Duty Pilot for guidance.

**Enroute:**

It is prohibited to commence an approach at an airport under the following conditions:

1. Active Heavy Sandstorms (+SS) or Heavy Dust storms (+DS) reported, or
2. Reported visibilities below 600 m or as per approach visibility requirements, whichever is greater.

## 8.3.8.9 Mountain Waves

### General

Mountain wave turbulence is a vertical air mass disturbance, the predominant feature of which a long wave like movement of the air, but within which higher frequency gusting may also occur. It is caused by strong winds blowing over and across high mountain ranges such as the Alps and characterised by complete upward and downward movement of the large mass of air covering a wide area. Where the mountains are very high the wave effects extend to very much higher altitudes, in certain cases to in excess of 40,000 ft. The blocking effect of the mountains causes a rising air motion and acceleration on the windward side and a descending motion and deceleration of the wind as the wave subsides to leeward.

The possibility of wave turbulence can be assessed by considering the upper wind strength and direction in relation to the orientation of the mountain range with the most likely geographical location to encounter turbulence being downwind of the mountain mass.

### Handling Procedures

The correct procedure for flying the aircraft in turbulence/severe turbulence/mountain wave conditions is described in detail in the Airplane Flight manual and in FCOM Vol 1, SP 16. The operational procedures for Jet Upset Recovery are presented in the QRH: Upset recovery Manoeuvre.

## 8.3.8.10 Significant Temperature Inversions

The most serious manifestation of significant temperature inversions is in the take-off/climb phase. Crews will notice higher than normal (up to and including red line EGT indications) in the initial climb, occasionally requiring thrust reduction to EGT limits, until clear of the inversion layer. This may present performance issues.

The maximum cruising altitude capability of the aeroplane can be significantly reduced if a temperature inversion, even of small magnitude, exists in the upper levels. This may prevent an aeroplane reaching its preferred cruising altitude. Should an aeroplane encounter an area of inversion once in the cruise at limiting altitude its buffet margins may be so eroded that a descent is necessary

The winds above and below the inversion are significantly different. The 2,000 ft wind in NYO on approach to RW 09 is frequently a strong tailwind, and below the inversion becomes a headwind. The unexpected and unforecast tailwind generally presents added difficulty in slowing down during the approach.

Temperature inversions at lower levels in the atmosphere are frequently associated with deteriorating visibility and can prevent the clearance of fog for prolonged periods.

## 8.3.9 Wake Turbulence

### 8.3.9.1 Causes of Wake Turbulence

Aircraft wake turbulence is caused by wing tip vortices generated by the production of aerodynamic lift and by jet efflux. Wing tip vortices behind a heavy transport aircraft can have sufficient intensity to cause control difficulties and the potential for a turbulence upset for a lighter aircraft which encounters them. They are consequently considered to be a safety hazard which shall be avoided or the effects minimised. Jet efflux does not create a significant hazard in the context of commercial aircraft operation. Care needs to be taken by larger aircraft to avoid jet blast damage while manoeuvring on the ramp, taxiing or during engine ground runs.

The intensity of vortex turbulence is directly proportional to aircraft weight and increases at high angles of attack (i.e. as airspeed is decreased). Thus the most severe vortex activity is generated and most likely to be encountered in the vicinity of the airfield as, in these areas, airspeeds will be low and aircraft taking off will be at maximum weight. Vortex generation starts at the lift-off point with rotation for take-off and stops on landing as the nosewheel contacts the runway.

Vortices are formed at the aircraft wing tip and rotate in the direction shown in Figure 1. They become fully established about 200 – 400 ft behind a large aircraft and have a downward sinking motion. When generated close to the ground, the vortices move downwards to ground level and then spread out laterally on either side of the runway in still air. In still air conditions, vortices will persist for several minutes while their energy slowly dissipates. In windy or turbulent conditions, this dissipation is more rapid. With a crosswind, the two vortices will move downwind. In a light crosswind, this may cause the upwind vortex to become more or less stationary along the runway centreline as shown in figure 2.

This could well be the most adverse case as dissipation will also be slower in the light wind. In slightly stronger crosswinds not only will both vortices be seen to move away off beyond the leeward edge of the runway, but they will also decay more rapidly.

The Airbus A380 (A380) has been assigned a Super-Heavy wake turbulence category. A Boeing 747-800 (B748) and the Boeing 787 family (B787) have been assigned a Heavy wake turbulence category and is therefore the same as a Boeing 747-400 (B744) for wake turbulence. The Boeing 737-800/8200 is medium.

Wake turbulence separation minima are based on a grouping of aircraft types into four categories according to the maximum certificated take-off mass as follows:

1. SUPER (J) – aircraft types listed as such in ICAO Doc 8643 ‘Aircraft Type Designators’, latest edition;

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

2. HEAVY (H) – all aircraft types of 136,000 kg or more, with the exception of aircraft types covered in point (1);
3. MEDIUM (M) – aircraft types of less than 136,000 kg but more than 7,000 kg; and
4. LIGHT (L) – aircraft types of 7,000 kg or less.

## 8.3.9.1.1 Arrivals

Arriving B737-800/8200 (both outside 4 DME) if following a:

- B747/B748/B787/A380 – 5 nm gap, 3 mins non radar;
- B757 – 4 nm gap;
- B737 – No min wake turbulence gap.

## 8.3.9.1.2 Departures

### Departing Criteria:

- The same runway;
- Parallel runways separated by less than 760 m (2,500 ft);
- Crossing runways if the projected flight path of the second aircraft will cross the projected flight path of the first aircraft at the same altitude or less than 300 m (1,000 ft) below;
- Parallel runways separated by 760 m (2,500 ft) or more, if the projected flight path of the second aircraft will cross the projected flight path of the first aircraft at the same altitude or less than 300 m (1,000 ft) below.

Departing B737-800/8200 from same position on a runway if following a:

- A380 – 3 mins;
- B747/B748/B787 – 2 minutes;
- B757 – No min wake turbulence gap;
- B737 – No min wake turbulence gap.

Departing B737-800/8200 from an intermediate holding point/intersection take-off if following:

- B744/B748/B787 (from full length) – 3 mins;
- B757 (from full length) – No min wake turbulence gap;
- B737 – No min wake turbulence gap.

## 8.3.9.2 Types of Upset

Figure 3 illustrates the type of turbulence deviations experienced when penetrating a vortex pair. When travelling in the same direction as the axis of the vortex core i.e. in the same direction as the preceding aircraft causing the wake, the effect can be a sink and loss of climb performance in the downdraft between the two vortex cores, or if laterally displaced relative to the cores a vigorous rolling action. When penetrating across a wake, the effect will be rapid pitch changes – up then down and finally up again.

## 8.3.9.3 Avoidance of Wake Turbulence

Aircraft have been classified by weight into “heavy”, “medium” and “light” and ATC apply distance separation criteria between the different classified types under radar control on the approach. Commanders may apply discretion as to acceptable separation between their aircraft and the previous take-off aircraft depending on wind conditions and expected lift-off point relative to the previous aircraft.

The downward displacement of the wake allows the following general rules for avoidance of wake turbulence caused by wingtip vortices of heavy swept wing aircraft to be formulated. In the absence of sufficient dissipating wind forces apply the following rules:

1. In following a large aircraft on approach it is desirable to fly the same, or a slightly higher path; but never a lower path. The use of a common ILS or VASI/glide slope by all aircraft is a desirable practice for this reason;
2. Flight directly under, and parallel to, the wake of another aircraft shall be avoided, because of the inherent sinking characteristic of the wake (See Fig.2).

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

If this is not possible, a take-off interval of at least two minutes is desirable. 9. Use extreme caution when taxing behind a heavy jet. Static test data indicate that the area of concern is within 750 ft behind the tail of a heavy jet;

10. When operating in the same VMC environment as a heavy jet and being provided with radar vectoring, at least 5 miles spacing behind the heavy jet may be expected in order to ensure that wake turbulence problems are not encountered;

11. These precautions do not apply to “Heavy” jets following “Heavy” jets;

12. Test data indicates potential wake turbulence problems may exist when parallel runways, separated by less than 3,500 are being used in conditions of crosswind. The wake turbulence on one runway may drift across and effect operations on the other runway. Use at least a two minute interval behind a heavy jet departing the same runway, parallel runway – separated by less than 3,500', or crossing runways, if the departure courses converge after take-off.

## 8.3.10 Crew Members at Their Stations

CAT.OP.MPA.210, AMC1 CAT.OP.MPA.210(b)

### General

#### Crew Members at Station – Flight Crew

During all critical phases of the flight, i.e. take-off, departure, approach and landing, all flight crew members required to be on flight deck duty, shall be at their station. (ORO.GEN.110(f))

During all other phases of flight, all flight crew members required to be on flight deck duty, shall remain at their station unless his absence is necessary for the performance of their duties in connection with the operation, or (before TOD) for physiological needs, provided at least one suitably qualified pilot remains at the controls of the aeroplane at all times. After TOD visits to the lavatory shall not occur.

**Note:** Flight crew shall not leave the flight deck in flight to intervene with an unruly passenger incident.

### Checking CBs in Flight

It is best practise for crew to remain in their seats. Normally a crew can check CBs without vacating a seat. If it is necessary to vacate a crew seat for CB inspection the following guidance applies:

1. The Captain becomes the PF and remains in his seat.
2. Select “speaker” with volume turned up on ACP 1 &amp; 2.
3. Select volume up on VHF 1 (operational ATC frequency) and VHF2 (121.5 usually).
4. The F/O will complete the CB inspection.

#### Crew Members at Station – Cabin Crew

On all decks of an aeroplane that are occupied by passengers, required cabin crew members shall be seated at their assigned stations during take-off and landing, and whenever deemed necessary by the Commander in the interest of safety.

The minimum altitude for releasing the CCMs from their seats shall be 3,000 ft AGL. This is achieved by pressing the Cabin Crew call bell once. When releasing the cabin, crews are reminded that the safety of the operation must take precedence over passenger service requirements. If the Commander considers that the departure routing could be affected by adverse weather he will delay releasing the Cabin Crew from their seats. Where weather forecasts indicate the possibility of adverse weather the Cabin Crew should be briefed prior to departure.

## 8.3.10.1 Controlled Rest for Flight Crew

CAT.OP.MPA.210

All crew members have a responsibility to be properly rested before flight and crews are expected to report fit for duty. However, in order to mitigate against unexpected fatigue, a controlled rest procedure for flight deck crew may be used on flights in compliance with the procedure below:

Controlled rest means a period of time ‘off task’ in the pilot’s seat and may include a period defined as ‘actual sleep’. Controlled rest may be used at the discretion of the Commander to manage unexpected fatigue. It cannot be planned prior to a flight. Unexpected fatigue is most likely to occur during extended periods of crew inactivity. Controlled rest can be used to mitigate against the effects of unexpected fatigue and increase alertness levels during the later phases of flight.

Controlled rest shall be used in conjunction with other on board fatigue management countermeasures such as, bright cockpit illumination at appropriate times, balanced eating and drinking, intellectual activity and physical exercise. A maximum rest time of 45 minutes has been chosen to limit deep sleep with consequent long recovery time (sleep inertia). The use of Controlled Rest procedure shall be in accordance with the following guidelines:

- Controlled rest shall only take place in the cruise during periods of low workload as determined by the commander.
- Controlled rest periods shall be agreed according to individual needs and the accepted principles of CRM.
- The Commander shall identify and brief the threats associated with the use of controlled rest on a particular flight.
- Only one crew member at a time shall take a controlled rest.
- The full harness shall be used/locked and the flight deck seat positioned to minimise unintentional interference with the controls.
- The commander shall ensure that the operational duties (including level change, frequency change, FIR crossing, etc.) of the non-resting crew member are clearly identified before the commencement of the rest period.
- The rest period shall be no longer than 45 minutes (in order to limit any actual sleep to approximately 30 minutes).
- After this 45-minute rest period, there should be a recovery period of 20 minutes during which sole control of the aeroplane will not be entrusted to the pilot who has availed of controlled rest.
- The non-resting flight crew member must remain alert. This may be achieved with the aid of crew alarm systems (clocks, etc.), but must include frequent cabin crew checks.

page 8-324

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

- The commander shall inform the SCCM of the intention of the flight crew member to take controlled rest, and the time of the end of that rest.
- The commander shall inform the SCCM that the F/D will call the cabin at 20 min intervals and if the call has not been initiated by the F/D the SCCM shall call the F/D in accordance with SOP.
- A minimum 20 minute period shall be allowed between rest &amp; recovery periods to overcome the effects of sleep inertia and allow for adequate briefing.
- If necessary, a flight crew member may take more than one rest period, if time permits, on longer sectors, subject to the restrictions above.
- Controlled rest &amp; recovery periods will terminate at least 30 minutes before top of descent.
- In the event of a non-normal situation arising or illumination of a Master Caution, the controlled rest period shall be immediately terminated.

## 8.3.11 Use of Restraint Devices for Crew and Passengers

CAT.OP.MPA.225

### Seats, Safety Belts and Harnesses – Crew Members

During take-off and landing, and whenever deemed necessary by the Commander in the interest of safety, each crew member shall be properly secured by all safety belts and harnesses provided.

### Flight Crew – Wearing of Safety Belt and Safety Harness

On boarding the aircraft, Commander and co-pilot will adjust and check the serviceability of the harness and its control and release mechanism.

Safety belt, and shoulder-harness will be worn by the occupants of all cockpit seats:

1. Prior to pushback, if practicable, but not later than commencement of taxi;

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

2. Below 10,000 ft;
3. Until vacating the runway after landing;
4. If a cockpit window cracks or de-laminates when the aircraft is pressurised;
5. At the first indication of circumstances which may effect the safety of the flight (indication of possible engine shutdown, etc.);
6. In conditions of turbulence.

Safety belts will be worn by Flight Crew members in the following circumstances:

1. By one Flight Crew member at the controls at all times;
2. By all Flight Crew members when occupying a pilot's seat.

## Seats and Safety Belts – Passengers

Seat belts shall be used as long the seat belt sign is on. If allowed by the Commander, a limited service may continue if the seat belt sign is switched on during flight. When the seat belt sign is switched on before approach, the Cabin Crew(s) are expected to complete their work in order to prepare the cabin for landing, until the message “Cabin Crew seats for landing” is announced at which point cabin crew are expected to take their seats for landing.

Before take-off, all passengers shall be briefed on how to fasten and unfasten seat belts.

Before take-off and landing, and during taxiing, and whenever deemed necessary in the interest of safety, the cabin crew shall ensure that each passenger on board occupies a seat with his safety belt, or harness where provided, properly secured.

Multiple occupancy of aeroplane seats shall only occur by one adult and one infant who is properly secured by a supplementary loop belt or other restraint device.

The seat belts shall be fastened whenever the fasten seat belt sign is on.

**Note:** As a precaution, during fuelling and defueling, the seat belt sign shall be OFF, and the seat belts shall be unfastened.

## 8.3.12 Admission to Flight Deck CAT.GEN.MPA.135; ORO.GEN.140

Ryanair ensures that no person, other than a flight crew member assigned to a flight is admitted to, or carried in the flight deck unless that person is:

- an operating crew member or

- a representative of the IAA or an inspecting authority if required to be there in the performance of his duties
- a person authorised by the NPFO

The commander shall ensure that:

1. admission to the flight crew compartment does not cause distraction or interference with the operation of the flight; and
2. all persons carried in the flight crew compartment are made familiar with the relevant safety procedures.

The commander shall make the final decision regarding the admission to the flight crew compartment.

## 8.3.12.1 Persons Entitled or Authorised to Use Aircraft Jumpseats

Inspectors of the Irish Aviation Authority have rights of access and use of the jumpseat in the exercise of their official duties. The NPFO may authorise members or employees of aviation organisations or aeronautical agencies to travel in the jumpseat for specific observation duties or familiarisation. In the exercise of discretion when giving approval to occupy the jumpseat, Commanders will consider both the suitability of the person and the best interests of the Company.

The NPFO may authorise any person to travel in any seat in the cabin and in vacant flight deck and cabin jumpseats, including without a ticket.

## 8.3.13 Use of Vacant Crew Seats/Jumpseats

The jumpseat may only be occupied at the discretion and with the express approval of the Commander. This approval may not unreasonably be withheld for legitimate checking personnel, training and familiarisation, observers monitoring aircraft systems or performance, or positioning crew.

The Commander shall satisfy himself that a jumpseat passenger is not of any known or apparent mental instability, is not under the influence of alcohol or drugs or any prohibited or prescribed substances, is able bodied/not prevented through any infirmity from reacting correctly to instructions and reacting correctly in an emergency situation.

The Commander of the aircraft is ultimately responsible for the safety and security of the aircraft and must satisfy themselves with the identity of all jumpseat passengers prior to departure and ensure that jumpseat occupancy will not endanger the aircraft or its occupants. OMA 8.2.3 refers. Prior to refusing flight deck or cabin jumpseat occupancy to any personnel or person(s) listed below, contact the Duty Pilot.

## 8.3.13.1 Flight Deck Jump Seats

To comply with security requirements, personnel occupying a flight deck jumpseat shall satisfy one of the following criteria:

- IAA (or NAA-National Aviation Authority) personnel with valid ID carrying out official duties;
- Authorised Officials of the Irish Department of Transport with valid ID carrying out official duties;
- Personnel conducting NPFO approved activities (e.g., Air Traffic Control/staff orientation) in possession of a duty travel boarding pass, with advance notice given to the Captain;
- Ryanair Group FOSA/Compliance Auditors with valid company ID;
- Ryanair Group Management Pilots with valid company ID;
- Ryanair Group personnel with valid company ID on staff travel, duty travel, or company related travel when seats are not available in the cabin (including cabin crew jumpseats);
- Ryanair Group Pilots in uniform with valid company ID on privilege travel positioning to/from base or duty location(s) when seats are not available in the cabin (including cabin crew jumpseats), or;
- Instructors with valid company ID travelling to SIM/Training locations to carry out duties.

**Note 1:** Company ID may be any Ryanair Group identification. The personnel’s credentials (e.g., CIC, AIC, Company issued/recognised IDs) shall be verified prior to boarding.

**Note 2:** Personnel on operational duties requiring occupation of a flight deck jumpseat are permitted to occupy a flight deck jumpseat regardless of seating availability in the cabin.

The flight deck jumpseat safety briefing shall be completed prior to dispatch.

Refer to the Operational Self-Briefing – Jumpseat Policy for additional guidance.

Revenue passengers are not permitted to occupy a jumpseat.

To comply with security requirements, personnel, or person(s) occupying a cabin jumpseat shall satisfy one of the following criteria:

1. Personnel entitled to occupy a flight deck jumpseat in accordance with OMA 8.3.13.1 above, or
