## 8.3.13.3 Locked Flight Deck Door Concept

ORO.SEC.100

### Normal Procedures

The FLIGHT DECK DOOR must be locked once the forward entry door is closed and should remain locked at all times until the completion of the flight, indicated by the opening of the forward entry door. Formal confirmation of the door being locked prior to departure will be made at the “FLIGHT DECK WINDOWS AND COCKPIT DOOR” challenge on the “BEFORE START CHECKLIST”.

Communications with the Cabin Crew while the cabin door is locked will be via the CABIN INTERPHONE.

At any time that the main aircraft doors are closed with passengers on board the Flight Crew shall not allow entry to the flight deck except in an emergency or in circumstances where one crew member can safely leave his/her seat to verify visually through the viewing lens that the vestibule area is clear.

A crew member shall not leave his/her seat during critical phases of flight for the purpose of opening the flight deck door. Critical phases of flight are defined as the phase of operation from the commencement of taxi to top of climb or from top of descent to the parking gate.

Ideally crews would not normally open the flight deck door or leave the flight deck until established in the cruise

Should either the Captain or First Officer find it necessary to leave the flight deck during flight the following additional procedures apply:

1. Fasten Seat Belt Sign...ON.
2. Establish communication with the SCCM and confirm that all passengers are seated.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

3. The SCCM must remain in the forward galley area while the cabin door is unlocked and the Captain or first Officer has vacated their seat.
4. The door shall only be opened from inside by means of the door handle.
5. A CCM shall lock the door and remain on the flight deck and verify that the immediate area is clear before allowing access to the returning crew member.

Note 1: The door shall only be unlocked with the agreement of either both pilots, or the pilot and CCM on the flight deck.

Note 2: The Cabin Crew member shall NOT operate any control on the flight deck or occupy any seat at the controls of the aircraft.

Note 3: It is a requirement that a crew member in the flight deck be standing directly facing the door in the flight deck whenever the door is open. It is not permitted to use the door stop to lock or hold the door in the open position.

Note 4: The Cabin Crew member shall not be ‘NEW’ crew member as defined by their certificate of competence.

Note 5: The pilot remaining on the flight deck must wear a headset as a PLOC prevention strategy.

The flight deck door must not be left open or unlocked at any time.

## 8.3.14 Incapacitation of Crew Members

Pilot incapacitation represents an operational risk and is defined operationally as “any physiological or psychological state or situation that adversely affects performance”. There are degrees of incapacitation and strategies to manage these are detailed in this section.

## 8.3.14.1 Crew Member Incapacitation

When a flight crew member is incapacitated, control of the aircraft reverts to the other pilot. After landing, the Commander may elect to taxi the aircraft to the parking location as the F/O has access to thrust and brakes to stop the aircraft at any time. The Commander (or next senior crewmember if the Commander was incapacitated) shall contact Ryanair Operations and the Duty Pilot as soon as practicable.

Operations/Duty Pilot will inform Crewing and Rostering if the crewmember is being stood down due to incapacitation, and the crewmember shall not be assigned flight duties until the medical requirements applicable to that licence/attestation holder are completed.

page 8–330

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## 8.3.14.1.1 Guidance for Flight Crew After Use of Emergency Oxygen in Flight

If Emergency Oxygen is used as part of a QRH Checklist (Rapid decompression/Smoke Fire Fumes etc.) contact the Duty Pilot and, if the decision is to stand down the crew or crew member, the minimum time is one rest period.

## 8.3.14.2 General

Incapacitation is a real air safety hazard which occurs more frequently than many of the other emergencies which are the subject of routine training. Incapacitation can occur in many forms varying from obvious sudden death to subtle, partial loss of function. It occurs in all age groups and during all phases of flight.

Sudden and total incapacity are obvious but subtle incapacity is insidious and may be difficult to recognise and take appropriate action. This section sets out intervention criteria for different phases of flight which are particularly important as the basis for the other pilot making a corrective input or taking control of the flight.

## 8.3.14.3 Recognition

The keys to early recognition of incapacitation are deviations or variation from SOP, path control, and, standard calls and responses which may be identified by the application of:

1. Standard Operating Procedures.
2. Routine monitoring and cross-checking of flight instruments, particularly during critical phases of flight.
3. Use of crew concept method of operation.
4. Use of the “Two Communications Rule”.

Flight Crew shall suspect “subtle incapacitation” if a crew member does not respond appropriately to two verbal communications, OR a crew member does not respond appropriately to a verbal communication associated with a significant deviation from a standard flight profile.

“Subtle” incapacitation means what it says! Any lack of, or inappropriate response to normal stimuli should cause the remaining Flight Crew member to consider some level of incapacitation.

## 8.3.14.4 Action

If an incapacitation occurs: (time permitting refer SEP Manual 5.1.11.8).

1. The remaining Flight Crew member shall take over the controls and maximise use of “automatics”.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

2. “No.1 to the Flight Deck”, admit CSS after receiving correct password using flight deck access system switch.
3. Declare PAN or MAYDAY as appropriate to the severity of the incapacitation. ATC will acknowledge your MAYDAY.
4. The incapacitated Flight Crew member will be appropriately restrained, and his seat will be moved back to ensure this crewmember has no access to controls.
5. Cabin Crew will be used (minimum number of two) to remove the incapacitated Flight Crew member from his seat if required, in accordance with OMA-SEP procedures, e.g., CPR/AED.
6. If there is a Ryanair Group qualified pilot on board, (subject to verification of ID), this pilot may assist and occupy a pilot seat. A non-Ryanair Group pilot may NOT occupy a pilot seat. Cabin crew may NOT occupy a pilot seat.
7. Conduct PIOSEE, NITS, PA, plan to land at nearest suitable airport and advise ATC.

## 8.3.14.5 Overview Guidance

If you don't feel well – SAY SO! Let the other Flight Crew member fly. The fully fit Flight Crew member takes over and keeps the aircraft under control maintaining a safe flight profile.

## 8.3.14.6 Operational Concept

### Two-communication Rule

The “two-communication” rule was developed to meet the need for a method of detecting subtle (including cognitive) incapacitations before they become operationally critical. The rule states: “Flight crew members should have a high index of suspicion of a ‘subtle’ incapacitation any time a crew member does not respond appropriately to two verbal communications, or any time a crew member does not respond appropriately to any verbal communication associated with a significant deviation from a standard operating procedure or a standard flight profile.”

### Obvious and Subtle Incapacitation

Incapacitations can be divided into several operational classifications such as impairment, cognitive, obvious and subtle. Obvious incapacitations are those immediately apparent to the other crew members. The course of onset can be “sudden” or “insidious” and complete loss of function can occur. Subtle incapacitations are frequently partial in nature and can be insidious because the affected pilot may look well and continue to operate but at a less than optimum level of performance. The pilot may not be aware of their problem or capable of rationally evaluating it.

page 8-332

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR
PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## Cognitive Incapacitation

A particular category of incapacitations has been identified as “cognitive.” The problem created by these incapacitations is how to deal with a pilot who is “mentally disoriented, mentally incapacitated or obstinate, while physically able and vocally responsive.” In most cases of cognitive incapacitation, the pilot demonstrates manifestly inappropriate behaviour involving action or inaction, and the inappropriate behaviour is associated with failures of comprehension, perception, or judgement.

## Crew Member Impairment

Gastroenteritis accounts for 75% of ‘incapacitation’ episodes. Recent UKCAA CMO guidance on gastroenteritis uses the term ‘impairment’ when the crewmember can continue to function as the Pilot Monitoring. This guidance advises crew members to seek AME advice if symptoms are not resolved after 48 hrs.

## Control of the Incapacitation Risk

This is dependent upon effective operational monitoring. All flight crew members must be aware of the aircraft status. This is one of the most important reasons for following standard operating procedures (SOPs), flying standard flight profiles, and making standard calls and responses, particularly when physical control inputs are unnecessary, e.g., auto-coupled operations.

## Duty Pilot (DP) Support

The DP shall be contacted for all incapacitation events, including impairment, referred to in this section. Upon notification, the DP shall liaise with OCC and notify the associated NPFO to obtain guidance on required process and facilitation.

## 8.3.15 Cabin Safety Requirements

## 8.3.15.0 Cabin Safety Requirements

All Ryanair aircraft are equipped with means for emergency evacuation including ropes in the flight deck and slides attached to L1, L2, R1 and R2 doors on the B737-800 and MED L, MED R on the B737-8200 only. This is in compliance with CAT.IDE.A.265.

All Ryanair aircraft are equipped with Life Jackets in compliance with CAT.IDE.A.285(a).

Ryanair aircraft are equipped with floatation devices, in compliance with CAT.IDE.A.285(a)(1).

For take-off and landing all means of assistance for emergency evacuation that deploy automatically are armed. CAT.OP.MPA.220.

## 8.3.15.1 Cabin Preparation CAT.OP.MPA.230

### Stowage of Baggage and Cargo

Only hand baggage which can be adequately and securely stowed is allowed to be carried in the passenger cabin. All baggage on board which might cause injury or damage, or obstruct aisles and exits if displaced, must be adequately and securely stowed to take account of the following:

1. Each item must be stowed only in a location that is capable of restraining it;
2. Mass limitations placarded on or adjacent to stowages must not be exceeded;
3. Under-seat stowage's must not be used unless the seat is equipped with a restraint bar and the baggage is of such a size that it may adequately be restrained by this equipment;
4. Items must not be stowed in toilets or against bulkheads that are incapable of restraining articles against movement forwards, sideways or upwards and unless the bulkheads carry a placard specifying the greatest mass that may be placed there;
5. Baggage placed in lockers must not be of such a size that they prevent latched doors from being closed securely;
6. Baggage must not be placed where it can impede access to emergency equipment; and
7. Checks must be made before take-off, before landing, and whenever the commander illuminates the fasten seat belts signs to ensure that baggage is stowed where it cannot impede evacuation from the aircraft or cause injury by falling (or other movement) as may be appropriate to the phase of flight.

## 8.3.15.1.1 Cellos/Paintings/Bulk Items in the Cabin

In exceptional cases it may be necessary to carry bulky and/or fragile items of baggage, e.g. cellos, or fine art paintings, in the cabin. This can only be done provided that the Commander checks that the baggage item is adequately secured/lashed to passenger seats and confirms that aisles, emergency exits, and escape paths are not obstructed, and that an emergency evacuation will not be impeded. In these cases the passenger purchases a seat for the item. The item is included in the headcount and is noted on the LID SI Box as 'ITEMSEAT' or on the paper loadsheet. "TOB 160, including 1 cello". This TOB figure is documented on the Electronic Flight Log/Paper Voyage report/journey log.

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## 8.3.15.1.2 Securing of the Passenger Cabin and Galleys CAT.OP.MPA.230

The CSS will ensure that before taxiing, take-off and landing all exits and escape paths are unobstructed, and that all equipment and baggage is properly secured.

With regard to galley items a specific check must be made that containers and trolleys are secured with restraining levers/bars, locker doors are latched, all equipment secured with the appropriate devices provided, and all fluids in open containers such as hot cups emptied.

The CSS will report to the Commander that the cabin is “SECURE” for take-off and landing having complied with the actions and checks required, and that the other Cabin Crew have fastened their safety harnesses.

The Commander shall ensure that this report is received before take-off and landing, and whenever deemed necessary in the interests of safety, that all equipment and baggage is properly secured. (If the cabin has not been secured before landing a go-around is required.)

The CSS will then return to her/his assigned position for take-off and landing and fasten her/his safety harness.

## 8.3.15.2 Passenger Seating in the Context of an Emergency Evacuation CAT.OP.MPA.165

Passengers must be seated where, in the event that an emergency evacuation is required, they may best assist and not hinder evacuation from the aircraft. In the preparation for an emergency evacuation (time available) (CAT.OP.MPA.220) able bodied persons (ABPs) will be briefed and re-seated to assist at emergency exits in accordance with the Ryanair Safety and Emergency Procedures Manual. Passengers are seated where they, in the event of an emergency evacuation, may best assist and not hinder the evacuation from the aircraft. Those passengers who are allocated direct access seats must appear to be reasonably fit, strong and able to assist the rapid evacuation of the aircraft in an emergency. (Please refer to SEP 5.4.5 for type specific reserved seating procedures.)

Cabin Crew must ensure the following are not seated in direct access seats:

- Passengers under 16 years of age.
- Passengers unwilling or unable to help in the event of an emergency.
- Passengers travelling with an infant.
- Persons who necessitate the use of a seat belt extender (which could get tangled in an emergency).
- Passengers with reduced mobility, blind/visual impairment or who require special assistance.

## 8.3.15.4 Procedures for Refuelling/Defuelling with Passengers on Board or Embarking/Disembarking CAT.OP.MPA.195

See OMA Section 8.2.2.

These procedures also comply with various Airport Directives, Bylaws and industry standards and meet the Company's obligation to its passengers to operate safely.

The procedures require the liaison and co-ordination of the Ramp Agent, the Engineer or other person supervising the refuelling, the Flight Crew members and CCMs on board.

One qualified pilot shall be in the flight deck during fuelling operations with passengers on board. This pilot shall be capable of handling emergency procedures concerning fire protection and fire-fighting, handling communications and initiating and directing an evacuation.

The following are the minimum egress requirements:

1. Aircraft equipped with automatic inflatable slides;
2. The main door normally used for passenger disembarkation open, clear of obstructions and manned;
3. Loading bridge, gangway, or passenger or internal aircraft steps in position at the aircraft door;
4. Rear exit doors closed or, if open, passenger steps positioned at the open door;
5. The areas beneath all exit doors clear of obstructions; and
6. Aisle to be kept clear, except for passengers embarking or disembarking with their carry-on luggage.

## 8.3.15.5 Carriage of Special Categories of Passengers (SCPs) CAT.OP.MPA.155, AMC1 CAT.OP.MPA.155(b)

1. Persons requiring special conditions, assistance and/or devices when carried on a flight shall be considered as SCPs including at least:

a. Persons with Reduced Mobility (PRMs) who, without prejudice to Regulation (EC) No 1107/2006, are understood to be any person whose mobility is reduced due to any physical disability, sensory or locomotory, permanent or temporary, intellectual disability or impairment, any other cause of disability, or age;

b. infants and unaccompanied children; and

c. deportees, inadmissible passengers or prisoners in custody.

2. SCPs shall be carried under conditions that ensure the safety of the aircraft and its occupants according to procedures established by Ryanair.

3. SCPs shall not be allocated, nor occupy, seats that permit direct access to emergency exits or where their presence could:

a. impede crew members in their duties;

b. obstruct access to emergency equipment; or

c. impede the emergency evacuation of the aircraft.

4. The commander shall be notified in advance when SCPs are to be carried on board.

## Direct Access Seat

‘Direct access’ means a seat from which a passenger can proceed directly to the exit without entering an aisle or passing around an obstruction.

Direct access Seating on the Boeing 737-800 is Rows 1ABC, 16 and 17

Direct access Seating on the Boeing 737-8200 is Rows 1BC, 2ABC, 17, 18, 28 and 29.

## 8.3.15.6.1 Policy

Ryanair operates a no-smoking policy on all its flights. There shall be no smoking in any part of the aircraft at any time by any person.

## 8.3.15.6.2 Implementation

The passenger pre-flight briefing covers the smoking regulations.

## 8.3.15.7 Infectious Disease Suspected on Board

In cases of suspected communicable/infectious disease on board the commander shall be notified, and he shall promptly advise ATC and request that port health is notified with the information listed below:

1. Aircraft identification;
2. Departure aerodrome;
3. Destination aerodrome;
4. Estimated time of arrival;
5. Number of persons on board;
6. Number of suspected cases on board; and
7. Nature of the public health risk, if known.

OCC shall be advised by the most expeditious means and shall provide the most up to date instructions to the crew as soon as practicable.

## Port Health (Public Health)

Every international airport in the world has a Port Health Unit. The function of the unit is:

- Health control – The prevention of importation of communicable diseases.
- Medical surveillance of all immigrants.

As a matter of routine they will only meet flights if they have been requested to do so by an airline’s Operations Department.

A request for Port Health will usually cause a short delay at the end of the flight. Doctors from Port Health will assess the nature of the illness, the number of people affected, the flight details, and where the aircraft has come from, before allowing any passengers or Aircrew to disembark. They may then refer the suspected passengers to isolation and may take details of nearby passengers, e.g. four rows in front and four behind. The Public Health Inspector will give surveillance forms to those who require them.

## 8.3.16 Contents and Timing of Passenger Briefing CAT.OP.MPA.170; AMC1 CAT.OP.MPA.170

## 8.3.16.1 Prior to Boarding

At check-in attention of passengers is drawn to the list of prohibited articles, including dangerous goods, displayed and which they are not allowed to carry on their person, or in their hand baggage. They are questioned on the packing of checked baggage and the inclusion of certain articles, see Chapter 10, Security for details.

Prior to boarding passengers will be instructed on the 'no smoking' requirements while boarding, and instructed or warned of any other safety matters relevant to their boarding such as excessive or bulky hand baggage, use of walkways and routing to avoid aircraft structure and servicing equipment, and ramp conditions.

## 8.3.16.2 Prior to Take-off

When the aircraft doors have been closed and the slides armed the passengers will be briefed on the regulations and procedures itemised below and concurrently the demonstrations specified above will be given:

- Instructions to fasten seat belts for take-off, including how to fasten and unfasten them, and the recommendation to keep seat belts fastened while in their seats during flight;
- Location and use of oxygen masks;
- The Ryanair policy and requirements of no smoking on all its flights;
- Location and use of life jackets;
- Back of the seat to be in the upright position and the tray table stowed;
- Location of emergency exits;
- The importance of leaving hand luggage behind in case of evacuation;
- Location and use of floor proximity escape path markings;
- Stowage of hand baggage;
- The use and stowage of portable electronic devices;
- The location and the contents of the safety briefing card and the need for passengers to review it prior take-off;
- Compliance with cabin signs and crewmember instructions.

## 8.3.16.3 After Take-off

- CSS will make an announcement reminding passengers of the "no smoking" regulations and the use of safety belts.

- CSS will include a Ryanair safety recommendation that seat belts are kept fastened at all times.

The CSS will make an announcement advising caution when opening overhead compartments.

## 8.3.16.4 Before Landing

CSS instructs or reminds the passengers:

- Of the smoking regulations, and no further smoking until inside the terminal building;
- To fasten seat belts/safety harnesses for landing;
- To put the seat back in the upright position and Stow the tray table;
- To re-stow hand baggage; and
- The use and stowage of portable electronic devices.
- The location and the contents of the safety briefing card and the need for passengers to review it prior landing;
- The importance of leaving hand baggage behind in case of evacuation.

## 8.3.16.5 After Landing

CSS instructs or reminds passengers:

- To observe the no smoking instruction until inside the terminal;
- The use and stowage of portable electronic devices;
- To keep their seat belts/safety harnesses fastened until the aircraft has stopped or reached the gate and the engines have been shut down; and
- Warn of any safety matters affecting their disembarkation such as the need for care when opening over head lockers, and conditions on the ramp.

## Emergencies in Flight

If an emergency occurs during flight, the passengers will be instructed in such emergency action as may be appropriate to the circumstances.

The procedures to be used have been developed for various scenarios and are contained in the Safety and Emergency Procedures Manual.

The call to initiate a line of communication with the Cabin Crew in an emergency is “No.1 to the flight deck”. This will inform the Cabin Crew that an emergency situation exists. On hearing this call the CSS will proceed to the cabin interphone and await a communication from the Captain.

## 8.3.16.6 NITS and Security

Security procedures associated with locked cockpit door are still valid in an emergency situation. A pilot must view the cabin and forward galley area prior to opening the cockpit door. It is therefore preferable that the NITS drill is accomplished over the cabin interphone with the cockpit door locked. The final decision as to how this communication occurs rests with the Commander.

## 8.3.17 Cosmic or Solar Radiation

Ryanair shall take account of the in-flight exposure to cosmic radiation of all crew members while on duty (including positioning) and shall take the following measures for those crew liable to be subject to exposure of more than 1 mSv per year:

1. Assess their exposure;
2. Take into account the assessed exposure when organising working schedules with a view to reduce the doses of highly exposed crew members;
3. Inform the crew members concerned of the health risks their work involves;
4. Ensure that the working schedules for female crew members, once they have notified Ryanair that they are pregnant, keep the equivalent dose to the foetus as low as can reasonably be achieved and in any case ensure that the dose does not exceed 1 mSv for the remainder of the pregnancy;
5. Ensure that individual records are kept for those crew members who are liable to high exposure. These exposures are to be notified to the individual on an annual basis, and also upon leaving Ryanair.

Ryanair has an approved cosmic radiation monitoring programme in place in accordance with SI 125/2000. Annual reports are submitted to the RPII (Radiological Protection Institute of Ireland).

For the purposes of this programme each sector is operated at maximum altitude, and the corresponding dosage for this altitude/time is assigned to this sector.

The current annual flight profile of any individual Ryanair pilot is significantly below the benchmark dosage requiring individual monitoring. Individual annual dosage reported is available from the Dir Flt Ops.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## 8.3.18 Policy on the Use of Automation, AFDS

The Flight Director system shall not be switched off when serviceable during flight operations. Crews are reminded that Flight Directors are to be used in all phases of flight unless it is directed to be turned off by procedure or checklist, and that flight director OFF flying for the maintaining of pilot proficiency practice shall NOT be practiced during line operations.

Procedures for re-engaging of AFDS automation is provided in FCOM Vol 1.

The Flight Director system shall not be switched off when serviceable during flight operations.

## 8.3.18.1 Practise Hand Flying

It is also recognised that “hand flying” the aircraft is a required skill and Commanders, having due consideration to workload and environmental conditions, are authorised to allow pilots to hand fly the aircraft in controlled airspace, only when the fasten seatbelt sign is switched on. Passenger safety and comfort should not be compromised.

Practice hand flying is restricted to below FL150, unless otherwise instructed by a non-normal checklist. The autopilot, when available, shall be engaged anytime the fasten seatbelt sign is OFF.

Practice hand flying is prohibited in the following circumstances:

- Thunderstorm activity
- Towering cumulus activity
- Areas of known or forecast windshear or turbulence
- Areas affected by gale warning
- Busy TMAs (high density traffic, numerous frequency changes, multiple procedural altitude restrictions, high pilot workload, etc ...)
- While any Non Normal procedure is in progress when the autopilot is available and it is appropriate to use it.
- Outside controlled airspace (including Class E), except for take-off and landing.
- VFR traffic announced by ATC or on ATIS or NOTAM.

## 8.4 Low Visibility Operations (SPA.LVO.100)

Ryanair shall only conduct the following Low Visibility Operations (LVO) when approved by the IAA:

- Low visibility take-off (LVTO) operation;
- Standard category II (CAT II) operation;
- Standard category III (CAT III) operation.

## Low-visibility Operations

The value of 125 m RVR for take-off with 15 m centre line light spacing has been selected because flight deck geometry means that this will provide at least a 90 m visual segment for the large majority of aircraft types. In a 90 m visual segment the pilot is expected to be able to see six centre line light intervals (seven centre line lights) at 15 m spacing once lined up on the runway centre line.

## 8.4.2 Instrument Approach Operations in Low Visibility Conditions – Classification of Standard Approach Operations

GM1 SPA.LVO.100(b)

### Low-visibility Operations

The different types of approach and landing operations are classified according to the lowest DH (or MDH) and RVR applicable to the approach type. The classification of approach types does not depend on the technology used for the approach. The lowest minima specified do not take account of ‘operational credits’ that may allow for lower operating minima.

The classification does not subdivide CAT III operations into CAT IIIA, IIIB, and IIIC. The actual minima applicable to any operation depends on the aircraft equipment and the specific LVO approval held by Ryanair.

The AFM for aircraft certified for CAT III operations states the lowest usable DH:

- Category III: DH below 100 feet but not less than 50 feet.

c. the DH to which the flight crew is qualified to operate; or
d. 100 ft.

Instrument Approach Operations in Low-visibility Conditions – Equipment Certification for Low Visibility Approach Operations Other than EFVS

GM2 SPA.LVO.100(b)

Certification requirements of CS-AWO:

Aircraft suitable for low visibility approach operations are certified according to the minimum usable DH which is stated in the AFM.

Legacy systems may be described as capable of ‘CAT 3A’ or ‘CAT IIIA’ operations. This implies a minimum DH of less than 100 ft but not less than 50 ft. Operations to a DH of less than 100 ft but not less than 50 ft will typically require a fail-passive automatic landing system.

CAT III Fail-passive Operations

GM3 SPA.LVO.100(b)

During an automatic landing the pilot needs to monitor the performance of the aircraft system, not in order to detect a failure that is better done by the monitoring devices built into the system, but so as to know precisely the flight situation. In the final stages the pilot should establish visual contact and, by the time the pilot reaches the DH, the pilot should have checked the aircraft position relative to the approach or runway centre line lights.

For this the pilot will need sight of horizontal elements (for roll reference) and part of the touchdown area. The pilot should check for lateral position and cross-track velocity and, if not within the pre-stated lateral limits, the pilot should carry out a missed approach procedure. The pilot should also check longitudinal progress and sight of the landing threshold is useful for this purpose, as is sight of the TDZ lights.

8.4.2.3 Operations Utilising EVS – AMC6 SPA.LVO.100

Not applicable to Ryanair operations.

## 8.4.2.4 Instrument Approach Operations in Low-visibility Conditions – Effect on Landing Minima of Temporarily Failed or Downgraded Equipment for Approach Operations with a DH Below 200 ft

GM4 SPA.LVO.100(b), AMC3 SPA.LVO.100(b)

## Low-visibility Operations

The instructions for the effect on landing minima of temporarily failed or downgraded equipment are intended for use both before flight and during flight. It is, however, not expected that the pilot-in-command/commander would consult such instructions after passing 1000 ft above the aerodrome.

If failures of ground aids are announced at such a late stage, the approach could be continued at the pilot-in-command/commander’s discretion.

If failures are announced before such a late stage in the approach, their effect on the approach should be considered as described in Table 6, and the approach may have to be abandoned.

1. Only those facilities mentioned in Table 6 should be acceptable to be used to determine the effect of temporarily failed or downgraded equipment on the required RVR for CAT II/III approach operations.
2. The following conditions should be applied to Table 6 below:

a. multiple failures of runway/FATO lights other than those indicated in Table 6 are not acceptable;
b. failures of approach and runway/FATO lights are acceptable at the same time, and the most demanding consequence should be applied;
c. for approach operations with a DH below 200 ft, a combination of deficiencies in runway/FATO lights and RVR assessment equipment are not permitted; and
d. failures other than ILS, GLS and MLS affect the RVR only and not the DH.

## 8.4.3 SPA.LVO.105 LVO Approval

### SPA.LVO.105 Specific Approval Criteria

Ryanair has obtained a specific approval for low visibility operations (as stated in the Operations Specifications), having demonstrated that:

1. For low visibility approach operations, the aircraft has been certified in the AFM for the intended operations;

page 8–350

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

2. The flight crew members are competent to conduct the intended operation and a training and checking programme for the flight crew members and relevant personnel involved in the flight preparation has been established, in accordance with (SPA.LVO.120) as described in OMD 2.1.9 and OMD 2.6.3;
3. Operating procedures for the intended operations have been established as described in FCOM;
4. Any relevant changes to the minimum equipment list (MEL) have been made;
5. Any relevant changes to the maintenance programme have been made;
6. Procedures have been established to ensure the suitability of aerodromes, including instrument flight procedures, for the intended operations, in accordance with SPA.LVO.110 as described in OMC 15.5; and
7. For the intended operations, a safety assessment has been carried out, and performance indicators have been established to monitor the level of safety.

## 8.4.3.1 Aircraft Certification for the Intended Operations

AMC1 SPA.LVO.105(a)

Ryanair aircraft used for low-visibility approach operations are equipped in accordance with the applicable airworthiness requirements and certified in the AFM as follows:

1. For CAT II operations, the aircraft should be certified for CAT II operations.
2. For CAT III operations, the aircraft should be certified for CAT III operations.

## 8.4.3.2 Safety Assessment – Monitoring, Data Collection and Performance Indicators

AMC1 SPA.LVO.105(g)

Ryanair monitors LVOs in order to validate the effectiveness of the applicable aircraft flight guidance systems, training, flight crew procedures, and aircraft maintenance programme, and to identify hazards.

1. Data is collected whenever an LVO is attempted regardless of whether the approach is abandoned, is unsatisfactory, or is concluded successfully. This data shall be recorded on the auto coupled approach report form. The data should include records of the following:

a. occasions when it was not possible to commence an approach due to deficiencies or unserviceabilities of related airborne equipment;

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

b. occasions when approaches were discontinued, including the reasons for discontinuing the approach and the height above the runway at which the approach was discontinued;

c. occasions when system abnormalities required pilot intervention to ensure a continued approach or safe landing;

d. landing performance, whether or not the aircraft landed satisfactorily within the desired touchdown area with acceptable lateral velocity or cross-track error. The approximate lateral and longitudinal position of the actual touchdown point in relation to the runway centre line and the runway threshold, respectively, should be recorded.

2. Data about LVOs should be collected by means of the Ryanairs flight data monitoring programme supplemented by other means including reports submitted by flight crew.

3. Performance indicators include the following:

a. the rate of unsuccessful low-visibility approaches, i.e. the number of attempted approaches terminating in discontinued approaches, approaches where pilot intervention was required to ensure a continued approach or safe landing or where landing performance was unsatisfactory, compared to the number of low-visibility approaches attempted;

b. measures of performance of the airborne equipment for low-visibility approaches;

c. safety performance indicators related to other specific risks associated with LVOs.

4. The following information are retained for at least 5 years:

a. the total number of low-visibility approaches or operations with an operational approval attempted or completed, including practice approaches, by aircraft type; and

b. reports of unsatisfactory approaches and/or landings, by runway and aircraft registration, in the following categories:

i. airborne equipment faults;

ii. ground facility difficulties;

iii. missed approaches because of air traffic control (ATC) instructions; or

iv. other reasons.

## 8.4.3.3 Safety Assessment Prior to Obtaining an Approval

AMC2 SPA.LVO.105(g)

Prior to commencing LVOs, Ryanair demonstrated to the IAA that such operations will achieve an acceptable level of safety. This required Ryanair to gather data from operations using the relevant systems and procedures and conduct safety assessments taking that data into account.

## 8.4.3.4 Successful Approach and Landing

GM1 SPA.LVO.105(g)

### Specific Approval Criteria

1. An approach may be considered to be successful if:

a. from 500 ft to start of the flare:
i. speed is maintained within ± 5 kt of the intended speed, disregarding rapid fluctuations due to turbulence;
ii. no relevant system failure occurs; and
b. from 300 ft to the DH:
i. no excess deviation occurs; and
ii. no centralised warning (including aural) gives a missed approach procedure command.

2. A landing may be considered to be successful if:

a. no relevant system failure occurs;
b. no flare failure occurs;
c. longitudinal touchdown is beyond a point on the runway 150 m after the threshold and before the end of the touchdown zone (TDZ) (750 m from the threshold);
d. lateral touchdown with the outboard landing gear is not outside the TDZ edge;
e. sink rate is not excessive;
f. bank angle does not exceed a bank angle limit.

## 8.4.3.5 Suitable Aerodromes – Assessment

The Flight Operations Performance Engineering Department maintains a register of Aerodromes and Runway ends assessed and approved for Low Visibility Operations (LVO). Any approved aerodrome (OMC Appendix A Approved Aerodrome list) with an LVO landing procedure that has not passed an assessment will be notified to Crew by Company NOTAM or Airfield Brief (AFB) as not authorised.

## 8.4.4 Operating Procedures – Cat II/Cat III

SPA.LVO.110, AMC3 SPA.LVO.105(c), AMC4 SPA.LVO.105(c)

### Specific Approval Criteria

Ryanair shall ensure that only aerodromes, including instrument flight procedures, suitable for the intended operations are used for LVOs.

Ryanair only conducts CAT II or CAT III operations when:

- Each aircraft concerned is certified for operations with a Decision Height (DH) below 200 ft, and signed off as CATII/III in the Technical Log and on the centre pedestal indicating compliance with applicable regulations

For CAT II operations, the following applies:

- The flight crew consists of at least two pilots.
- The approach is flown using a certified system (autopilot/autoland) as identified in the AFM.
- All height callouts below 200 ft above the runway threshold elevation are determined by the use of a radio altimeter.
- The DH is determined by the use of a radio altimeter.
- At DH, the following visual references are distinctly visible and identifiable to the pilot:

1. A segment of at least three consecutive lights, which are the centre line of the approach lights or TDZ lights or runway centre line lights or edge lights or a combination of these.
2. A visual reference that should include a lateral element of the ground pattern, such as an approach lighting crossbar, or the landing threshold, or a barrette of the TDZ lighting unless the operation is conducted using a HUD or an equivalent system to touchdown.

For CAT III operations, the following applies:

- The flight crew consists of at least two pilots.

page 8-354

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR
PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

- The approach is flown using a certified system (autopilot/autoland) as identified in the AFM.
- All height callouts below 200 ft above the runway threshold elevation are determined by the use of a radio altimeter.
- The DH is determined by the use of a radio altimeter.
- At DH, the following visual references are distinctly visible and identifiable to the pilot:

1. A segment of at least three consecutive lights, which are the centre line of the approach lights or TDZ lights or runway centre line lights or edge lights or a combination of these.

Note: The approach/autoland shall be recorded on the auto coupled approach report form.

## 8.4.5 Aerodrome Related Requirements

Ryanair shall not use an aerodrome for LVOs below a visibility of 800 m unless:

- the aerodrome has been approved for such operations by the State of the aerodrome; and
- Low Visibility Procedures (LVP) have been established.

If the term LVP is not used, the equivalent procedures that adhere to the requirements of LVP at the aerodrome must be stated in the Jeppesen charts in use thereby assuring an equivalent level of safety. If an LVP or equivalent procedure is not presented in the Jeppesen charts or Airfield Brief, autolands shall not be conducted in LVP.

## Practice CAT II/III Approaches

Practice CAT II/III approaches are permitted to unprotected runways, with visibility 1,500 m or greater and ceiling 500 ft or higher, including at night.

Verify all Landing Distance Required for all practice autolands. During a practice approach, system malfunction in itself does not require a Go-Around. In a practise CAT II/III approach the captain may decide to take over manually and land provided the approach can be continued safely.

## Autoland Upgrade

Ideally the autoland for the purpose of an autoland upgrade will be conducted to an operational and protected CATII/III runway. However a satisfactory autoland for upgrade is valid if conducted on any runway with a serviceable and operational ILS.

## 8.4.6 Competence of the Flight Crew for the Intended Operations – Experience in Type or Class, or as Pilot-in-command/commander

SPA.LVO.120, AMC1 SPA.LVO.120

1. Ryanair shall ensure that the flight crew is competent to conduct the intended operations.

2. Ryanair shall ensure that each flight crew member successfully completes training and checking for all types of LVOs for which an approval has been granted. Such training and checking shall:

a. include initial and recurrent training and checking;
b. include normal, abnormal and emergency procedures;
c. be tailored to the type of technologies used in the intended operations; and
d. take into account the human factor risks associated with the intended operations.

3. Ryanair shall keep records of the training and qualifications of the flight crew members.

4. The training and checking shall be conducted by appropriately qualified personnel. In the case of flight and flight simulation training and checking, the personnel providing the training and conducting the checks shall be qualified in accordance with Annex I (Part-FCL) to Regulation (EU) No 1178/2011.

To ensure that the flight crew is competent to conduct the intended operations, Ryanair has assessed the risks associated with the conduct of low-visibility approach operations by pilots new to the aircraft type or class and take the necessary mitigations. Such mitigations include an increment to the visibility or RVR for LVOs in accordance with OMA 5.1.3.1.

Prior to conducting LVO Ryanair, ensures that each flight crew member:

- complies with the training and checking requirements prescribed in the operations manual Part D, including Flight Simulation Training Device (FSTD) training, in operating to the limiting values of RVR/VIS (visibility) and DH specific to the operation and the aircraft type;
- is qualified in accordance with the standards prescribed in the operations manual;
- training and checking is conducted in accordance with a detailed syllabus.

The syllabus for an initial Low Visibility Operations qualification includes three practice approaches and landings (CAT II or CAT III as appropriate) to be carried out during Line Flying under Supervision.

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

To clarify this, a First or Second Officer may conduct these practice approaches, in CAT I conditions or better, under the supervision of a Line Captain who is himself LVO qualified on the aircraft being flown. A Captain completing this part of his initial LVO training will require the supervision of either a Line Training Captain or TRE.

The Flight Crew qualification is specific to the operation (take-off, CAT II/III) and the aircraft type. The Ryanair approvals for Flight Crew operating on the B737-800/8200 are as follows:

Take-off: 125 m RVR

Landing CAT II 100 ft DH/300 m RVR

Landing CAT III 50 ft DH/175 m RVR

## 8.4.6.1 CAT II/III ILS

For any CAT II or CAT III approach the ILS must be promulgated as operating to the required standard, i.e. “ILS CAT II” or “ILS CAT III” as appropriate. The performance criteria are contained in ICAO document Annex 10. In order to prevent electromagnetic interference with the ILS ATC is required to establish an ILS CRITICAL AREA. The associated “sensitive area” must be protected from infringement by taxiing aircraft and other vehicles when any automatic approach/landing is in progress. The effect of such interference, should it occur, is excessive aileron and elevator activity during the approach.

Anytime an Autoland is planned the cabin crew must be notified to make a PA to instruct passengers to switch OFF all PED’s (PA as per SEP manual). If a go-around is completed as a result of suspected spurious electromagnetic interference, the aircraft Commander shall instruct the Cabin Crew to ensure that all portable electronic devices are switched off before a subsequent approach is carried out. On landing a Technical Log entry shall be made and a ASR submitted.

## 8.4.6.2 Competence of the Flight Crew for the Intended Operations – Recent Experience for Cat II and Cat III Approach Operations

AMC3 SPA.LVO.120(a)

To be considered competent:

1. Pilots authorised to conduct low-visibility approach operations should complete at least two approaches using Ryanair’s procedures for low-visibility approach operations during the validity period of each operator proficiency check or periodic demonstration of competence, unless credits related to recent experience when operating more than one type are defined in the operational suitability data established in accordance with Regulation (EU) No 748/2012.

## 8.4.7 Operating Procedures for LVOs

AMC1 SPA.LVO.105(c)

Ryanair has established procedures and instructions to be used for LVOs in this manual and FCOM Vol 1, containing the duties of flight crew members during taxiing, take-off, approach, flare, landing, rollout and missed approach operations, as appropriate.

Prior to commencing an LVO, the pilot-in-command/commander shall be satisfied that:

- The status of the visual and non-visual facilities is as required;
- Appropriate LVPs are in force according to information received from Air Traffic Services (ATS);
- If LVPs are required for such operations, LVPs are in effect; and
- The flight crew members are appropriately ‘qualified’;
- The aircraft is ‘qualified’; (Technical Log and CAT I/II/III placard status)
- ILS ‘qualified’;
- Runway ‘qualified’. (OMC)

## General

LVOs include the following when the applicable RVR is less than 550 m.

- manual take-off,
- auto-coupled approach followed by auto-flare, auto-landing and manual rollout.

## Procedures

Ryanair procedures and instructions applicable to LVO on B737-800/8200 series aircraft are also stated in the following manuals: FCOM Vol 1, OMD and MEL.

The following parameters for LVO are approved by the IAA:

1. Ryanair has been approved for CAT II (100 ft DH, 300 m RVR) and CAT III (50 ft DH, 175 m RVR).
2. Max. headwind component: 25 kts.
3. Max crosswind component: 20 kts.

page 8-358

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

4. Max tailwind component: 10 kts.
5. Dual Channel Approach.
6. Two engine approach, flaps 30/40.
7. No reported windshear.
8. Glideslope capture by 1500 ft AAL.
9. Mid-point RVR 125 m and stop end RVR 75 m.
10. The Flight Crew consists of 2 qualified flight crew members (LHS/RHS).

## Runway Markings and Lighting System

Runway lighting and markings must conform to the specifications of ICAO Annex 14.

The significant addition to CAT I systems are the supplementary approach lights on the inner 300 m (red barrettes), and the touchdown zone lighting.

The inner 300 m (1,000 ft) of the HIALS is supplemented with red approach barrettes. Their colour indicates clearly the pre-threshold area. The lateral dimensions of the red barrettes is the same as the white touchdown zone lights.

Between the two there is a full width green bar marking the runway threshold.

Stop-end lighting: To indicate that the aircraft is coming towards the end of the runway during the stopping roll, at 910 m (3,000 ft) from the stop-end, the centreline lights are alternatively red and white, and for the last 300 m (1,000 ft) they become red only.

Approach lighting system patterns are shown in the Jeppesen General Airway Manual (Intro/Chart Legend/Charting Symbol Legend).

Further information is available in the All Weather Operations Guide which is a Flight Operations Controlled Document. This document must be revised in conjunction with the Ryanair OMA and Boeing FCOM.

## 8.4.8 Minimum Equipment (SPA.LVO.130)

The minimum equipment that has to be serviceable at the commencement of an LVO in accordance with the aircraft flight manual (AFM) is stated in the MEL.

The commander shall be satisfied that the status of the aircraft and of the relevant airborne systems is appropriate for the specific operation to be conducted.

## 8.6 Minimum Equipment List

ORO.MLR.105, AMC1 ORO.MLR.105(c)

A minimum equipment list (MEL) is established as specified under 8.a.3 of Annex IV to Regulation (EU) 2018/1139, based on the relevant master minimum equipment list (MMEL) as defined in the data established in accordance with Regulation (EC) 748/2012.

The MEL and any amendment thereto shall be approved by the IAA. Ryanair shall submit an amended MEL to the IAA within 90 days from the effective date specified in the approval page of the MMEL.

In addition to the list of MEL items, the MEL shall contain:

- a preamble, including guidance and definitions for flight crews and maintenance personnel using the MEL;
- the revision status of the MMEL upon which the MEL is based and the revision status of the MEL;
- the scope, extent and purpose of the MEL.

Ryanair has;

- reviewed the MEL to ensure that rectification intervals for each inoperative instrument, item of equipment or function listed in the MEL are established, and ensures that the rectification interval in the MEL is not less restrictive than the corresponding rectification interval in the MMEL;
- establish an effective rectification programme;
- ensured that operation of the aircraft after expiry of the rectification interval specified in the MEL occurs only when the defect has been rectified or the rectification interval has been extended in accordance with approved maintenance procedures.

Ryanair has established a procedure controlled by the DENG and approved by the IAA for ‘one time extension’ of category B, C and D rectification intervals. DENG has approved the specific duties and responsibilities for controlling extensions. This procedure is subject to the rules below:

1. The extension of the rectification interval is within the scope of the MMEL for the aircraft type;

page 8-360

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

2. The extension of the rectification interval is, as a maximum, of the same duration as the rectification interval specified in the MEL;
3. The rectification interval extension is not used as a normal means of conducting MEL item rectification and is used only when events beyond the control of Ryanair have precluded rectification;
4. The IAA is notified of any extension of the applicable rectification interval; and
5. A plan to accomplish the rectification at the earliest opportunity is established.

The operational (O) and maintenance (M) procedures referenced in the MEL are compliant with the MMEL and are documented in the DDG and shall be completed prior to dispatch. The DDG and MEL are on board all Ryanair operating aircraft and are readily accessible to aircrew.

Subject to a specific case-by-case approval by the IAA, Ryanair may operate an aircraft with inoperative instruments, items of equipment or functions outside the constraints of the MEL but within the constraints of the MMEL, provided that:

- The concerned instruments, items of equipment or functions are within the scope of the MMEL as defined in the data established in accordance with Regulation (EC) No 748/2012;
- The approval is not used as a normal means of conducting operations outside the constraints of the approved MEL and is used only when events beyond the control of Ryanair have precluded the MEL compliance;
- A description of specific duties and responsibilities for controlling the operation of the aircraft under such approval is established by the Ryanair; and
- A plan to rectify the inoperative instruments, items of equipment or functions or to return operating the aircraft under the MEL constraints at the earliest opportunity is established.

## 8.6.1 Minimum Equipment List MEL

CAT.IDE.A.105

### GENERAL

The MEL is a document that lists the equipment that may be temporarily inoperative, subject to certain conditions, at the commencement of flight. This document is prepared by Ryanair for particular aircraft taking account of their aircraft configuration and all those individual variables that cannot be addressed

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

at MMEL level, such as operating environment, route structure, geographic location, aerodromes where spare parts and maintenance capabilities are available etc., in accordance with a procedure approved by the IAA.

- Where non-safety related equipment serves a second function, such as lockable cockpit door, Ryanair has developed and includes operational contingency procedures in the MEL in case of an equipment malfunction.
- Where non-safety related equipment is part of another aircraft system, for example the electrical system, procedures should be developed and included in the MEL for deactivating and securing in case of malfunction. In these cases, the item should be listed in the MEL, with compensating provisions and deactivation instructions if applicable. The rectification interval will be dependent on the secondary function of the item and the extent of its effect on other systems.

If an item is not listed in the MEL the commander can verify the serviceability of the part by the use of contacting MAINTROL.

The MEL provides the Commander with the necessary authority to operate a flight with inoperative equipment. The Commander retains the final discretion to accept and operate the aircraft in accordance with the MEL. In planning a flight, the Commander will take account of any operational constraints imposed on him as a consequence of having to operate with inoperative equipment.

Whereas the format of the MEL lists equipment which may be inoperative for dispatch, it does not consider items which are clearly necessary for airworthiness such as flight controls etc, nor does it list items which are obviously not required for safe operation such as passenger reading lights, etc. It is recognised that acceptance of multiple “MEL items' which individually might be safe but taken together may interact to compound the operational situation.

The NPFO controls the MEL/DDG content with the approval of the IAA and provides only current MEL/DDG which are carried on board.

## EXTENT OF THE MEL Minimum equipment list

The MEL shall be used when required before the commencement of the flight (i.e before the aircraft first moves under its own power). If a failure occurs between the commencement of the flight and the start of the take-off, any decision to continue the flight is subject to pilot judgement and good airmanship. It is prudent for the commander to refer to the MEL before any decision to continue the flight is taken.

It is prudent to review the MEL as unservicabilities arise to ensure the most expeditious access to Maintenance. This frequently includes interaction with Maintrol during flight, relayed by ATC or handling agents enroute

## 8.6.2 Configuration Deviation List (CDL)

The CDL contains items of secondary airframe structure or engine parts which may be missing or otherwise non-functioning, but for which the aircraft may be dispatched. The CDL is promulgated as an appendix in the AFM and may be included in the MEL.

Operation with a “CDL item” may incur a performance penalty or an operational limit, cognisance of which may also be needed at the planning stage of a flight as well as during flight.

If an MEL/CDL item is entered into the LIDO flight planning system, the OFP is capable of reflecting certain performance penalties and operational limits. MEL and CDL items are also accounted for in the OPT take-off and landing performance solution.

## 8.7 Non Commercial Operations

ORO.AOC.125

Ryanair conducts a limited number of Non-Commercial flights using aircraft listed in the Operations Specifications Certificate. A Non-Commercial flight is a flight conducted entirely at the expense of Ryanair. If passengers are on board, they are non-revenue passengers.

All non-commercial flights except Check flights and Display flights must be operated in accordance with the operating procedures specified in Annexes III, IV and V of Commission Regulation (EU) 965/2012 and the Ryanair DAC Operations Manual, as if the flight were commercial air transport.

The type of flight shall be specified in flight related documents such as OFP and other equivalent documents. ORO.AOC.125(d). Non commercial flights are operated in accordance with the Ryanair DAC Operations Manuals suite, as if the flight were commercial air transport. AMC2 ORO.AOC.125(a)

Check flights are operated under the Ryanair DAC OMA 8.7 or a Permit to Fly.

Display Flights are operated under an associated Flight Authorisation issued by the IAA (which would include any flight conditions specified by EASA under Part 21).

A company notam will indicate the status of the non-commercial flight.

On any flight without Cabin Crew, it is the responsibility of the Pilot in Command to complete a security check of the galley, cabin and toilets of the aircraft. The Pilot in Command is to ensure that all equipment is properly secured. This duty may be delegated to the First Officer. Both forward doors shall be armed. Care must be taken to disarm the doors before they are opened. The carriage of

non-revenue passengers on non-commercial flights without cabin crew is permitted, provided that the Pilot in Command ensures compliance with the additional provisions and procedures described in Section 8.7.3.2.

## 8.7.1 Ryanair Conducts the Following Types of Non Commercial Operations

GM1 ORO.AOC.125(a)(2)

- Demonstration Flights;
- Check Flights;

Additional considerations or special approvals specified for Non-Commercial flights as listed in this section are provided to the operating crew, including:

- a clear identification of any differences between operating procedures used when conducting commercial air transport and check flights, including such notification to IAA in advance;

## 8.7.1.1 Demonstration Flights

ARO.GEN.310(a), ORO.AOC.125(a)(2)

The purpose of a demonstration flight is to demonstrate the aircraft’s handling, performance, and functionalities to the IAA, the media, or to actual or potential buyers or lessees. These flights are conducted under the normal requirements of the Ryanair Operations Manual, as if the flight were commercial air transport and contain no non-standard manoeuvres or profiles. The flights must be authorised by the NPFO.

1. ‘Route proving flight’ is a flight to demonstrate the aircraft’s flying characteristics and systems to the IAA, for verification of compliance with the operational Requirements. ARO.GEN.310(a).
2. A public relations flight or a commercial ‘fly-around’ is a flight targeting official or media representatives.

These flights may or may not have cabin crew as authorised by the NPFO.

## 8.7.1.2 Maintenance Check Flights (MCFs)

### 1. Levels of Maintenance Check Flights

SPO.SPEC.MCF.110

The Company carries out Level ‘A’ and Level ‘B’ Maintenance Check Flights (MCFs).

Level ‘A’ check flights are flights where the use of abnormal or emergency procedures is expected as defined in the Aircraft Flight Manual or where it is required to prove the functioning of a backup system or other safety device.

The Level ‘A’ MCFs conducted by The Company are:

a. Check Flight for Renewal of Certificate of Airworthiness/End of Lease/Full Check
b. Check Flight for Elevator Hydraulic Power Off
c. Check Flight to Correct Asymmetrical Flight Control Forces

End of Lease Check flights are elective Check Flights for the purpose of demonstrating an aircraft and may not follow a maintenance procedure but will be treated as MCFs as the items checked are as per Level ‘A’ Flights.

Level ‘B’ check flights are any other maintenance check flight other than a Level ‘A’ check flight. No use of abnormal or emergency procedures is required on these flights.

### 2. Flight Programme for “Level A” Flights

SPO.SPEC.MCF.105

A full flight programme for “Level A” flights is published in the Ryanair Maintenance Check Flight Manual.

### 3. Maintenance Check Flight Manual for Level A Flights

SPO.SPEC.MCF.110

All operations and associated procedures to be used in “Level A” check flights are detailed in the Ryanair Maintenance Check Flight Manual.

This manual will be updated by a person nominated by NPFO and such updates will be promulgated in Docunet to all qualified Maintenance Check Pilots.

### 4. Flight Crew Requirements for “Level A” Maintenance Check Flights

SPO.SPEC.MCF.115

“Level A” maintenance check flights will be conducted only by qualified and current Maintenance Check Flight Pilots. The entry requirements, course syllabus and recency requirements for Maintenance Check Pilots are detailed in OMD.

## 8.7.1.3 Relocation Flights

The following flights are operated in accordance with the normal requirements of the Ryanair DAC Operations Manual, as if the flight were commercial air transport, except that Ryanair DAC may designate a pilot for the flight as Pilot in Command or co-pilot, who has attained the age of 65 or greater (AMC2 ORO.AOC.125(a)). Non-commercial operational differences are described below.

Non-Ryanair DAC crew may operate designated non-commercial operations on a Ryanair DAC (EI registered) aircraft. Both the Pilot in Command and the co-pilot must:

- Be from the same (Boeing) Ryanair Group AOC
- Hold a current EASA Part-FCL License, or a Non-EASA License with an IAA validation issued in accordance with EASA Part-FCL
- Hold an EASA Class 1 medical certificate in accordance with Part-MED
- Be current and recent on the aircraft type/variant to be operated
- Operate the flight in accordance with the Ryanair DAC Operations Manual suite. Prior to the commencement of the flight, both crewmembers must be briefed of any differences by the Ryanair DAC NPFO or his nominee.
- Either or both flight crew members may be over 65 years of age

The Ryanair DAC NPFO accepts the non Ryanair DAC operating crew prior to the commencement of the flight. Rostering of non commercial flights under this section of the Operations Manual is done by the operating crew AOC and all legality checks must be valid. The FTL records are maintained in the operating crew AOC crew management system and shared with the Ryanair DAC NPFO ASAP on completion of the flight(s). Any reports required are filed on the operating crew AOC reporting system and shared ASAP with the Ryanair DAC NPFO and Safety Manager, (a non Ryanair DAC pilot will not have access to the Ryanair DAC reporting system).

1.  ‘Ferry flight\*’: On occasion it may be necessary for Ryanair DAC to operate a flight for maintenance purposes.

The aircraft may not be fully serviceable. This flight will be operated with any additional briefing material required made available to the operating crew in advance. The Operations Control Centre will coordinate with

page 8-368

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

operating crew and with the Performance Department for any specialist evaluations required for flight planning. This flight is authorised by the Ryanair DAC NPFO or his nominee in advance.

Examples of ferry flights conducted by Ryanair DAC:

a. Unpressurised ferry flight,
b. Gear-down ferry flight.
c. AOG maintenance ferry flight.

2. ‘Delivery flight’: a flight to transfer the aircraft from the acceptance location (manufacturer, refurbishment location, previous owner, lessor/lessee, long-term storage) to delivery location.

Ryanair has approval from the IAA to conduct delivery flights in accordance with the Operations Specifications Certificate.

OMD details a special crew qualification to be designated Pilot in Command of a delivery flight and minimum experience levels for the co-pilot. If a co-pilot is assigned to operate this must be authorised by the NPFO.

If non-revenue passengers are carried the number is limited to 19 and each will be approved by the NPFO. The requirements of carriage of passengers without cabin crew will be applied.

Report time for crew on Delivery flights is normally 60 mins prior to the planned departure time. This may be reduced by the commander to not less than 45 mins. Operational procedures are detailed in the Delivery Flight Brief (Boeing). The OFP is prepared by Jeppesen and issued by Boeing Dispatch. The format is the same as the standard LIDO OFP.

Ryanair DAC applies ‘Dispatch Permissible’ minima to aerodromes identified as adequate enroute aerodromes for Delivery flights. This means that at the relevant time of potential use, the forecast weather must be such that will allow the conduct of the relevant Instrument Flight Procedure to make an approach to the aerodrome.

Diversion Distance for non-commercial operations Delivery flights, is 90 minutes flying time at an OEI cruising speed according to the AFM in still air standard conditions based on the actual take-off mass (624 nm for the B737-8200).

In order to apply this extended diversion distance, the following conditions must be met:

a. The aircraft may not dispatch with any deferred defect of a critical system.
b. No Dangerous Goods carried in the aircraft holds.

This flight is authorized by the Ryanair DAC NPFO or his nominee.

3. ‘Positioning flight’: a flight to position the aircraft to a location from which a further flight will be performed. This flight is authorised by the Ryanair DAC NPFO or his nominee in advance. Positioning flights carry no fare paying passengers.

4. ‘Recovery flight\*’: a flight to position a serviceable aircraft from its current location to an adequately secure location for various reasons e.g. to remove it from a hazardous area. This flight is authorised by the Ryanair DAC NPFO or his nominee in advance.

Note: \* Ferry flights and Recovery flights which operate under a flight permit require a briefing from the duty pilot prior to dispatch.

8.7.1.4

## Training Flights: Base Training

The conduct of Base Training is described in OMD. Base training flights are authorised by the NPFO.

## Weather Minima and Flight Limitations for Base Training Flights

It is the instructor’s responsibility to ensure that training is not carried out in conditions of ceiling, visibility and crosswind that are inappropriate to the exercises involved, the experience and competency of the trainee.

The following limitations apply to all students under instruction on Base Training flights.

- Cloud base greater than 1000 ft, visibility greater than 6 km, weather must be suitable for the candidate’s overall experience level.
- Crosswind less than 20 kts (15 kts for cadets).
- Braking action good (RWYCC 5).
- Daylight for pilots not previously rated on an EASA CS-25 type (Jet &gt;20,000 kg).
- No abnormal or emergency scenarios should be simulated or induced during base training flights.

## Additional Limitations for Night Base Training

- Only pilots who have been previously rated on a CS 25 type aircraft may be base trained at night.
- Night Base Training is not approved between the hours of 0200 and 0600 local.

- Cloud ceiling 2000 feet AGL.
- 10 km or greater Met. Visibility.
- Maximum Crosswind component 15 knots.

1. Ryanair staff who have a particular function to carry out on the flight may be authorised by the NPCT, provided they have been listed as crew members; and

## 8.7.2 Safety Management and Risk Assessment AMC1 ORO.AOC.125(a)(2)(ii)

Ryanair manages the risks associated with flights described in this section under the Ryanair Safety Management System as described in the Safety Management Manual. The NPFO may require completion of a Safety Risk Assessment for any non-commercial flight. Each safety risk assessment will follow established Ryanair protocols and will include at least the following:

1. Flight profile (including manoeuvres to be performed, any simulated abnormal situations in flight, duties and responsibilities of the crew members);
2. Levels of functional equipment and systems (MEL, CDL);

## 8.7.3 Crew Requirements

A non Ryanair DAC operating crew under OPS 8.7 must be from the same AOC, i.e. either a Malta Air or Ryanair Sun crew. No interoperable crewing is permitted.

## 8.7.3.1 Non Commercial Flights: Operating Crew

Non-commercial Flights are operated in accordance with the normal requirements of the Ryanair DAC Operations Manual, as if the flight were commercial air transport, except that Ryanair DAC may designate a pilot for the flight as Pilot in Command or co-pilot, who has attained the age of 65 or greater (AMC2 ORO.AOC.125(a)).

### Maintenance Check Flights

Any pilot operating a Maintenance Check Flight on a Ryanair DAC (EI registered) aircraft must have completed training to comply with Ryanair DAC OMA and Ryanair DAC OMD training and checking criteria. Maintenance Check Pilots-Seattle will be Maintenance Check Pilots who have also completed at least 1 Boeing Customer Acceptance Flight or completed pre-Customer Acceptance Flight simulator training. These pilots are qualified to accept new aircraft from the Boeing Delivery Centre, Seattle.

First Officers rostered for Maintenance Check flights will have a minimum of 1,500 hrs flight time on type.

The Maintenance Check Pilot shall be the Pilot in Command and the responsibility for the safe completion of the Maintenance Check Flight in accordance with the guidance contained in this manual lies solely with him or her.

Responsibilities and Duties of the individual crew members are as detailed in the Ryanair DAC Flight Crew Operations Manual. Where differences/additions to duties exist in a specific test sequence they are detailed in the relevant chapter of the MCF Manual and as appropriate in the Permit to Fly. These duties shall be thoroughly briefed by the Pilot in Command prior to the flight.

8.7.3.2 Non Commercial Flights Without Cabin Crew

ORO.CC.100AMC1, ORO.CC.100(d)(2)AMC3, ORO.AOC.125(a)(2)(ii)GM1, ORO.CC.100(D)(2), CAT.OP.MPA.155

Non Commercial flights may be carried out without cabin crew if the passenger number is 19 or less. The following differences and restrictions apply:

1. Passengers are restricted as follows

a. The maximum number of passengers is 19.
b. Revenue (ticketed) passengers are prohibited.
c. Each passenger must be approved by the NPFO and or Security Manager. Such approval will be communicated to the Captain before the flight.
d. All passengers must be ‘known’ i.e. a staff member or directly vouched for by NPFO/Security Manager.
e. The number of SCP/PRMs permitted depends on having an assigned crewmember or other passenger to each SCP/PRM who will assist the SCP/PRM in an emergency/evacuation. The number of SCP/PRMs permitted is determined by the NPFO.
f. Passengers and crew will be subject to security screening at the departure airfield.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

2. Flight crew assigned to these duties are trained in first aid procedures and familiar with operational differences applicable to these flights.

a. Communication and coordination between flight crew and passengers:

i. the flight deck door remains open during all non-critical phases of flight enabling cabin surveillance and communication with passengers,

ii. during critical phases of flight, the flight deck door is closed and locked – the passenger will use the cabin attendant call button, which the flight crew can hear, to get flight crew attention (both ACP PA selected.),

iii. direction from the flight deck will be made on the PA system initially,

iv. during non-critical phases of flight, as the flight deck door is unlocked, passengers may communicate directly with flight crew.

b. Flight Crew member Incapacitation

i. The non-incapacitated flight crew member will direct passengers to secure and/or remove the incapacitated crew member from his/her station. First Aid should be rendered by the passenger(s) with direction from the operating pilot via PA if appropriate. The operating crew member will declare an Emergency and initiate a diversion

c. Cabin surveillance will be achieved by securing the flight deck door in the OPEN position by use of the foot operated lock. During checklist operation pre-departure and pre-landing, a visual check will be made of the cabin by the PM. A PA instructing all passengers to be seated will be made before this check. During the cruise the non-flying pilot will conduct a check of the cabin approximately every 30 mins if safe to do so.

d. Procedures for rapid egress from the aircraft in case of rapid disembarkation or evacuation are included in the passenger briefing. The flight crew will refer the passengers to the relevant section of the safety card as well as physically pointing out the exits.

e. The operation and use of emergency exits is included in the passenger briefing and the flight crew will assist with evacuation if possible as per standard evacuation procedures.

f. The location and use of Oxygen is included in the passenger briefing.

g. The location and use of life jackets is included in the passenger briefing.

page 8-374

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

h. Seating of passengers will be as far forward in the cabin as can be allowed by Weight and Balance. If passengers need to occupy the MID zone they should be seated in the front rows of the MID Zone. This will facilitate cabin surveillance from the flight deck as well as rapid egress using the L1 door. (If needed any emergency exit may be used).

i. Passenger briefing will be completed by a member of the flight crew using the general safety brief on EFB and by reference to the ‘Non-Commercial Ops without Cabin Crew Checklist’ to provide information on communication methods and the location and use of equipment not included in the general safety briefing such as a fire extinguisher, first-aid equipment (e.g. first-aid kit, defibrillator if fitted), smoke hood, etc. In the event of a requirement to use this equipment inflight, the non-flying pilot will use the equipment and utilize the assistance of passenger(s) if appropriate.

j. If all the passengers are qualified (crew members or engineering staff) the Captain may elect not to deliver the general safety brief and instead brief using the ‘Non-Commercial Ops without Cabin Crew Checklist’ to concentrate on specific differences pertaining to the actual flight.

k. In the case of additional emergency/safety equipment being onboard, passengers will be shown its location and briefed on its operation by the briefing crew member at the briefing stage. Examples of such equipment would be portable ELT and life raft on a delivery flight.

## 8.7.3.4 Flight and Duty Time Limitations and Rest Requirements for Commercial and Non-Commercial Operations

AMC2 ORO.AOC.125(a)(2)(ii), ORO.FTL.210

When conducting a mix of commercial and non-commercial flights in the same duty period the provisions of the Ryanair DAC FTL scheme shall apply. Additionally, any potential increase in fatigue associated with the non-commercial flight, e.g. degree of difficulty or complexity, shall be considered.

## 8.7.3.4.1 Flight and Duty Time Limitations and Rest Requirements for Mixed Operations

AMC1 ORO.AOC.125(a)

When conducting a mix of commercial and non-commercial flights by non Ryanair DAC crew members, the operating crew AOC ensures:

1. The flight duty complies with the provisions of ‘Flight times and duty periods’ of the operating crew to ensure compliance with Subpart FTL. ORO.FTL.210.
2. Mixed operations are included in its safety risk management process to ensure that the fatigue risks arising from any combination of commercial and non-commercial flights do not affect the operations safety margins.

## 8.8 Oxygen Requirements

## 8.8.1 Use of supplemental oxygen (CAT.OP.MPA.285)

The commander shall ensure that flight crew members engaged in performing duties essential to the safe operation of an aircraft in flight use supplemental oxygen continuously whenever the cabin altitude exceeds 10,000 ft for a period of more than 30 minutes and whenever the cabin altitude exceeds 13,000 ft.

## 8.8.2 Supplemental oxygen — Pressurised Aeroplanes (CAT.IDE.A.235)

1. Ryanair aircraft are pressurised, operate at pressure altitudes above 10,000 ft and are equipped with supplemental oxygen equipment that is capable of storing and dispensing the oxygen supplies in accordance with Table 1.

2. Ryanair aircraft are pressurised, operated at pressure altitudes above 25,000 ft and are equipped with:

a. Quick donning types of masks for flight crew members;

b. Sufficient spare outlets and masks or portable oxygen units with masks distributed evenly throughout the passenger compartment, to ensure immediate availability of oxygen for use by each required cabin crew member;

c. An oxygen dispensing unit connected to oxygen supply terminals immediately available to each cabin crew member, additional crew member and occupants of passenger seats, wherever seated; and

d. A device to provide a warning indication to the flight crew of any loss of pressurisation.

3. The individual oxygen dispensing units are automatically deployable.
