PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

4. The total number of dispensing units and outlets exceed the number of seats by at least 10%. The extra units are evenly distributed throughout the passenger compartment.

Oxygen Minimum Requirements for Pressurised Aeroplanes

| Supply for                                                                     | Duration and Cabin Pressure Altitude                                          |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| 1. Occupants of flight crew compartment seats on flight crew compartment duty. | a. The entire flying time when the cabin pressure altitude exceeds 13,000 ft. |

b. The remainder of the flying time when the cabin pressure altitude exceeds 10,000 ft but does not exceed 13,000 ft, after the initial 30 minutes at these altitudes, but in no case less than:

1. 30 minutes’ supply for aeroplanes certified to fly at altitudes not exceeding 25,000 ft; and
2. 2 hours’ supply for aeroplanes certified to fly at altitudes of more than 25,000 ft. |
   | 2. Required cabin crew members. | a. The entire flying time when the cabin pressure altitude exceeds 13,000 ft, but not less than 30 minutes’ supply.
   b. The remainder of the flying time when the cabin pressure altitude exceeds 10,000 ft, but does not exceed 13,000 ft, after the initial 30 minutes at these altitudes. |
   | Supply for | Duration and Cabin Pressure Altitude |
   | 3. 100% of passengers (_) | The entire flying time when the cabin pressure altitude exceeds 15,000 ft, but in no case less than 10 minutes’ supply. |
   | 4. 30% of passengers (_) | The entire flying time when the cabin pressure altitude exceeds 14,000 ft, but does not exceed 15,000 ft. |
   | 5. 10% of passengers (_) | The remainder of the flying time when the cabin pressure altitude exceeds 10,000 ft, but does not exceed 14,000 ft, after the initial 30 minutes at these altitudes. |
   | (_) Passenger numbers Table 1 refer to passengers actually carried on board, including persons younger than 24 months. | |

## 8.8.2.1 Determination of Oxygen AMC1 CAT.IDE.A.235

In the determination of the amount of supplemental oxygen required for the routes to be flown, it is assumed that the aeroplane will descend in accordance with the emergency procedures specified in the operations manual, without exceeding its operating limitations, to a flight altitude that will allow the flight to be completed safely (i.e. flight altitudes ensuring adequate terrain clearance, navigational accuracy, hazardous weather avoidance etc.).

The amount of supplemental oxygen shall be determined on the basis of cabin pressure altitude, flight duration and on the assumption that a cabin pressurisation failure will occur at the pressure altitude or point of flight that is most critical from the standpoint of oxygen need.

- The amount of oxygen required for Flight Crew, including all persons in the cockpit supplied from the Flight Crew oxygen supply source is as specified in Operations manual Part B, Vol 1, Performance Dispatch and MEL Sec 35.

Following a cabin pressurisation failure, the cabin pressure altitude shall be considered to be the same as the aeroplane pressure altitude.

If a flight deck Oxygen mask has been removed make a Technical Log entry. Do not attempt to clean a flight deck Oxygen mask visor without consulting engineering.

## 8.8.2.2 Supplemental Oxygen – Pressurised Aeroplanes (AMC2 CAT.IDE.A.235)

OXYGEN REQUIREMENTS FOR FLIGHT CREW COMPARTMENT SEAT OCCUPANTS AND CABIN CREW IN ADDITION TO THE REQUIRED MINIMUM NUMBER OF CABIN CREW.

1. For the purpose of supplemental oxygen supply, flight crew compartment seat occupants who are:

- supplied with oxygen from the flight crew source of oxygen will be considered as flight crew members; and
- not supplied with oxygen by the flight crew source of oxygen will be considered as passengers.

2. Cabin crew members in addition to the minimum number of cabin crew and additional crew members will be considered as passengers for the purpose of supplemental oxygen supply.

The amount of supplemental oxygen required can be determined from data provided in the AOM.

The oxygen supply is calculated in compliance with applicable regulations. (CAT.IDE.A.235)

There shall be a sufficient number of dispensing units, but in no case less than two, with a means for cabin crew to use the supply.

The first-aid oxygen equipment shall be capable of generating a mass flow to each user of at least 4 litres STPD per minute.

The location of the first-aid oxygen bottles is given in the Safety and Emergency Procedures Manual, and Part B, Vol 2, Chap 1. Passengers can pre order supplemental oxygen. To accommodate this requirement all aircraft are fitted with 2 extra Oxygen bottles, Ryanair aircraft have 6 bottles on board.

The amount of first-aid oxygen required is specified in the MEL. Also an additional amount of first-aid oxygen required as specified in the MEL is carried in accordance with CAT.IDE.A.230.

When Oxygen is used this is entered in the Cabin defects Log, the SCCM will advise the Commander to include this entry in the Technical Log. If no engineer is available at a destination the Commander shall contact Maintrol and raise a HIL on Maintrol instructions. Nothing in this procedure shall prevent any passenger receiving as much oxygen as is required for medicinal or emergency reasons.

## 8.9 Procedures Related to the Use of Type B EFB Applications

## 8.9.1 Electronic Flight Bag

Ryanair has approval for the use of Type B applications for portable Electronic Flight Bag (EFB) in accordance with CAT.GEN.MPA.140.

The EFB (C-PED) is a company issued and authorised device, approved for use in non-transmitting mode only during flight so as to not affect the performance of aircraft systems, equipment or the ability to operate the aircraft. Procedures in FCOM Vol 1 require the EFB to be switched to ‘AIRPLANE MODE’ prior to pushback to comply with this requirement.

As part of the approval process both the EFB hardware and software have undergone a comprehensive safety risk assessment. Human–Machine Interface (HMI) is also evaluated. Additionally, operational trials were conducted to identify potential malfunctions and failure scenarios, analyse their operational effects, and, if necessary, propose mitigation means which are contained later in this section.

The internal EFB Policy and Procedures Manual contains procedures for the EFB Administrator to manage and maintain the EFB, and each EFB function. EFB Flight Crew training is conducted in accordance with OMD 2.1.38 and 3.1.36.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

Crew members must ensure that the EFB is charged and in good condition for operational use. This includes battery, screen and overall condition. Crew are expected to maintain the unit in the protective casing provided.

Utilisation of device ‘sleep mode’ is recommended whenever possible. This is a battery conservation technique.

The following EFB hardware is approved for operational use:

- iPad Air
- iPad Air 2
- iPad 9.7'
- iPad 10.2'

The following Type B applications are approved for operational use:

- Electronic Flight Log (EFL)
- Boeing OPT (Performance)
- Boeing OPT (Mass and Balance)
- Jeppesen FD Pro (Navigational Charts)
- Docunet
- Flight Dispatch Folder (FDF)

New App versions are advised to crew via memo. Current App versions are listed in the EFB Admin update emails.

AMC1 CAT.POL.MAB.105(c)

Electronic Signature is required for submission of eLID, EFL and OPT Weight and Balance. The use of the PIN code is, from a legal and responsibility point of view, considered to be fully equivalent to signature by hand.

## Management of Change

AMC1 ORO.GEN.200(a)(3)(e)

Ryanair has a requirement to manage changes to existing systems, equipment, programmes, products and services; and introduction of new equipment or procedures.

page 8-382

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

Hazards may inadvertently be introduced into the operation whenever change occurs. Effective safety risk management requires that hazards that are a by-product of change are systematically and proactively identified and that strategies to manage the safety risks of the consequences of hazards are developed, implemented and subsequently evaluated.

Modifications to an EFB may have to be introduced, either by the EFB system suppliers, the EFB applications developers, or by the operator itself. The modifications which:

1. Do not bring any change to the calculation algorithm and/or to the HMI of a type B application,
2. Introduce a new Type A app or modify an existing one (provided its software classification remains Type A),
3. Do not introduce any additional functionality to an existing Type B application, or
4. Update an existing database necessary to use an existing Type B,
5. May be introduced by the operator without the need to notify the competent authority. These changes should, nevertheless, be controlled and properly tested prior to use in flight.

The modifications in the following non-exhaustive list are considered to meet these criteria:

1. Operating system updates;
2. Chart or airport database update;
3. Update to introduce fixes (patch); and
4. Type A application installation and modification.

For all other types of modification, the operator should apply the change management procedure approved by the competent authority in accordance with rule ARO.GEN.310(c). Chapter 3 of the Ryanair Management Safety Manual documents the change management process.

The formal process for the management of change takes into account the criticality of the EFB and Type B applications. Criticality is closely related to safety risk. Criticality relates to the potential consequences of failing to properly manage the change. It may relate to the consequence of equipment being improperly operated or an activity being incorrectly executed.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

Determination of which process and activities are safety critical is normally a consideration that should be made during the system design process; however it becomes relevant during a situation of change. Clearly, some activities are more essential for safe delivery of services than others.

Equipment and activities that have higher safety criticality should be reviewed as part of the change process to ensure that appropriate mitigation actions are taken to control potentially emerging safety risks.

## Change Impact Assessment Procedure

1. Identify the nature and scope of the changes (e.g. Algorithm changes, HMI changes, New Type B applications, Type B functionality changes, Type B database changes, Operating system changes, Hardware changes);
2. Identify hazards related to implementing the proposed change and their possible consequences;

## 8.9.1.1 Use of Mounting Device

There is an EFB mounting device fitted to the sliding window in the flight deck. EFB may be used during all phases of flight provided it is retained in its mounting device during critical phases of flight (Taxi out/Takeoff/Climb/Descent/Approach/Taxi in). Crew may remove the device from its mount during pre-flight briefing on the ground and for approach briefings during cruise. The EFB will not normally be handled below 1000 ft AGL.

**Note:** If the mounting device is not available, then crew must place the EFB in their flight bag during critical phases of flight.

## 8.9.1.2 EFB Flight Crew Operating Procedures

Any EFB defects or degradation of any kind must be reported as soon as practical via Zendesk or the dedicated EFB support line in order to ensure no delays to the operation occur.

## 8.9.1.3 EFB Pre-flight Procedures

Crew must satisfy themselves that the EFB device is suitable for operational use during pre-flight preparation.

Applications to be used during flight will be opened at check-in to confirm their availability. The database validity for all operational applications must be checked against the validity published in the Crew Alert section of the Lido briefing pack.

page 8-384

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## 8.9.1.4 EFB Normal Cockpit Procedures

Normal cockpit procedures allow the use of a single EFB for Ryanair operations. The F/O's EFB is considered the primary device for operational reference. Both crew are permitted to reference individual EFBs for appropriate phases of flight. PF shall handover control of the aircraft when referencing the EFB to ensure that flight crew members do not become preoccupied with the EFB system at the same time.

Pre-flight performance calculations shall normally be performed using both EFB's. The Captain and co-pilot must independently perform the take-off performance calculation and inputs to confirm accuracy. If any differences are evident between the separate calculations then the reasons for the difference must be established.

During taxi, the taxi charts shall always be active on the co-pilots EFB.

Operating policy is not to have two heads in the cockpit at any time. EFB may be referenced at any time once this policy is adhered to.

## 8.9.1.5 EFB Phase of Flight Procedures

Briefings shall be conducted with the EFB 'in hand' in order to promote CRM. The mounting device provided shall be used to stow the EFB during all critical phases of flight. The PM may have the SID charts active during departure, and the Approach charts active during arrival if required.

## 8.9.1.6 Contingency or Abnormal Procedures

If any system malfunction (e.g. display unit interference) is encountered that could be related to the EFB device being active, immediately disconnect and switch off the device.

Devices can overheat when left in direct sunlight for extended periods of time, rendering the device unusable. EFB operating temperature (0° to 35°C) will be adhered to in order to ensure operability. The EFB will be mounted when not in use. It is not permissible to store the device on the flight deck glareshield.

## Downloading to FDF App:

Select the required document within the FDF App to download. A grey icon indicates that the document has not been downloaded yet. A green icon indicates that the document is up to date. An orange icon indicates that an updated version of the document is available for download. If you see an orange icon, you must download the required document prior to use.

## 8.9.1.6.3 Single EFB Failure Prior to Departure

Ryanair operations are authorised for single EFB use. However, if an EFB becomes inoperative prior to departure, a replacement will be sought in the base. If a replacement EFB is not available, then crew will follow the steps contained in the ‘EFB Contingency Flow Chart’ to determine an alternative means of compliance.

For single EFB operations, when the F/O has completed the calculation and noted the speeds, the OPT App will be reset and the serviceable EFB will be handed to the Captain. The Captain and co-pilot must independently perform the take-off performance calculation to confirm accuracy.

## 8.9.1.6.4 Single EFB Failure During Flight

Should one EFB become inoperative during flight, then the flight may be continued using the second EFB. Ryanair operations are authorised for single EFB dispatch.

## 8.9.1.6.5 Dual EFB Failure Prior to Departure

Should both EFBs become inoperative prior to departure, replacements will be sought in the base. If one replacement EFB is available, then crew will follow guidance contained in ‘Single EFB failure prior to departure’ above. If no replacement EFBs are available, then crew will follow the steps contained in the ‘EFB Contingency Flow Chart’ to determine an alternative means of compliance. Paper Trip Kits are authorised for Ryanair operations. Should the dual EFB failure occur out of base, contact Ryanair operations. The crew will be provided with a replacement EFB device or paper backup documentation.

An ASR shall be filed in the event of dual EFB failure.

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

## 8.9.1.6.6 Dual EFB Failure During Flight

Should both EFBs become inoperative during flight, with no back up paper charts available on board, crew should use available FMC information and obtain necessary enroute or approach details from the ATC unit on VHF to allow safe approach and landing at the destination/diversion airport. Crew will also be aware that much of this information required for landing is readily available in the FMC. FMC info may be used in this case to limit the time spent transmitting on a potentially busy frequency. Landing performance data shall be extracted from the QRH Performance Inflight section.

An ASR shall be filed in the event of dual EFB failure.

## 8.9.1.6.7 EFB Not Charged Prior to Departure (Both Aircraft USB Chargers Unavailable)

Portable EFB batteries shall normally be charged above 70% at the pre-flight stage. USB chargers are installed on all aircraft. However, with both USB chargers unavailable, and, with pre-flight EFB battery levels below 70%, the chart below should be used to determine the minimum required EFB battery charge.

| Flight Duty Period | Minimum Battery Power % |
| ------------------ | ----------------------- |
| < 6 hours          | 60 %                    |
| < 5 hours          | 50 %                    |
| < 4 hours          | 40 %                    |
| < 3 hours          | 30 %                    |
| < 2 hours          | 20 %                    |

(Moderate use of the EFB will result in approx. 7% battery discharge per hour). Should either device have less than the minimum battery required for your duty and with both aircraft USB chargers unavailable, crew will follow the steps contained in the ‘EFB Contingency Flow Chart – EFB(s) unavailable’ to determine an alternative means of compliance.

## 8.9.1.7 In-flight Charging

The internal battery is the primary power supply for the EFB. A USB power supply is located on each side of the flight deck if required. EFB may be connected to the USB power supply. Charging is allowed during all phases of flight.

The EFB being charged must be secured in the mounting device.

The only approved source for inflight charging is the installed USB power supply.

## 8.9.1.8 Post Flight Procedures

Crew shall ensure the relevant flight documents have been forwarded to the appropriate recipients.

The crew will check to ensure that the following documentation which has been previously submitted electronically prior to dispatch has been acknowledged:

Note: Flight information must be submitted by the PM at the end of each sector. Flight information must be verified by the Captain at the end of each duty.

1.  OPT take-off performance calculations (Including Weight and Balance).
2.  Electronic Flight Log (EFL).

Any EFB hardware or software defects shall be promptly reported via Zendesk or the dedicated EFB support line as appropriate.

## 8.9.1.9 Boeing OPT (On Board Performance Tool) Application

The Boeing OPT is a software application for computing aircraft mass and balance, all engine climb gradient, take-off and landing performance. OPT calculates “real-time” RTOWs for all actual conditions existing at time of departure, such as variations of aircraft configuration, ambient conditions and runway length. The airport, runway, take-off obstacle database and aircraft weights and W&amp;B envelope is maintained and administrated by the Ryanair Performance Department. OPT allows crew to adjust performance for MEL &amp; CDL items. Flight crew may also enter airport/runway &amp; NOTAM information that is unavailable in the database. Dispatch landing distances, In-flight landing performance data and QRH non normal configuration landing distances are also available for use. Crew must ensure the application content is up to date prior to flight by crosschecking database validity against the Crew Alert section of the OFP.

## 8.9.1.10 Jeppesen (Electronic Charting) Application

The Jeppesen FD Pro App is an electronic charting application providing terminal and enroute aeronautical charts as well as the associated airways manual. The application also hosts Airfield Briefing (AFB) documents as published by the performance department. These are available via the INFO/WX tab on the Jeppesen FD Pro App. Crew must ensure the application content is up to date

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

prior to flight by crosschecking database validity against the Crew Alert section of the OFP. Updates will occur with the 28-day AIRAC cycle and mid cycle revisions. Crew can expect to update their Jeppesen FD Pro App database weekly.

Note: The use of Jeppesen IFW (In-Flight Weather) should not be used to support tactical decisions and/or as a substitute for certified aircraft systems (e.g., weather radar)

## 8.9.1.11 Docunet (Electronic Manuals) Application

Flight manuals will be stored in an electronic format accessible on the EFB device. The Docunet App is the primary reference for all manuals except the QRH. Crews must use the paper QRH when operationally necessary. Should crew need to reference the QRH on the EFB, ensure the screen displays the full content, selecting the ‘fit page to screen’ icon within the App if necessary. Electronic manual revisions will be in the form of a file comprising the entire revised manual or section. Crew will be advised when a revision to a manual has been made and available to download.

## 8.9.1.12 Flight Dispatch Folder (FDF) Application

The FDF App is a secondary source of flight information for crew. FDF provides access to Performance (RTOWs and AFBs), Navigational (Terminal and Enroute Charts) and Flight Documentation (Flight Manuals) required for dispatch. Should any operational App become unavailable, then crew should follow the guidance in 8.9.1.6.1 (EFB Contingency Flow Chart – App(s) unavailable). If FDF is required for use as a contingency measure, then crew must select the required flight documentation within the FDF App and download prior to flight. The FDF App should only be used in the event of any failure that prevents flight crew from accessing or updating operational application data (Boeing OPT, or Docunet).

## 8.9.1.13 Electronic Flight Log (EFL) Application

The EFL App provides required flight information in electronic format. The EFL must be completed correctly and verified by the Commander on completion of the series of sectors which constitute his assigned flight duty. The EFL contains details of flight times, delay reports, fuel data, FDT, DT, HIL and weather data and electronic briefing pack. The electronic flight plan (eFPL) is accessed within the EFL App and submitted to the storage database electronically at the end of flight. EFL contains all required flight documentation; OFP (eFPL), ATS flight plan, weather, NOTAMs, company NOTAMs, crew alerts, additional info, RAIM, significant weather charts, upper air data charts, and vertical profile information.

## 8.10 Emergency and Special Operating Procedures

### 8.10.1 General Criteria to Improve Safety and Handle Emergencies

Ryanair operating philosophy and Ryanair operational policies are presented in FCOM Vol 1 and QRH introduction, and additional guidance is provided in the Boeing FCTM, Chapter 8, Non Normal Operations.

### 8.10.2 International Emergency Procedures, Distress Communications

The OMC, Jeppesen Publications (Pubs) &gt; General Airway Manual &gt; Emergency Data – ICAO &gt; Distress and Urgency Radiotelephony Communication Procedures contains a section which gathers together extracts from the various ICAO publications on Emergency Procedures.

The section covers:

- Emergency Procedures,
- Unlawful Interference (Special Emergency),
- Emergency Descent,
- Distress and Urgency R.T. Communications Procedures,
- Communications Failure,
- Interception, and
- Search and Rescue.

The section also identifies variations to these procedures which are specific to individual countries.

Flight Crew members shall have a general understanding and appreciation of these procedures, and in addition some specific knowledge of certain items including distress frequencies, transponder “squawk” codes used in these procedures, and the Communication Failure procedures as well as the Distress and Urgency message format.

### 8.10.2.1 Distress Procedures

These procedures are presented in the OMC, Jeppesen Publications (Pubs) &gt; General Airway Manual &gt; Emergency Data – ICAO, Emergency section and ATC Section.

### 8.10.2.1.1 Intercepting a Distress Call

These procedures are presented in the OMC, Jeppesen Publications (Pubs) &gt; General Airway Manual &gt; Emergency Data – ICAO, Emergency section and ATC Section.

## 8.10.2.2 Urgency Procedures

These procedures are presented in the OMC, Jeppesen Publications (Pubs) &gt; _General Airway Manual &gt; Emergency Data – ICAO_, Emergency section and ATC Section.

## 8.10.2.3 Information on Emergency and Survival Equipment Carried (AMC1 CAT.GEN.MPA.145)

Ryanair shall ensure that there are available for immediate communication to rescue coordination centres, lists containing information on the emergency and survival equipment carried on board all of our aeroplanes. The information shall include, as applicable, the number, colour and type of life-rafts and pyrotechnics, details of emergency medical supplies, water supplies and the type and frequencies of emergency portable radio equipment.

## 8.10.3 Declaration of Emergency, Alerting ATC to Aircraft Problems

“MAYDAY MAYDAY MAYDAY Ryanair call sign”

Squawk 7700.

## 8.10.3.1 Reporting Malfunctions or System Failures

Any aircraft malfunction or system failure which significantly or potentially degrades the normal levels of safety for the continuation of the flight shall be reported to ATC either on an advisory basis or because facilitation or assistance may be required.

Additionally, the degree of severity of other problems such as an impending fuel situation or environmental difficulty (turbulence, thunderstorms, icing etc.) warrant contact with ATC and possible facilitation or assistance in terms of a change of clearance or flight plan.

It is not practically possible to give guidance on how to deal with every abnormal or emergency situation. Once actions have been taken to bring the immediate problems under control and to stabilise the situation, then judgement will dictate whether the subsequent follow-up action should be precautionary or an emergency should be declared.

Certain situations may become more serious with the passing of time (classically, for example, an impending fuel shortage) and it is important that in such a deteriorating situation a timely emergency declaration shall be made.

An emergency situation is one in which there is a real potential for an accident and where the aircraft requires facilitation and assistance and priority over other traffic in order to minimise the potential risk.

## 8.10.3.2 Situations Warranting Declaration of an Emergency

A situation in which the aircraft may not be able to reach a suitable airfield for landing is clearly a full emergency. Other cases where the Commander has cause to doubt the aircraft's serviceability to land, or where a warning circuit gives evidence of hazard, shall also be treated as an emergency. Typical examples would be:

1. Unsafe landing gear indications or wheel or tyre damage;
2. Suspected complete brake failure;
3. Indications of fire or smoke in the passenger cabin or freight compartment;
4. Unexplainable adverse response from fly controls or degradation in aircraft control capability;
5. Complete electrical generator failure; and
6. Insufficient instrument/navigation capability for the approach.

Where it is considered to be an emergency situation, the Commander will declare an emergency with ATC and indicate his intentions and the assistance required.

Given the nature of the emergency ATC can be expected to have the airport emergency services fully deployed and take any further action considered appropriate.

For SSR procedures, refer to Jeppesen Publications (Pubs) &gt; General Airway Manual &gt; ATC &gt; Flight Procedures SSR Transponder Operating Procedures &gt; Emergency Procedures.

## 8.10.3.3 Actions to be Taken After Declaration of Emergency

In the case of an emergency, the Commander will take the following actions:

1. Direct the Cabin Crew to prepare for an emergency landing;
2. Advise passengers on the P.A. of the situation (time available);
3. Brief the co-pilot on his required function and elaborate on his intentions;
4. Determine whether a delay in landing is necessary to allow full deployment of ground emergency services;
5. Where possible, ensure that the full procedures detailed in the appropriate QRH checklist have been completed. (Don't rush the process if there is no benefit from an early landing. If time available, manage the situation;
6. Decide and brief the crew on emergency procedures to be followed after landing; and
7. Safety harnesses tight and locked for landing.

# 8.10.3.4 Precautionary Landing

8.10.3.4.1 A precautionary landing is one where the Commander considers the fire, rescue/medical services might be required after landing, but the aircraft is expected to be capable of manoeuvring under its own power. Typical examples would include:

1. Single engine landing;
2. Hydraulic failure, but leaving the aircraft with the standby brake system;
3. Landing gear unsafe light ON but mechanically visual indicators showing locked down;
4. Flapless landing;
5. A fire warning, but apart from the light no other indications of fire.

If it is considered that the situation requires only precautionary actions to be taken, then advise ATC of the nature of the malfunction or problem and to what extent the operation of the aircraft is constrained or limited.

If required, indicate what facilitation or assistance would be needed or helpful.

If it is considered desirable for fire and rescue services to stand-by, do not refer to them as the "emergency services". According to the procedures of the airport authority these services may be deployed to the landing runway.

# 8.10.3.4.2 Actions in the Event of a Precautionary Landing

In the case of a precautionary landing the Commander will take the following actions:

1. Inform the Cabin Staff of the situation and that action shall be limited to full observance of the normal landing checks;
2. Advise the Cabin Crew what if anything he intends to say to passengers and the element of discretion needed in dealing with passengers;
3. Advise the Cabin Crew of any procedures required after landing and that they may or may not expect to see the fire and rescue services in attendance;
4. Ensure that the abnormal or emergency procedure outlined in the Emergency Checklist have been completed. (Don't rush the process. Time is expected to be available so it shall be used to manage the situation – e.g. extend the approach/circuit as appropriate, see Section 8.10.1);
5. Based on the nature of the unserviceability determine with ATC, any facilitation required whether an approach priority is desirable or whether a delay would facilitate deployment of fire and rescue services;

page 8-394

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

6. If it is considered necessary to inform passengers, care will be taken not to over-dramatise the situation or use operational jargon. A lot of passengers get upset with the mention of malfunction and systems not working as they have no concept of the use of back-up or standby systems. Any announcement shall convey a sense of routine.

## 8.10.3.5 Actions after Emergency/Precautionary Landing

After an emergency or precautionary landing, the Commander will inform ATC whether he can manoeuvre under his own power and whether he wishes to dispense with the fire and rescue services.

In some cases, an “escort” of the fire services to the ramp may be necessary or desirable but in other cases this practice is neither necessary nor desirable.

In cases of fire or smoke indication, the airport procedures could call for the situation to be dealt with away from terminal buildings so that the aircraft may be required to hold its position.

The Commander must keep abreast of the situation which can be confused by a three-way relationship involving ATC and the fire services, and continually assess the possible need for an emergency evacuation.

## 8.10.3.6 Reporting

“All aircraft shall report to the appropriate aeronautical communications station, as soon as possible, any hazardous flight conditions encountered during a flight” – Article 44 Air Navigation (Operations) Order, 1986.

The report shall include such details as may be pertinent to the safety of other aircraft.

After landing all crew will consult with the Commander to ensure that all pertinent information is captured, and affording the Commander the opportunity to debrief the crew prior to any other intervention/distraction.

Then the Commander shall file a ASR giving all relevant details of the emergency, malfunction and the subsequent action taken including the emergency or precautionary landing.

## 8.10.4.0.1 Public Address System

8.10.4.0.1.1 Ryanair shall not operate an aeroplane with a maximum approved passenger seating configuration of more than 19 unless a public address system is installed.

8.10.4.0.1.2 The public address system required by this paragraph must:

1. Operate independently of the interphone systems except for handsets, headsets, microphones, selector switches and signalling devices;
2. Be readily accessible for immediate use from each required Flight Crew member station;
3. For each required floor level passenger emergency exit which has an adjacent cabin crew seat, have a microphone which is readily accessible to the seated cabin crew member, except that one microphone may serve more than one exit, provided the proximity of the exits allows unassisted verbal communication between seated cabin crew members;
4. Be capable of operation within 10 seconds by a cabin crew member at each of those stations in the compartment from which its use is accessible; and
5. Be audible and intelligible at all passenger seats, toilets and cabin crew seats and work stations.

## 8.10.5 Bird Strike on Final Approach

8.10.5.1 An Aircraft is Hit by Birds While on Final Approach to Land

Should the pilot continue the approach or initiate a go-around?

Having encountered birds, the question to be answered is “what is the damage to the aircraft and what effect will this have on the safe conduct of the flight?”.

The full extent of any damage, to the engines and/or the control surfaces and landing gear, may not be apparent until applying power, configuring, or manoeuvring the aircraft. It might therefore be the case that, if a go-around is initiated, the pilot rapidly finds himself in a situation where the runway is disappearing beneath him but the aircraft cannot safely fly the missed approach procedure.

Therefore, in the above scenario, it is advisable to continue the approach to land.

## 8.10.5.2 Flocks of Birds Seen on Final Approach

A pilot sees a flock of birds ahead of him on final approach. Should the pilot continue the approach or initiate the go-around? Having seen the birds, the question to be answered is “if a go-around is initiated, how likely is it that the aircraft will avoid a bird strike?” There are two matters to consider. Firstly, when a flock of birds perceives a threat, it typically moves upwards, potentially into the path of the aircraft initiating a go-around. Secondly, the greater the engine thrust,

page 8-396

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

the greater the damage caused by ingesting birds – it is probable that less damage will be caused if the birds are hit while engines are at low speed or idle. Therefore, in the scenario described above, unless a go-around can be achieved with a responsible degree of confidence that the aircraft will not hit birds, it is less hazardous to continue that approach to land.

## 8.10.7 ATC Avoidance Manoeuvers at High ALT

Guidance for high altitude high speed flight is given in FCTM, chapter 3 and guidance for optimum altitude in FCOM Vol 1.

1.  10° bank shall be selected at FL 300 for operations above FL300, and 25° bank shall be selected below FL 300. If the airfield brief specifies bank angle for an approach/go-around, this shall be selected as appropriate.
2.  ATC Avoidance manoeuvre at high altitude in response to phrases “Avoiding Action” and “immediately” shall result in the following actions by RYR crews:

- Execute the required action immediately and acknowledge the instruction.
- Do NOT disengage the AutoThrottle or AutoPilot in the execution of the ATC instruction.
- HDG mode shall be immediately engaged and the required heading selected.
- The Bank Angle selector shall not be changed to achieve increased bank angle.
- The aircraft shall always be in a position to respond to a RA, i.e. not conducting a manoeuvre that may lead to an excursion from the flight envelope at high altitude.
- Radius of turn required for a controlled turn is 15nm at or below FL 190 and 22.5 nm at or above FL 200. 10 deg bank at or above FL 300 satisfies the 22.5 nm radius of turn requirement.

# 8.10.8 Medical Emergency in Flight

The primary task is to conduct the flight safely and then to endeavour to present the patient in a suitable airport as safely and as expeditiously as possible. The following guidance applies:

1. Flight deck advised by Cabin Crew.

Give:

- Age.
- Sex.
- Chief complaint.
- Level of consciousness.

**Note:** This allows medics to take the correct equipment to the passenger/patient and minimise any delay in the treatment or management of their condition once on the ground.

2. PAN × 3, inform ATC that “MEDICAL SERVICES” are required. ATC may assign a discrete frequency. In this case PM deals with discrete frequency, PF has control of the aircraft and operational ATC.

3. ATC will usually seek a report on the patient, this information may be passed to medical professionals, e.g. MEDAIRE. A VHF patch on discrete frequency may be established to enable crew to receive professional medical advice.

4. Plan to land at the nearest suitable airport. In this context the Ryanair network in Europe is extensive and resourced to deal with these events. The most suitable airport is a Ryanair Base, followed by a Ryanair destination. If neither of these is in equivalent proximity to other suitable airports then consider the closest reasonably large airport. It is probably prudent to avoid very large airports especially those which the crews are fundamentally unfamiliar.

5. Consider the level of medical service likely to be available, estimated time to landing (including direct routings from ATC). Consider crew familiarity with the intended destination. It may be quicker to fly a little farther to a familiar destination than to take a short cut to an unfamiliar airport.

6. The flight shall continue to be conducted at all times in accordance with the terms of the OMA and FCOM Vol I. A Medical emergency shall not be considered a reason to rush an approach or deviate from Ryanair SOP.

7. When time and operational conditions permit, the Flight Crew shall, after landing, confirm with the Tower that the medical services have been alerted.

8. It is likely the patient has consumed oxygen and this may need to be replenished prior to next departure.

9. When the aircraft arrives at the stand CSS shall make a PA requiring all passengers to remain seated so that the paramedic can access the aircraft and commence treatment on the passenger/patient. Disembarkation can commence via exits not involving the medics/patient.

## 8.10.8.1 Deceased Passenger

Where a passenger is pronounced dead on board (by a Doctor/Nurse) the decision for the crew is to return to departure point, divert or continue to destination. Operational experience shows that returning to departure point or continuing to destination provide the best level of support for family members. Diverting to an alternate will require the deceased passenger to be disembarked along with traveling companion at a location that was not part of any travel arrangements and can lead to additional stress at what will be inevitably a difficult time for passengers and crew.

## 8.10.9 Laser or High-Powered Light Exposure

If the aircraft is illuminated (attacked) by a laser or high-powered lighting, the following actions and precautions are recommended:

- Maintain aircraft control. Consider re-engaging the autopilot (if disengaged) and/or transferring aircraft control to the pilot less affected or less likely to be affected by the laser attack.
- Do not look into the beam and shield your eyes to the maximum extent possible. Note that even a low power “eye-safe laser” can impair vision for up to 8 minutes.
- If the laser or high-powered light strikes the vision of the PF, turn your head away, look down, and transfer aircraft control.
- Increase cockpit lighting to mitigate the laser beam’s impact on vision.
- If on approach and landing is not assured, consider executing a missed approach.
- Inform ATC as soon as possible, especially if diverging from the cleared flight path.
- If vision is impacted, avoid rubbing eyes to reduce the potential for corneal abrasion.

Following the laser attack and when flight safety permits:

- Advise ATC and include as much detail as possible (e.g., description of the laser’s/light’s location, direction of beam, colour, and the length of exposure).

# 8.10.10 Global Aeronautical Distress and Safety System (GADSS)

CAT.IDE.A.190, 280, 285, CAT.GEN.MPA.205, 210

Regulation (EU) 2015/2338, Global Aeronautical Distress and Safety System (GADSS) delivers aircraft tracking (AT), autonomous distress tracking (ADT), and post flight localization and recovery (PFLR).

The GADSS objectives are to ensure:

- timely detection of aircraft in distress
- tracking of aircraft in distress and timely, accurate determination of end-of-flight location
- timely initiation and accurate direction of SAR actions
- efficient and effective SAR operations
- timely retrieval of Flight Recorder Data

The Ryanair Operations Control Centre shall maintain oversight and ensure operational compliance with respect to GADSS regulation. Ryanair’s Operations Control Centre shall identify and coordinate GADSS requirements when generating operational flight plans, and ensure AT, ADT, PFLR compliance when the flight planed route transits through airspace which does not provide required four-dimensional tracking services at regular time intervals (less than 15 minutes) which includes:

- aircraft’s latitude and longitude,
- altitude,
- and time at that position.

## 8.10.10.1 General Description

Ryanair DAC maintains, as part of the system for exercising operational control over the flights, an aircraft tracking system via GlobalBeacon℠ systems and service contracts.

Ryanair’s flights shall be tracked from take-off to landing, except when the planned route and the planned diversion routes remain within airspace whereby:

1. ATS surveillance service is normally provided which is supported by ATC surveillance systems locating the aircraft at time intervals with adequate duration; and
2. the operator has provided to competent air navigation service providers necessary contact information.

Ryanair’s aircraft, tracking systems, and associated procedures comply with Regulation (EU) 2015/2338 requirements to ensure four-dimensional tracking (latitude, longitude, altitude, and time) when operating within designated airspace.

## 8.10.10.2 Operational Control and Response (GADSS)

GADSS compliance is an Operations Control function and requires no additional crew procedures.

Ryanair Operations Control Centre is responsible for scheduling, equipage, notification, tracking, and response actions associated with aircraft operating in GADSS-affected airspace, as outlined in the Operations Control Centre Manual.

### Aircraft Tracking

Aircraft tracking systems and services provide four-dimensional tracking (latitude, longitude, altitude, and time) via:

- Automated position reporting at least every 15 minutes
- Latitude, longitude, altitude and time stamp available (as a minimum)
- Latency from plane to operational control max 10 minutes
- Maintenance of operational control
- Recording and preservation aircraft tracking data

### Autonomous Distress Tracking

In response to recognised events (e.g., significant divergence from flight plan/flight level, high rates of descent, or loss of thrust on both engines), ADT systems alert the Operations Control Centre and transmits AT information at

## Post Flight Localization and Recovery

CAT.IDE.A.190, 280, 285

GADSS information management and PFLR procedures are outlined within the Ryanair Operations Control Manual and integrated into the Emergency Response Plan.

Ryanair DAC aircraft are equipped with robust and automatic means via EASA specification components to accurately determine, following an accident where the aeroplane is severely damaged, the aircraft’s end-of-flight location:

- Ryanair aircraft flight recorders and cockpit voice recorders are fitted with EASA-compliant, 90-day transmitting Underwater Locator Beacons.
- When scheduled on HLA routings after January 1st 2019, Ryanair Operations Control Centre will ensure the equipage of EASA-compliant, Low-Frequency Underwater Locating Devices. The device’s installation, location (above aft galley ceiling panels, next to flight data recorder), and operation are independent of the crew. Aircraft equipped with Low-Frequency Underwater Locating Devices are identifiable by Technical Log Notice to Crew: ‘THIS AIRCRAFT IS FITTED WITH A FIXED LOW FREQUENCY UNDERWATER LOCATING DEVICE. INSTALLED IAW ERT-SB-1612’.

## 8.10.11 Rapid Disembarkation

A non-normal situation may occur at any time while boarding/disembarking. Rapid disembarkation is a precautionary egress from the aircraft in situations assessed by the Commander or in his absence the FO or CSS (following the chain of command) as deviating from normal conditions but not being an immediate emergency, i.e. not posing an immediate threat to passengers and crew members on board, but which may escalate into an emergency.

A rapid disembarkation occurs with the aircraft parked on stand and an immediate disembarkation is required using airstairs/air bridge or attached stairs. Rapid disembarkation shall be applied when a non-normal situation arises that requires an expeditious disembarkation but is not immediately life-threatening.

Possible reasons for a rapid disembarkation include a security alert, smoke or fumes in the cabin, overheating PEDs, a fuel spillage, a fire outside the aircraft which could threaten the aircraft, or any situation that the Commander, FO or CSS decides that the passengers should leave the aircraft urgently.

page 8-402

Issue: 22, Revision: 0, Date: 01/01/26

RYANAIR

PART A – OPERATIONS MANUAL

The Command to commence a Rapid Disembarkation is similar to that of an evacuation.

“Disembark the passengers immediately using all available doors, disembark the passengers immediately using all available doors.”

The cabin crew actions will be to stop the boarding if it is in progress and disembark passengers using the aircraft doors, but not the overwing exits or escape slides.

- L1 door using Airbridge, mobile steps or Airstairs as appropriate,
- L2 door if mobile steps are available,
- R2/R1 door if an Ambulift is available and there are PRMs on board.

The cabin crew will instruct the passengers to leave their belongings behind. The aim will be to disembark the passengers as quickly as possible but in a controlled manner not using the slides.

At ALL times the Commander or CSS retains the authority to upgrade to a full evacuation if they decides that the situation has deteriorated and has now become immediately life threatening.

They will make the Evacuation PA and in response the Cabin Crew will deploy the available escape slide(s).

Communications with ATC and members of ground staff are critical during this event.
